A Functional (Monadic) Second-Order Theory of Infinite Trees
Anupam Das, Colin Riba

Abstract
. This paper presents a complete axiomatization of Monadic Second-Order Logic ( 𝖬𝖲𝖮 \mathsf{MSO} ) over infinite trees. 𝖬𝖲𝖮 \mathsf{MSO} on infinite trees is a rich system, and its decidability (“Rabin’s Tree Theorem”) is one of the most powerful known results concerning the decidability of logics. By a complete axiomatization we mean a complete deduction system with a polynomial-time recognizable set of axioms. By naive enumeration of formal derivations, this formally gives a proof of Rabin’s Tree Theorem. The deduction system consists of the usual rules for second-order logic seen as two-sorted first-order logic, together with the natural adaptation to infinite trees of the axioms of 𝖬𝖲𝖮 \mathsf{MSO} on ω \omega -words. In addition, it contains an axiom scheme expressing the (positional) determinacy of certain parity games. The main difficulty resides in the limited expressive power of the language of 𝖬𝖲𝖮 \mathsf{MSO} . We actually devise an extension of 𝖬𝖲𝖮 \mathsf{MSO} , called Functional (Monadic) Second-Order Logic ( 𝖥𝖲𝖮 \mathsf{FSO} ), which allows us to uniformly manipulate (hereditarily) finite sets and corresponding labeled trees, and whose language allows for higher abstraction than that of 𝖬𝖲𝖮 \mathsf{MSO} .



Mar. 27, 2019Oct. 23, 2020

†
†
lmcs-doi:
Volume 16, Issue 4, Paper 6

1.
Introduction

This paper presents a complete axiomatization of Monadic Second-Order
Logic (
\mathsf{MSO}
) over infinite trees.

\mathsf{MSO}
on infinite trees is a rich system which
contains non trivial mathematical theories (see e.g.
[
Rab69
,
BGG97
]
)
and subsumes many logics,
including modal logics (see e.g.
[
BdRV02
]
)
and logics for verification (see e.g.
[
VW08
]
).
Rabin’s Tree Theorem
[
Rab69
]
, the decidability of
\mathsf{MSO}
on infinite trees,
is one of the most powerful known results concerning the decidability of logics
(see e.g.
[
BGG97
]
).

The original decidability proof of
[
Rab69
]
relied on
an effective translation
of formulae to finite state automata running on infinite trees.
Since then, there has been considerable work
on Rabin’s Tree Theorem, culminating in streamlined decidability proofs,
as presented e.g. in
[
Tho97
,
GTW02
,
PP04
]
.
Most current approaches to
\mathsf{MSO}
on infinite trees
(with the notable exception of
[
Blu13
]
)
are based on translations of formulae to automata.

By a ‘complete axiomatization’ we mean a
complete deduction system
with a
polynomial-time
recognizable set of axioms and rules.
This condition on axiom/rule recognizability is typical in proof theory, where it is known as the
Cook-Reckhow criterion

[
CR79
]
.
The point is that proofs should be ‘easily checkable’,
which rules out axiomatizations based on enumerations of all true formulae.
In this way, a complete axiomatization not only constitutes an alternative demonstration of Rabin’s Tree theorem itself, by naive enumeration of formal derivations, but also yields a meaningful notion of ‘proof certificate’ for theorems.

Our deduction system consists of the usual rules
for second-order logic seen as two-sorted first-order logic
(see e.g.
[
Rib12
]
), together with the natural adaptation
to infinite trees of the axioms of
\mathsf{MSO}
on
\omega
-words
[
Sie70
]
.
In addition, it contains an axiom scheme expressing the (positional)
determinacy of certain parity games.

We continue a line of work begun by Büchi and Siefkes,
who gave axiomatizations of
\mathsf{MSO}
on various classes of linear orders
(see e.g.
[
Sie70
,
BS73
]
),
as well as an axiomatization of
Weak

\mathsf{MSO}
(
\mathsf{WMSO}
) over infinite
trees
[
Sie78
]

(
\mathsf{WMSO}
is
\mathsf{MSO}
with set quantifications restricted to
finite
sets).
These works essentially rely on formalizations of automata in the logic.
A major result in the axiomatic treatment of logics over infinite structures
is Walukiewicz’s proof of completeness of Kozen’s axiomatization
of the modal
\mu
-calculus
[
Wal00
]

(see also
[
AL17
]
for an alternative recent proof of this result).
Another trend relies on model-theoretic techniques.
For instance
[
tCF10
,
GtC12
]

give complete axiomatizations of

\mathsf{MSO}
and the modal
\mu
-calculus over finite trees;
a reworking of the completeness of
\mathsf{MSO}
on
\omega
-words
[
Sie70
]

is proposed in
[
Rib12
]
;
and
[
SV10
]
gives a model-theoretic completeness proof
for a fragment of the modal
\mu
-calculus.
An attractive feature of model-theoretic completeness proofs
for the aforementioned logics
is that they
allow elegant reformulations of algebraic approaches to these logics.
Unfortunately, in the case of
\mathsf{MSO}
over infinite trees,
the only known algebraic approach
[
Blu13
]

seems too complex to be easily formalized.
We therefore directly formalize a translation
of formulae to automata in the axiomatic theory.

Mirroring usual automata based decidability proofs
(see e.g.
[
Tho97
,
GTW02
,
PP04
]
),
our method for proving completeness proceeds in two steps.
We first formalize
a translation of
\mathsf{MSO}
-formulae to tree automata
(using the positional determinacy of parity games to prove the complementation lemma),
so that each closed formula is provably equivalent to an automaton over the
singleton alphabet.
The second (and much shorter) step is a variant of the Büchi-Landweber Theorem
[
BL69
]

which states that
\mathsf{MSO}
decides winning for (definable) games of finite graphs,
and which is obtained thanks to the completeness of
\mathsf{MSO}
over
\omega
-words.

The main expositional difficulty resides in the limited expressive power of the language of
\mathsf{MSO}
.
To ameliorate this we actually devise an extension of
\mathsf{MSO}
,
called
Functional (Monadic) Second-Order Logic
(
\mathsf{FSO}
),
allowing uniform manipulation of (hereditarily) finite sets
and corresponding labeled infinite trees.
We intuitively see
\mathsf{FSO}
as providing a language for higher
abstraction than that of
\mathsf{MSO}
,
allowing a uniform formalization of automata and games
which would have been difficult to write down in
\mathsf{MSO}
.
However, since
\mathsf{FSO}
is interpretable in
\mathsf{MSO}
(as we show), its language
has the same intrinsic limitations as the language
of
\mathsf{MSO}
. In particular it suffers from the inexpressibility of choice over tree
positions
[
GS83
,
CL07
]
,
and so predicates such as length comparison
of tree positions are not expressible in
\mathsf{FSO}
.
This implies that only positional strategies
(w.r.t. our specific notion of acceptance games),
are expressible in
\mathsf{FSO}

and moreover that usually unproblematic reasoning on infinite plays
can become cumbersome in this setting.

There are several ways to translate
\mathsf{MSO}
to tree automata.
We choose to translate formulae to alternating parity automata,
following
[
Wal02
]
.
The two non-trivial steps in the translation are negation and (existential) quantification.
Negation requires the complementation of automata,
relying on the determinacy of acceptance games,
while
existential quantifiers
require us
to simulate an alternating automaton by an equivalent
non-deterministic one
(this is the
Simulation Theorem

[
EJ91
,
MS95
]
),
thence obtaining an automaton computing the appropriate projection.

As usual with translations of
\mathsf{MSO}
to tree automata,
we rely on McNaughton’s Theorem
[
McN66
]

(see also e.g.
[
Tho90
,
PP04
]
),
stating that non-deterministic Büchi automata on
\omega
-words
are effectively equivalent to deterministic
parity
(or Muller, Rabin, Streett) automata
on
\omega
-words.
In translations of
\mathsf{MSO}
to alternating tree automata,
McNaughton’s Theorem is usually invoked for the Simulation Theorem.
1
1

1

The approach of
[
MS95
]

to the Simulation Theorem actually contains a proof of McNaughton Theorem,
but we do not see how to easily formalize it in our context.

In our context, the relevant instances of McNaughton’s Theorem
are imported into
\mathsf{FSO}
via the completeness of
\mathsf{MSO}
on

\omega
-words
[
Sie70
]
.

It is well-known that the
\mathsf{MSO}
theory of
k
-ary trees
can be embedded in that of the binary tree
[
Rab69
]
.
However, it does not seem that such an embedding
yields an axiomatization of
k
-ary trees from an axiomatization of the binary tree.
Therefore, in this work, we axiomatize the
\mathsf{MSO}
theory of the full infinite
\mathscr{D}
-ary
tree for an arbitrary non-empty finite set
\mathscr{D}
.

This paper is a corrected version of
[
DR15
]
,
which contains a flaw in the positional determinacy argument (Thm. VI.15).
In the present paper, we augment the systems
\mathsf{FSO}
and
\mathsf{MSO}

with an axiom expressing the positional determinacy of parity games,
thereby obtaining complete axiomatizations.
We do not know yet whether the theory
\mathsf{MSO}
of
[
DR15
]
is complete,
but let us mention that the axiomatization of
\mathsf{WMSO}

over infinite trees given in
[
Sie78
]
augments the natural analogue
for trees of Peano’s arithmetic with an axiom of induction over finite trees.

Outline.

The paper is organized as follows.
We present the basic formal theory for
\mathsf{MSO}
in §
2

Our theory
\mathsf{FSO}
is then presented in §
3

and we sketch its mutual interpretability with
\mathsf{MSO}
.
§
4
and §
5
discuss a formalization
of two-players infinite games in
\mathsf{FSO}
,
and, in particular, we give a formulation of the axiom
(\mathit{PosDet})

of positional determinacy of parity games.
This provides us with the required tools to formalize in §
6

(alternating) tree automata, acceptance games
and basic operations on them
(including complementation in
\mathsf{FSO}+(\mathit{PosDet})
).
§
7
is an interlude discussing
a complete theory of
\mathsf{MSO}
over
\omega
-words
within the infinite paths of
\mathsf{FSO}
.
Building on §
6
and §
7
,
we then give our completeness argument for

\mathsf{FSO}+(\mathit{PosDet})

and
\mathsf{MSO}+(\mathit{PosDet})

in §
8
.
Finally, §
9
contains a proof of the Simulation Theorem in
\mathsf{FSO}
.
The full version
[
DR20
]
gives details of proofs omitted here.

2.
Preliminaries:
\mathsf{MSO}
on Infinite Trees as a Second-Order Logic

We present here
a basic formal theory
of
Monadic Second-Order Logic
(
\mathsf{MSO}
) over infinite trees.
This theory can be seen as an analogue for trees of Peano’s axioms
for second order arithmetic.
In order to obtain a complete theory,
\mathsf{MSO}

will be augmented with
an axiom of positional determinacy of parity games
(see §
3.6
, §
5.6
and §
8
).

We are going to define the theory
{\mathsf{MSO}_{\mathscr{D}}}
of the infinite full
\mathscr{D}
-ary tree
\mathscr{D}^{*}
,
for
\mathscr{D}
a finite non-empty set.
Both the language and the axioms of
{\mathsf{MSO}_{\mathscr{D}}}
will depend on
\mathscr{D}
.
The language of
{\mathsf{MSO}_{\mathscr{D}}}
is the usual language of two-sorted first-order
logic, with one sort for
Individuals

and one sort for
(Monadic)

Predicates
.
The axioms of
{\mathsf{MSO}_{\mathscr{D}}}
are the expected axioms on the relational structure
of the full
\mathscr{D}
-ary tree, together with induction and comprehension.
The theory
{\mathsf{MSO}_{\mathscr{D}}}
is essentially that of
[
Sie78
]
,
but with second-order quantifications intended to range over arbitrary
subsets of
\mathscr{D}^{*}
(instead of just finite ones), and without the axiom
of induction over finite trees.

We fix for the rest of this Section
a finite non-empty set
\mathscr{D}
of
tree directions
.

2.1.
The Language of
{\mathsf{MSO}_{\mathscr{D}}}

The language of
{\mathsf{MSO}_{\mathscr{D}}}
has two sorts:

•

The sort of
Individuals
,
intended to range over tree positions
p\in\mathscr{D}^{*}
.
We have infinitely many Individual variables
x,y,z
etc.
We also have one constant symbol
\dot{\varepsilon}
(for the root of
\mathscr{D}^{*}
),
and one unary function symbol
\mathsf{S}_{d}

for each
d\in\mathscr{D}
(for the successor function
p\mapsto p.d
).
Individual terms, written
t,u,\text{etc}.
are given by:

t\quad\mathrel{::=}\quad x\quad|\quad\dot{\varepsilon}\quad|\quad\mathsf{S}_{d}(t)

(for
d\in\mathscr{D}
)

•

The sort of
(Monadic) Predicates
,
with variables
X,Y,Z,\text{etc}
,
intended to range over sets of tree positions
A\in\mathcal{P}(\mathscr{D}^{*})
.
There are no other term formers for this sort.

Formulae of
{\mathsf{MSO}_{\mathscr{D}}}
are given by the following grammar:

\varphi,\psi\in\bm{\Lambda}_{\mathscr{D}}\quad\mathrel{::=}\quad X(t)\quad|\quad t\mathrel{\doteq}u\quad|\quad t\mathrel{\dot{<}}u\quad|\quad(\varphi\lor\psi)\quad|\quad\lnot\varphi\quad|\quad(\exists x)\varphi\quad|\quad(\exists X)\varphi

where
t
and
u
are Individual terms.
We use the usual derived formulae:

\begin{array}[]{r !{~~\deq~~} l !{\qquad} r !{~~\deq~~} l}(\forall x)\varphi\lx@intercol\penalty\ \penalty\ \mathrel{:=}\penalty\ \penalty\ &\lnot(\exists x)(\lnot\varphi)&\varphi\land\psi\lx@intercol\penalty\ \penalty\ \mathrel{:=}\penalty\ \penalty\ &\lnot(\lnot\varphi\lor\lnot\psi)\\
(\forall X)\varphi\lx@intercol\penalty\ \penalty\ \mathrel{:=}\penalty\ \penalty\ &\lnot(\exists X)(\lnot\varphi)&\varphi\Rightarrow\psi\lx@intercol\penalty\ \penalty\ \mathrel{:=}\penalty\ \penalty\ &\lnot\varphi\lor\psi\\
\top\lx@intercol\penalty\ \penalty\ \mathrel{:=}\penalty\ \penalty\ &(\forall x)(x\mathrel{\doteq}x)&\bot\lx@intercol\penalty\ \penalty\ \mathrel{:=}\penalty\ \penalty\ &\lnot\top\\
(t\mathrel{\dot{\leq}}u)\lx@intercol\penalty\ \penalty\ \mathrel{:=}\penalty\ \penalty\ &(t\mathrel{\dot{<}}u)\lor(t\mathrel{\doteq}u)\end{array}

We employ usual writing conventions for formulae,
for instance omitting internal and external brackets when appropriate.

2.2.
The Deduction System of
{\mathsf{MSO}_{\mathscr{D}}}

Deduction for
{\mathsf{MSO}_{\mathscr{D}}}
is defined by the system presented
in Figure
1
and Figure
2

(where
\Phi
stands for
a multiset of formulae),
together with the following axioms.

•

Equality on Individuals:

(\forall x)(x\mathrel{\doteq}x)\qquad\text{and}\qquad(\forall x)(\forall y)\big(x\mathrel{\doteq}y\penalty\ \penalty\ \Longrightarrow\penalty\ \penalty\ \varphi[x/z]\penalty\ \penalty\ \Longrightarrow\penalty\ \penalty\ \varphi[y/z]\big)

(for each
\varphi
)

•

The
Tree Axioms
of Figure
3
.

•

Comprehension Scheme:

(\exists X)(\forall y)\big[X(y)\penalty\ \penalty\ \Longleftrightarrow\penalty\ \penalty\ \varphi\big]

(for each
\varphi
, with
X
not free in
\varphi
)

•

Induction Axiom:

(\forall X)\left(X(\dot{\varepsilon})\penalty\ \penalty\ \Longrightarrow\penalty\ \penalty\ \mathord{\mathop{\bigwedge}}_{d\in\mathscr{D}}(\forall y)\big[X(y)\penalty\ \penalty\ \Longrightarrow\penalty\ \penalty\ X(\mathsf{S}_{d}(y))\big]\penalty\ \penalty\ \Longrightarrow\penalty\ \penalty\ (\forall y)X(y)\right)

\begin{array}[]{c !{\qquad} c !{\qquad} c}\dfrac{}{\Phi\vdash\varphi\lor\lnot\varphi}&\dfrac{}{\Phi,\varphi\vdash\varphi}&\dfrac{\Phi\vdash\varphi\qquad\Phi\vdash\lnot\varphi}{\Phi\vdash\psi}\\
\\
\dfrac{\Phi\vdash\varphi}{\Phi\vdash\varphi\lor\psi}&\dfrac{\Phi\vdash\psi}{\Phi\vdash\varphi\lor\psi}&\dfrac{\Phi\vdash\varphi\lor\psi\qquad\Phi,\varphi\vdash\vartheta\qquad\Phi,\psi\vdash\vartheta}{\Phi\vdash\vartheta}\\[10.00002pt]
\end{array}

Figure 1.
Deduction Rules for Propositional Logic.

\begin{array}[]{c}\dfrac{\Phi\vdash\varphi[t/x]}{\Phi\vdash(\exists x)\varphi}\qquad\qquad\dfrac{\Phi\vdash(\exists x)\varphi\qquad\Phi,\varphi\vdash\psi}{\Phi\vdash\psi}\penalty\ \text{($x$ not free in $\Phi,\psi$)}\\
\\
\dfrac{\Phi\vdash\varphi[Y/X]}{\Phi\vdash(\exists X)\varphi}\qquad\qquad\dfrac{\Phi\vdash(\exists X)\varphi\qquad\Phi,\varphi\vdash\psi}{\Phi\vdash\psi}\penalty\ \text{($X$ not free in $\Phi,\psi$)}\\[10.00002pt]
\end{array}

Figure 2.
Deduction Rules for Predicate Logic.

Remark 1
.

As usual, one can derive

\vdash(\varphi\Rightarrow\psi\Rightarrow\vartheta)\penalty\ \Leftrightarrow\penalty\ ((\varphi\land\psi)\Rightarrow\vartheta)

and we have the
Deduction Theorem
:

\Phi,\varphi\vdash\psi\qquad\text{iff}\qquad\Phi\vdash\varphi\Rightarrow\psi

Indeed,
if
\Phi,\varphi\vdash\psi
, then one gets
\Phi\vdash\lnot\varphi\lor\psi

by
\lor
-Elimination on the Excluded Middle
\Phi\vdash\varphi\lor\lnot\varphi
.
Conversely, if
\Phi\vdash\lnot\varphi\lor\psi
,
then one gets
\Phi,\varphi\vdash\psi
by
\lor
-Elimination.
One similarly obtains the
Modus Ponens
as a derived rule

\dfrac{\Phi\vdash\psi\Rightarrow\varphi\qquad\Phi\vdash\psi}{\Phi\vdash\varphi}

\begin{array}[]{c !{\qquad\quad} c}\lnot(\exists x)\mathop{\bigvee}_{d\neq d^{\prime}}\big(\mathsf{S}_{d}(x)\mathrel{\doteq}\mathsf{S}_{d^{\prime}}(x)\big)&(\forall x)(\forall y)\mathop{\bigwedge}_{d\in\mathscr{D}}\big(\mathsf{S}_{d}(x)\mathrel{\doteq}\mathsf{S}_{d}(y)\penalty\ \penalty\ \Rightarrow\penalty\ \penalty\ x\mathrel{\doteq}y\big)\\
\\
\lnot(\exists x)\big(x\mathrel{\dot{<}}x\big)&(\forall x)(\forall y)(\forall z)\big(x\mathrel{\dot{<}}y\penalty\ \penalty\ \Rightarrow\penalty\ \penalty\ y\mathrel{\dot{<}}z\penalty\ \penalty\ \Rightarrow\penalty\ \penalty\ x\mathrel{\dot{<}}z\big)\\
\\
(\forall x)\big(\dot{\varepsilon}\mathrel{\dot{\leq}}x\big)&(\forall x)(\forall y)\Big(\big(\mathop{\bigvee}_{d\in\mathscr{D}}x\mathrel{\dot{<}}\mathsf{S}_{d}(y)\big)\penalty\ \penalty\ \Leftrightarrow\penalty\ \penalty\ x\mathrel{\dot{\leq}}y\Big)\end{array}

Figure 3.
Tree Axioms of
{\mathsf{MSO}_{\mathscr{D}}}
and
{\mathsf{FSO}_{\mathscr{D}}}

(where
(x\mathrel{\dot{\leq}}y)
stands for
(x\mathrel{\dot{<}}y\lor x\mathrel{\doteq}y)
).

{nota}

Henceforth, we write
\mathsf{MSO}
instead of
{\mathsf{MSO}_{\mathscr{D}}}
when the set of directions

\mathscr{D}
is clear from the context.

3.
A Functional Extension of
\mathsf{MSO}
on Infinite Trees

In this Section,
we present (bounded)
Functional (Monadic) Second-Order Logic

over the full
\mathscr{D}
-ary tree (
{\mathsf{FSO}_{\mathscr{D}}}
),
an extension of
{\mathsf{MSO}_{\mathscr{D}}}
with (hereditarily)
finite sets
and bounded quantification over them.
As with
{\mathsf{MSO}_{\mathscr{D}}}
in §
2
, we will simply write
\mathsf{FSO}

for
{\mathsf{FSO}_{\mathscr{D}}}
when
\mathscr{D}
is irrelevant or clear from the context.

{\mathsf{FSO}_{\mathscr{D}}}
is equipped with a basic axiomatization which will allow us,
in §
4
-§
6
, to formalize a basic theory of games
and automata,
and in particular to state an axiom scheme
(\mathit{PosDet})

expressing the positional determinacy of (suitably represented)
parity games (§
5.6
).
We will then show in §
8

that
{\mathsf{FSO}_{\mathscr{D}}}+(\mathit{PosDet})
is complete.

3.1.
Motivations and Overview.

Let us first discuss the motivations and guiding principles
in the design of
{\mathsf{FSO}_{\mathscr{D}}}
.
As usual, within the language of
{\mathsf{MSO}_{\mathscr{D}}}
presented in §
2
,
we can simulate a labeling of
\mathscr{D}^{*}
over a finite non-empty set
\Sigma

T:\mathscr{D}^{*}\longrightarrow\Sigma

There are different ways to achieve this.
A possibility is, for say
\Sigma=\{\mathtt{a}_{1},\dots,\mathtt{a}_{n}\}
,
to code
T:\mathscr{D}^{*}\to\Sigma
using a tuple of Monadic variables

X_{1},\dots,X_{n}
such that

x\in X_{i}\quad\text{iff}\quad T(x)=\mathtt{a}_{i}

(for
i=1,\dots,n
)

A more succinct coding could be obtained using
\lceil\log n\rceil

monadic variables to encode the letter index
i
of
\mathtt{a}_{i}
in binary.
However, directly working with such codings would make it
cumbersome to formalize games and automata
as presented in this paper.
We will therefore rather work in the system
{\mathsf{FSO}_{\mathscr{D}}}
,
which is built around the following principles:

(1)

{\mathsf{FSO}_{\mathscr{D}}}
has no primitive notion of
Monadic variables
.
Instead,
{\mathsf{FSO}_{\mathscr{D}}}
has a primitive notion of
Function variables
,
of the form

F:\mathscr{D}^{*}\longrightarrow\Sigma

(
\Sigma
a finite set)

(2)

In addition,
{\mathsf{FSO}_{\mathscr{D}}}
allows us to work
uniformly
with arbitrary
finite sets.
In particular, we have an explicit sort for them, including
terms, variables and quantifications.

(3)

{\mathsf{FSO}_{\mathscr{D}}}
is faithfully interpretable in
{\mathsf{MSO}_{\mathscr{D}}}
.
To this end, all quantifications over finite sets in
{\mathsf{FSO}_{\mathscr{D}}}
-formulae
are required to be bounded.

In particular, there is a syntactic translation
\langle-\rangle

of
{\mathsf{FSO}_{\mathscr{D}}}
-formulae to
{\mathsf{MSO}_{\mathscr{D}}}
-formulae.
The basic idea of this translation is to interpret finite sets
using propositional logic,
and to interpret Functions

F:\mathscr{D}^{*}\to\{\mathtt{a}_{1},\dots,\mathtt{a}_{n}\}

as partitions
X_{1},\dots,X_{n}
of
\mathscr{D}^{*}
.
But while
{\mathsf{FSO}_{\mathscr{D}}}
handles free variables over finite sets in a uniform way,
the translation
\langle-\rangle
only applies to
{\mathsf{FSO}_{\mathscr{D}}}
-formulae
without free variables over finite sets.
This means that for an
{\mathsf{FSO}_{\mathscr{D}}}
-formula
\varphi(k)
with
k
a variable over
finite sets, for each finite set
\kappa
we will have a specific

{\mathsf{MSO}_{\mathscr{D}}}
-formula
\langle\varphi(\kappa)\rangle
.

Technically, the finite sets of
{\mathsf{FSO}_{\mathscr{D}}}
will be the usual

hereditarily
finite sets.

{defi}

Let
V_{0}\mathrel{:=}\varnothing
,
and
V_{n+1}\mathrel{:=}\mathcal{P}(V_{n})
for each
n\in\mathbb{N}
.
The set
V_{\omega}

of
hereditarily finite
sets (HF-sets)
is defined as

V_{\omega}\quad\mathrel{:=}\quad\bigcup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}V_{n}

Remark 2
.

In the context of this paper, it is useful to note that,
as is well-known (see e.g.
[
Jec06
, Exercise 12.9]
),

V_{\omega}

is a model of
\mathsf{ZFC}^{-}

(i.e. of
\mathsf{ZFC}
without the infinity axiom).

{conv}

We will always assume the finite non-empty set
\mathscr{D}

of
tree directions
to be an
\mathrm{HF}
-set.

The language of
{\mathsf{FSO}_{\mathscr{D}}}
will have the same sort of Individuals as
{\mathsf{MSO}_{\mathscr{D}}}

and a sort for
\mathrm{HF}
-sets, and
its Function variables will be
of the form
F:\mathscr{D}^{*}\to K
for
K
a term over
\mathrm{HF}
-sets (
\mathrm{HF}
-term).
The design of
{\mathsf{FSO}_{\mathscr{D}}}
is obtained as a compromise between the following two
conflicting desiderata:

(1)

To be as flexible as possible to allow an easy formalization of games
and automata.

(2)

To be as simple as possible to allow an easy translation to
{\mathsf{MSO}_{\mathscr{D}}}
.

This leads us to two peculiar design choices.

(1)

We have, in addition to the above mentioned sorts, a distinct sort of

Functions over
\mathrm{HF}
-sets
.
This sort contains only constants (so these functions cannot be quantified over),
whose purpose is to provide Skolem functions
for those
\forall\exists
(bounded) statements over
\mathrm{HF}
-sets
which are provable in
\mathsf{ZFC}^{-}
.

(2)

In order to facilitate the translation of
{\mathsf{FSO}_{\mathscr{D}}}
to
{\mathsf{MSO}_{\mathscr{D}}}
,
Function variables, written
(F:K)
(“
F
has codomain
K
”),
cannot occur in
\mathrm{HF}
-terms.
Formally, Functions
(F:K)
are only allowed in atomic formulae of the form

F(t)\mathrel{\doteq}L

The axioms of
{\mathsf{FSO}_{\mathscr{D}}}
will contain the obvious adaptation of the Tree Axioms
and the Induction Axiom of
{\mathsf{MSO}_{\mathscr{D}}}
.
We also have axioms defining the aforementioned Skolem functions.
In addition, the Comprehension Scheme of
{\mathsf{MSO}_{\mathscr{D}}}
will be replaced
by
Functional Choice Axioms

allowing us to define Functions
F:\mathscr{D}^{*}\to K

from
\forall\exists
-statements:

(\forall x)(\exists k\in K)\varphi(x,k)\penalty\ \penalty\ \Longrightarrow\penalty\ \penalty\ (\exists F:\mathscr{D}^{*}\to K)(\forall x)\varphi(x,F(x))

Remark 3
.

Functional Choice Axioms
as above
actually amount to
Comprehension
in
\mathsf{MSO}
(§
2.2
).
Such axioms do not create choice predicates for Individuals,
which are known to be undefinable in

\mathsf{MSO}

[
GS83
,
CL07
]
,
and moreover to break decidability when added to the language of

\mathsf{MSO}

[
BG00
,
CL07
]
.

The rest of this Section is organized as follows.
The system
{\mathsf{FSO}_{\mathscr{D}}}
is defined in §
3.2
-
3.4
,
and its (expected)
interpretation in the standard model of
\mathscr{D}
-ary trees
is given in §
3.5
.
Then in §
3.6
we discuss the interpretation of
{\mathsf{FSO}_{\mathscr{D}}}
in
{\mathsf{MSO}_{\mathscr{D}}}

and a straightforward embedding of
{\mathsf{MSO}_{\mathscr{D}}}
in
{\mathsf{FSO}_{\mathscr{D}}}
.
Finally, §
3.7
presents notation whose purpose is to
allow some flexibility in the manipulation of functions.
The language and axioms of
{\mathsf{FSO}_{\mathscr{D}}}
are summarized in Figure
5
,
with references to the relevant parts of the text.

3.2.
The Language of
{\mathsf{FSO}_{\mathscr{D}}}

We now formally define the language of
{\mathsf{FSO}_{\mathscr{D}}}
, for
\mathscr{D}
an
\mathrm{HF}
-set.
It consists of the the following sorts:

•

The sort of
Hereditarily finite (
\mathrm{HF}
) sets
,
with infinitely many
\mathrm{HF}
-variables
k,\ell
etc.,
and with one constant symbol
\dot{\mathsf{\kappa}}
for each

\kappa\in V_{\omega}

(we often simply write
\kappa
for
\dot{\mathsf{\kappa}}
in formulae, omitting the overset dot).

•

The same sort of
Individuals
as
{\mathsf{MSO}_{\mathscr{D}}}
(see §
2.1
).

•

The sort of
Functions
, with infinitely many variables
F,G,H,\text{etc}
.

•

The sort of
\mathrm{HF}
-Functions
, with no variable.
For each pair
(n,m)\in\mathbb{N}\times\mathbb{N}
, we assume given
a constant symbol
\dot{\mathsf{g}}_{n,m}
of arity
n
.
The interpretation of these constant symbols is discussed in §
3.4.4
.

The language of
{\mathsf{FSO}_{\mathscr{D}}}
has two kinds of terms.
The
Individual terms
are the same as those of
{\mathsf{MSO}_{\mathscr{D}}}
.
In addition,
{\mathsf{FSO}_{\mathscr{D}}}
also has
\mathrm{HF}
-terms
, which are given by

K,L\quad\mathrel{::=}\quad k\quad|\quad\dot{\mathsf{\kappa}}\quad|\quad\dot{\mathsf{g}}_{n,m}(L_{1},\dots,L_{n})

The
formulae
of
{\mathsf{FSO}_{\mathscr{D}}}
are built as follows:

\begin{array}[]{r r l}\varphi,\psi&\mathrel{::=}&t\mathrel{\doteq}u\quad|\quad t\mathrel{\dot{<}}u\\
&\quad|&K\mathrel{\doteq}L\quad|\quad K\mathrel{\dot{\in}}L\quad|\quad K\mathrel{\dot{\subseteq}}L\quad|\quad F(t)\mathrel{\doteq}K\\
&\quad|&\varphi\lor\psi\quad|\quad\lnot\varphi\\
&\quad|&(\exists x)\varphi\quad|\quad(\exists F:K)\varphi\quad|\quad(\exists k\mathrel{\dot{\in}}L)\varphi\quad|\quad(\exists k\mathrel{\dot{\subseteq}}L)\varphi\end{array}

An
{\mathsf{FSO}_{\mathscr{D}}}
-formula
\varphi
is
\mathrm{HF}
-closed

if it contains no free
\mathrm{HF}
-variable.

{nota}

(1)

Usual derived formulae are defined similarly as with
\mathsf{MSO}

(where
\mathrel{\star}
is either
\mathrel{\dot{\in}}
or
\mathrel{\dot{\subseteq}}
):

\begin{array}[]{r !{~~\deq~~} l !{\qquad} r !{~~\deq~~} l}(\forall x)\varphi\lx@intercol\penalty\ \penalty\ \mathrel{:=}\penalty\ \penalty\ &\lnot(\exists x)(\lnot\varphi)&\varphi\land\psi\lx@intercol\penalty\ \penalty\ \mathrel{:=}\penalty\ \penalty\ &\lnot(\lnot\varphi\lor\lnot\psi)\\
(\forall F:L)\varphi\lx@intercol\penalty\ \penalty\ \mathrel{:=}\penalty\ \penalty\ &\lnot(\exists F:L)(\lnot\varphi)&\varphi\Rightarrow\psi\lx@intercol\penalty\ \penalty\ \mathrel{:=}\penalty\ \penalty\ &\lnot\varphi\lor\psi\\
(\forall k\mathrel{\star}L)\varphi\lx@intercol\penalty\ \penalty\ \mathrel{:=}\penalty\ \penalty\ &\lnot(\exists k\mathrel{\star}L)(\lnot\varphi)&(t\mathrel{\dot{\leq}}u)\lx@intercol\penalty\ \penalty\ \mathrel{:=}\penalty\ \penalty\ &(t\mathrel{\dot{<}}u)\lor(t\mathrel{\doteq}u)\\
\top\lx@intercol\penalty\ \penalty\ \mathrel{:=}\penalty\ \penalty\ &(\forall x)(x\mathrel{\doteq}x)&\bot\lx@intercol\penalty\ \penalty\ \mathrel{:=}\penalty\ \penalty\ &\lnot\top\end{array}

(2)

In addition to bounded quantification
(\exists F:K)
,
we use the notation
(F:K)
within formulae
as the defined formula:

(F:K)\quad\mathrel{:=}\quad(\forall x)(\exists k\mathrel{\dot{\in}}K)(F(x)\mathrel{\doteq}k)

(3)

For
variables

\bm{K}=K_{1},\dots,K_{n}

and

\bm{L}=L_{1},\dots,L_{n}
,
and

\mathrel{\star}
either
\mathrel{\doteq}
,
\mathrel{\dot{\in}}
or
\mathrel{\dot{\subseteq}}
,
we let

\bm{K}\mathrel{\star}\bm{L}\quad=\quad(K_{1},\dots,K_{n})\mathrel{\star}(L_{1},\dots,L_{n})\quad\mathrel{:=}\quad\mathop{\bigwedge}_{1\leq i\leq n}K_{i}\mathrel{\star}L_{i}

Remark 4
.

The (hereditarily) finite set
\mathscr{D}
of tree directions is considered
both as a parameter in the definition of
{\mathsf{FSO}_{\mathscr{D}}}
,
via the successor term constructors
\mathsf{S}_{d}
(for
d\in\mathscr{D}
)
and the corresponding axioms (see §
3.4
),
and as a (constant)
\mathrm{HF}
set, which can occur as such in
{\mathsf{FSO}_{\mathscr{D}}}
formulae.
Strictly speaking, we should write
\dot{\mathsf{\mathscr{D}}}
rather than
\mathscr{D}
in the latter case, but we usually simply omit the overset dot, as with other HF-sets.

3.3.
The Deduction System of
{\mathsf{FSO}_{\mathscr{D}}}

Deduction for
{\mathsf{FSO}_{\mathscr{D}}}
is defined by the system presented
on Figure
1

(with
{\mathsf{FSO}_{\mathscr{D}}}
formulae instead of
{\mathsf{MSO}_{\mathscr{D}}}
formulae)
and Figure
4
,
together with all the axioms of §
3.4
.
The language and axioms of
{\mathsf{FSO}_{\mathscr{D}}}
are summarized in Figure
5
.

\begin{array}[]{c}\dfrac{\Phi\vdash\varphi[t/x]}{\Phi\vdash(\exists x)\varphi}\qquad\qquad\dfrac{\Phi\vdash(\exists x)\varphi\qquad\Phi,\varphi\vdash\psi}{\Phi\vdash\psi}\penalty\ \penalty\ \text{($x$ not free in $\Phi,\psi$)}\\
\\
\dfrac{\Phi\vdash\varphi[G/F]\qquad\Phi\vdash(G:K)}{\Phi\vdash(\exists F:K)\varphi}\quad\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \,\dfrac{\Phi\vdash(\exists F:K)\varphi\qquad\Phi,\,(F:K),\,\varphi\vdash\psi}{\Phi\vdash\psi}\penalty\ \text{($F$ not free in $\Phi,\psi$)}\\
\\
\dfrac{\Phi\vdash\varphi[K/k]\qquad\Phi\vdash K\mathrel{\star}L}{\Phi\vdash(\exists k\mathrel{\star}L)\varphi}\penalty\ \penalty\ \text{(for $\mathrel{\star}$ either $\mathrel{\dot{\in}}$ or $\mathrel{\dot{\subseteq}}$)}\\
\\
\dfrac{\Phi\vdash(\exists k\mathrel{\star}K)\varphi\qquad\Phi,\,k\mathrel{\star}K,\,\varphi\vdash\psi}{\Phi\vdash\psi}\penalty\ \penalty\ \text{(for $\mathrel{\star}$ either $\mathrel{\dot{\in}}$ or $\mathrel{\dot{\subseteq}}$, and $k$ not free in $\Phi,\psi$)}\\
\\
\dfrac{\Phi\vdash\varphi}{\Phi[F(t)/k]\vdash\varphi[F(t)/k]}\penalty\ \penalty\ \text{($\Phi[F(t)/k],\varphi[F(t)/k]$ $\mathsf{FSO}$-formulae)}\\[10.00002pt]
\end{array}

Figure 4.
Deduction Rules for
{\mathsf{FSO}_{\mathscr{D}}}
.

\begin{array}[]{l l r c}\hline\cr\hline\cr\lx@intercol\textbf{Language}\hfil\lx@intercol\\
\hline\cr\text{Individual Terms}&t\quad\mathrel{::=}\quad x\quad|\quad\dot{\varepsilon}\quad|\quad\mathsf{S}_{d}(t)&\text{($d\in\mathscr{D}$)}&\text{(\lx@sectionsign\ref{sec:mso:lgge})}\\
\text{Functions}&F,G,H,\text{etc}&\text{(only variables)}&\text{(\lx@sectionsign\ref{sec:fso:fso})}\\
\text{$\mathrm{HF}$-Terms}&K,L\quad\mathrel{::=}\quad k&\text{($k$ $\mathrm{HF}$-variable)}&\text{(\lx@sectionsign\ref{sec:fso:fso})}\\
&\quad|\quad\dot{\mathsf{\kappa}}&\text{($\kappa\in V_{\omega}$)}\\
&\quad|\quad\dot{\mathsf{g}}_{n,m}(L_{1},\dots,L_{n})&\text{($\dot{\mathsf{g}}_{n,m}$ $\mathrm{HF}$-Function)}\\
\\
\text{Formulae}&\varphi,\psi\quad\mathrel{::=}&&\text{(\lx@sectionsign\ref{sec:fso:fso})}\\
&\lx@intercol\quad|\quad t\mathrel{\doteq}u\quad|\quad t\mathrel{\dot{<}}u\quad|\quad F(t)\mathrel{\doteq}K\hfil\lx@intercol\\
&\lx@intercol\quad|\quad K\mathrel{\doteq}L\quad|\quad K\mathrel{\dot{\in}}L\quad|\quad K\mathrel{\dot{\subseteq}}L\hfil\lx@intercol\\
&\lx@intercol\quad|\quad\varphi\lor\psi\quad|\quad\lnot\varphi\hfil\lx@intercol\\
&\lx@intercol\quad|\quad(\exists x)\varphi\quad|\quad(\exists F:K)\varphi\quad|\quad(\exists k\mathrel{\dot{\in}}L)\varphi\quad|\quad(\exists k\mathrel{\dot{\subseteq}}L)\varphi\hfil\lx@intercol\par\par\\
\\
\hline\cr\hline\cr\lx@intercol\textbf{Axioms}\hfil\lx@intercol\\
\hline\cr\lx@intercol\text{\emph{Equality:}}\hfil\lx@intercol&&\text{(\lx@sectionsign\ref{sec:ax:eq})}\\
\lx@intercol\hfil\begin{array}[]{c !{\qquad} c}(\forall x)(x\mathrel{\doteq}x)&(\forall x)(\forall y)\big(x\mathrel{\doteq}y\penalty\ \penalty\ \Longrightarrow\penalty\ \penalty\ \varphi[x/z]\penalty\ \penalty\ \Longrightarrow\penalty\ \penalty\ \varphi[y/z]\big)\\
\\
K\mathrel{\doteq}K&\left(K\mathrel{\doteq}L\penalty\ \penalty\ \Longrightarrow\penalty\ \penalty\ \varphi[K/m]\penalty\ \penalty\ \Longrightarrow\penalty\ \penalty\ \varphi[L/m]\right)\end{array}\hfil\lx@intercol\\
\\
\lx@intercol\text{\emph{Induction:}}\hfil\lx@intercol&&\text{(\lx@sectionsign\ref{sec:ax:ind})}\\
\lx@intercol\hfil\varphi(\dot{\varepsilon})\quad\Longrightarrow\quad\mathord{\mathop{\bigwedge}}_{d\in\mathscr{D}}(\forall x)\big[\varphi(x)\penalty\ \penalty\ \Longrightarrow\penalty\ \penalty\ \varphi(\mathsf{S}_{d}(x))\big]\quad\Longrightarrow\quad(\forall x)\varphi(x)\hfil\lx@intercol\\
\\
\lx@intercol\text{\emph{Tree Axioms:}}\hfil\lx@intercol&&\text{(\lx@sectionsign\ref{sec:ax:tree})}\\
\lx@intercol\hfil\begin{array}[]{c !{\quad} c}\lnot(\exists x)\mathop{\bigvee}_{d\neq d^{\prime}}\big(\mathsf{S}_{d}(x)\mathrel{\doteq}\mathsf{S}_{d^{\prime}}(x)\big)&(\forall x)(\forall y)\mathop{\bigwedge}_{d\in\mathscr{D}}\big(\mathsf{S}_{d}(x)\mathrel{\doteq}\mathsf{S}_{d}(y)\penalty\ \penalty\ \Rightarrow\penalty\ \penalty\ x\mathrel{\doteq}y\big)\\
\\
\lnot(\exists x)\big(x\mathrel{\dot{<}}x\big)&(\forall x)(\forall y)(\forall z)\big(x\mathrel{\dot{<}}y\penalty\ \penalty\ \Rightarrow\penalty\ \penalty\ y\mathrel{\dot{<}}z\penalty\ \penalty\ \Rightarrow\penalty\ \penalty\ x\mathrel{\dot{<}}z\big)\\
\\
(\forall x)\big(\dot{\varepsilon}\mathrel{\dot{\leq}}x\big)&(\forall x)(\forall y)\Big(\big(\mathop{\bigvee}_{d\in\mathscr{D}}x\mathrel{\dot{<}}\mathsf{S}_{d}(y)\big)\penalty\ \penalty\ \Leftrightarrow\penalty\ \penalty\ x\mathrel{\dot{\leq}}y\Big)\end{array}\hfil\lx@intercol\\
\\
\lx@intercol\text{\emph{Axioms on $\mathrm{HF}$-Sets:}}\hfil\lx@intercol&&\text{(\lx@sectionsign\ref{sec:ax:hf})}\\
&\lx@intercol\varphi_{n,m}[\bm{K}/\bm{k}][\dot{\mathsf{g}}_{n,m}(\bm{K})/\ell]\hfil\lx@intercol\\
&\lx@intercol\hfil\text{(provided\quad$\mathbf{Sk}(\mathsf{ZFC}^{-})\vdash(\forall k_{1},\dots,k_{n})(\exists!\ell)\varphi_{n,m}$)}\lx@intercol\\
\\
\lx@intercol\text{\emph{Functional Choice Axioms:}}\hfil\lx@intercol&&\text{(\lx@sectionsign\ref{sec:ax:choice})}\\
\lx@intercol\hfil\begin{array}[]{r !{\quad\longlimp\quad} l}(\forall k\mathrel{\dot{\in}}K)(\exists\ell\mathrel{\dot{\in}}L)\varphi(k,\ell)\lx@intercol\quad\Longrightarrow&\big(\exists f\mathrel{\dot{\in}}L^{K}\big)(\forall k\mathrel{\dot{\in}}K)\varphi(k,f(k))\\[10.00002pt]
(\forall x)(\exists k\mathrel{\dot{\in}}K)\varphi(x,k)\lx@intercol\quad\Longrightarrow&(\exists F:K)(\forall x)(\exists k\mathrel{\dot{\in}}K)\left(F(x)\mathrel{\doteq}k\penalty\ \land\penalty\ \varphi(x,k)\right)\\[10.00002pt]
(\forall k\mathrel{\dot{\in}}K)(\exists F:L)\varphi(k,F)\lx@intercol\quad\Longrightarrow&\big(\exists G:L^{K}\big)(\forall k\mathrel{\dot{\in}}K)\varphi(k,F)[G(k)\sslash F]\end{array}\hfil\lx@intercol\par\\
\\
\hline\cr\hline\cr\end{array}

Figure 5.
Summary of
{\mathsf{FSO}_{\mathscr{D}}}
.

3.4.
Basic Axiomatization

We now present the axioms of
{\mathsf{FSO}_{\mathscr{D}}}
.
The first group
(Equality, Tree Axioms and Induction, §
3.4.1
-§
3.4.2
)
corresponds to its counterpart in
{\mathsf{MSO}_{\mathscr{D}}}
.
We then present our specific axioms for
\mathrm{HF}
-Sets in §
3.4.4

and our Functional Choice Axioms in §
3.4.5
.

3.4.1.
Equality

The theory
{\mathsf{FSO}_{\mathscr{D}}}
has
usual equality axioms for individuals and
\mathrm{HF}
-Sets.

•

Equality on Individuals.

(\forall x)(x\mathrel{\doteq}x)\qquad\text{and}\qquad(\forall x)(\forall y)\big(x\mathrel{\doteq}y\penalty\ \penalty\ \Longrightarrow\penalty\ \penalty\ \varphi[x/z]\penalty\ \penalty\ \Longrightarrow\penalty\ \penalty\ \varphi[y/z]\big)

(for all formula
\varphi
)

•

Equality on
\mathrm{HF}
-Sets

(for all formula
\varphi
, all
\mathrm{HF}
-terms
K,L
and all
\mathrm{HF}
-variable
m
):

K\mathrel{\doteq}K\qquad\text{and}\qquad\left(K\mathrel{\doteq}L\penalty\ \penalty\ \Longrightarrow\penalty\ \penalty\ \varphi[K/m]\penalty\ \penalty\ \Longrightarrow\penalty\ \penalty\ \varphi[L/m]\right)

Remark 5
.

Note that
{\mathsf{FSO}_{\mathscr{D}}}
is equipped with an explicit
Substitution Rule

\dfrac{\Phi\vdash\varphi}{\Phi[F(t)/k]\vdash\varphi[F(t)/k]}

(
\Phi[F(t)/k],\varphi[F(t)/k]

\mathsf{FSO}
-formulae)

Substitution entails the following
(where
\varphi(F(t))
is an
\mathsf{FSO}
-formula):

(F(t)\mathrel{\doteq}K)\penalty\ \penalty\ \Longrightarrow\penalty\ \penalty\ \varphi(K)\penalty\ \penalty\ \Longrightarrow\penalty\ \penalty\ \varphi(F(t))

as well as
the derived rule

\dfrac{\Phi\vdash\varphi(F(t))\qquad\Phi\vdash(F:K)}{\Phi\vdash(\exists k\mathrel{\dot{\in}}K)\varphi(k)}

The former is a direct consequence of the Substitution rule
together with elimination of equality on
\mathrm{HF}
-Sets.
For the latter, first note that
Remark
1
also holds for
{\mathsf{FSO}_{\mathscr{D}}}
.
In particular, one can derive

(k\mathrel{\dot{\in}}K)\penalty\ \penalty\ \Longrightarrow\penalty\ \penalty\ \varphi(k)\penalty\ \penalty\ \Longrightarrow\penalty\ \penalty\ (\exists k\mathrel{\dot{\in}}K)\varphi(k)

On the other hand, we have

(\exists\ell\mathrel{\dot{\in}}K)(k\mathrel{\doteq}\ell)\penalty\ \penalty\ \Longrightarrow\penalty\ \penalty\ (k\mathrel{\dot{\in}}K)

We therefore get

(\exists\ell\mathrel{\dot{\in}}K)(k\mathrel{\doteq}\ell)\penalty\ \penalty\ \Longrightarrow\penalty\ \penalty\ \varphi(k)\penalty\ \penalty\ \Longrightarrow\penalty\ \penalty\ (\exists k\mathrel{\dot{\in}}K)\varphi(k)

and the Substitution rule gives

(\exists\ell\mathrel{\dot{\in}}K)(F(t)\mathrel{\doteq}\ell)\penalty\ \penalty\ \Longrightarrow\penalty\ \penalty\ \varphi(F(t))\penalty\ \penalty\ \Longrightarrow\penalty\ \penalty\ (\exists k\mathrel{\dot{\in}}K)\varphi(k)

3.4.2.
Induction

We have the following
Induction Scheme
:

\varphi(\dot{\varepsilon})\penalty\ \penalty\ \Longrightarrow\penalty\ \penalty\ \mathord{\mathop{\bigwedge}}_{d\in\mathscr{D}}(\forall x)\big[\varphi(x)\penalty\ \penalty\ \Longrightarrow\penalty\ \penalty\ \varphi(\mathsf{S}_{d}(x))\big]\penalty\ \penalty\ \Longrightarrow\penalty\ \penalty\ (\forall x)\varphi(x)

(for each formula
\varphi
)

3.4.3.
Tree Axioms

For the tree structure of
\mathscr{D}^{*}
,
we have the same Tree Axioms as
{\mathsf{MSO}_{\mathscr{D}}}
, displayed in Figure
3

(recall that
{\mathsf{FSO}_{\mathscr{D}}}
has the same Individuals as
{\mathsf{MSO}_{\mathscr{D}}}
).

We now state expected results on the axioms so far introduced.
To this end, let
{\mathsf{FSO}_{\mathscr{D}}^{0}}
be the system consisting of
the deduction rules of Figures
1
and
4
,
together with the Equality Axioms (§
3.4.1
)
the Induction Scheme (§
3.4.2
)
and the Tree Axioms (Figure
3
).

Proposition 6
.

{\mathsf{FSO}_{\mathscr{D}}^{0}}
proves the following.

(1)

(\forall x)(\forall y)(x\mathrel{\dot{\leq}}y\mathrel{\dot{\leq}}x\penalty\ \penalty\ \Longrightarrow\penalty\ \penalty\ x\mathrel{\doteq}y)

(2)

(\forall x)(x\mathrel{\dot{\leq}}\dot{\varepsilon}\penalty\ \penalty\ \Longrightarrow\penalty\ \penalty\ x\mathrel{\doteq}\dot{\varepsilon})

(3)

\lnot(\exists x)(x\mathrel{\dot{<}}\dot{\varepsilon})

(4)

\lnot(\exists x)(\mathsf{S}_{d}(x)\mathrel{\doteq}\dot{\varepsilon})

(5)

(\forall x)(x\mathrel{\doteq}\dot{\varepsilon}\penalty\ \penalty\ \lor\penalty\ \penalty\ (\exists y)\lor_{d\in\mathscr{D}}x\mathrel{\doteq}\mathsf{S}_{d}(y))

(6)

(\forall x)(\forall y)(x\mathrel{\dot{<}}y\penalty\ \penalty\ \Longrightarrow\penalty\ \penalty\ \lor_{d\in\mathscr{D}}\mathsf{S}_{d}(x)\mathrel{\dot{\leq}}y)

A consequence of Proposition
6

is that the Induction Scheme of
{\mathsf{FSO}_{\mathscr{D}}}
(§
3.4.2
)
implies the usual scheme of
Well-Founded Induction
w.r.t.
the strict prefix order
\mathrel{\dot{<}}
.

Theorem 7

(Well-Founded Induction)
.

{\mathsf{FSO}_{\mathscr{D}}^{0}}

proves the following form of well-founded induction:

(\forall x)\big[(\forall y\mathrel{\dot{<}}x)(\varphi(y))\penalty\ \penalty\ \Longrightarrow\penalty\ \penalty\ \varphi(x)\big]\penalty\ \penalty\ \Longrightarrow\penalty\ \penalty\ (\forall x)\varphi(x)

Remark 8
.

Both Proposition
6

and Theorem
7
also hold for
{\mathsf{MSO}_{\mathscr{D}}}
.

3.4.4.
\mathrm{HF}
-Sets

We now present our axioms on
\mathrm{HF}
-Sets.
Their
purpose is to ease formalization in
{\mathsf{FSO}_{\mathscr{D}}}
.
Recall that
\mathrm{HF}
-sets range over
V_{\omega}

(Definition
3.1
).
The idea of these axioms
is to incorporate in
{\mathsf{FSO}_{\mathscr{D}}}
as much of the theory of
V_{\omega}
as possible,
while keeping
{\mathsf{FSO}_{\mathscr{D}}}
interpretable in
{\mathsf{MSO}_{\mathscr{D}}}

and with a semi-recursive notion of provability.
The interpretation of
{\mathsf{FSO}_{\mathscr{D}}}
in
{\mathsf{MSO}_{\mathscr{D}}}

relies on the fact that in
{\mathsf{FSO}_{\mathscr{D}}}
-formulae,
all quantifications over
\mathrm{HF}
-Sets are bounded (either by
\mathrel{\dot{\in}}
or
\mathrel{\dot{\subseteq}}
),
so that in a closed
{\mathsf{FSO}_{\mathscr{D}}}
-formula, quantifications
over
\mathrm{HF}
-Sets can be interpreted using usual propositional logic.

We will have, as particular cases of our axioms on
\mathrm{HF}
-Sets,
all bounded formulae over
\mathrm{HF}
-Sets which are true in
V_{\omega}
.
Moreover, w.r.t. the interpretation of
{\mathsf{FSO}_{\mathscr{D}}}
in
{\mathsf{MSO}_{\mathscr{D}}}
(§
3.6
)
and in particular w.r.t. its application to
{\mathsf{MSO}_{\mathscr{D}}}
over
\omega
-words
(§
7
, §
8
and §
9
),
it is important to have sufficiently many functions over
V_{\omega}

available within
closed

\mathrm{HF}
-terms.
This is the main purpose of our axioms on
\mathrm{HF}
-Sets.
They state that the
\mathrm{HF}
-Functions
\dot{\mathsf{g}}_{n,m}

are Skolem functions for

\forall\exists!
-statements over
\mathrm{HF}
-Sets.
These axioms are further commented in §
8.5
.

{defi}

[
\mathrm{HF}
-Formula]

An
\mathrm{HF}
-formula
is an
{\mathsf{FSO}_{\mathscr{D}}}
-formula with atoms
of the form
K\mathrel{\doteq}L
,
K\mathrel{\dot{\in}}L
or
K\mathrel{\dot{\subseteq}}L

where
K
and
L
are
\mathrm{HF}
-terms.

Fix a distinguished
\mathrm{HF}
-variable
\ell
,
and an enumeration
k_{1},k_{2},\dots
of distinct
\mathrm{HF}
-variables all different from
\ell
.
Furthermore, fix an enumeration
(\varphi_{n,m})_{n,m\in\mathbb{N}}

of
\mathrm{HF}
-formulae satisfying the following conditions:

(1)

Each formula
\varphi_{n,m}
has free variables among
k_{1},\dots,k_{n},\ell
.

(2)

All
\mathrm{HF}
-Functions occurring in
\varphi_{n,m}

have the form
\dot{\mathsf{g}}_{n^{\prime},m^{\prime}}
with
m^{\prime}<m
.

(3)

Each
\mathrm{HF}
-formula
\varphi
satisfying (1) and (2) occurs infinitely often in

(\varphi_{n,m})_{n,m\in\mathbb{N}}
, in the following sense.
If
\varphi
has free variables among
k_{1},\dots,k_{n},\ell
,
then there are infinitely many
m\in\mathbb{N}
such that
\varphi

is
\varphi_{n,m}
.

Recall from Remark
2
that
V_{\omega}

is a model of
\mathsf{ZFC}^{-}
.
The idea of our Axioms on
\mathrm{HF}
-Sets is that

{\mathsf{FSO}_{\mathscr{D}}}\vdash\varphi_{n,m}[\dot{\mathsf{g}}_{n,m}(k_{1},\dots,k_{n})/\ell]\qquad\text{whenever}\qquad\mathsf{ZFC}^{-}\vdash(\forall k_{1},\dots,k_{n})(\exists!\ell)\varphi_{n,m}

(1)

(where
(k\mathrel{\dot{\subseteq}}k^{\prime})
is interpreted as
(\forall m\mathrel{\dot{\in}}k)(m\mathrel{\dot{\in}}k^{\prime})
).
However, recall that
\varphi_{n,m}
in (
1
)
may contain
\mathrm{HF}
-Functions
\dot{\mathsf{g}}_{n^{\prime},m^{\prime}}
with
m^{\prime}<m
.
The premise of (
1
) can thus not be formulated in
\mathsf{ZFC}^{-}
,
but requires a suitable extension of it.
We let
\mathbf{Sk}(\mathsf{ZFC}^{-})
consist of
\mathsf{ZFC}^{-}
augmented with the axioms

(\forall k_{1},\dots,k_{n})(\exists!\ell)(\varphi_{n,m})\penalty\ \penalty\ \Longrightarrow\penalty\ \penalty\ (\forall k_{1},\dots,k_{n})\varphi_{n,m}[\dot{\mathsf{g}}_{n,m}(k_{1},\dots,k_{n})/\ell]

(for each
n,m\in\mathbb{N}
)

It is well-known that
\mathbf{Sk}(\mathsf{ZFC}^{-})
is thus a conservative extension of
\mathsf{ZFC}^{-}

(see e.g.
[
vD04
, §3.4]
).

{\mathsf{FSO}_{\mathscr{D}}}
has the following axiom scheme for
\mathrm{HF}
-Sets,
which simply consists of (
1
) formulated for
\mathbf{Sk}(\mathsf{ZFC}^{-})

rather than
\mathsf{ZFC}^{-}
:

•

Axioms on
\mathrm{HF}
-Sets.

For each
n,m\in\mathbb{N}

such that

\mathbf{Sk}(\mathsf{ZFC}^{-})\vdash\quad(\forall k_{1},\dots,k_{n})(\exists!\ell)\varphi_{n,m}

(2)

and for all
\mathrm{HF}
-terms
\bm{K}=K_{1},\dots,K_{n}
, we have the axiom

\varphi_{n,m}[\bm{K}/\bm{k}][\dot{\mathsf{g}}_{n,m}(\bm{K})/\ell]

Remark 9
.

Note that this axiom scheme makes the axiom set of
{\mathsf{FSO}_{\mathscr{D}}}
not recursive.
But as expected for a proof system,

provability
in
{\mathsf{FSO}_{\mathscr{D}}}
remains semi-recursive.

We fix here once and for all an interpretation
of the
\mathrm{HF}
-Function symbols
\dot{\mathsf{g}}_{n,m}

as functions over
V_{\omega}
.
The idea is
that if (
2
) holds,
then
\dot{\mathsf{g}}_{n,m}
is interpreted
as a computable function
\mathtt{g}_{n,m}:V_{\omega}^{n}\to V_{\omega}

such that

V_{\omega}\models\quad(\forall k_{1},\dots,k_{n})\varphi_{n,m}[\mathtt{g}_{n,m}(k_{1},\dots,k_{n})/\ell]

(3)

But again recall that
\varphi_{n,m}

may contain
\mathrm{HF}
-Functions
\dot{\mathsf{g}}_{n^{\prime},m^{\prime}}
with
m^{\prime}<m
.
We therefore proceed inductively, as follows.

{conv}

By induction on
m\in\mathbb{N}
,
we interpret the

\mathrm{HF}
-Function symbols
\dot{\mathsf{g}}_{n,m}

as computable functions

\mathtt{g}_{n,m}\penalty\ \penalty\ :\penalty\ \penalty\ V_{\omega}^{n}\penalty\ \penalty\ \longrightarrow\penalty\ \penalty\ V_{\omega}

Consider the formula
\varphi_{n,m}
, and assume that all
\mathrm{HF}
-Functions

\dot{\mathsf{g}}_{n^{\prime},m^{\prime}}
with
m^{\prime}<m
are already interpreted.
If (
2
) holds
then by the Countable Axiom of Choice
we interpret
\dot{\mathsf{g}}_{n,m}
as the unique function

\mathtt{g}_{n,m}:V^{n}_{\omega}\to V_{\omega}
such that (
3
)
holds.
Note that such functions are computable.
Otherwise, we interpret
\dot{\mathsf{g}}_{n,m}
as the function with constant value

\varnothing
.

Remark 10
.

Note each
\mathrm{HF}
-Function symbol is interpreted by a recursive function
in Convention
3.4.4
.
However, since (
2
) is undecidable,
there is no algorithm taking
(n,m)\in\mathbb{N}^{2}
to
the interpretation of
\dot{\mathsf{g}}_{n,m}
.
This point is further discussed in §
8.5
,
where a natural workaround is proposed,
as well as some explanations for our present choice of Axioms on
\mathrm{HF}
-Sets.

We now discuss some consequences of these axioms.
First note that if
\varphi
is a
closed

\mathrm{HF}
-formula,
then it is provable in
\mathbf{Sk}(\mathsf{ZFC}^{-})
if and only if it holds in
V_{\omega}
.
We state this fact as a Remark for the record, and also to reiterate how much deductive power underlies the axioms on
\mathrm{HF}
-sets.

Remark 11
.

Given a closed
\mathrm{HF}
-formula
\varphi
,

{\mathsf{FSO}_{\mathscr{D}}}\vdash\varphi\qquad\text{whenever}\qquad V_{\omega}\models\varphi

Moreover, we have all instances of the following:

(1)

Extensionality
.

(\forall m\mathrel{\dot{\in}}k)(m\mathrel{\dot{\in}}\ell)\penalty\ \penalty\ \Rightarrow\penalty\ \penalty\ (\forall m\mathrel{\dot{\in}}\ell)(m\mathrel{\dot{\in}}k)\penalty\ \penalty\ \Rightarrow\penalty\ \penalty\ k\mathrel{\doteq}\ell

(2)

Finite sets
.
For each
n\in\mathbb{N}
we have an
n
-ary
\mathrm{HF}
-Function symbol

\{-,\dots,-\}
such that

\mathop{\bigwedge}_{1\leq i\leq n}(k_{i}\mathrel{\dot{\in}}\{k_{1},\dots,k_{n}\})\quad\land\quad(\forall m\mathrel{\dot{\in}}\{k_{1},\dots,k_{n}\})\mathop{\bigvee}_{1\leq i\leq n}(m\mathrel{\doteq}k_{i})

We have in particular singletons
\{-\}
and unordered pairs

\{-,-\}
.
Using Extensionality,
{\mathsf{FSO}_{\mathscr{D}}}
proves that

\{\{k\},\{k,\ell\}\}\mathrel{\doteq}\{\{k^{\prime}\},\{k^{\prime},\ell^{\prime}\}\}\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad k\mathrel{\doteq}k^{\prime}\land\ell\mathrel{\doteq}\ell^{\prime}

We use the following shorthand:

(k,\ell)\quad\mathrel{:=}\quad\{\{k\},\{k,\ell\}\}

(3)

Union
.
We have an
\mathrm{HF}
-Function symbol
\cup(-)
such that

(\forall\ell\mathrel{\dot{\in}}\cup(k))(\exists m\mathrel{\dot{\in}}k)(\ell\mathrel{\dot{\in}}m)\quad\land\quad(\forall\ell\mathrel{\dot{\in}}k)(\forall m\mathrel{\dot{\in}}\ell)(m\mathrel{\dot{\in}}\cup(k))

(4)

Powerset.

We have an
\mathrm{HF}
-Function symbol
\mathcal{P}(-)
such that

(\forall\ell\mathrel{\dot{\in}}\mathcal{P}(k))(\forall m\mathrel{\dot{\in}}\ell)(m\mathrel{\dot{\in}}k)\quad\land\quad(\forall\ell\mathrel{\dot{\subseteq}}k)(\ell\mathrel{\dot{\in}}\mathcal{P}(k))

The powerset is the reason for our introduction of inclusion (
\mathrel{\dot{\subseteq}}
)
as an atomic formula:
It is well-known that the powerset cannot be defined by a
\Delta_{0}(\mathrel{\dot{\in}})
-formula.
A possible formula defining it is:

(\forall\ell\mathrel{\dot{\in}}\mathcal{P}(k))(\forall m\mathrel{\dot{\in}}\ell)(m\mathrel{\dot{\in}}k)\quad\land\quad(\forall\ell)\big[(\forall m\mathrel{\dot{\in}}\ell)(m\mathrel{\dot{\in}}k)\penalty\ \penalty\ \Longrightarrow\penalty\ \penalty\ \ell\mathrel{\dot{\in}}\mathcal{P}(k)\big]

The quantification
\forall\ell
in the right conjunct is not
\mathrel{\dot{\in}}
-bounded,
and cannot be so.
In addition, we also have an
\mathrm{HF}
-Function symbol
\mathcal{P}_{*}(-)

for the
non-empty powerset
, that is such that

k\mathrel{\dot{\in}}\mathcal{P}_{*}(\ell)\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad\big(k\mathrel{\dot{\in}}\mathcal{P}(\ell)\penalty\ \land\penalty\ (\exists m\mathrel{\dot{\in}}k)\big)

(5)

Comprehension.

Given an
\mathrm{HF}
-formula
\varphi
with free variables
among
k_{1},\dots,k_{n},k
, we have an
\mathrm{HF}
-Function

\{k\mathrel{\dot{\in}}(-)\penalty\ |\penalty\ \varphi[-,\dots,-,k]\}

such that

m\mathrel{\dot{\in}}\{k\mathrel{\dot{\in}}k_{0}\penalty\ |\penalty\ \varphi[k_{1},\dots,k_{n},k]\}\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad m\mathrel{\dot{\in}}k_{0}\land\varphi[k_{1},\dots,k_{n},m]

(6)

Products.

We have a binary
\mathrm{HF}
-Function
(-)\times(-)

such that

k\times\ell\penalty\ \penalty\ \mathrel{:=}\penalty\ \penalty\ \left\{m\mathrel{\dot{\in}}\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{P}(k\cup\ell))\penalty\ |\penalty\ (\exists k_{0}\mathrel{\dot{\in}}k)(\exists\ell_{0}\mathrel{\dot{\in}}\ell)\big[m\mathrel{\doteq}(k_{0},\ell_{0})\big]\right\}

Moreover, we have binary projections given by
\mathrm{HF}
-Functions

\pi_{1}^{-,-}(-)
and
\pi_{2}^{-,-}(-)

such that

\pi_{1}^{k,\ell}(m)\penalty\ \penalty\ =\penalty\ \penalty\ \left\{n\in\ell\penalty\ |\penalty\ m\mathrel{\dot{\subseteq}}k\times\ell\penalty\ \land\penalty\ (\exists n^{\prime}\in k)\big[(n,n^{\prime})\mathrel{\dot{\in}}m\big]\right\}

and similarly for
\pi_{2}^{-,-}(-)
.
Whenever possible, we write
\pi_{i}(-)
instead of
\pi_{i}^{k,\ell}(-)
.
Note that by composing binary projections
\pi_{1}
and
\pi_{2}

we obtain projections

\pi^{n}_{i}\penalty\ \penalty\ :\penalty\ \penalty\ k_{1}\times\dots\times k_{n}\penalty\ \penalty\ \longrightarrow\penalty\ \penalty\ k_{i}

for any
k_{1},\dots,k_{n}
and
i\in\{1,\dots,n\}

(7)

Function Spaces and Application.

We have an exponent
\mathrm{HF}
-Function
(-)^{(-)}

such that

\ell^{k}\penalty\ \penalty\ \mathrel{:=}\penalty\ \penalty\ \left\{m\mathrel{\dot{\in}}\mathcal{P}(k\times\ell)\penalty\ |\penalty\ (\forall k_{0}\mathrel{\dot{\in}}k)(\exists!\ell_{0}\mathrel{\dot{\in}}\ell)\big[(k_{0},\ell_{0})\mathrel{\dot{\in}}m\big]\right\}

Moreover, function application is given by the
\mathrm{HF}
-Function

@_{-,-}(-,-)
with

@_{k,\ell}(f,a)\penalty\ \penalty\ \mathrel{:=}\penalty\ \penalty\ \left\{m\mathrel{\dot{\in}}\cup(\ell)\penalty\ |\penalty\ (\exists\ell_{0}\mathrel{\dot{\in}}\ell)\big[m\mathrel{\dot{\in}}\ell_{0}\land(a,\ell_{0})\mathrel{\dot{\in}}f\big]\right\}

(here
f
and
a
are
\mathrm{HF}
-variables).
Whenever possible, we omit the subscripts
k,\ell
of
@_{k,\ell}(f,a)

and write simply
f(a)
for
@_{k,\ell}(f,a)
.

(8)

Disjoint Unions.

We have a binary
\mathrm{HF}
-Function
(-)+(-)
with

k+\ell\penalty\ \penalty\ \mathrel{\doteq}\penalty\ \penalty\ (\{0\}\times k)\cup(\{1\}\times\ell)

(see Convention
5.5

for a further discussion on finite ordinals in our context).
We moreover have
\mathrm{HF}
-Functions

\mathsf{in}^{k,\ell}_{k}(-)
,
\mathsf{in}^{k,\ell}_{\ell}(-)
, and
[-,-]_{k,\ell}^{\imath}

such that (dropping subscripts and superscripts)

\mathsf{in}(m)\mathrel{\doteq}(0,m)\qquad\mathsf{in}(n)\mathrel{\doteq}(1,n)\qquad[f,g](0,m)\mathrel{\doteq}f(m)\qquad[f,g](1,n)\mathrel{\doteq}g(n)

for
m\mathrel{\dot{\in}}k
,
\ell\mathrel{\dot{\in}}n
and
f\mathrel{\dot{\in}}\imath^{k}
,
g\mathrel{\dot{\in}}\imath^{\ell}
.

{conv}

Regarding function spaces as in
7
above,

{\mathsf{FSO}_{\mathscr{D}}}
proves

(k^{\ell})^{m}\quad\cong\quad k^{\ell\times m}

In the following, we reason modulo that bijection, and simply
identify
(k^{\ell})^{m}
with
k^{\ell\times m}
.

Remark 12
.

An
\mathrm{HF}
-relation
\mathord{\preceq}\subseteq K\times K
is a
partial order

on an
\mathrm{HF}
-term
K
, if the formula
\mathrm{PO}(\preceq,K)
holds in
V_{\omega}
, where

\mathrm{PO}(\preceq,K)
is the
\mathrm{HF}
-formula:

(\forall k,\ell,m\mathrel{\dot{\in}}K)\Big[\big(k\preceq\ell\penalty\ \penalty\ \Rightarrow\penalty\ \penalty\ \ell\preceq k\penalty\ \penalty\ \Rightarrow\penalty\ \penalty\ k\mathrel{\doteq}\ell\big)\penalty\ \penalty\ \land\penalty\ \penalty\ \big(k\preceq\ell\penalty\ \penalty\ \Rightarrow\penalty\ \penalty\ \ell\preceq m\penalty\ \penalty\ \Rightarrow\penalty\ \penalty\ k\preceq m\big)\Big]

A partial order
\mathord{\preceq}\subseteq K\times K
is a
well-order

if every subset of
K
has a
\preceq
-least element,
that is, if the following formula
\mathrm{WO}(\preceq,K)
holds
in
V_{\omega}
:

(\forall\ell\mathrel{\dot{\subseteq}}K)\big[\ell\neq\varnothing\penalty\ \penalty\ \Rightarrow\penalty\ \penalty\ (\exists m\mathrel{\dot{\in}}\ell)(\forall n\mathrel{\dot{\in}}\ell)(m\preceq n)\big]

Since every
\mathrm{HF}
-set is finite and can be well-ordered, we have

V_{\omega}\quad\models\quad(\forall k)\underbrace{(\exists\mathord{\preceq}\mathrel{\dot{\subseteq}}k\times k)\big[\mathrm{PO}(\preceq,k)\penalty\ \penalty\ \land\penalty\ \penalty\ \mathrm{WO}(\preceq,k)\penalty\ \penalty\ \land\penalty\ \penalty\ \mathrm{WO}(\succeq,k)\big]}_{\varphi(k)}

Since
\varphi(k)
is an
\mathrm{HF}
-formula
\varphi(k)
,
it follows that
{\mathsf{FSO}_{\mathscr{D}}}
proves
\varphi(k)
,
hence in particular that every
\mathrm{HF}
-set is well-ordered.
∎

3.4.5.
Functional Choice Axioms

We have the following functional choice axiom schemes.

•

\mathrm{HF}
-Bounded Choice for
\mathrm{HF}
-Sets
.

(\forall k\mathrel{\dot{\in}}K)(\exists\ell\mathrel{\dot{\in}}L)\varphi(k,\ell)\penalty\ \penalty\ \Longrightarrow\penalty\ \penalty\ \big(\exists f\mathrel{\dot{\in}}L^{K}\big)(\forall k\mathrel{\dot{\in}}K)\varphi(k,f(k))

•

\mathrm{HF}
-Bounded Choice for Functions
.

(\forall x)(\exists k\mathrel{\dot{\in}}K)\varphi(x,k)\penalty\ \penalty\ \Longrightarrow\penalty\ \penalty\ (\exists F:K)(\forall x)(\exists k\mathrel{\dot{\in}}K)\left(F(x)\mathrel{\doteq}k\penalty\ \land\penalty\ \varphi(x,k)\right)

•

Iterated
\mathrm{HF}
-Bounded Choice
.

(\forall k\mathrel{\dot{\in}}K)(\exists F:L)\varphi(k,F)\penalty\ \penalty\ \Longrightarrow\penalty\ \penalty\ \big(\exists G:L^{K}\big)(\forall k\mathrel{\dot{\in}}K)\varphi(k,F)[G(k)\sslash F]

where the substitution
[G(k)\sslash F]
is defined as
the usual substitution operation
but with

(F(t)\mathrel{\doteq}M)[G(k)\sslash F]\penalty\ \penalty\ \mathrel{:=}\penalty\ \penalty\ \big(\exists f\mathrel{\dot{\in}}L^{K}\big)\left(G(t)\mathrel{\doteq}f\penalty\ \land\penalty\ f(k)\mathrel{\doteq}M\right)

We insist
that none of these axioms create choice functions
for the
individuals
of
{\mathsf{FSO}_{\mathscr{D}}}

(cf Remark
3
).
Despite their common shape, these three axiom schemes are actually
of different nature.
First, the axiom of

\mathrm{HF}
-Bounded Choice for Functions

(\forall x)(\exists k\mathrel{\dot{\in}}K)\varphi(x,k)\penalty\ \penalty\ \Longrightarrow\penalty\ \penalty\ (\exists F:K)(\forall x)(\exists k\mathrel{\dot{\in}}K)\left(F(x)\mathrel{\doteq}k\penalty\ \land\penalty\ \varphi(x,k)\right)

(4)

is a counterpart in
{\mathsf{FSO}_{\mathscr{D}}}
of the Comprehension Scheme of
{\mathsf{MSO}_{\mathscr{D}}}
.
Recalling the informal discussion in §
3.1

and anticipating §
3.5
and §
3.6
,
let us assume a translation
\langle-\rangle
from (
\mathrm{HF}
-closed)

{\mathsf{FSO}_{\mathscr{D}}}
-formulae to
{\mathsf{MSO}_{\mathscr{D}}}
-formulae, and
let us assume that
K
is a closed
\mathrm{HF}
-term
representing the
\mathrm{HF}
-set
\{\kappa_{1},\dots,\kappa_{n}\}
.
Then the premise of (
4
)
can be read as

(\forall x)\mathop{\bigvee}_{1\leq i\leq n}\langle\varphi(x,\kappa_{i})\rangle

The conclusion easily follows from the fact that
using Comprehension, one can
define in
{\mathsf{MSO}_{\mathscr{D}}}
a partition
X_{1},\dots,X_{n}
of
\mathscr{D}^{*}

such that

(\forall x)\mathop{\bigvee}_{1\leq i\leq n}\Big(X_{i}(x)\penalty\ \penalty\ \land\penalty\ \penalty\ \langle\varphi(x,\kappa_{i})\rangle\Big)

Second,
\mathrm{HF}
-Bounded Choice for
\mathrm{HF}
-Sets

(\forall k\mathrel{\dot{\in}}K)(\exists\ell\mathrel{\dot{\in}}L)\varphi(k,\ell)\penalty\ \penalty\ \Longrightarrow\penalty\ \penalty\ \big(\exists f\mathrel{\dot{\in}}L^{K}\big)(\forall k\mathrel{\dot{\in}}K)\varphi(k,f(k))

(5)

may look similar to the Axioms on
\mathrm{HF}
-Sets of §
3.4.4
.
The differences are that the formula
\varphi
here
is an
arbitrary
formula of
{\mathsf{FSO}_{\mathscr{D}}}
,
not necessarily an
\mathrm{HF}
-formula in the sense of Definition
3.4.4
,
and moreover that this axiom only involves
{\mathsf{FSO}_{\mathscr{D}}}
(
i.e.

bounded
)
quantifications, contrary to (
2
).
Note that for
\mathrm{HF}
-formulae
\varphi
,
this axiom is indeed an instance of the axioms of §
3.4.4
.
In the general case, this axiom can be seen as following
from the fact that
quantifications over
\mathrm{HF}
-Sets in
{\mathsf{FSO}_{\mathscr{D}}}
are
ultimately interpreted in propositional logic.
Assume that the
\mathrm{HF}
-terms
K
and
L
are closed, and correspond
to the
\mathrm{HF}
-sets
\kappa
and
\lambda
respectively.
Then the premise of (
5
)
can be read as

\mathop{\bigwedge}_{\kappa_{0}\in\kappa}\mathop{\bigvee}_{\lambda_{0}\in\lambda}\langle\varphi(\kappa_{0},\lambda_{0})\rangle

which is equivalent in propositional logic to the interpretation of the conclusion

\mathop{\bigvee}_{f\in\lambda^{\kappa}}\mathop{\bigwedge}_{\kappa_{0}\in\kappa}\langle\varphi(\kappa_{0},f(\kappa_{0}))\rangle

Similarly,
Iterated
\mathrm{HF}
-Bounded Choice

reduces to an equivalence of the form

\mathop{\bigwedge}_{1\leq i\leq n}(\exists\bm{X})\varphi(\kappa_{i},\bm{X})\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad(\exists\bm{X}_{1})\dots(\exists\bm{X}_{n})\mathop{\bigwedge}_{1\leq i\leq n}\varphi(\kappa_{i},\bm{X}_{i})

and follows from Comprehension.

The definition of
{\mathsf{FSO}_{\mathscr{D}}}
is now complete.

{nota}

Similarly as with
{\mathsf{MSO}_{\mathscr{D}}}
, we shall write
\mathsf{FSO}
for
{\mathsf{FSO}_{\mathscr{D}}}

when the set of tree directions
\mathscr{D}
is clear from the context.

3.5.
The Standard Model of
\mathsf{FSO}

The
standard model

\mathfrak{T}

of
{\mathsf{FSO}_{\mathscr{D}}}
is the full
\mathscr{D}
-ary tree
\mathscr{D}^{*}

equipped with suitable domains for each sort:

•

\mathrm{HF}
-Sets
range over
V_{\omega}
, and each constant

\dot{\mathsf{\kappa}}
is interpreted by the corresponding
\mathrm{HF}
-set
\kappa\in V_{\omega}
.

•

Individuals
range over
\mathscr{D}^{*}
,
the constant
\dot{\varepsilon}

is interpreted by the empty sequence
\varepsilon\in\mathscr{D}^{*}

and
\mathsf{S}_{d}
as the map
taking
p\in\mathscr{D}^{*}
to
p.d\in\mathscr{D}^{*}
.
Moreover, we write
<
for the strict prefix order on
\mathscr{D}^{*}
.

•

Functions

range over

\bigcup_{\kappa\in V_{\omega}}\left(\mathscr{D}^{*}\penalty\ \penalty\ \longrightarrow\penalty\ \penalty\ \kappa\right)

•

For each
n,m\in\mathbb{N}
, the
\mathrm{HF}
-Function
\dot{\mathsf{g}}_{n,m}

(of arity
n
) is interpreted as the function

\mathtt{g}_{n,m}\penalty\ \penalty\ :\penalty\ \penalty\ V_{\omega}^{n}\penalty\ \penalty\ \longrightarrow\penalty\ \penalty\ V_{\omega}

fixed in Convention
3.4.4
.

Remark 13
.

Note that
\mathfrak{T}
has the same individuals as the standard model of
{\mathsf{MSO}_{\mathscr{D}}}
.
Moreover we write
\mathfrak{T}
for both the standard model
of
{\mathsf{FSO}_{\mathscr{D}}}
and that of
{\mathsf{MSO}_{\mathscr{D}}}
, as an abuse of notation.

We have the usual
interpretation
\llbracket t\rrbracket\in\mathscr{D}^{*}
for each closed
individual term
t
with parameters in
\mathfrak{T}
,
and
an interpretation
\llbracket K\rrbracket\in V_{\omega}
for each closed
\mathrm{HF}
-term
K

with parameters in
\mathfrak{T}
.
The relation
\mathfrak{T}\models\varphi
,
for a closed
\mathsf{FSO}
-formula
\varphi
with parameters in
\mathfrak{T}
,
is defined by induction on
\varphi
as follows:

\begin{array}[]{!{\Std\models} l !{\quad\text{iff}\quad} l !{\quad} l}\mathfrak{T}\models\lx@intercol K\mathrel{\dot{\mathsf{\mathrel{\star}}}}L&\llbracket K\rrbracket\mathrel{\star}\llbracket L\rrbracket&\text{(for $\mathrel{\star}$ either $=$, $\in$, or $\subseteq$)}\\
\mathfrak{T}\models\lx@intercol t\mathrel{\dot{\mathsf{\mathrel{\star}}}}u&\llbracket t\rrbracket\mathrel{\star}\llbracket u\rrbracket&\text{(for $\mathrel{\star}$ either $=$ or $<$)}\\
\mathfrak{T}\models\lx@intercol\varphi\lor\psi&(\mathfrak{T}\models\varphi)\text{ or }(\mathfrak{T}\models\psi)\\
\mathfrak{T}\models\lx@intercol\lnot\varphi&\mathfrak{T}\not\models\varphi\\
\mathfrak{T}\models\lx@intercol(\exists x)\varphi&\mathfrak{T}\models\varphi[p/x]\penalty\ \penalty\ \text{for some $p\in\mathscr{D}^{*}$}\\
\mathfrak{T}\models\lx@intercol(\exists k\mathrel{\dot{\mathsf{\mathrel{\star}}}}L)\varphi&\mathfrak{T}\models\varphi[\kappa/k]\penalty\ \penalty\ \text{for some $\kappa\mathrel{\star}\llbracket L\rrbracket$}&\text{(for $\mathrel{\star}$ either $\in$ or $\subseteq$)}\\
\mathfrak{T}\models\lx@intercol(\exists F:L)\varphi&\mathfrak{T}\models\varphi[\mathtt{F}/F]\penalty\ \penalty\ \text{for some $\mathtt{F}\in\llbracket L\rrbracket^{\mathscr{D}^{*}}$}\end{array}

By a routine induction argument, we can show the soundness of
{\mathsf{FSO}_{\mathscr{D}}}
w.r.t.
\mathfrak{T}
:

Proposition 14
.

Given
\mathsf{FSO}
-formulae
\psi_{1},\dots,\psi_{n},\varphi

with free
\mathrm{HF}
-variables among
\bm{k}
,
free Individual variables among
\bm{x}
,
and free Function variables among
\bm{F}
,
if

\psi_{1},\dots,\psi_{n}\vdash_{\mathsf{FSO}}\varphi

then
for all
\mathrm{HF}
-Sets
\bm{\kappa}
,
all
\bm{p}\in\mathscr{D}^{*}

and all

\bm{\mathtt{F}}\in\bigcup_{\kappa\in V_{\omega}}\left(\mathscr{D}^{*}\to\kappa\right)
,
we have

\mathfrak{T}\models\varphi[\bm{\kappa}/\bm{k},\bm{p}/\bm{x},\bm{\mathtt{F}}/\bm{F}]\qquad\text{whenever}\qquad\mathfrak{T}\models\mathop{\bigwedge}_{1\leq i\leq n}\psi_{i}[\bm{\kappa}/\bm{k},\bm{p}/\bm{x},\bm{\mathtt{F}}/\bm{F}]

Remark 15
.

It follows from Remark
10
that the map

\llbracket-\rrbracket
is not computable on
\mathrm{HF}
-terms.
We refer to §
8.5
for a discussion and a workaround.

3.6.
Mutual Interpretability of
\mathsf{FSO}
and
\mathsf{MSO}

While
\mathsf{FSO}
seems more expressive than
\mathsf{MSO}
(and, indeed, is easier to work with),
the two theories can mutually interpret each other
via two formula-level translations:

\langle-\rangle\penalty\ \penalty\ :\penalty\ \penalty\ \mathsf{FSO}\penalty\ \penalty\ \longrightarrow\penalty\ \penalty\ \mathsf{MSO}\qquad\text{and}\qquad(-)^{\circ}\penalty\ \penalty\ :\penalty\ \penalty\ \mathsf{MSO}\penalty\ \penalty\ \longrightarrow\penalty\ \penalty\ \mathsf{FSO}

As we shall see, both translations preserve and
reflect provability:

\displaystyle\mathsf{FSO}\vdash\varphi\quad\text{if and only if}\quad\mathsf{MSO}\vdash\langle\varphi\rangle

(
\varphi
closed
\mathsf{FSO}
-formula)

\displaystyle\mathsf{MSO}\vdash\varphi\quad\text{if and only if}\quad\mathsf{FSO}\vdash\varphi^{\circ}

(
\varphi
closed
\mathsf{MSO}
-formula)

The interpretation
(-)^{\circ}

of
\mathsf{MSO}
in
\mathsf{FSO}

simply amounts to simulate the (Monadic) Predicate variables of
\mathsf{MSO}

by
\mathsf{FSO}
-Function variables
\mathscr{D}^{*}\to 2
.
We therefore see
(-)^{\circ}
as an embedding, and see
\mathsf{FSO}

as a conservative extension of
\mathsf{MSO}
which is faithfully interpretable in
\mathsf{MSO}
.
This property is not only a sanity check:
we actually rely on it in our completeness argument
(see Rem.
21
).
We discuss the translations
\langle-\rangle
and
(-)^{\circ}

separately in §
3.6.1

and §
3.6.2
below.
Full proofs are detailed in
[
DR20
, App. A]
.

3.6.1.
From
\mathsf{FSO}
to
\mathsf{MSO}

The translation

\langle-\rangle:\mathsf{FSO}\to\mathsf{MSO}

interprets the
\mathrm{HF}
-part of
\mathsf{FSO}
using propositional logic.
It is essentially straightforward, except for the case of Functions,
which require some care.
We will work with the following convention:

{conv}

We assume that each
\mathrm{HF}
-set
\kappa
comes with a fixed enumeration

\bm{\kappa}=\kappa_{1},\dots,\kappa_{n}
of its elements.

The translation
\langle-\rangle
will map an
\mathrm{HF}
-closed
\mathsf{FSO}
-formula
\varphi

without free Function variables
to an
\mathsf{MSO}
-formula
\langle\varphi\rangle
.
As stated earlier, quantifications over
\mathrm{HF}
-Sets will be interpreted using
propositional logic.
For instance we have,

\left\langle(\exists k\mathrel{\dot{\in}}K)\varphi\right\rangle\penalty\ \penalty\ =\penalty\ \penalty\ \mathop{\bigvee}_{\kappa\in\llbracket K\rrbracket}\langle\varphi[\kappa/k]\rangle

where
\llbracket K\rrbracket\in V_{\omega}
is the standard interpretation
of the closed
\mathrm{HF}
-term
K
defined in §
3.5
.
As a consequence, the translation
\langle-\rangle
is
non-uniform

w.r.t.
\mathrm{HF}
-Sets.
In particular, for an
\mathsf{FSO}
-formula
\varphi

with free
\mathrm{HF}
-variables among
\bm{k}=k_{1},\dots,k_{p}
,
each tuple of
\mathrm{HF}
-sets
\bm{\kappa}=\kappa_{1},\dots,\kappa_{p}

will induce a specific
\mathsf{MSO}
-formula

\langle\varphi[\bm{\kappa}/\bm{k}]\rangle
.

The interpretation of Function variables is more complex.
Consider a closed
\mathrm{HF}
-term
K

and assume
\llbracket K\rrbracket=\{\kappa_{1},\dots,\kappa_{c}\}
.
Then a Function
(F:K)
can be seen as a function

F\penalty\ \penalty\ :\penalty\ \penalty\ \mathscr{D}^{*}\penalty\ \penalty\ \longrightarrow\penalty\ \penalty\ \{\kappa_{1},\dots,\kappa_{c}\}

As indicated in §
3.1
,
we interpret
F
as a tuple
X_{1},\dots,X_{c}
of Monadic variables
such that

x\in X_{i}\quad\text{iff}\quad F(x)=\kappa_{i}

(for
i=1,\dots,c
)

In other words,

F:\mathscr{D}^{*}\to\{\kappa_{1},\dots,\kappa_{c}\}

is seen as a partition
X_{1},\dots,X_{c}
of
\mathscr{D}^{*}
.
To handle the interpretation of Functions in the inductive definition
of
\langle-\rangle
,
it is actually convenient to temporarily work
in an extension of
\mathsf{FSO}
with the following atomic formulae:

•

\arrowvert X_{1}\dots X_{n}(t)\mathrel{\doteq}_{\bm{\kappa}}L\arrowvert

where
\bm{\kappa}=\kappa_{1},\dots,\kappa_{n}

enumerates an
\mathrm{HF}
-set
and
X_{1},\dots,X_{n}
are monadic variables of
\mathsf{MSO}
.

Extended

\mathsf{FSO}
-formulae are built just like

\mathsf{FSO}
-formulae, but possibly using the atomic formulae above.
Extended atomic formulae are useful for dealing with

\mathrm{HF}
-bounded quantifications over Functions, say

(\exists F:K)\varphi
.
The point is that

F
occurs
in subformulae of
\varphi
of the form
F(t)\mathrel{\doteq}L
,
where the
\mathrm{HF}
-term
L
may contain free
\mathrm{HF}
-variables.
Hence the value of
L
is not known when the translation
of
(\exists F:K)
has to be computed.
Extended atomic formulae allow us to delay the interpretation of
F(t)\mathrel{\doteq}L

until
\llbracket L\rrbracket
is known.

The interpretation of an extended

\mathrm{HF}
-closed
\mathsf{FSO}
-formula
\varphi

without free Function variables

is the
\mathsf{MSO}
-formula

\langle\varphi\rangle
defined by induction on
\varphi
as follows:

\begin{array}[]{r !{~~}@{}c@{}!{~~} l !{\qquad} l}\langle\arrowvert X_{1}\dots X_{n}(t)\mathrel{\doteq}_{\bm{\kappa}}L\arrowvert\rangle\lx@intercol\penalty\ \penalty\ &\mathrel{:=}\hfil\penalty\ \penalty\ &\mathop{\bigvee}_{1\leq i\leq n\ \&\ \kappa_{i}=\llbracket L\rrbracket}X_{i}(t)\\
\langle K\mathrel{\dot{\mathsf{\mathrel{\star}}}}L\rangle\lx@intercol\penalty\ \penalty\ &\mathrel{:=}\hfil\penalty\ \penalty\ &\left\{\begin{array}[]{l l}\top&\text{if $\llbracket K\rrbracket\mathrel{\star}\llbracket L\rrbracket$}\\
\bot&\text{otherwise}\end{array}\right.&\text{(where $\mathord{\mathrel{\star}}\in\{=,\in,\subseteq\}$)}\\
\langle t\mathrel{\star}u\rangle\lx@intercol\penalty\ \penalty\ &\mathrel{:=}\hfil\penalty\ \penalty\ &t\mathrel{\star}u&\text{(where $\mathord{\mathrel{\star}}\in\{\mathrel{\doteq},\mathrel{\dot{<}}\}$)}\\
\langle\lnot\varphi\rangle\lx@intercol\penalty\ \penalty\ &\mathrel{:=}\hfil\penalty\ \penalty\ &\lnot\langle\varphi\rangle\\
\langle\varphi\lor\psi\rangle\lx@intercol\penalty\ \penalty\ &\mathrel{:=}\hfil\penalty\ \penalty\ &\langle\varphi\rangle\lor\langle\psi\rangle\\
\langle(\exists k\mathrel{\dot{\mathsf{\mathrel{\star}}}}K)\varphi\rangle\lx@intercol\penalty\ \penalty\ &\mathrel{:=}\hfil\penalty\ \penalty\ &\mathop{\bigvee}_{\kappa\mathrel{\star}\llbracket K\rrbracket}\langle\varphi[\kappa/k]\rangle&\text{(where $\mathord{\mathrel{\star}}\in\{\in,\subseteq\}$)}\\
\langle(\exists x)\varphi\rangle\lx@intercol\penalty\ \penalty\ &\mathrel{:=}\hfil\penalty\ \penalty\ &(\exists x)\langle\varphi\rangle\\
\langle(\exists F:K)\varphi\rangle\lx@intercol\penalty\ \penalty\ &\mathrel{:=}\hfil\penalty\ \penalty\ &(\exists X_{1})\dots(\exists X_{c})\\
\lx@intercol\penalty\ \penalty\ &\hfil\penalty\ \penalty\ &\lx@intercol\hfil\left\{\begin{array}[]{c l}&\Upsilon_{c}(X_{1},\dots,X_{c})\\
\land&\langle\varphi[\arrowvert X_{1}\dots X_{c}(t)\mathrel{\doteq}_{\bm{\kappa}}L\arrowvert\penalty\ /\penalty\ (F(t)\mathrel{\doteq}L)]\rangle\end{array}\right.\lx@intercol\end{array}

where in the last clause,

{\llbracket K\rrbracket}
is enumerated by
\bm{\kappa}=\kappa_{1},\dots,\kappa_{c}
,
and
\Upsilon_{c}(X_{1},\dots,X_{c})

is the following
\mathsf{MSO}
-formula,
expressing that
X_{1},\dots,X_{c}
form a partition of
\mathscr{D}^{*}
:

\Upsilon_{c}(X_{1},\dots,X_{c})\penalty\ \penalty\ \mathrel{:=}\penalty\ \penalty\ (\forall x)\left[\mathop{\bigvee}_{1\leq i\leq c}\left(X_{i}(x)\quad\land\quad\mathop{\bigwedge}_{j\neq i}\lnot X_{j}(x)\right)\right]

Note that in the definition of
\langle\varphi\rangle
above,
since
\varphi
is assumed to be
\mathrm{HF}
-closed,
the displayed
\mathrm{HF}
-terms
K
and
L
are closed,
so that
their
\mathfrak{T}
-interpretation
\llbracket K\rrbracket,\llbracket L\rrbracket\in V_{\omega}
is defined
(see §
3.5
).

Remark 16
.

Since it involves the standard interpretation map
\llbracket-\rrbracket

on
\mathrm{HF}
-terms,
it follows from Remark
15
(§
3.5
)
that the interpretation
\langle-\rangle
is not recursive.
We refer to §
5.6.1

and §
8.5

for discussions and workarounds.

Theorem 17
.

For every closed
\mathsf{FSO}
-formula
\varphi
, we have

\mathsf{MSO}\vdash\langle\varphi\rangle\qquad\text{whenever}\qquad\mathsf{FSO}\vdash\varphi

The proof of Theorem
17
is deferred
to
[
DR20
, App. A]
.
The logical rules of
\mathsf{FSO}
are handled routinely.
The interpretations of most of the axioms of
\mathsf{FSO}

are almost trivially provable from the corresponding axioms of
\mathsf{MSO}
.
The Functional Choice Axioms are dealt-with essentially as explained
in §
3.4.5
.

3.6.2.
From
\mathsf{MSO}
to
\mathsf{FSO}

The translation

(-)^{\circ}:\mathsf{MSO}\to\mathsf{FSO}

is much simpler than
\langle-\rangle
.
Assume given
a
\mathsf{FSO}
-Function variable
F_{X}

for each monadic
\mathsf{MSO}
-variable
X
.
The map
(-)^{\circ}
is inductively defined as follows:

\begin{array}[]{r !{~~}@{}c@{}!{~~} l !{\qquad\qquad} r !{~~\deq~~} l}(X(t))^{\circ}\lx@intercol\penalty\ \penalty\ &\mathrel{:=}\hfil\penalty\ \penalty\ &F_{X}(t)\mathrel{\doteq}1&(\varphi\lor\psi)^{\circ}\lx@intercol\penalty\ \penalty\ \mathrel{:=}\penalty\ \penalty\ &\varphi^{\circ}\lor\psi^{\circ}\\
(t\mathrel{\doteq}u)^{\circ}\lx@intercol\penalty\ \penalty\ &\mathrel{:=}\hfil\penalty\ \penalty\ &t\mathrel{\doteq}u&(\lnot\varphi)^{\circ}\lx@intercol\penalty\ \penalty\ \mathrel{:=}\penalty\ \penalty\ &\lnot(\varphi^{\circ})\\
(t\mathrel{\dot{\leq}}u)^{\circ}\lx@intercol\penalty\ \penalty\ &\mathrel{:=}\hfil\penalty\ \penalty\ &t\mathrel{\dot{\leq}}u&((\exists x)\varphi)^{\circ}\lx@intercol\penalty\ \penalty\ \mathrel{:=}\penalty\ \penalty\ &(\exists x)\varphi^{\circ}\\
\lx@intercol\penalty\ \penalty\ &\hfil\penalty\ \penalty\ &&((\exists X)\varphi)^{\circ}\lx@intercol\penalty\ \penalty\ \mathrel{:=}\penalty\ \penalty\ &(\exists F_{X}:2)\varphi^{\circ}\end{array}

It is easy to see that
(-)^{\circ}
is truth preserving (and reflecting) w.r.t. the standard model
\mathfrak{T}
,
by a direct induction on formulae
relying on the bijection
\mathcal{P}(\mathscr{D}^{*})\cong 2^{\mathscr{D}^{*}}
.

Lemma 18
.

Given a closed
\mathsf{MSO}
-formula
\varphi
, we have

\mathfrak{T}\models\varphi\qquad\text{if and only if}\qquad\mathfrak{T}\models\varphi^{\circ}

The main result on
(-)^{\circ}
is the following.
Its proof is deferred to
[
DR20
, App. A]
.

Theorem 19
.

Given a closed
\mathsf{MSO}
-formula
\varphi
,

\mathsf{FSO}\vdash\varphi^{\circ}\qquad\text{if and only if}\qquad\mathsf{MSO}\vdash\varphi

Theorem
19
can actually be extended to
\mathsf{FSO}
formulae.
This is essentially the content of the following result.

Proposition 20
.

For a closed
\mathsf{FSO}
-formula
\varphi
, we have the following.

\displaystyle\mathsf{FSO}\vdash\quad\varphi\penalty\ \penalty\ \Longleftrightarrow\penalty\ \penalty\ \langle\varphi\rangle^{\circ}

(6)

\displaystyle\mathsf{FSO}\vdash\varphi\quad\text{iff}\quad\mathsf{MSO}\vdash\langle\varphi\rangle

(7)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{T}\models\varphi\quad\text{iff}\quad\mathfrak{T}\models\langle\varphi\rangle

(8)

Remark 21
.

Theorem
19

and Proposition
20
will be used
in our completeness argument (§
8
) in two different ways:

(1)

We first obtain completeness of
\mathsf{FSO}

(augmented with the Axiom
(\mathit{PosDet})
of §
5.6
)
for
\mathsf{MSO}
formulae via a usual translation of formulae to automata.
From this result, completeness of
\mathsf{FSO}+(\mathit{PosDet})

follows by Proposition
20
,
while completeness of
\mathsf{MSO}

(augmented with
\langle-\rangle
-translations of suitable instances of
(\mathit{PosDet})
)
follows by Theorem
19
.

(2)

In addition,
we will use Proposition
20
in §
7

in order to import the
\mathsf{MSO}
-theory of
\mathbb{N}
for the infinite paths of
\mathfrak{T}
.
We rely on this for
the version of the Büchi-Landweber Theorem
(namely that
\mathsf{FSO}
decides parity games on finite graphs)
used in the completeness argument of §
8
,
as well as for the Simulation Theorem in §
9
.

3.7.
Notations

We now introduce some notation that we will use throughout our formalization
of games and automata in
\mathsf{FSO}
.

3.7.1.
\mathsf{FSO}
with Extended
\mathrm{HF}
-Terms

First, recall that the syntax of
\mathsf{FSO}
formally disallows Functions in
\mathrm{HF}
-terms.
We propose here a notation system that allows them in some circumstances.
For instance, assuming
(F:K)
,
we can use the notation

(F(t)\mathrel{\dot{\in}}L)\penalty\ \penalty\ \mathrel{:=}\penalty\ \penalty\ (\exists k\mathrel{\dot{\in}}K)\big(F(t)\mathrel{\doteq}k\penalty\ \penalty\ \land\penalty\ \penalty\ k\mathrel{\dot{\in}}L\big)

More generally, consider an atomic formula

M\mathrel{\star}N

(for
\mathord{\mathrel{\star}}\in\{\mathrel{\doteq},\mathrel{\dot{\in}},\mathrel{\dot{\subseteq}}\}
)

with
M
and
N
terms on the following grammar:

M,N\quad\mathrel{::=}\quad k\quad|\quad\dot{\mathsf{\kappa}}\quad|\quad F(t)\quad|\quad\dot{\mathsf{g}}_{n,m}(L_{1},\dots,L_{n})

(9)

Such formulae can be interpreted in
\mathsf{FSO}
,
provided one assumes bounds for the Function variables occurring in them.
Let
M
and
N
be as above, and assume their free Function variables
to be among
\bm{F}=F_{1},\dots,F_{n}
.
Note that there are (proper)
\mathrm{HF}
-terms
M^{\prime}
and
N^{\prime}
such that

M=M^{\prime}[\bm{F(t)}/\bm{\ell}]\qquad\text{and}\qquad N=N^{\prime}[\bm{F(t)}/\bm{\ell}]

for some
\mathrm{HF}
-variables
\bm{\ell}=\ell_{1},\dots,\ell_{c}

and where
\bm{F(t)}=F_{i_{1}}(t_{1}),\dots,F_{i_{c}}(t_{c})
.
Given proper
\mathrm{HF}
-terms
K_{1},\dots,K_{n}
,
assuming
\bm{F}:\bm{K}
, one can let

M\mathrel{\star}N\penalty\ \penalty\ \mathrel{:=}\penalty\ \penalty\ \big(\exists\bm{\ell}\in\bm{L}\big)\left(\bm{F(t)}\mathrel{\doteq}\bm{\ell}\penalty\ \penalty\ \land\penalty\ \penalty\ M^{\prime}\mathrel{\star}N^{\prime}\right)

where
\bm{L}=L_{1},\dots,L_{c}
is such that
L_{j}=K_{i}

iff the
j
th element of
\bm{F(t)}
is
F_{i}(t_{j})
.
Note however that the above defined formula
M\mathrel{\star}N

actually depends on the choice of
\bm{K}
, so we rather write it as:

(M\mathrel{\star}N)_{\bm{F},\bm{K}}

Generalizing further we can, with the above method, interpret in
\mathsf{FSO}

formulae build with
\mathrm{HF}
-terms in the sense of (
9
).
The interpretation in
\mathsf{FSO}
of such a formula
\varphi
with free Function variables among
\bm{F}=F_{1},\dots,F_{n}

is defined by induction, and depends on a choice of proper

\mathrm{HF}
-terms
\bm{K}=K_{1},\dots,K_{n}
.
Using notation as above, we arrive at the following definition:

\begin{array}[]{r !{~~\deq~~} l @{~~} l}(t\mathrel{\star}u)_{\bm{F},\bm{K}}\lx@intercol\penalty\ \penalty\ \mathrel{:=}\penalty\ \penalty\ &(t\mathrel{\star}u)\hfil\penalty\ \penalty\ &\text{(for $\mathord{\mathrel{\star}}\in\{\mathrel{\doteq},\mathrel{\dot{\in}}\}$)}\\
(M\mathrel{\star}N)_{\bm{F},\bm{K}}\lx@intercol\penalty\ \penalty\ \mathrel{:=}\penalty\ \penalty\ &\left\{\begin{array}[]{l l}G(u)\mathrel{\doteq}N&\text{if $(M\mathrel{\star}N)=(G(u)\mathrel{\doteq}N)$}\\
&\text{with $N$ a proper $\mathrm{HF}$-term}\\
\lx@intercol(\exists\bm{\ell}\mathrel{\dot{\in}}\bm{L})\left(\bm{F(t)}\mathrel{\doteq}\bm{\ell}\penalty\ \penalty\ \land\penalty\ \penalty\ M^{\prime}\mathrel{\star}N^{\prime}\right)\hfil\lx@intercol\\
&\text{otherwise}\end{array}\right.\hfil\penalty\ \penalty\ &\text{(for $\mathord{\mathrel{\star}}\in\{\mathrel{\doteq},\mathrel{\dot{\in}},\mathrel{\dot{\subseteq}}\}$)}\\
(\lnot\varphi)_{\bm{F},\bm{K}}\lx@intercol\penalty\ \penalty\ \mathrel{:=}\penalty\ \penalty\ &\lnot(\varphi_{\bm{F},\bm{K}})\hfil\penalty\ \penalty\ \\
(\varphi\lor\psi)_{\bm{F},\bm{K}}\lx@intercol\penalty\ \penalty\ \mathrel{:=}\penalty\ \penalty\ &\varphi_{\bm{F},\bm{K}}\lor\psi_{\bm{F},\bm{K}}\hfil\penalty\ \penalty\ \\
((\exists x)\varphi)_{\bm{F},\bm{K}}\lx@intercol\penalty\ \penalty\ \mathrel{:=}\penalty\ \penalty\ &(\exists x)\varphi_{\bm{F},\bm{K}}\hfil\penalty\ \penalty\ \\
((\exists m\mathrel{\star}M)\varphi)_{\bm{F},\bm{K}}\lx@intercol\penalty\ \penalty\ \mathrel{:=}\penalty\ \penalty\ &(\exists\bm{\ell}\mathrel{\dot{\in}}\bm{L})(\exists m\mathrel{\star}M^{\prime})\left(\bm{F(t)}\mathrel{\doteq}\bm{\ell}\penalty\ \penalty\ \land\penalty\ \penalty\ \varphi_{\bm{F},\bm{K}}\right)\hfil\penalty\ \penalty\ &\text{(for $\mathord{\mathrel{\star}}\in\{\mathrel{\dot{\in}},\mathrel{\dot{\subseteq}}\}$)}\\
((\exists G:M)\varphi)_{\bm{F},\bm{K}}\lx@intercol\penalty\ \penalty\ \mathrel{:=}\penalty\ \penalty\ &\lx@intercol(\exists\bm{\ell}\mathrel{\dot{\in}}\bm{L})(\exists G:M^{\prime})\left(\bm{F(t)}\mathrel{\doteq}\bm{\ell}\penalty\ \penalty\ \land\penalty\ \penalty\ \varphi_{G\bm{F},M^{\prime}\bm{K}}\right)\hfil\lx@intercol\end{array}

Beware that
(\varphi)_{\bm{F},\bm{K}}
only makes sense under the assumptions
