\bm{F}:\bm{K}
.
Keeping this in mind we may obtain, for instance, the following formulations
of the Functional Choice Axioms of §
3.4.5
.

•

\mathrm{HF}
-Bounded Choice for Functions
.

(\forall x)(\exists k\mathrel{\dot{\in}}K)\varphi(x,k)\penalty\ \penalty\ \Longrightarrow\penalty\ \penalty\ (\exists F:K)(\forall x)\varphi(x,F(x))

•

Iterated
\mathrm{HF}
-Bounded Choice
.

(\forall k\mathrel{\dot{\in}}K)(\exists F:L)\varphi(k,F)\penalty\ \penalty\ \Longrightarrow\penalty\ \penalty\ \left(\exists F:L^{K}\right)(\forall k\mathrel{\dot{\in}}K)\varphi(k,F(-,k))

3.7.2.
Notations for Products and Functions

We now introduce notation for a form of
product type
, based
on the function spaces and application functions
of §
3.4.4
.
7
.
The main idea is to be able to manipulate a Function variable

F\penalty\ \penalty\ :\penalty\ \penalty\ K^{L}

as a function

F\penalty\ \penalty\ :\penalty\ \penalty\ \mathscr{D}^{*}\times L\penalty\ \penalty\ \longrightarrow\penalty\ \penalty\ K

Furthermore, it is convenient to allow such
F
to have a domain
defined by an
\mathsf{FSO}
formula
\psi(-)
, and to write

F\penalty\ \penalty\ :\penalty\ \penalty\ \psi(-)\times L\penalty\ \penalty\ \longrightarrow\penalty\ \penalty\ K\qquad\text{for}\qquad(\forall x)\big(\psi(x)\penalty\ \penalty\ \Longrightarrow\penalty\ \penalty\ F(x)\mathrel{\dot{\in}}K^{L}\big)

We develop here a notation system for such “function” and “product types”.
In §
3.7.3
, we discuss formulations
of the Functional Choice Axioms of §
3.4.5

induced by this notation.
In order not to overload the arrow symbol
\longrightarrow

(which will be used with games later on),
we will write typing declarations as

F:\mathscr{D}^{*}\times L\mathrel{\mathfrak{to}}K\qquad\text{instead of}\qquad F:\mathscr{D}^{*}\times L\longrightarrow K

{nota}

[Product Types]
Product types
are given by the following grammar,
where
\psi(-)
is an
\mathsf{FSO}
formula of an individual variable
(with possibly other free variables of any sort),
and where
K
is an
\mathrm{HF}
-term.

\Pi\quad\mathrel{::=}\quad\psi(-)\quad|\quad K\quad|\quad\Pi\times K

The
arity
of a product type
\Pi
is:

•

(1,n)
if
\Pi
is of the form

\psi(-)\times K_{1}\times\dots\times K_{n}
,

•

(0,n)
if
\Pi
is of the form

K_{1}\times\dots\times K_{n}
.

Product types are to be used with the following defined formulae.

\begin{array}[]{r !{~~\deq~~} l}(t,\bm{K})=(u,\bm{L})\lx@intercol\penalty\ \penalty\ \mathrel{:=}\penalty\ \penalty\ &t\mathrel{\doteq}u\penalty\ \penalty\ \land\penalty\ \penalty\ \bm{K}\mathrel{\doteq}\bm{L}\\
(t,\bm{K})\in\psi(-)\times\bm{L}\lx@intercol\penalty\ \penalty\ \mathrel{:=}\penalty\ \penalty\ &\psi(t)\penalty\ \penalty\ \land\penalty\ \penalty\ \bm{K}\mathrel{\dot{\in}}\bm{L}\\[10.00002pt]
f:K\mathrel{\mathfrak{to}}L\lx@intercol\penalty\ \penalty\ \mathrel{:=}\penalty\ \penalty\ &f\mathrel{\dot{\in}}L^{K}\\
F:\psi(-)\mathrel{\mathfrak{to}}L\lx@intercol\penalty\ \penalty\ \mathrel{:=}\penalty\ \penalty\ &(\forall x)(\psi(x)\penalty\ \penalty\ \Rightarrow\penalty\ \penalty\ F(x)\mathrel{\dot{\in}}L)\\
\textsc{{f}}:\Pi\times K\mathrel{\mathfrak{to}}L\lx@intercol\penalty\ \penalty\ \mathrel{:=}\penalty\ \penalty\ &\textsc{{f}}:\Pi\mathrel{\mathfrak{to}}L^{K}\end{array}

Here
f
stands for a Function variable
F
if the arity of
\Pi

is of the form
(1,n)
,
and for an
\mathrm{HF}
-variable
f
if the arity of
\Pi
is of the form
(0,n)
.
Moreover,
for
\Pi=\psi(-)\times K_{1}\times\dots\times K_{n}
,
we let

(\exists F:\Pi\mathrel{\mathfrak{to}}L)\varphi\penalty\ \penalty\ \mathrel{:=}\penalty\ \penalty\ \left(\exists F:L^{K_{n}\times\dots\times K_{1}}\right)\big[F:\Pi\mathrel{\mathfrak{to}}L\penalty\ \penalty\ \land\penalty\ \penalty\ \varphi\big]

{rems}

(1)

Thanks to Rem.
12
,
using the Axioms of
\mathrm{HF}
-Bounded Choice
(§
3.4.5
),
we have

\big(\textsc{{f}}:\Pi\mathrel{\mathfrak{to}}L\penalty\ \penalty\ \land\penalty\ \penalty\ \varphi\big)\penalty\ \penalty\ \Longrightarrow\penalty\ \penalty\ (\exists\textsc{{f}}:\Pi\mathrel{\mathfrak{to}}L)\varphi

(2)

Using Convention
3.4.4
,
for each product type
\Pi
we have

(\textsc{{f}}:\Pi\times K_{1}\times\dots\times K_{n}\mathrel{\mathfrak{to}}K)\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad\left(\textsc{{f}}:\Pi\mathrel{\mathfrak{to}}K^{K_{n}\times\dots\times K_{1}}\right)

It follows that
for each product type
\Pi

and each formula
\varphi

we have

(\exists\textsc{{f}}:\Pi\times K_{1}\times\dots\times K_{n}\mathrel{\mathfrak{to}}K)\varphi\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad\left(\exists\textsc{{f}}:\Pi\mathrel{\mathfrak{to}}K^{K_{n}\times\dots\times K_{1}}\right)\varphi

{nota}

In the following, given a product type
\Pi
,
we use the notation
\tilde{t}:\Pi
,
where
\tilde{t}
stands for a tuple of the form
(t,K_{1},\dots,K_{n})

if
\Pi
has arity
(1,n)
,
or of the form
(K_{1},\dots,K_{n})
if
\Pi
has arity
(0,n)
.
When
\Pi
is clear from the context, we write
\tilde{t}

instead of
\tilde{t}:\Pi
, and furthermore
we may omit the overset tilde, writing
t
instead of
\tilde{t}
.

Write
\tilde{x}
for tuples of variables of the form

(x,k_{1},\dots,k_{n})
or of the form
(k_{1},\dots,k_{n})
.

(1)

If
\Pi=\psi(-)\times K_{1}\times\dots\times K_{n}

and
\tilde{t}=(t,L_{1},\dots,L_{n})
,
we write
F^{\Pi\to K}(\tilde{t})
for the
\mathrm{HF}
-term

@_{K_{1}\times\dots\times K_{n},K}(F(t),(L_{1},\dots,L_{n}))

If
\Pi=K_{1}\times\dots\times K_{n}

and
\tilde{t}=(L_{1},\dots,L_{n})
,
we write
f^{\Pi\to K}(\tilde{t})
for the
\mathrm{HF}
-term

@_{K_{1}\times\dots\times K_{n},K}(f,(L_{1},\dots,L_{n}))

When
\Pi
and
K
are
clear from the context, in either case above we write

\textsc{{f}}(\tilde{t})

for

\textsc{{f}}^{\Pi\to K}(\tilde{t})
.

(2)

We furthermore write
\tilde{t}\mathrel{\dot{\in}}\textsc{{f}}
or even
\textsc{{f}}(\tilde{t})

for the formula

\textsc{{f}}(\tilde{t})\mathrel{\doteq}1

(3)

We extend product types as follows

\Pi\quad\mathrel{::=}\quad\ldots\quad|\quad\mathscr{D}^{*}\quad|\quad X

where
X
is a Function variable. We let

\begin{array}[]{r !{~~\deq~~} l}F:X\mathrel{\mathfrak{to}}L\lx@intercol\penalty\ \penalty\ \mathrel{:=}\penalty\ \penalty\ &(\forall x)(X(x)\penalty\ \penalty\ \Rightarrow\penalty\ \penalty\ F(x)\mathrel{\dot{\in}}L)\\
F:\mathscr{D}^{*}\mathrel{\mathfrak{to}}L\lx@intercol\penalty\ \penalty\ \mathrel{:=}\penalty\ \penalty\ &(\forall x)(F(x)\mathrel{\dot{\in}}L)\\[10.00002pt]
(t,\bm{K})\in\mathscr{D}^{*}\times\bm{L}\lx@intercol\penalty\ \penalty\ \mathrel{:=}\penalty\ \penalty\ &\top\penalty\ \penalty\ \land\penalty\ \penalty\ \bm{K}\mathrel{\dot{\in}}\bm{L}\\
(t,\bm{K})\in X\times\bm{L}\lx@intercol\penalty\ \penalty\ \mathrel{:=}\penalty\ \penalty\ &X(t)\mathrel{\doteq}1\penalty\ \penalty\ \land\penalty\ \penalty\ \bm{K}\mathrel{\dot{\in}}\bm{L}\par\end{array}

Note that

\begin{array}[]{r !{\quad\longliff\quad} l}F:(\mathscr{D}^{*}\times K_{1}\times\dots\times K_{n})\mathrel{\mathfrak{to}}L\lx@intercol\quad\Longleftrightarrow&F:(\top\times K_{1}\times\dots\times K_{n})\mathrel{\mathfrak{to}}L\\
(\exists F:\mathscr{D}^{*}\mathrel{\mathfrak{to}}L)\varphi\lx@intercol\quad\Longleftrightarrow&(\exists F:L)\varphi\end{array}

3.7.3.
Choice and Comprehension

We list here some important straightforward consequences
of the Functional Choice Axioms of §
3.4.5
pertaining to Product Types.

Theorem 22
.

{\mathsf{FSO}_{\mathscr{D}}}
proves the following generalizations of the
Functional Choice Axioms of §
3.4.5
:

•

\mathrm{HF}
-Bounded Choice
.

(\forall\tilde{x}\mathrel{\dot{\in}}\Pi)(\exists k\mathrel{\dot{\in}}L)\varphi(\tilde{x},k)\penalty\ \penalty\ \Longrightarrow\penalty\ \penalty\ (\exists\textsc{{f}}:\Pi\mathrel{\mathfrak{to}}L)(\forall\tilde{x}\mathrel{\dot{\in}}\Pi)\varphi(\tilde{x},\textsc{{f}}(\tilde{x}))

•

Iterated
\mathrm{HF}
-Bounded Choice
.

(\forall k\mathrel{\dot{\in}}K)(\exists\textsc{{f}}:\Pi\mathrel{\mathfrak{to}}L)\varphi(k,\textsc{{f}})\penalty\ \penalty\ \Longrightarrow\penalty\ \penalty\ \left(\exists\textsc{{f}}:\Pi\mathrel{\mathfrak{to}}L^{K}\right)(\forall k\mathrel{\dot{\in}}K)\varphi(k,\textsc{{f}}(-,k))

Theorem 23

(Comprehension for Product Types)
.

{\mathsf{FSO}_{\mathscr{D}}}

proves the following form of Comprehension, where
V
does not occur free in
\varphi
:

(\exists V:\Pi\mathrel{\mathfrak{to}}2)(\forall\tilde{x}\mathrel{\dot{\in}}\Pi)\big(V(\tilde{x})\penalty\ \penalty\ \Longleftrightarrow\penalty\ \penalty\ \varphi(\tilde{x})\big)

Proof 3.1
.

We require

(\exists V:\Pi\mathrel{\mathfrak{to}}2)(\forall\tilde{x}\mathrel{\dot{\in}}\Pi)\big(V(\tilde{x})\mathrel{\doteq}1\penalty\ \penalty\ \Longleftrightarrow\penalty\ \penalty\ \varphi(x)\big)

By excluded middle, bounded existentials and generalization we have,

(\forall\tilde{x}\mathrel{\dot{\in}}\Pi)(\exists k\mathrel{\dot{\in}}2)\big(k\mathrel{\doteq}1\penalty\ \penalty\ \Longleftrightarrow\penalty\ \penalty\ \varphi(x)\big)

and we conclude by
\mathrm{HF}
-Bounded Choice.

{rems}

(1)

In the case of
\Pi=\mathscr{D}^{*}
,
Theorem
23
gives Comprehension for characteristic functions:

(\exists X:\mathscr{D}^{*}\mathrel{\mathfrak{to}}2)(\forall x)\big(x\in X\penalty\ \penalty\ \Longleftrightarrow\penalty\ \penalty\ \varphi(x)\big)

(
X
not free in
\varphi
)

(2)

We have the following form of Comprehension
for
\mathrm{HF}
-Sets:

(\exists\ell\mathrel{\dot{\subseteq}}K)(\forall k\mathrel{\dot{\in}}K)\big(k\mathrel{\dot{\in}}\ell\penalty\ \penalty\ \Longleftrightarrow\penalty\ \penalty\ \varphi(k)\big)

(
\ell
not free in
\varphi
)

(3)

Using Comprehension for
\mathrm{HF}
-Sets, the well-orders on
\mathrm{HF}
-Sets
given by Remark
12

give the following Induction Scheme for
\mathrm{HF}
-Sets:

(\forall k\mathrel{\dot{\in}}K)\Big[(\forall\ell\mathrel{\dot{\in}}K)\big(\ell\prec_{K}k\penalty\ \Rightarrow\penalty\ \varphi(\ell)\big)\penalty\ \penalty\ \Longrightarrow\penalty\ \penalty\ \varphi(k)\Big]\quad\Longrightarrow\quad(\forall k\mathrel{\dot{\in}}K)\varphi(k)

where
\preceq_{K}
is the well-order on
K
given by Remark
12
.

4.
Game Positions

This Section and the next one describe our setting for games.
The games we consider ultimately aim at formalizing acceptance games of tree automata
(§
6
),
and thus must encompass acceptance games for
non-deterministic
tree automata.
We shall therefore give a setting for infinite games, with players

Proponent

\mathsf{P}
(corresponding to
Automaton
or
\exists
loïse)
and
Opponent

\mathsf{O}
(corresponding to
Pathfinder
or
\forall
bérlard).
In the case of acceptance games,
\mathsf{P}
plays for acceptance and
\mathsf{O}

plays for rejection, and in the particular case of
non-deterministic automata,
\mathsf{P}
chooses transitions
from the non-deterministic transition relation, while
\mathsf{O}

chooses tree directions
d\in\mathscr{D}
, with the aim of building an infinite path.
This leads to an inherent asymmetry in the very notion of games, where,
from a game position with a given tree position
x\in\mathscr{D}^{*}
,

\mathsf{P}
can only go to game positions with tree position
x
,
while
\mathsf{O}
must go to a game position with tree position a successor of
x
.

Due to the fact that we cannot access the usual primitive recursive codings in
the monadic language, we will only consider games that are ‘superposed’ onto
the infinite
\mathscr{D}
-tree,
with only boundedly many positions associated with each tree node.
Such a setting indeed suffices for the case of acceptance games arising from tree automata.
Assume that we are given disjoint non-empty
\mathrm{HF}
-Sets
\mathsf{P}_{\mathcal{G}}
and
\mathsf{O}_{\mathcal{G}}
of

Proponent
and
Opponent
labels respectively.
Intuitively,
Proponent
will play from game positions of the form

\mathscr{D}^{*}\times\mathsf{P}_{\mathcal{G}}

while
Opponent
will play from positions of the form

\mathscr{D}^{*}\times\mathsf{O}_{\mathcal{G}}

A game will be given by specifying edge relations of the form

(x,k)\mathchoice{\mathrel{\overset{}{\underset{\mathsf{P}}{\longrightarrow}}}}{\mathrel{\longrightarrow_{\mathsf{P}}}}{\longrightarrow_{\mathsf{P}}}{\longrightarrow_{\mathsf{P}}}(x,\ell)\quad\text{or}\quad(x,\ell)\mathchoice{\mathrel{\overset{}{\underset{\mathsf{O}}{\longrightarrow}}}}{\mathrel{\longrightarrow_{\mathsf{O}}}}{\longrightarrow_{\mathsf{O}}}{\longrightarrow_{\mathsf{O}}}(x.d,k)\qquad\text{where $k\in\mathsf{P}_{\mathcal{G}}$, $\ell\in\mathsf{O}_{\mathcal{G}}$ and $d\in\mathscr{D}$.}

So
\mathsf{P}
can only move to a game position with the same
underlying tree position,
while
\mathsf{O}
is forced to move to a game position with a
successor underlying tree position.
This induces a dag structure on game positions,
whose underlying partial order
\trianglelefteq_{\mathcal{G}}

is the lexicographic product of the usual tree order with
the one setting
\mathsf{P}
-labels smaller than all
\mathsf{O}
-labels.
The games we shall consider will all be subrelations of
\trianglelefteq_{\mathcal{G}}
.

This Section is devoted to the definition of this dag structure.
We shall also prove some basic results
related to induction in §
4.2

and to infinite paths in §
4.3
.
These will help proving some similar results for games in §
5
,
for which arguments are more naturally given at the level of
\trianglelefteq_{\mathcal{G}}
.

4.1.
A Partial Order of Game Positions

We first introduce the formal notion of labels of game positions.

{defi}

[Labels of Game Positions]

Labels of game positions
are pairs
(\mathsf{P}_{\mathcal{G}},\mathsf{O}_{\mathcal{G}})

of
\mathrm{HF}
-terms satisfying the following formula:

\mathrm{Labels}(\mathsf{P}_{\mathcal{G}},\mathsf{O}_{\mathcal{G}})\quad\mathrel{:=}\quad\lnot(\exists k\mathrel{\dot{\in}}\mathsf{P}_{\mathcal{G}}\cap\mathsf{O}_{\mathcal{G}})\penalty\ \penalty\ \land\penalty\ \penalty\ (\exists k\mathrel{\dot{\in}}\mathsf{P}_{\mathcal{G}})\penalty\ \penalty\ \land\penalty\ \penalty\ (\exists\ell\mathrel{\dot{\in}}\mathsf{O}_{\mathcal{G}})

We write
\mathsf{P}\mathsf{O}_{\mathcal{G}}
for
\mathsf{P}_{\mathcal{G}}\cup\mathsf{O}_{\mathcal{G}}
.
When no ambiguity arises, we write
\mathsf{P}
,
\mathsf{O}
and
\mathsf{P}\mathsf{O}

for
\mathsf{P}_{\mathcal{G}}
,
\mathsf{O}_{\mathcal{G}}
and
\mathsf{P}\mathsf{O}_{\mathcal{G}}
respectively.

Assume
(\mathsf{P},\mathsf{O})
are labels of game positions.
Intuitively, game positions are pairs
(x,k)
with
x\in\mathscr{D}^{*}
and
k\in\mathsf{P}\mathsf{O}
,
Proponent’s positions are game positions with
k\in\mathsf{P}

and Opponent’s positions are game positions with
k\in\mathsf{O}
.
To summarize, we have the informal correspondence:

\begin{array}[]{l !{\qquad} l}\mathscr{D}^{*}\times\mathsf{P}\mathsf{O}&\text{Game positions}\\
\mathscr{D}^{*}\times\mathsf{P}&\text{Proponent's positions}\\
\mathscr{D}^{*}\times\mathsf{O}&\text{Opponent's positions}\end{array}

We will throughout the paper use the following notation
to manipulate game positions and sets of game positions.

{nota}

[Game Positions]

We introduce the following notation,
assuming
\mathrm{Labels}(\mathsf{P}_{\mathcal{G}},\mathsf{O}_{\mathcal{G}})
.

(1)

Variables, written
u,v,w,\text{etc}
, range over game positions,
that is over pairs
(x,k)
with
x
an Individual variable and

k
an
\mathrm{HF}
-variable ranging over
\mathsf{P}\mathsf{O}_{\mathcal{G}}
.

(2)

Sets of game positions, written
U,V,W,\text{etc}
,
range over Functions
\mathscr{D}^{*}\times\mathsf{P}\mathsf{O}_{\mathcal{G}}\mathrel{\mathfrak{to}}2
.
We will systematically use the following notation:

\begin{array}[]{l !{~~\deq~~} l !{\qquad} l}V:\mathcal{G}_{\phantom{\mathsf{O}}}\mathrel{\mathfrak{to}}2\hfil\lx@intercol\penalty\ \penalty\ \mathrel{:=}\penalty\ \penalty\ &V:\mathscr{D}^{*}\times\mathsf{P}\mathsf{O}_{\mathcal{G}}\mathrel{\mathfrak{to}}2&\text{(sets of Game positions)}\\
V:\mathcal{G}_{\mathsf{P}}\mathrel{\mathfrak{to}}2\hfil\lx@intercol\penalty\ \penalty\ \mathrel{:=}\penalty\ \penalty\ &V:\mathscr{D}^{*}\times\mathsf{P}_{\mathcal{G}}\phantom{\mathsf{O}}\mathrel{\mathfrak{to}}2&\text{(sets of Proponent's positions)}\\
V:\mathcal{G}_{\mathsf{O}}\mathrel{\mathfrak{to}}2\hfil\lx@intercol\penalty\ \penalty\ \mathrel{:=}\penalty\ \penalty\ &V:\mathscr{D}^{*}\times\mathsf{O}_{\mathcal{G}}\phantom{\mathsf{P}}\mathrel{\mathfrak{to}}2&\text{(sets of Opponent's positions)}\end{array}

We often write
v\in V
or
V(v)
for
V(v)\mathrel{\doteq}1
.

(3)

For a set of game positions
V
,
we write
V_{\mathsf{P}}
and
V_{\mathsf{O}}

for the
\mathsf{P}
and
\mathsf{O}
subsets of
V
respectively.
This amounts to the following abbreviations:

\begin{array}[]{r !{~~\deq~~} l}v\in V_{\mathsf{P}}\lx@intercol\penalty\ \penalty\ \mathrel{:=}\penalty\ \penalty\ &v\in V\penalty\ \penalty\ \land\penalty\ \penalty\ v\in(\mathscr{D}^{*}\times\mathsf{P}_{\mathcal{G}})\\
v\in V_{\mathsf{O}}\lx@intercol\penalty\ \penalty\ \mathrel{:=}\penalty\ \penalty\ &v\in V\penalty\ \penalty\ \land\penalty\ \penalty\ v\in(\mathscr{D}^{*}\times\mathsf{O}_{\mathcal{G}})\end{array}

Intuitively,

V_{\mathsf{P}}
represents
V\cap(\mathscr{D}^{*}\times\mathsf{P}_{\mathcal{G}})

while

V_{\mathsf{O}}
represents
V\cap(\mathscr{D}^{*}\times\mathsf{O}_{\mathcal{G}})
.

(4)

In formulae we interpret quantifiers over (sets of) game positions as follows:

\begin{array}[]{r !{~~\deq~~} l}(\exists v)\varphi\lx@intercol\penalty\ \penalty\ \mathrel{:=}\penalty\ \penalty\ &(\exists x)(\exists\ell\mathrel{\dot{\in}}\mathsf{P}\mathsf{O}_{\mathcal{G}})\varphi[(x,\ell)/v]\\
(\exists V)\varphi\lx@intercol\penalty\ \penalty\ \mathrel{:=}\penalty\ \penalty\ &(\exists V:\mathcal{G}\mathrel{\mathfrak{to}}2)\varphi\end{array}

where, in the
\exists v
case, we choose
x,\ell
not free in
\varphi
.

We now introduce the partial order
\trianglelefteq
on game positions.

{defi}

[Partial Order on Game Positions]

The relations

\vartriangleleft_{(\mathsf{P}_{\mathcal{G}},\mathsf{O}_{\mathcal{G}})}
,
\trianglelefteq_{(\mathsf{P}_{\mathcal{G}},\mathsf{O}_{\mathcal{G}})}
and
\mathsf{s}^{\vartriangleleft}_{(\mathsf{P}_{\mathcal{G}},\mathsf{O}_{\mathcal{G}})}
,
where
\mathsf{P}_{\mathcal{G}}
and
\mathsf{O}_{\mathcal{G}}
are
\mathrm{HF}
-variables,
are defined as follows:

\begin{array}[]{r @{~~}c@{~~} l c l}(x,k)\penalty\ \penalty\ &\vartriangleleft_{(\mathsf{P}_{\mathcal{G}},\mathsf{O}_{\mathcal{G}})}\hfil\penalty\ \penalty\ &(y,\ell)&\mathrel{:=}&x\mathrel{\dot{<}}y\penalty\ \lor\penalty\ (x\mathrel{\doteq}y\penalty\ \land\penalty\ k\mathrel{\dot{\in}}\mathsf{P}_{\mathcal{G}}\penalty\ \land\penalty\ \ell\mathrel{\dot{\in}}\mathsf{O}_{\mathcal{G}})\\
u\penalty\ \penalty\ &\trianglelefteq_{(\mathsf{P}_{\mathcal{G}},\mathsf{O}_{\mathcal{G}})}\hfil\penalty\ \penalty\ &v&\mathrel{:=}&u\vartriangleleft_{(\mathsf{P}_{\mathcal{G}},\mathsf{O}_{\mathcal{G}})}v\penalty\ \lor\penalty\ u=v\\
\lx@intercol\hfil\mathsf{s}^{\vartriangleleft}_{(\mathsf{P}_{\mathcal{G}},\mathsf{O}_{\mathcal{G}})}(u,v)\lx@intercol&\mathrel{:=}&u\vartriangleleft_{(\mathsf{P}_{\mathcal{G}},\mathsf{O}_{\mathcal{G}})}v\penalty\ \land\penalty\ \lnot(\exists w)\left(u\vartriangleleft_{(\mathsf{P}_{\mathcal{G}},\mathsf{O}_{\mathcal{G}})}w\vartriangleleft_{(\mathsf{P}_{\mathcal{G}},\mathsf{O}_{\mathcal{G}})}v\right)\end{array}

When no ambiguity arises, we write

\vartriangleleft_{\mathcal{G}}
,
\trianglelefteq_{\mathcal{G}}
and
\mathsf{s}^{\vartriangleleft}_{\mathcal{G}}
,
or even
\vartriangleleft
,
\trianglelefteq
and
\mathsf{s}^{\vartriangleleft}

for

\vartriangleleft_{(\mathsf{P}_{\mathcal{G}},\mathsf{O}_{\mathcal{G}})}
,
\trianglelefteq_{(\mathsf{P}_{\mathcal{G}},\mathsf{O}_{\mathcal{G}})}
and
\mathsf{s}^{\vartriangleleft}_{(\mathsf{P}_{\mathcal{G}},\mathsf{O}_{\mathcal{G}})}

respectively.

Note that the formula
\mathsf{s}^{\vartriangleleft}(-,-)
is actually bounded,
since by Notation
4.1
.(
1
),
the variable
w
ranges over game positions,
so that
(\exists w)
stands for

(\exists w\in\mathscr{D}^{*}\times\mathsf{P}\mathsf{O})
.

We note a number of useful properties of
\vartriangleleft
,
in particular that it is a discrete partial order.

Proposition 24
.

{\mathsf{FSO}_{\mathscr{D}}}
proves following, under the assumption
\mathrm{Labels}(\mathsf{P},\mathsf{O})
.

(1)

u\vartriangleleft v\vartriangleleft w\penalty\ \penalty\ \Longrightarrow\penalty\ \penalty\ u\vartriangleleft w

(2)

\lnot(u\vartriangleleft u)

(3)

u\trianglelefteq v\trianglelefteq u\penalty\ \penalty\ \Longrightarrow\penalty\ \penalty\ u=v

(4)

u\vartriangleleft v\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad(\exists w\trianglelefteq v)(\mathsf{s}^{\vartriangleleft}(u,w))\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad(\exists w^{\prime}\trianglerighteq u)(\mathsf{s}^{\vartriangleleft}(w^{\prime},v))

(5)

(\forall k\in\mathsf{P})\big(u\trianglelefteq(\dot{\varepsilon},k)\penalty\ \penalty\ \Longrightarrow\penalty\ \penalty\ u=(\dot{\varepsilon},k)\big)

4.2.
Induction and Recursion

We now present some basic results on induction and recursion w.r.t.
the partial order on game positions.

We can show that
\vartriangleleft

satisfies well-founded induction from the induction principle on the underlying tree.

Theorem 25

(
\vartriangleleft
-Induction)
.

{\mathsf{FSO}_{\mathscr{D}}}
proves the following, under the assumption
\mathrm{Labels}(\mathsf{P},\mathsf{O})
.

(\forall V)\Big((\forall v)\big[(\forall u\vartriangleleft v)(u\in V)\penalty\ \penalty\ \Longrightarrow\penalty\ \penalty\ v\in V\big]\penalty\ \penalty\ \Longrightarrow\penalty\ \penalty\ (\forall v)(v\in V)\Big)

Proof 4.1
.

Let
V
be such that, for any game position
v
:

(\forall u\vartriangleleft v)\penalty\ \big(V(u)\penalty\ \penalty\ \Longrightarrow\penalty\ \penalty\ V(v)\big)

(10)

We show that

(\forall x)(\forall y\mathrel{\dot{\leq}}x)(\forall\ell\mathrel{\dot{\in}}\mathsf{P}\mathsf{O})\big((y,\ell)\in V\big)

by induction on
x
, whence the theorem will follow.

Suppose that
x=\dot{\varepsilon}
, and so
y=\dot{\varepsilon}
.
We first prove the statement for arbitrary
\ell\mathrel{\dot{\in}}\mathsf{P}
;
in this case notice that there is no
u
such that
u\vartriangleleft(\dot{\varepsilon},\ell)
,
and so we vacuously satisfy the LHS of (
10
) above.
Therefore we have that
(\dot{\varepsilon},\ell)\in V
.
Otherwise
\ell\mathrel{\dot{\in}}\mathsf{O}
and every
u\vartriangleleft(\dot{\varepsilon},\ell)
is of the form
(\dot{\varepsilon},k)

for some
k\mathrel{\dot{\in}}\mathsf{P}
, and we have just shown that such
u
must be contained in
V
.
Therefore we can conclude that
(\dot{\varepsilon},\ell)\in V
,
again by (
10
), as required.

Now we consider the inductive step, assuming the statement above is already true
for
x
and considering the case of
\mathsf{S}_{d}x
.
If
y\mathrel{\dot{\leq}}\mathsf{S}_{d}x
then either
y\mathrel{\dot{\leq}}x
or
y\mathrel{\doteq}\mathsf{S}_{d}x
.
In the former case we have by the inductive hypothesis that,
for any
\ell\mathrel{\dot{\in}}\mathsf{P}\mathsf{O}
,
(y,\ell)\in V
.
So assume that
y\mathrel{\doteq}\mathsf{S}_{d}x
.
Again we distinguish when
\ell\mathrel{\dot{\in}}\mathsf{P}

and when
\ell\mathrel{\dot{\in}}\mathsf{O}
in order to exhibit the
LHS of (
10
) above.
In the former case, notice that any
(z,k)\vartriangleleft(y,\ell)
is such that
z\mathrel{\dot{\leq}}x
,
and so we have that
(z,k)\in V
by the inductive hypothesis;
thus
(y,\ell)\in V
by (
10
).
In the latter case (when
\ell\mathrel{\dot{\in}}\mathsf{O}
) we have for any
(z,k)\vartriangleleft(y,\ell)

either
z\mathrel{\dot{\leq}}x
or (
z\mathrel{\doteq}\mathsf{S}_{d}x
and
k\mathrel{\dot{\in}}\mathsf{P}
).
In both cases we have seen that
(z,k)\in V
,
and so again we have that
(y,\ell)\in V
by (
10
).

Since
\vartriangleleft
is a partial order with induction,
comprehension (Theorem
23
)
gives a
Recursion Theorem
,
which allows us to
define a set of game positions
V
by induction on game positions.
This requires the value of
V
at a position
v

to be determined by its values at positions
u\vartriangleleft v
.
Thus, if the value of
V
at
v
is given by a formula
\varphi(V,v)
,
we assume that the following
formula holds

\mathrm{Rec}(\varphi)\penalty\ \penalty\ \mathrel{:=}\penalty\ \penalty\ (\forall v)(\forall V,V^{\prime})\Big[(\forall w\vartriangleleft v)\big(Vw\penalty\ \Leftrightarrow\penalty\ V^{\prime}w\big)\penalty\ \penalty\ \Longrightarrow\penalty\ \penalty\ \big(\varphi(V,v)\penalty\ \Leftrightarrow\penalty\ \varphi(V^{\prime},v)\big)\Big]

The Recursion Theorem says that, assuming
\mathrm{Rec}(\varphi)
,
the set of game positions
V
given by

Vv\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad(\forall U)\Big[(\forall u\trianglelefteq v)\big(Uu\penalty\ \Leftrightarrow\penalty\ \varphi(U,u)\big)\penalty\ \penalty\ \Longrightarrow\penalty\ \penalty\ Uv\Big]

is the unique set of game positions such that

Vv\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad\varphi(V,v)

Proposition 26

(Recursion Theorem)
.

{\mathsf{FSO}_{\mathscr{D}}}
proves that

\mathrm{Labels}(\mathsf{P},\mathsf{O})\land\mathrm{Rec}(\varphi)
implies

\begin{array}[]{l r !{~~\longlimp~~} l}\lx@intercol\hfil(\forall v)\bigg(Vv\penalty\ \Longleftrightarrow\penalty\ (\forall U)\Big[(\forall u\trianglelefteq v)\big(Uu\penalty\ \Leftrightarrow\penalty\ \varphi(U,u)\big)\penalty\ \penalty\ \Rightarrow\penalty\ \penalty\ Uv\Big]\bigg)\penalty\ \penalty\ \Longrightarrow\lx@intercol&(\forall v)\left(Vv\penalty\ \Leftrightarrow\penalty\ \varphi(V,v)\right)\\
\land&(\forall v)(Vv\penalty\ \Leftrightarrow\penalty\ \varphi(V,v))\penalty\ \penalty\ \Longrightarrow\penalty\ \penalty\ (\forall v)(Uv\penalty\ \Leftrightarrow\penalty\ \varphi(U,v))\lx@intercol\penalty\ \penalty\ \Longrightarrow\penalty\ \penalty\ &(\forall v)\left(Vv\penalty\ \Leftrightarrow\penalty\ Uv\right)\end{array}

Proof 4.2
.

Consider a formula
\varphi(V,v)
and assume
\mathrm{Rec}(\varphi)

and
\mathrm{Labels}(\mathsf{P},\mathsf{O})
.
We begin with the second part of the statement, namely the uniqueness part.
Fix
V,U
.
By
\vartriangleleft
-induction on
v
, we show that

\mathsf{FSO}
proves the following formula
\psi(v)=\psi(V,U,v)
:

(\forall u\trianglelefteq v)(Vu\Leftrightarrow\varphi(V,u))\penalty\ \penalty\ \Longrightarrow\penalty\ \penalty\ (\forall u\trianglelefteq v)(Uu\Leftrightarrow\varphi(U,u))\penalty\ \penalty\ \Longrightarrow\penalty\ \penalty\ (\forall u\trianglelefteq v)(Vu\Leftrightarrow Uu)

Let
v
and assume both premises of
\psi(v)
,
as well as
\psi(w)
for all
w\vartriangleleft v
.
The premises of
\psi(v)
imply those of
\psi(w)
for
w\vartriangleleft v
,
so that we have
(Vw\Leftrightarrow Uw)
for all
w\vartriangleleft v
.
Hence, given
u\trianglelefteq v
, if
u\vartriangleleft v
then we are done.
It thus remains to show
(Vv\Leftrightarrow Uv)
.
Thanks to the premises of
\psi(v)
, this amounts to showing

\varphi(V,v)\Leftrightarrow\varphi(U,v)
,
which itself follows from
\mathrm{Rec}(\varphi)
,
since
(Vw\Leftrightarrow Uw)
for all
w\vartriangleleft v
.

We now turn to the first part of the statement.
Let
V
such that

Vv\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad(\forall U)\Big[(\forall u\trianglelefteq v)\big(Uu\penalty\ \Leftrightarrow\penalty\ \varphi(U,u)\big)\penalty\ \penalty\ \Longrightarrow\penalty\ \penalty\ Uv\Big]

By
\vartriangleleft
-induction on
v
, we show that
\mathsf{FSO}
proves the following formula

\theta(v)\quad\mathrel{:=}\quad(\forall u\trianglelefteq v)\underbrace{\big(Vu\penalty\ \penalty\ \Longleftrightarrow\penalty\ \penalty\ \varphi(V,u)\big)}_{\vartheta(u)}

So let
v
and assume
\theta(w)
for all
w\vartriangleleft v
.
Given
u\trianglelefteq v
, if
u\vartriangleleft v
then

\vartheta(u)
follows from
\theta(u)
.
It thus remains to show
\vartheta(v)
.
We consider the two implications separately.

•

Case of
\varphi(V,v)\Longrightarrow Vv
.

Assume
\varphi(V,v)
.
By definition of
V
, we are done if we show

(\forall U)\Big[(\forall u\trianglelefteq v)\big(Uu\penalty\ \Leftrightarrow\penalty\ \varphi(U,u)\big)\penalty\ \penalty\ \Longrightarrow\penalty\ \penalty\ Uv\Big]

Given
U
such that

\big(Uu\penalty\ \Leftrightarrow\penalty\ \varphi(U,u)\big)

for all
u\trianglelefteq v
, we obtain
Uv
from
\varphi(U,v)
,
which itself follows
\varphi(V,v)
and
\mathrm{Rec}(\varphi)
.
The premise of
\mathrm{Rec}(\varphi)
follows from

(\forall w\vartriangleleft v)\psi(V,U,w)
,
whose premises are in turn given by resp.

(\forall w\vartriangleleft v)\vartheta(w)
and the assumption on
U
.

•

Case of
Vv\Longrightarrow\varphi(V,v)
.

Assume
Vv
.
By comprehension (Theorem
23
)
let
U
such that

Uu\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad\Big[(u\vartriangleleft v\penalty\ \land\penalty\ Vu)\penalty\ \lor\penalty\ \big(u=v\penalty\ \land\penalty\ \varphi(V,v)\big)\Big]

We obtain
\varphi(V,v)
from
Uv
,
which in turn by def. of
V

follows from

(\forall u\trianglelefteq v)\big(Uu\penalty\ \Leftrightarrow\penalty\ \varphi(U,u)\big)
.
In order to show the latter,
note that by definition of
U
we have

(Uu\Leftrightarrow Vu)
for all
u\vartriangleleft v
.
Hence
\mathrm{Rec}(\varphi)
gives

\varphi(U,v)\Leftrightarrow\varphi(V,v)

and we get

(Uv\Leftrightarrow\varphi(U,v))
from the definition of
U
.
In the case of
u\vartriangleleft v
, namely
(Uu\Leftrightarrow\varphi(U,u))
,
we have
(\forall w\trianglelefteq u)(Uw\Leftrightarrow Vw)

so that
\mathrm{Rec}(\varphi)
implies

\varphi(U,u)\Leftrightarrow\varphi(V,u)

and the result follows form
\vartheta(u)
.

4.3.
Infinite Paths

We develop here a notion of infinite paths
(
i.e.
unbounded linearly order sets) for the partial order
\trianglelefteq

on game positions.
This material will be useful in Section
5.2

to handle properties of infinite plays in games
which intrinsically rely on the particular structure of
the relation
\trianglelefteq
on game positions.
A typical example is the Predecessor Lemma
37
.

{defi}

[Game Paths]

Let
\mathsf{P},\mathsf{O}
be
\mathrm{HF}
-variables.
Given a game position
u
and a set of game positions
U
,
we say that
U
is a
path from
u
if the following
formula
\mathrm{Path}(\mathsf{P},\mathsf{O},u,U)
holds:

\mathrm{Path}(\mathsf{P},\mathsf{O},u,U)\quad\mathrel{:=}\quad\left\{\begin{array}[]{c l}&u\in U\\
\land&(\forall v\in U)(u\trianglelefteq v)\\
\land&(\forall v\in U)(\exists w\in U)(\mathsf{s}^{\vartriangleleft}(v,w))\\
\land&(\forall v,w\in U)(w\vartriangleleft v\penalty\ \lor\penalty\ v=w\penalty\ \lor\penalty\ v\vartriangleleft w)\end{array}\right.

We write
\mathrm{Path}(u,U)
when
\mathsf{P}
and
\mathsf{O}
are clear from the context.

As a preparation to the Predecessor Lemma
37

for Infinite Plays, we prove here the analogous property
for infinite paths.

Lemma 27

(Predecessor Lemma for Game Paths)
.

{\mathsf{FSO}_{\mathscr{D}}}
proves the following.
Assuming that
\mathrm{Labels}(\mathsf{P},\mathsf{O})
and
that
\mathrm{Path}(\mathsf{P},\mathsf{O},u_{0},U)
hold
for a game position
u_{0}
and a set of game positions
U
,
we have

(\forall v\in U)\left[u_{0}\vartriangleleft v\penalty\ \penalty\ \Rightarrow\penalty\ \penalty\ (\exists u\in U)(\mathsf{s}^{\vartriangleleft}(u,v))\right]

The proof of Lemma
27

relies on the following usual maximality principle
for non-empty linearly-ordered bounded sets.

Lemma 28
.

{\mathsf{FSO}_{\mathscr{D}}}
proves the following, assuming
\mathrm{Labels}(\mathsf{P},\mathsf{O})
.
Given a set of game positions
V
, if

V

is bounded
(i.e.
(\exists u)(\forall v\in V)(v\vartriangleleft u)
),
non-empty
and linearly ordered,
then
V
has a maximum element:

(\exists u\in V)(\forall v\in V)(v\trianglelefteq u)
.

Proof 4.3
.

By
\vartriangleleft
-induction, we prove the following property:

(1)

For all
u
,
for all
V
,
if
V
is non-empty, linearly ordered by
\vartriangleleft

and such that
\forall v\in V(v\trianglelefteq u)
,
then
V
has a
\vartriangleleft
-maximal element.

Let
u
and
V
satisfy the assumptions from
1
above,
and assume
1
for all
c\vartriangleleft u
.
First, if
u=v
for some
v\in V
, then
u
is indeed the maximal element
of
V
.
So we can assume
v\vartriangleleft u
for all
v\in V
.

By Comprehension for Product Types (Thm.
23
),
let
U
be the set of all

w
such that
\mathsf{s}^{\vartriangleleft}(w,u)

and such that
v\trianglelefteq w
for some
v\in V
.
For each
v\in V
,
it follows from Proposition
24
.(
4
)
that there is some
w\in U
such that
v\trianglelefteq w
.
In particular,
U
is non-empty since
V
is non-empty.

We claim the following:

Claim 29
.

(\forall w\in U)(\exists!\tilde{w}\in V)\underbrace{(\forall v\in V)\Big(v\trianglelefteq w\penalty\ \penalty\ \Rightarrow\penalty\ \penalty\ v\trianglelefteq\tilde{w}\Big)}_{\vartheta(w,\tilde{w})}

Proof 4.4

(Proof of Claim 29)
.

Let
w\in U
.
By Comprehension for Product Types (Thm.
23
),
let
W
be the set of all
v\in V
such that
v\trianglelefteq w
.
Note that
W
is non-empty by definition of
U
.
It is inherits the property of being linearly ordered from
V
,
and by construction it is bounded by
w
with
w\vartriangleleft u
.
By induction hypothesis,
W
has a maximal element, say
\tilde{w}
.
We indeed have
\tilde{w}\in V

and
v\trianglelefteq\tilde{w}

for all
v\in V
with
v\trianglelefteq w
.
Since
\tilde{w}\trianglelefteq w
, uniqueness follows from the antisymmetry of
\trianglelefteq
.

The remainder of the argument relies on the particular structure of
\vartriangleleft
.
Using Comprehension on
\mathrm{HF}
-Sets,
it follows from the definition of
\vartriangleleft
that there is some
x\in\mathscr{D}^{*}

and some
\mathrm{HF}
-Set
k
such that

U
is exactly the set of all
(x,\ell)
with
\ell\in k
.
This observation allows us to show

Claim 30
.

(\exists\tilde{w}_{m}\in V)\underbrace{(\forall w\in U)(\forall\tilde{w}\in V)\big(\vartheta(w,\tilde{w})\penalty\ \penalty\ \Rightarrow\penalty\ \penalty\ \tilde{w}\trianglelefteq\tilde{w}_{m}\big)}_{\varphi(\tilde{w}_{m})}

Proof 4.5

(Proof of Claim 30)
.

Write
\preceq
for the well-order on
k
given by Remark
12
.
By
\preceq
-Induction
(Remark
3.7.3
.(
3
))
we show the following:

(\forall\ell\in k)(\exists m\in k)\underbrace{(\forall n\preceq\ell)(\forall\tilde{w}_{n},\tilde{w}_{m}\in V)\big(\vartheta((x,n),\tilde{w}_{n})\penalty\ \penalty\ \Rightarrow\penalty\ \penalty\ \vartheta((x,m),\tilde{w}_{m})\penalty\ \penalty\ \Rightarrow\penalty\ \penalty\ \tilde{w}_{n}\trianglelefteq\tilde{w}_{m}\big)}_{\psi(\ell,m)}

Let
\ell\in k
be such that the property holds for all
\ell^{\prime}\prec\ell
.
If
\ell
is
\preceq
-minimal, the result follows from the
existence of a unique
\tilde{w}
such that
\vartheta((x,\ell),\tilde{w})
.
Otherwise, let
\ell^{\prime}
be the
\preceq
-predecessor of
\ell
,
and let
m\in k
such that
\psi(\ell^{\prime},m)
be given by induction hypothesis.
By Claim
29
, let

\tilde{w}_{\ell},\tilde{w}_{m}
be the unique elements of
V
such that

\vartheta((x,\ell),\tilde{w}_{\ell})
and
\vartheta((x,m),\tilde{w}_{m})
.
Since
V
is linearly ordered, we have either that

\tilde{w}_{m}\trianglelefteq\tilde{w}_{\ell}
or that
\tilde{w}_{m}\trianglelefteq\tilde{w}_{\ell}
.
In the former case, we take
\ell
for the new
m
, and in the latter
we keep the same
m
.

Since
U
is non-empty, there is a
\preceq
-maximal
\ell\in k
.
Let
m\in k
such that
\psi(\ell,m)
,
and by Claim
29
,
let
\tilde{w}_{m}\in V

such that
\vartheta((x,m),\tilde{w}_{m})
.
By definition of
k
, we do have

\tilde{w}\trianglelefteq\tilde{w}_{m}

for all
\tilde{w}\in V
with
\vartheta(w,\tilde{w})

for some
w\in U
.
Hence we have that
\varphi(\tilde{w}_{m})
.

Consider now
\tilde{w}_{m}\in V
such that
\varphi(\tilde{w}_{m})
.
As noted above, for all
v\in V
there is some
w\in U
such that
v\vartriangleleft w
.
But we also have
v\trianglelefteq\tilde{w}
where
\tilde{w}
is unique such that

\vartheta(w,\tilde{w})
.
It thus follows
that
v\trianglelefteq\tilde{w}_{m}
for all
v\in V
.

This concludes the proof of Lemma
28
.

We can now prove Lemma
27
.

Proof 4.6

(Proof of Lemma
27
)
.

Fix
v\in U
with
u_{0}\vartriangleleft v
.
By Comprehension for Product Types (Thm.
23
),
let
W
be the set of all
w\in U
such that
w\vartriangleleft v
.
Since
u_{0}\vartriangleleft v
and
\mathrm{Path}(u_{0},U)
, the set
W

is non-empty, linearly ordered and bounded by
v
.
By Lemma
28
, it has a maximal element, say
w
.
We have
u_{0}\trianglelefteq w
and
w\vartriangleleft v
.
Moreover, by
\mathrm{Path}(u_{0},U)

there is some
\tilde{w}\in U
such that
\mathsf{s}^{\vartriangleleft}(w,\tilde{w})
.
Again by
\mathrm{Path}(u_{0},U)
, we have

\left(\tilde{w}\vartriangleleft v\quad\lor\quad\tilde{w}=v\quad\lor\quad v\vartriangleleft\tilde{w}\right)

But
\tilde{w}\vartriangleleft v
implies
\tilde{w}\trianglelefteq w
, a contradiction,
while
v\vartriangleleft\tilde{w}
implies
w\vartriangleleft v\vartriangleleft\tilde{w}
,
contradicting
\mathsf{s}^{\vartriangleleft}(w,\tilde{w})
.
It thus follows that
\tilde{w}=v

and we are done.

5.
Infinite Two-Player Games

This Section is devoted to definitions and basic properties relating to games,
building on §
4
.
We will use these games in §
6
and §
9
to formalize
a basic theory of tree automata in
\mathsf{FSO}
.

Our games are played on bipartite dags (with partial order
\trianglelefteq_{\mathcal{G}}
)
induced by labels of game positions
(\mathsf{P}_{\mathcal{G}},\mathsf{O}_{\mathcal{G}})
in the sense of §
4
.
Continuing §
4
,

Proponent
will play from positions of the form

\mathcal{G}_{\mathsf{P}}\quad=\quad\mathscr{D}^{*}\times\mathsf{P}_{\mathcal{G}}

while
Opponent
will play from positions of the form

\mathcal{G}_{\mathsf{O}}\quad=\quad\mathscr{D}^{*}\times\mathsf{O}_{\mathcal{G}}

A game will be given by specifying edge relations of the form

(x,k)\mathchoice{\mathrel{\overset{}{\underset{\mathsf{P}}{\longrightarrow}}}}{\mathrel{\longrightarrow_{\mathsf{P}}}}{\longrightarrow_{\mathsf{P}}}{\longrightarrow_{\mathsf{P}}}(x,\ell)\quad\text{and}\quad(x,\ell)\mathchoice{\mathrel{\overset{}{\underset{\mathsf{O}}{\longrightarrow}}}}{\mathrel{\longrightarrow_{\mathsf{O}}}}{\longrightarrow_{\mathsf{O}}}{\longrightarrow_{\mathsf{O}}}(x.d,k)\qquad\text{where $k\in\mathsf{P}_{\mathcal{G}}$, $\ell\in\mathsf{O}_{\mathcal{G}}$ and $d\in\mathscr{D}$,}

so that, for
\mathsf{J}
either
\mathsf{P}
or
\mathsf{O}
,

u\mathchoice{\mathrel{\overset{}{\underset{\mathsf{J}}{\longrightarrow}}}}{\mathrel{\longrightarrow_{\mathsf{J}}}}{\longrightarrow_{\mathsf{J}}}{\longrightarrow_{\mathsf{J}}}v\qquad\text{implies}\qquad u\vartriangleleft v

(actually even
\mathsf{s}^{\vartriangleleft}(u,v)
).
We insist on the fact that
\mathsf{P}
can only move to a game position with the same
underlying tree position,
while
\mathsf{O}
is forced to move to a game position with a
successor tree position.

We first give basic definitions and results on games
(§
5.1
)
and infinite plays
(§
5.2
).
Besides the above mentioned constraints on the shape of games,
these notions are standard.
Our notion of strategy is presented in §
5.3
.
A crucial point here is that, w.r.t. our games,
the monadic language imposes all strategies to be
by construction
positional
in the usual sense
(see e.g.
[
Tho97
]
).
Finally, §
5.4
briefly discusses our setting
for
winning
in games, and §
5.5
presents in more detail
the important particular case of
parity
conditions.
Parity conditions are one of the prominent formulations of winning conditions
for
\omega
-regular games.
This is
in particular due to the fact that they are
positionally
determined,

i.e.
the winner of a parity game can always win with a
positional

winning strategy
[
EJ91
]

(see also
[
Tho97
,
Wal02
,
PP04
]
).
This is of crucial importance in our setting as all our strategies
are inherently positional, due to the underlying limits on expressiveness in the language of
\mathsf{MSO}
.
Finally, the Axiom
(\mathit{PosDet})
of Positional Determinacy
of Parity Games is formulated in §
5.6
.

5.1.
Games

A game
\mathcal{G}
will be given by labels of game positions

\mathsf{P}_{\mathcal{G}}
and
\mathsf{O}_{\mathcal{G}}
together with Functions

E_{\mathsf{P}}:\mathcal{G}_{\mathsf{P}}\mathrel{\mathfrak{to}}\mathcal{P}_{*}(\mathsf{O}_{\mathcal{G}})\qquad\text{and}\qquad E_{\mathsf{O}}:\mathcal{G}_{\mathsf{O}}\mathrel{\mathfrak{to}}\mathcal{P}_{*}(\mathscr{D}\times\mathsf{P}_{\mathcal{G}})

where
\mathcal{P}_{*}(-)
is the
\mathrm{HF}
-Function of §
3.4.4
.
4
.
Such Functions
E_{\mathsf{P}},E_{\mathsf{O}}
induce edge relations

\mathchoice{\mathrel{\overset{}{\underset{\mathsf{P}_{\mathcal{G}}}{\longrightarrow}}}}{\mathrel{\longrightarrow_{\mathsf{P}_{\mathcal{G}}}}}{\longrightarrow_{\mathsf{P}_{\mathcal{G}}}}{\longrightarrow_{\mathsf{P}_{\mathcal{G}}}}
and
\mathchoice{\mathrel{\overset{}{\underset{\mathsf{O}_{\mathcal{G}}}{\longrightarrow}}}}{\mathrel{\longrightarrow_{\mathsf{O}_{\mathcal{G}}}}}{\longrightarrow_{\mathsf{O}_{\mathcal{G}}}}{\longrightarrow_{\mathsf{O}_{\mathcal{G}}}}

given by

\begin{array}[]{l !{\quad\text{iff}\quad} l}(x,k)\mathchoice{\mathrel{\overset{}{\underset{\mathsf{P}_{\mathcal{G}}}{\longrightarrow}}}}{\mathrel{\longrightarrow_{\mathsf{P}_{\mathcal{G}}}}}{\longrightarrow_{\mathsf{P}_{\mathcal{G}}}}{\longrightarrow_{\mathsf{P}_{\mathcal{G}}}}(x,\ell)&\ell\in E_{\mathsf{P}}(x,k)\\
(x,\ell)\mathchoice{\mathrel{\overset{}{\underset{\mathsf{O}_{\mathcal{G}}}{\longrightarrow}}}}{\mathrel{\longrightarrow_{\mathsf{O}_{\mathcal{G}}}}}{\longrightarrow_{\mathsf{O}_{\mathcal{G}}}}{\longrightarrow_{\mathsf{O}_{\mathcal{G}}}}(x.d,k)&(d,k)\in E_{\mathsf{O}}(x,\ell)\end{array}

We make this formal in the following definition.

{defi}

[Games and Edge Relations]

(1)

A
game

\mathcal{G}

is given by
\mathrm{HF}
-terms
\mathsf{P}_{\mathcal{G}},\mathsf{O}_{\mathcal{G}}
and Functions
E(\mathcal{G})_{\mathsf{P}},E(\mathcal{G})_{\mathsf{O}}

which satisfy the following formula

\mathrm{Game}(\mathsf{P}_{\mathcal{G}},\mathsf{O}_{\mathcal{G}},E(\mathcal{G})_{\mathsf{P}},E(\mathcal{G})_{\mathsf{O}})\quad\mathrel{:=}\quad\left\{\begin{array}[]{c l}&\mathrm{Labels}(\mathsf{P}_{\mathcal{G}},\mathsf{O}_{\mathcal{G}})\\
\land&E(\mathcal{G})_{\mathsf{P}}:\mathcal{G}_{\mathsf{P}}\mathrel{\mathfrak{to}}\mathcal{P}_{*}(\mathsf{O}_{\mathcal{G}})\\
\land&E(\mathcal{G})_{\mathsf{O}}:\mathcal{G}_{\mathsf{O}}\mathrel{\mathfrak{to}}\mathcal{P}_{*}(\mathscr{D}\times\mathsf{P}_{\mathcal{G}})\end{array}\right.

We often write
\mathrm{Game}(\mathcal{G})
for

\mathrm{Game}(\mathsf{P}_{\mathcal{G}},\mathsf{O}_{\mathcal{G}},E(\mathcal{G})_{\mathsf{P}},E(\mathcal{G})_{\mathsf{O}})
.
Moreover, when no ambiguity arises, we abbreviate

\mathcal{G}=(\mathsf{P}_{\mathcal{G}},\mathsf{O}_{\mathcal{G}},E(\mathcal{G})_{\mathsf{P}},E(\mathcal{G})_{\mathsf{O}})
as

\mathcal{G}=(\mathsf{P}_{\mathcal{G}},\mathsf{O}_{\mathcal{G}},E_{\mathsf{P}},E_{\mathsf{O}})

or even

\mathcal{G}=(\mathsf{P}_{\mathcal{G}},\mathsf{O}_{\mathcal{G}},E)

or

\mathcal{G}=(\mathsf{P},\mathsf{O},E)
.

(2)

The
edge relations
induced by

\mathcal{G}=(\mathsf{P},\mathsf{O},E_{\mathsf{P}},E_{\mathsf{O}})

are defined as follows:

\begin{array}[]{c !{\quad\deq\quad} l}(x,k)\mathchoice{\mathrel{\overset{}{\underset{\mathsf{P}_{\mathcal{G}}}{\longrightarrow}}}}{\mathrel{\longrightarrow_{\mathsf{P}_{\mathcal{G}}}}}{\longrightarrow_{\mathsf{P}_{\mathcal{G}}}}{\longrightarrow_{\mathsf{P}_{\mathcal{G}}}}(y,\ell)\hfil\lx@intercol\quad\mathrel{:=}&k\mathrel{\dot{\in}}\mathsf{P}\penalty\ \land\penalty\ x\mathrel{\doteq}y\penalty\ \land\penalty\ \ell\mathrel{\dot{\in}}E_{\mathsf{P}}(x,k)\\
(x,\ell)\mathchoice{\mathrel{\overset{}{\underset{\mathsf{O}_{\mathcal{G}}}{\longrightarrow}}}}{\mathrel{\longrightarrow_{\mathsf{O}_{\mathcal{G}}}}}{\longrightarrow_{\mathsf{O}_{\mathcal{G}}}}{\longrightarrow_{\mathsf{O}_{\mathcal{G}}}}(y,k)\hfil\lx@intercol\quad\mathrel{:=}&\ell\mathrel{\dot{\in}}\mathsf{O}\penalty\ \land\penalty\ \mathop{\bigvee}_{d\in\mathscr{D}}\big(y\mathrel{\doteq}\mathsf{S}_{d}(x)\penalty\ \land\penalty\ (d,k)\mathrel{\dot{\in}}E_{\mathsf{O}}(x,\ell)\big)\\
u\mathchoice{\mathrel{\overset{}{\underset{\mathcal{G}}{\longrightarrow}}}}{\mathrel{\longrightarrow_{\mathcal{G}}}}{\longrightarrow_{\mathcal{G}}}{\longrightarrow_{\mathcal{G}}}v\hfil\lx@intercol\quad\mathrel{:=}&u\mathchoice{\mathrel{\overset{}{\underset{\mathsf{P}_{\mathcal{G}}}{\longrightarrow}}}}{\mathrel{\longrightarrow_{\mathsf{P}_{\mathcal{G}}}}}{\longrightarrow_{\mathsf{P}_{\mathcal{G}}}}{\longrightarrow_{\mathsf{P}_{\mathcal{G}}}}v\penalty\ \lor\penalty\ u\mathchoice{\mathrel{\overset{}{\underset{\mathsf{O}_{\mathcal{G}}}{\longrightarrow}}}}{\mathrel{\longrightarrow_{\mathsf{O}_{\mathcal{G}}}}}{\longrightarrow_{\mathsf{O}_{\mathcal{G}}}}{\longrightarrow_{\mathsf{O}_{\mathcal{G}}}}v\end{array}

When no ambiguity arises, we write

\mathchoice{\mathrel{\overset{}{\underset{\mathsf{P}}{\longrightarrow}}}}{\mathrel{\longrightarrow_{\mathsf{P}}}}{\longrightarrow_{\mathsf{P}}}{\longrightarrow_{\mathsf{P}}}
,

\mathchoice{\mathrel{\overset{}{\underset{\mathsf{O}}{\longrightarrow}}}}{\mathrel{\longrightarrow_{\mathsf{O}}}}{\longrightarrow_{\mathsf{O}}}{\longrightarrow_{\mathsf{O}}}

and

\mathchoice{\mathrel{\overset{}{\underset{}{\longrightarrow}}}}{\mathrel{\longrightarrow}}{\longrightarrow}{\longrightarrow}
,
for

\mathchoice{\mathrel{\overset{}{\underset{\mathsf{P}_{\mathcal{G}}}{\longrightarrow}}}}{\mathrel{\longrightarrow_{\mathsf{P}_{\mathcal{G}}}}}{\longrightarrow_{\mathsf{P}_{\mathcal{G}}}}{\longrightarrow_{\mathsf{P}_{\mathcal{G}}}}
,

\mathchoice{\mathrel{\overset{}{\underset{\mathsf{O}_{\mathcal{G}}}{\longrightarrow}}}}{\mathrel{\longrightarrow_{\mathsf{O}_{\mathcal{G}}}}}{\longrightarrow_{\mathsf{O}_{\mathcal{G}}}}{\longrightarrow_{\mathsf{O}_{\mathcal{G}}}}

and

\mathchoice{\mathrel{\overset{}{\underset{\mathcal{G}}{\longrightarrow}}}}{\mathrel{\longrightarrow_{\mathcal{G}}}}{\longrightarrow_{\mathcal{G}}}{\longrightarrow_{\mathcal{G}}}
.

Note that
\mathrm{Game}(\mathcal{G})

implies that the edge relation
\mathchoice{\mathrel{\overset{}{\underset{}{\longrightarrow}}}}{\mathrel{\longrightarrow}}{\longrightarrow}{\longrightarrow}
has no dead ends,

i.e.
that from any position, a move can always be made by one of the players.
It follows that
the edge relation
\mathchoice{\mathrel{\overset{}{\underset{}{\longrightarrow}}}}{\mathrel{\longrightarrow}}{\longrightarrow}{\longrightarrow}
induces
an unbounded partial order.
(Note that it already follows from the structure of
\mathchoice{\mathrel{\overset{}{\underset{}{\longrightarrow}}}}{\mathrel{\longrightarrow}}{\longrightarrow}{\longrightarrow}
that
it induces a partial order.)

Lemma 31
.

{\mathsf{FSO}_{\mathscr{D}}}
proves

\mathrm{Game}(\mathcal{G})\quad\Longrightarrow\quad(\forall u)(\exists v)\left(u\mathchoice{\mathrel{\overset{}{\underset{}{\longrightarrow}}}}{\mathrel{\longrightarrow}}{\longrightarrow}{\longrightarrow}v\right)

Games are equipped with a natural notion of subgame.
In this paper we will use subgames to ease some reasoning on automata
(in particular in §
9
),
and also to more easily define certain strategies that are more naturally seen as concepts at the game level
(see §
5.3
).
We only need the following weak notion of subgame.

{defi}

[Subgame]

We say that
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
is a
subgame
of
\mathcal{G}
whenever the following
formula holds

\mathrm{Sub}(\mathcal{G}^{\prime},\mathcal{G})\quad\mathrel{:=}\quad\mathsf{P}_{\mathcal{G}^{\prime}}\mathrel{\doteq}\mathsf{P}_{\mathcal{G}}\penalty\ \penalty\ \land\penalty\ \penalty\ \mathsf{O}_{\mathcal{G}^{\prime}}\mathrel{\doteq}\mathsf{O}_{\mathcal{G}}\penalty\ \penalty\ \land\penalty\ \penalty\ (\forall u,v)\left(u\mathchoice{\mathrel{\overset{}{\underset{\mathcal{G}^{\prime}}{\longrightarrow}}}}{\mathrel{\longrightarrow_{\mathcal{G}^{\prime}}}}{\longrightarrow_{\mathcal{G}^{\prime}}}{\longrightarrow_{\mathcal{G}^{\prime}}}v\penalty\ \penalty\ \Rightarrow\penalty\ \penalty\ u\mathchoice{\mathrel{\overset{}{\underset{\mathcal{G}}{\longrightarrow}}}}{\mathrel{\longrightarrow_{\mathcal{G}}}}{\longrightarrow_{\mathcal{G}}}{\longrightarrow_{\mathcal{G}}}v\right)

Remark 32
.

Let
\mathcal{G}=(\mathsf{P}_{\mathcal{G}},\mathsf{O}_{\mathcal{G}},E(\mathcal{G})_{\mathsf{P}},E(\mathcal{G})_{\mathsf{O}})
with
\mathrm{Game}(\mathcal{G})
.
Then we have
\mathrm{Sub}(\mathcal{G},\mathcal{G}(\trianglelefteq))
,
where
\mathcal{G}(\trianglelefteq)

stands for the game

(\mathsf{P}_{\mathcal{G}},\mathsf{O}_{\mathcal{G}},E_{\mathsf{O}},E_{\mathsf{P}})

in which by
\mathrm{HF}
-Bounded Choice we let

E_{\mathsf{P}}(x,k)\mathrel{:=}\mathsf{O}_{\mathcal{G}}\qquad\text{and}\qquad E_{\mathsf{O}}(x,\ell)\mathrel{:=}(\mathscr{D}\times\mathsf{P}_{\mathcal{G}})

Note that the edge relation of
\mathcal{G}(\trianglelefteq)

is precisely the relation
\trianglelefteq_{(\mathsf{P}_{\mathcal{G}},\mathsf{O}_{\mathcal{G}})}

of Definition
4.1
,
hence the notation.

The edge relation
\mathchoice{\mathrel{\overset{}{\underset{}{\longrightarrow}}}}{\mathrel{\longrightarrow}}{\longrightarrow}{\longrightarrow}
of a game
\mathcal{G}
only specifies the
moves
of
\mathcal{G}
.
In order to manipulate plays (
i.e.
sequences of moves)
we define the reflexive-transitive closure
\mathchoice{\mathrel{\overset{*}{\underset{}{\longrightarrow}}}}{\mathrel{\longrightarrow^{*}}}{\longrightarrow^{*}}{\longrightarrow^{*}}

and the transitive closure
\mathchoice{\mathrel{\overset{+}{\underset{}{\longrightarrow}}}}{\mathrel{\longrightarrow^{+}}}{\longrightarrow^{+}}{\longrightarrow^{+}}
of
\mathchoice{\mathrel{\overset{}{\underset{}{\longrightarrow}}}}{\mathrel{\longrightarrow}}{\longrightarrow}{\longrightarrow}
.
As expected, these are second-order notions.
2
2

2

It is well known (see e.g.
[
Lib04
, Chap. 4]
)
that transitive closure in graphs
is not expressible in first-order logic over the edge relation.

{defi}

Let
\mathcal{G}=(\mathsf{P},\mathsf{O},E_{\mathsf{P}},E_{\mathsf{O}})

where
\mathsf{P},\mathsf{O}
are
\mathrm{HF}
-variables and
E_{\mathsf{P}},E_{\mathsf{O}}
are Function variables.
We define the following formulae.

\begin{array}[]{r !{\quad\deq\quad} l !{\qquad} l}\mathrm{DC}_{\mathcal{G}}(V)\lx@intercol\quad\mathrel{:=}&(\forall v\in V)(\forall u)\left(u\mathchoice{\mathrel{\overset{}{\underset{\mathcal{G}}{\longrightarrow}}}}{\mathrel{\longrightarrow_{\mathcal{G}}}}{\longrightarrow_{\mathcal{G}}}{\longrightarrow_{\mathcal{G}}}v\penalty\ \penalty\ \Rightarrow\penalty\ \penalty\ u\in V\right)&\text{\emph{($V$ is downward-closed)}}\\
u\mathchoice{\mathrel{\overset{*}{\underset{\mathcal{G}}{\longrightarrow}}}}{\mathrel{\longrightarrow^{*}_{\mathcal{G}}}}{\longrightarrow^{*}_{\mathcal{G}}}{\longrightarrow^{*}_{\mathcal{G}}}v\lx@intercol\quad\mathrel{:=}&(\forall V)\big(\mathrm{DC}_{\mathcal{G}}(V)\penalty\ \penalty\ \Rightarrow\penalty\ \penalty\ v\in V\penalty\ \penalty\ \Rightarrow\penalty\ \penalty\ u\in V\big)\\
u\mathchoice{\mathrel{\overset{+}{\underset{\mathcal{G}}{\longrightarrow}}}}{\mathrel{\longrightarrow^{+}_{\mathcal{G}}}}{\longrightarrow^{+}_{\mathcal{G}}}{\longrightarrow^{+}_{\mathcal{G}}}v\lx@intercol\quad\mathrel{:=}&u\mathchoice{\mathrel{\overset{*}{\underset{\mathcal{G}}{\longrightarrow}}}}{\mathrel{\longrightarrow^{*}_{\mathcal{G}}}}{\longrightarrow^{*}_{\mathcal{G}}}{\longrightarrow^{*}_{\mathcal{G}}}v\penalty\ \land\penalty\ \lnot(u=v)\end{array}

Whenever possible, we write
\mathchoice{\mathrel{\overset{*}{\underset{}{\longrightarrow}}}}{\mathrel{\longrightarrow^{*}}}{\longrightarrow^{*}}{\longrightarrow^{*}}
and
\mathchoice{\mathrel{\overset{+}{\underset{}{\longrightarrow}}}}{\mathrel{\longrightarrow^{+}}}{\longrightarrow^{+}}{\longrightarrow^{+}}

for
\mathchoice{\mathrel{\overset{*}{\underset{\mathcal{G}}{\longrightarrow}}}}{\mathrel{\longrightarrow^{*}_{\mathcal{G}}}}{\longrightarrow^{*}_{\mathcal{G}}}{\longrightarrow^{*}_{\mathcal{G}}}
and
\mathchoice{\mathrel{\overset{+}{\underset{\mathcal{G}}{\longrightarrow}}}}{\mathrel{\longrightarrow^{+}_{\mathcal{G}}}}{\longrightarrow^{+}_{\mathcal{G}}}{\longrightarrow^{+}_{\mathcal{G}}}
.

The relations
\mathchoice{\mathrel{\overset{*}{\underset{}{\longrightarrow}}}}{\mathrel{\longrightarrow^{*}}}{\longrightarrow^{*}}{\longrightarrow^{*}}
and
\mathchoice{\mathrel{\overset{+}{\underset{}{\longrightarrow}}}}{\mathrel{\longrightarrow^{+}}}{\longrightarrow^{+}}{\longrightarrow^{+}}

satisfy properties analogous to those of Proposition
24
:

Proposition 33

(Properties of Edge Relations)
.

{\mathsf{FSO}_{\mathscr{D}}}
proves the following, under the assumption
\mathrm{Game}(\mathcal{G})
.

(1)

u\mathchoice{\mathrel{\overset{}{\underset{}{\longrightarrow}}}}{\mathrel{\longrightarrow}}{\longrightarrow}{\longrightarrow}v\penalty\ \penalty\ \Rightarrow\penalty\ \penalty\ \mathsf{s}^{\vartriangleleft}(u,v)

(2)

\mathchoice{\mathrel{\overset{}{\underset{}{\longrightarrow}}}}{\mathrel{\longrightarrow}}{\longrightarrow}{\longrightarrow}
is irreflexive and asymmetric.

(3)

\mathchoice{\mathrel{\overset{*}{\underset{}{\longrightarrow}}}}{\mathrel{\longrightarrow^{*}}}{\longrightarrow^{*}}{\longrightarrow^{*}}

is reflexive and transitive.

(4)

u\mathchoice{\mathrel{\overset{*}{\underset{}{\longrightarrow}}}}{\mathrel{\longrightarrow^{*}}}{\longrightarrow^{*}}{\longrightarrow^{*}}v\quad\Leftrightarrow\quad u=v\lor(\exists w)\left(u\mathchoice{\mathrel{\overset{*}{\underset{}{\longrightarrow}}}}{\mathrel{\longrightarrow^{*}}}{\longrightarrow^{*}}{\longrightarrow^{*}}w\mathchoice{\mathrel{\overset{}{\underset{}{\longrightarrow}}}}{\mathrel{\longrightarrow}}{\longrightarrow}{\longrightarrow}v\right)\quad\Leftrightarrow\quad u=v\lor(\exists w)\left(u\mathchoice{\mathrel{\overset{}{\underset{}{\longrightarrow}}}}{\mathrel{\longrightarrow}}{\longrightarrow}{\longrightarrow}w\mathchoice{\mathrel{\overset{*}{\underset{}{\longrightarrow}}}}{\mathrel{\longrightarrow^{*}}}{\longrightarrow^{*}}{\longrightarrow^{*}}v\right)

(5)

u\mathchoice{\mathrel{\overset{*}{\underset{}{\longrightarrow}}}}{\mathrel{\longrightarrow^{*}}}{\longrightarrow^{*}}{\longrightarrow^{*}}v\penalty\ \penalty\ \Rightarrow\penalty\ \penalty\ u\trianglelefteq v

(6)

\mathchoice{\mathrel{\overset{*}{\underset{}{\longrightarrow}}}}{\mathrel{\longrightarrow^{*}}}{\longrightarrow^{*}}{\longrightarrow^{*}}
is antisymmetric.

(7)

u\mathchoice{\mathrel{\overset{+}{\underset{}{\longrightarrow}}}}{\mathrel{\longrightarrow^{+}}}{\longrightarrow^{+}}{\longrightarrow^{+}}v\penalty\ \penalty\ \Rightarrow\penalty\ \penalty\ u\vartriangleleft v

(8)

\mathchoice{\mathrel{\overset{+}{\underset{}{\longrightarrow}}}}{\mathrel{\longrightarrow^{+}}}{\longrightarrow^{+}}{\longrightarrow^{+}}
is irreflexive and transitive.

(9)

(\forall k\in\mathsf{P})\left(u\mathchoice{\mathrel{\overset{*}{\underset{}{\longrightarrow}}}}{\mathrel{\longrightarrow^{*}}}{\longrightarrow^{*}}{\longrightarrow^{*}}(\dot{\varepsilon},k)\penalty\ \penalty\ \Rightarrow\penalty\ \penalty\ u=(\dot{\varepsilon},k)\right)

Induction for games (
i.e.
w.r.t. edge relations)
is an immediate corollary to Theorem
25

and Proposition
33
.

Corollary 34

(Game Induction)
.

{\mathsf{FSO}_{\mathscr{D}}}
proves the following,
under the assumption
\mathrm{Game}(\mathcal{G})
.

(\forall V)\left((\forall v)\left[\left(\forall u\mathchoice{\mathrel{\overset{+}{\underset{}{\longrightarrow}}}}{\mathrel{\longrightarrow^{+}}}{\longrightarrow^{+}}{\longrightarrow^{+}}v\right)(u\in V)\penalty\ \penalty\ \Longrightarrow\penalty\ \penalty\ v\in V\right]\penalty\ \penalty\ \Longrightarrow\penalty\ \penalty\ (\forall v)\big(v\in V\big)\right)

5.2.
Infinite Plays

We now define our notion of infinite play.
They are sets of game positions which are unbounded and
linearly ordered w.r.t.
\mathchoice{\mathrel{\overset{}{\underset{}{\longrightarrow}}}}{\mathrel{\longrightarrow}}{\longrightarrow}{\longrightarrow}
.
Infinite plays will allow us to define winning in games (§
5.4
)
and thus acceptance for tree automata (§
6
).
Furthermore, we prove a number of basic properties on infinite plays
on which we rely for the formalization of usual operations
on tree automata.

In the following, given
\mathcal{G}=(\mathsf{P},\mathsf{O},E)
,
we write
\mathrm{Path}(\mathcal{G},u,U)
for
\mathrm{Path}(\mathsf{P},\mathsf{O},u,U)
,
where
\mathrm{Path}
is as in Definition
4.3
.

{defi}

[Infinite Plays]
Let
\mathcal{G}=(\mathsf{P},\mathsf{O},E_{\mathsf{P}},E_{\mathsf{O}})

where
\mathsf{P},\mathsf{O}
are
\mathrm{HF}
-variables and
E_{\mathsf{P}},E_{\mathsf{O}}
are Function variables.
Given a position
u
and a set of game positions
U
,
we say that
U
is an
infinite play in
\mathcal{G}
from
u
when the following
formula
\mathrm{Play}(\mathcal{G},u,U)
holds:

\mathrm{Play}(\mathcal{G},u,U)\quad\mathrel{:=}\quad\left\{\begin{array}[]{c l}&(u\in U)\\
\land&(\forall v\in U)\big(u\mathchoice{\mathrel{\overset{*}{\underset{\mathcal{G}}{\longrightarrow}}}}{\mathrel{\longrightarrow^{*}_{\mathcal{G}}}}{\longrightarrow^{*}_{\mathcal{G}}}{\longrightarrow^{*}_{\mathcal{G}}}v\big)\\
\land&(\forall v\in U)(\exists w\in U)\big(v\mathchoice{\mathrel{\overset{}{\underset{\mathcal{G}}{\longrightarrow}}}}{\mathrel{\longrightarrow_{\mathcal{G}}}}{\longrightarrow_{\mathcal{G}}}{\longrightarrow_{\mathcal{G}}}w\big)\\
\land&(\forall v,w\in U)\big(v\mathchoice{\mathrel{\overset{+}{\underset{\mathcal{G}}{\longrightarrow}}}}{\mathrel{\longrightarrow^{+}_{\mathcal{G}}}}{\longrightarrow^{+}_{\mathcal{G}}}{\longrightarrow^{+}_{\mathcal{G}}}w\penalty\ \lor\penalty\ v=w\penalty\ \lor\penalty\ w\mathchoice{\mathrel{\overset{+}{\underset{\mathcal{G}}{\longrightarrow}}}}{\mathrel{\longrightarrow^{+}_{\mathcal{G}}}}{\longrightarrow^{+}_{\mathcal{G}}}{\longrightarrow^{+}_{\mathcal{G}}}v\big)\end{array}\right.

Note that
\mathrm{Play}(\mathcal{G},u,U)
is literally just the formula
\mathrm{Path}(\mathcal{G},u,U)

in which
\mathchoice{\mathrel{\overset{*}{\underset{\mathcal{G}}{\longrightarrow}}}}{\mathrel{\longrightarrow^{*}_{\mathcal{G}}}}{\longrightarrow^{*}_{\mathcal{G}}}{\longrightarrow^{*}_{\mathcal{G}}}
replaces
\trianglelefteq
,

\mathchoice{\mathrel{\overset{}{\underset{\mathcal{G}}{\longrightarrow}}}}{\mathrel{\longrightarrow_{\mathcal{G}}}}{\longrightarrow_{\mathcal{G}}}{\longrightarrow_{\mathcal{G}}}
replaces
\mathsf{s}^{\vartriangleleft}(-,-)
and

\mathchoice{\mathrel{\overset{+}{\underset{\mathcal{G}}{\longrightarrow}}}}{\mathrel{\longrightarrow^{+}_{\mathcal{G}}}}{\longrightarrow^{+}_{\mathcal{G}}}{\longrightarrow^{+}_{\mathcal{G}}}
replaces
\vartriangleleft
.
It follows from Proposition
33

that
\mathrm{Play}(\mathcal{G},u,U)
implies
\mathrm{Path}(\mathcal{G},u,U)
.
In other words, an infinite play in
\mathcal{G}=(\mathsf{P},\mathsf{O},E)

is simply an infinite path of the underlying
partial order
\trianglelefteq_{(\mathsf{P},\mathsf{O})}

which respects the transitions of
\mathcal{G}
induced by
E
.
Also, if
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
is a subgame of
\mathcal{G}
,
then
\mathrm{Play}(\mathcal{G}^{\prime},u,U)
implies
\mathrm{Play}(\mathcal{G},u,U)
.

We now gather some basic properties on infinite plays.
The first one will help to show that a set of game positions
is linearly ordered.

Proposition 35
.

{\mathsf{FSO}_{\mathscr{D}}}
proves the following, assuming
\mathrm{Game}(\mathcal{G})
.
Let
V
and
u_{0}\in V
be such that

\left\{\begin{array}[]{c l}&(\forall v\in V)(u_{0}\mathchoice{\mathrel{\overset{*}{\underset{}{\longrightarrow}}}}{\mathrel{\longrightarrow^{*}}}{\longrightarrow^{*}}{\longrightarrow^{*}}v)\\
\land&(\forall u\in V)(\exists!v\in V)(u\mathchoice{\mathrel{\overset{}{\underset{}{\longrightarrow}}}}{\mathrel{\longrightarrow}}{\longrightarrow}{\longrightarrow}v)\\
\land&(\forall v\in V)\left[v\neq u_{0}\penalty\ \penalty\ \Rightarrow\penalty\ \penalty\ (\exists u\in V)(u\mathchoice{\mathrel{\overset{}{\underset{}{\longrightarrow}}}}{\mathrel{\longrightarrow}}{\longrightarrow}{\longrightarrow}v)\right]\end{array}\right.

Then

(\forall v,w\in V)\left(v\mathchoice{\mathrel{\overset{+}{\underset{}{\longrightarrow}}}}{\mathrel{\longrightarrow^{+}}}{\longrightarrow^{+}}{\longrightarrow^{+}}w\quad\lor\quad v=w\quad\lor\quad w\mathchoice{\mathrel{\overset{+}{\underset{}{\longrightarrow}}}}{\mathrel{\longrightarrow^{+}}}{\longrightarrow^{+}}{\longrightarrow^{+}}v\right)

Proof 5.1
.

First,
it follows from Proposition
33
.(
6
)
that
u_{0}
is unique such that

(\forall v\in V)(u_{0}\mathchoice{\mathrel{\overset{*}{\underset{}{\longrightarrow}}}}{\mathrel{\longrightarrow^{*}}}{\longrightarrow^{*}}{\longrightarrow^{*}}v)
.
By induction on the edge relation
\mathchoice{\mathrel{\overset{+}{\underset{}{\longrightarrow}}}}{\mathrel{\longrightarrow^{+}}}{\longrightarrow^{+}}{\longrightarrow^{+}}

(cf. Corollary
34
) we show

(\forall u\in V)\underbrace{(\forall v\in V)\left(u\mathchoice{\mathrel{\overset{+}{\underset{}{\longrightarrow}}}}{\mathrel{\longrightarrow^{+}}}{\longrightarrow^{+}}{\longrightarrow^{+}}v\penalty\ \penalty\ \lor\penalty\ \penalty\ u=v\penalty\ \penalty\ \lor\penalty\ \penalty\ v\mathchoice{\mathrel{\overset{+}{\underset{}{\longrightarrow}}}}{\mathrel{\longrightarrow^{+}}}{\longrightarrow^{+}}{\longrightarrow^{+}}u\right)}_{\theta(u)}

Let
u\in V
, and assume that
\theta(v)
holds for all
v\in V

such that
v\mathchoice{\mathrel{\overset{+}{\underset{}{\longrightarrow}}}}{\mathrel{\longrightarrow^{+}}}{\longrightarrow^{+}}{\longrightarrow^{+}}u
.
If
u=u_{0}
then we are done since

u_{0}\mathchoice{\mathrel{\overset{*}{\underset{}{\longrightarrow}}}}{\mathrel{\longrightarrow^{*}}}{\longrightarrow^{*}}{\longrightarrow^{*}}v
for all
v\in V
.
Otherwise, by assumption there is
v\in V

with
v\mathchoice{\mathrel{\overset{}{\underset{}{\longrightarrow}}}}{\mathrel{\longrightarrow}}{\longrightarrow}{\longrightarrow}u
, and moreover such that

u
is the unique
\mathchoice{\mathrel{\overset{}{\underset{}{\longrightarrow}}}}{\mathrel{\longrightarrow}}{\longrightarrow}{\longrightarrow}
-successor of
v
in
U
.

Note that
v\mathchoice{\mathrel{\overset{}{\underset{}{\longrightarrow}}}}{\mathrel{\longrightarrow}}{\longrightarrow}{\longrightarrow}u
implies

v\mathchoice{\mathrel{\overset{+}{\underset{}{\longrightarrow}}}}{\mathrel{\longrightarrow^{+}}}{\longrightarrow^{+}}{\longrightarrow^{+}}u

(Proposition
33
,
(
2
) & (
4
)),
so that
\theta(v)
follows from the induction hypothesis.
Given
w\in V
, if
w\mathchoice{\mathrel{\overset{*}{\underset{}{\longrightarrow}}}}{\mathrel{\longrightarrow^{*}}}{\longrightarrow^{*}}{\longrightarrow^{*}}v
then we get
w\mathchoice{\mathrel{\overset{*}{\underset{}{\longrightarrow}}}}{\mathrel{\longrightarrow^{*}}}{\longrightarrow^{*}}{\longrightarrow^{*}}u
and we are done.
Otherwise, since
\theta(v)
implies
v\mathchoice{\mathrel{\overset{+}{\underset{}{\longrightarrow}}}}{\mathrel{\longrightarrow^{+}}}{\longrightarrow^{+}}{\longrightarrow^{+}}w
,
we may appeal to the following.

Claim 36
.

(\forall w\in V)\left(v\mathchoice{\mathrel{\overset{+}{\underset{}{\longrightarrow}}}}{\mathrel{\longrightarrow^{+}}}{\longrightarrow^{+}}{\longrightarrow^{+}}w\penalty\ \penalty\ \Rightarrow\penalty\ \penalty\ u\mathchoice{\mathrel{\overset{*}{\underset{}{\longrightarrow}}}}{\mathrel{\longrightarrow^{*}}}{\longrightarrow^{*}}{\longrightarrow^{*}}w\right)

Proof 5.2

(Proof of Claim 36)
.

We reason by induction on
\mathchoice{\mathrel{\overset{+}{\underset{}{\longrightarrow}}}}{\mathrel{\longrightarrow^{+}}}{\longrightarrow^{+}}{\longrightarrow^{+}}
.
So let
w\in V
with
v\mathchoice{\mathrel{\overset{+}{\underset{}{\longrightarrow}}}}{\mathrel{\longrightarrow^{+}}}{\longrightarrow^{+}}{\longrightarrow^{+}}w

and such that

(\forall w^{\prime}\in V)\left(w^{\prime}\mathchoice{\mathrel{\overset{+}{\underset{}{\longrightarrow}}}}{\mathrel{\longrightarrow^{+}}}{\longrightarrow^{+}}{\longrightarrow^{+}}w\penalty\ \penalty\ \Rightarrow\penalty\ \penalty\ v\mathchoice{\mathrel{\overset{+}{\underset{}{\longrightarrow}}}}{\mathrel{\longrightarrow^{+}}}{\longrightarrow^{+}}{\longrightarrow^{+}}w^{\prime}\penalty\ \penalty\ \Rightarrow\penalty\ \penalty\ u\mathchoice{\mathrel{\overset{*}{\underset{}{\longrightarrow}}}}{\mathrel{\longrightarrow^{*}}}{\longrightarrow^{*}}{\longrightarrow^{*}}w^{\prime}\right)

Since
u_{0}\mathchoice{\mathrel{\overset{*}{\underset{}{\longrightarrow}}}}{\mathrel{\longrightarrow^{*}}}{\longrightarrow^{*}}{\longrightarrow^{*}}v\mathchoice{\mathrel{\overset{+}{\underset{}{\longrightarrow}}}}{\mathrel{\longrightarrow^{+}}}{\longrightarrow^{+}}{\longrightarrow^{+}}w

we have
w\neq u_{0}
by
Proposition
33
.(
6
),
so that there is
w^{\prime}\in V
with
w^{\prime}\mathchoice{\mathrel{\overset{}{\underset{}{\longrightarrow}}}}{\mathrel{\longrightarrow}}{\longrightarrow}{\longrightarrow}w
.
If
v\mathchoice{\mathrel{\overset{+}{\underset{}{\longrightarrow}}}}{\mathrel{\longrightarrow^{+}}}{\longrightarrow^{+}}{\longrightarrow^{+}}w^{\prime}
then the induction hypothesis implies
u\mathchoice{\mathrel{\overset{*}{\underset{}{\longrightarrow}}}}{\mathrel{\longrightarrow^{*}}}{\longrightarrow^{*}}{\longrightarrow^{*}}w^{\prime}
,
so that
u\mathchoice{\mathrel{\overset{+}{\underset{}{\longrightarrow}}}}{\mathrel{\longrightarrow^{+}}}{\longrightarrow^{+}}{\longrightarrow^{+}}w
and we are done.
Otherwise
\theta(v)
implies
w^{\prime}\mathchoice{\mathrel{\overset{*}{\underset{}{\longrightarrow}}}}{\mathrel{\longrightarrow^{*}}}{\longrightarrow^{*}}{\longrightarrow^{*}}v
.
Assume for contradiction that
w^{\prime}\mathchoice{\mathrel{\overset{+}{\underset{}{\longrightarrow}}}}{\mathrel{\longrightarrow^{+}}}{\longrightarrow^{+}}{\longrightarrow^{+}}v
.
We thus have

w^{\prime}\mathchoice{\mathrel{\overset{+}{\underset{}{\longrightarrow}}}}{\mathrel{\longrightarrow^{+}}}{\longrightarrow^{+}}{\longrightarrow^{+}}v\mathchoice{\mathrel{\overset{+}{\underset{}{\longrightarrow}}}}{\mathrel{\longrightarrow^{+}}}{\longrightarrow^{+}}{\longrightarrow^{+}}w

Proposition
33
.(
7
)
then gives

w^{\prime}\vartriangleleft v\vartriangleleft w
.
But this contradicts

w^{\prime}\mathchoice{\mathrel{\overset{}{\underset{}{\longrightarrow}}}}{\mathrel{\longrightarrow}}{\longrightarrow}{\longrightarrow}w

since the latter implies

\mathsf{s}^{\vartriangleleft}(w^{\prime},w)

by
Proposition
33
.(
1
).
Hence
w^{\prime}=v
. But then
v=w^{\prime}\mathchoice{\mathrel{\overset{}{\underset{}{\longrightarrow}}}}{\mathrel{\longrightarrow}}{\longrightarrow}{\longrightarrow}w\in V
and, since
u

is the unique
\mathchoice{\mathrel{\overset{}{\underset{}{\longrightarrow}}}}{\mathrel{\longrightarrow}}{\longrightarrow}{\longrightarrow}
-successor of
v
in
V
,
we have
u=w
, as required.

This concludes the proof of Proposition
35
.

Proposition
35
is a useful tool to prove
that given sets of game positions are infinite plays.
Some constructions on automata
(see §
6
, §
9
)
furthermore require us to build plays
in one game from plays in another game.
To this end, we note here the following property,
which we informally see as a partial converse to
Proposition
35
.

Lemma 37

(Predecessor Lemma for Infinite Plays)
.

{\mathsf{FSO}_{\mathscr{D}}}
proves the following.
Assuming
\mathrm{Game}(\mathcal{G})
and
\mathrm{Play}(\mathcal{G},u_{0},U)
,
we have

(\forall v\in U)\left[u_{0}\mathchoice{\mathrel{\overset{+}{\underset{}{\longrightarrow}}}}{\mathrel{\longrightarrow^{+}}}{\longrightarrow^{+}}{\longrightarrow^{+}}v\penalty\ \penalty\ \Longrightarrow\penalty\ \penalty\ (\exists u\in U)\left(u\mathchoice{\mathrel{\overset{}{\underset{}{\longrightarrow}}}}{\mathrel{\longrightarrow}}{\longrightarrow}{\longrightarrow}v\right)\right]

Proof 5.3
.

First, it follows from Proposition
33

that
\mathrm{Play}(\mathcal{G},u_{0},U)
implies
\mathrm{Path}(\mathcal{G},u_{0},U)
.
We invoke the Predecessor Lemma
27
for Game Paths.
Assuming
u_{0}\mathchoice{\mathrel{\overset{+}{\underset{}{\longrightarrow}}}}{\mathrel{\longrightarrow^{+}}}{\longrightarrow^{+}}{\longrightarrow^{+}}v
, Proposition
33

implies
u_{0}\vartriangleleft v
,
so there is
u\in U
such that
\mathsf{s}^{\vartriangleleft}(u,v)
.
Since
U
is an infinite play,
u\in U
has an
\mathchoice{\mathrel{\overset{}{\underset{}{\longrightarrow}}}}{\mathrel{\longrightarrow}}{\longrightarrow}{\longrightarrow}
-successor in
U
,
i.e. there is some
u^{\prime}\in U
such that
u\mathchoice{\mathrel{\overset{}{\underset{}{\longrightarrow}}}}{\mathrel{\longrightarrow}}{\longrightarrow}{\longrightarrow}u^{\prime}
.
Again since
U
is an infinite play, we have

\left(v\mathchoice{\mathrel{\overset{+}{\underset{}{\longrightarrow}}}}{\mathrel{\longrightarrow^{+}}}{\longrightarrow^{+}}{\longrightarrow^{+}}u^{\prime}\quad\lor\quad u^{\prime}=u\quad\lor\quad u^{\prime}\mathchoice{\mathrel{\overset{+}{\underset{}{\longrightarrow}}}}{\mathrel{\longrightarrow^{+}}}{\longrightarrow^{+}}{\longrightarrow^{+}}v\right)

But by Proposition
33
again,

v\mathchoice{\mathrel{\overset{+}{\underset{}{\longrightarrow}}}}{\mathrel{\longrightarrow^{+}}}{\longrightarrow^{+}}{\longrightarrow^{+}}u^{\prime}

implies
u\vartriangleleft v\vartriangleleft u^{\prime}
, contradicting
\mathsf{s}^{\vartriangleleft}(u,u^{\prime})
,
while
u^{\prime}\mathchoice{\mathrel{\overset{+}{\underset{}{\longrightarrow}}}}{\mathrel{\longrightarrow^{+}}}{\longrightarrow^{+}}{\longrightarrow^{+}}v
implies
u\vartriangleleft u^{\prime}\vartriangleleft v
,
contradicting
\mathsf{s}^{\vartriangleleft}(u,v)
.
Hence
u^{\prime}=v
and we are done.

Next, we show that games have infinite plays from any position, relying on Remark
12
.

Lemma 38
.

{\mathsf{FSO}_{\mathscr{D}}}
proves that
\mathrm{Game}(\mathcal{G})

implies

(\forall v)(\exists U)\mathrm{Play}(\mathcal{G},v,U)

Proof 5.4
.

Let
\mathcal{G}=(\mathsf{P},\mathsf{O},E_{\mathsf{P}},E_{\mathsf{O}})
.
Fix
v\in V
.
Using Remark
12
,
let
\preceq
be a well-order on
\mathsf{P}\cup\mathsf{O}
.
We extend the relation
\preceq
to
\mathscr{D}^{*}\times(\mathsf{P}\cup\mathsf{O})
by setting:

(x,k)\prec(y,\ell)\quad\mathrel{:=}\quad\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}(x\mathrel{\doteq}y\penalty\ \land\penalty\ k\preceq\ell)\quad\lor\\
(\exists z)\mathop{\bigvee}_{d<d^{\prime}\in\mathscr{D}}\left(x\mathrel{\doteq}\mathsf{S}_{d}(z)\land y\mathrel{\doteq}\mathsf{S}_{d^{\prime}}(z)\right)\end{array}\right.

Remark
12
implies that
every non-empty
W
such that

(\forall(x,k)\in W)(x=\dot{\varepsilon})\quad\lor\quad(\exists z)(\forall(x,k)\in W)\mathop{\bigvee}_{d\in\mathscr{D}}(x=\mathsf{S}_{d}(z))

has a
\preceq
-least element.
By
\mathrm{HF}
-Bounded Choice (Theorem
22
), we define

E^{\prime}_{\mathsf{P}}:\mathscr{D}^{*}\times\mathsf{P}\mathrel{\mathfrak{to}}\mathcal{P}_{*}(\mathsf{O})\qquad\text{and}\qquad E^{\prime}_{\mathsf{O}}:\mathscr{D}^{*}\times\mathsf{O}\mathrel{\mathfrak{to}}\mathcal{P}_{*}(\mathscr{D}\times\mathsf{P})

by setting, for
\mathsf{J}
either
\mathsf{P}
or
\mathsf{O}
,

E^{\prime}_{\mathsf{J}}(u)\quad\mathrel{:=}\quad\{\text{the $\preceq$-least element of $E_{\mathsf{J}}(u)$}\}

Let
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}\mathrel{:=}(\mathsf{P},\mathsf{O},E^{\prime})
.
Note that we have
\mathrm{Game}(\mathcal{G}^{\prime})

and that

(\forall u,v)\left(u\mathchoice{\mathrel{\overset{}{\underset{\mathcal{G}^{\prime}}{\longrightarrow}}}}{\mathrel{\longrightarrow_{\mathcal{G}^{\prime}}}}{\longrightarrow_{\mathcal{G}^{\prime}}}{\longrightarrow_{\mathcal{G}^{\prime}}}v\penalty\ \penalty\ \Rightarrow\penalty\ \penalty\ u\mathchoice{\mathrel{\overset{}{\underset{\mathcal{G}}{\longrightarrow}}}}{\mathrel{\longrightarrow_{\mathcal{G}}}}{\longrightarrow_{\mathcal{G}}}{\longrightarrow_{\mathcal{G}}}v\right)

(11)

By Comprehension for Product Types (Theorem
23
),
we then let

U\quad\mathrel{:=}\quad\{u\penalty\ |\penalty\ v\mathchoice{\mathrel{\overset{*}{\underset{\mathcal{G}^{\prime}}{\longrightarrow}}}}{\mathrel{\longrightarrow^{*}_{\mathcal{G}^{\prime}}}}{\longrightarrow^{*}_{\mathcal{G}^{\prime}}}{\longrightarrow^{*}_{\mathcal{G}^{\prime}}}u\}

It remains to show

\mathrm{Play}(\mathcal{G},v,U)

First, we have
v\in U
by reflexivity of
\mathchoice{\mathrel{\overset{*}{\underset{\mathcal{G}^{\prime}}{\longrightarrow}}}}{\mathrel{\longrightarrow^{*}_{\mathcal{G}^{\prime}}}}{\longrightarrow^{*}_{\mathcal{G}^{\prime}}}{\longrightarrow^{*}_{\mathcal{G}^{\prime}}}

(Proposition
33
.(
3
)),
and
(\forall u\in U)\left(v\mathchoice{\mathrel{\overset{*}{\underset{\mathcal{G}}{\longrightarrow}}}}{\mathrel{\longrightarrow^{*}_{\mathcal{G}}}}{\longrightarrow^{*}_{\mathcal{G}}}{\longrightarrow^{*}_{\mathcal{G}}}u\right)

follows from (
11
).
Moreover, we have

(\forall u\in U)(\exists w\in U)\left(u\mathchoice{\mathrel{\overset{}{\underset{\mathcal{G}}{\longrightarrow}}}}{\mathrel{\longrightarrow_{\mathcal{G}}}}{\longrightarrow_{\mathcal{G}}}{\longrightarrow_{\mathcal{G}}}w\right)

thanks to (
11
),
since this property already holds for
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
.
It remains to show that
U
is linearly ordered w.r.t.
\mathchoice{\mathrel{\overset{*}{\underset{\mathcal{G}}{\longrightarrow}}}}{\mathrel{\longrightarrow^{*}_{\mathcal{G}}}}{\longrightarrow^{*}_{\mathcal{G}}}{\longrightarrow^{*}_{\mathcal{G}}}
.
We invoke Proposition
35
:
its first premise has already been discussed,
its second follows from the definition of
E^{\prime}
,
and its last one is
Proposition
33
.(
4
).

Finally, in some situations
(typically for the
Simulation Theorem
in §
9
),
it is convenient to build infinite plays from
paths (in the sense of Definition
4.3
).

Lemma 39

(Infinite Plays From Paths)
.

Assume
\mathrm{Game}(\mathcal{G})

and let
u_{0}
and
U

be such that

\mathrm{Path}(\mathcal{G},u_{0},U)\penalty\ \land\penalty\ (\forall u,v\in U)\left[\mathsf{s}^{\vartriangleleft}(u,v)\penalty\ \Rightarrow\penalty\ u\mathchoice{\mathrel{\overset{}{\underset{}{\longrightarrow}}}}{\mathrel{\longrightarrow}}{\longrightarrow}{\longrightarrow}v\right]

Then
{\mathsf{FSO}_{\mathscr{D}}}
proves
\mathrm{Play}(\mathcal{G},u_{0},U)
.

Proof 5.5
.

Thanks to Proposition
33
,
the result directly follows from the fact that

(\forall u,v\in U)\left(u\trianglelefteq v\penalty\ \penalty\ \Longrightarrow\penalty\ \penalty\ u\mathchoice{\mathrel{\overset{*}{\underset{}{\longrightarrow}}}}{\mathrel{\longrightarrow^{*}}}{\longrightarrow^{*}}{\longrightarrow^{*}}v\right)

Fix
u\in U
. By
\vartriangleleft
-induction we show

(\forall v\in U)(u\trianglelefteq v\penalty\ \penalty\ \Rightarrow\penalty\ \penalty\ u\mathchoice{\mathrel{\overset{*}{\underset{}{\longrightarrow}}}}{\mathrel{\longrightarrow^{*}}}{\longrightarrow^{*}}{\longrightarrow^{*}}v)
.
So let
v\in U
such that the property holds for all
w\vartriangleleft v
,
and assume
u\trianglelefteq v
.
If
u=v
then we are done.
Otherwise, by the Predecessor Lemma
27
for Paths,
we have
\mathsf{s}^{\vartriangleleft}(w,v)
for some
w\in U
with
u\trianglelefteq w
.
By induction hypothesis we get
u\mathchoice{\mathrel{\overset{*}{\underset{}{\longrightarrow}}}}{\mathrel{\longrightarrow^{*}}}{\longrightarrow^{*}}{\longrightarrow^{*}}w\mathchoice{\mathrel{\overset{}{\underset{}{\longrightarrow}}}}{\mathrel{\longrightarrow}}{\longrightarrow}{\longrightarrow}v

and we conclude by Proposition
33
.

5.3.
Strategies

We now turn to strategies.
Our strategies are Functions from the positions of one player to the set
of labels of the other player,
which
must respect the edge relations.
This implies that all our strategies are, by definition, positional.

{defi}

[Strategies]

Let
\mathcal{G}=(\mathsf{P},\mathsf{O},E_{\mathsf{P}},E_{\mathsf{O}})

where
\mathsf{P},\mathsf{O}
are
\mathrm{HF}
-variables and where
E_{\mathsf{P}},E_{\mathsf{O}}
are Function variables.

(1)

A
\mathsf{P}
-strategy on
\mathcal{G}

is a Function
\sigma

which satisfies the formula

\mathrm{Strat}_{\mathsf{P}}(\mathcal{G},\sigma)\quad\mathrel{:=}\quad\sigma:\mathcal{G}_{\mathsf{P}}\mathrel{\mathfrak{to}}\mathsf{O}\penalty\ \penalty\ \land\penalty\ \penalty\ (\forall v)\left(\sigma(v)\in E_{\mathsf{P}}(v)\right)

(2)

An
\mathsf{O}
-strategy on
\mathcal{G}
is a Function

\sigma

which satisfies the formula

\mathrm{Strat}_{\mathsf{O}}(\mathcal{G},\sigma)\quad\mathrel{:=}\quad\sigma:\mathcal{G}_{\mathsf{O}}\mathrel{\mathfrak{to}}\mathscr{D}\times\mathsf{P}\penalty\ \penalty\ \land\penalty\ \penalty\ (\forall v)\left(\sigma(v)\in E_{\mathsf{O}}(v)\right)

Strategies naturally induce subgames in the sense of Definition
5.1
.
This will allow us to lift to strategies notions
which are more naturally defined at the level of games.

{defi}

[Subgame induced by a Strategy]
Given a player
\mathsf{J}
(either
\mathsf{P}
or
\mathsf{O}
)
and a
\mathsf{J}
-strategy
\sigma
on
\mathcal{G}
,
we let

\mathcal{G}\mathord{\upharpoonright}\{\sigma\}_{\mathsf{J}}\quad\mathrel{:=}\quad(\mathsf{P}_{\mathcal{G}}\,,\,\mathsf{O}_{\mathcal{G}}\,,\,E(\mathcal{G})\mathord{\upharpoonright}\{\sigma\}_{\mathsf{J}})

where

E(\mathcal{G})\mathord{\upharpoonright}\{\sigma\}_{\mathsf{P}}\penalty\ \penalty\ \mathrel{:=}\penalty\ \penalty\ (\{\sigma\}_{\mathsf{P}},E(\mathcal{G})_{\mathsf{O}})\qquad\text{and}\qquad E(\mathcal{G})\mathord{\upharpoonright}\{\sigma\}_{\mathsf{O}}\penalty\ \penalty\ \mathrel{:=}\penalty\ \penalty\ (E(\mathcal{G})_{\mathsf{P}},\{\sigma\}_{\mathsf{O}})

and where
\{\sigma\}_{\mathsf{J}}\subseteq E(\mathcal{G})_{\mathsf{J}}

is defined by
\mathrm{HF}
-Bounded Choice to be the Function
taking
u\in\mathscr{D}^{*}\times\mathcal{G}_{\mathsf{J}}

to the singleton
\{\sigma(u)\}
.

Whenever possible, we write
\mathcal{G}\mathord{\upharpoonright}\{\sigma\}

or even just
\sigma
for
\mathcal{G}\mathord{\upharpoonright}\{\sigma\}_{\mathsf{J}}
, when it is unambiguous.

Lemma 40
.

{\mathsf{FSO}_{\mathscr{D}}}
proves the following, where
\mathsf{J}
is a player
(either
\mathsf{P}
or
\mathsf{O}
):

\big(\mathrm{Game}(\mathcal{G})\penalty\ \penalty\ \land\penalty\ \penalty\ \mathrm{Strat}_{\mathsf{J}}(\mathcal{G},\sigma)\big)\quad\Longrightarrow\quad\mathrm{Game}(\sigma)

This in particular allows us to speak of the infinite plays of a strategy

\sigma
on
\mathcal{G}
simply as infinite plays of the game
\mathcal{G}\mathord{\upharpoonright}\{\sigma\}
.

5.4.
Winning

In order to deal with acceptance for automata, we equip games
with a notion of
winning
.
Given a game
\mathcal{G}
, a
winning condition
on
\mathcal{G}

is a formula
\mathcal{W}(U)
where
U
is intended to range over the infinite
plays of
\mathcal{G}
.
As usual a
\mathsf{P}
-strategy
\sigma
on
(\mathcal{G},\mathcal{W})

is winning from a position
v

whenever all the infinite plays
U
of
\sigma
from
v
satisfy
\mathcal{W}(U)
.
Dually, an
\mathsf{O}
-strategy is winning from
v

when all its infinite plays
U
from
v
satisfy
\lnot\mathcal{W}(U)
.

We formally proceed as follows.

{defi}

Let
\mathcal{G}=(\mathsf{P},\mathsf{O},E_{\mathsf{P}},E_{\mathsf{O}})

where
\mathsf{P},\mathsf{O}
are
\mathrm{HF}
-variables and
E_{\mathsf{P}},E_{\mathsf{O}}
are Function variables.
Let
\mathcal{W}(U)
be a given
\mathsf{FSO}
-formula where
U
is a Function variable.

(1)

We define the following formulae.

\begin{array}[]{r !{\quad\deq\quad} l}\mathrm{WonGame}_{\mathsf{P}}(\mathcal{G},v,\mathcal{W})\lx@intercol\quad\mathrel{:=}&(\forall U)\big(\mathrm{Play}(\mathcal{G},v,U)\penalty\ \penalty\ \Rightarrow\penalty\ \penalty\ \mathcal{W}(U)\big)\\
\mathrm{WonGame}_{\mathsf{O}}(\mathcal{G},v,\mathcal{W})\lx@intercol\quad\mathrel{:=}&(\forall U)\big(\mathrm{Play}(\mathcal{G},v,U)\penalty\ \penalty\ \Rightarrow\penalty\ \penalty\ \lnot\mathcal{W}(U)\big)\end{array}

(2)

Given a player
\mathsf{J}
(either
\mathsf{P}
or
\mathsf{O}
),
we say that a
\mathsf{J}
-strategy
\sigma
is

winning in
(\mathcal{G},\mathcal{W})
from
v

if the game
(\mathcal{G}\mathord{\upharpoonright}\{\sigma\}_{\mathsf{J}},\mathcal{W})
is won by
\mathsf{J}
from
v
,

i.e.
if
the following formula holds

\mathrm{WinStrat}_{\mathsf{J}}(\mathcal{G},\sigma,v,\mathcal{W})\quad\mathrel{:=}\quad\mathrm{WonGame}_{\mathsf{J}}(\mathcal{G}\mathord{\upharpoonright}\{\sigma\}_{\mathsf{J}},v,\mathcal{W})

Strictly speaking, in Definition
5.4
above,

\mathrm{WonGame}_{\mathsf{J}}

and

\mathrm{WinStrat}_{\mathsf{J}}

are actually families of
\mathsf{FSO}
formulae, parametrized by the choice of
\mathsf{FSO}
-formula
\mathcal{W}
.

As expected, a game position cannot be winning for both players.

Lemma 41
.

{\mathsf{FSO}_{\mathscr{D}}}
proves the following.

\mathrm{Game}(\mathcal{G})\quad\Longrightarrow\quad\mathrm{Strat}_{\mathsf{P}}(\mathcal{G},\sigma_{\mathsf{P}})\quad\Longrightarrow\quad\mathrm{Strat}_{\mathsf{O}}(\mathcal{G},\sigma_{\mathsf{O}})\quad\Longrightarrow\\
