\lnot(\exists v)\bigg[\mathrm{WinStrat}_{\mathsf{P}}(\mathcal{G},\sigma_{\mathsf{P}},v,\mathcal{W})\penalty\ \penalty\ \land\penalty\ \penalty\ \mathrm{WinStrat}_{\mathsf{O}}(\mathcal{G},\sigma_{\mathsf{O}},v,\mathcal{W})\bigg]

Proof 5.6
.

Assume for contradiction that for some
v
we have

\mathrm{WinStrat}_{\mathsf{P}}(\mathcal{G},\sigma_{\mathsf{P}},v,\mathcal{W})\penalty\ \penalty\ \land\penalty\ \penalty\ \mathrm{WinStrat}_{\mathsf{O}}(\mathcal{G},\sigma_{\mathsf{O}},v,\mathcal{W})

that is

(\forall U)\Big[\mathrm{Play}(\mathcal{G}\mathord{\upharpoonright}\{\sigma_{\mathsf{P}}\},v,U)\penalty\ \penalty\ \Rightarrow\penalty\ \penalty\ \mathcal{W}(U)\Big]\penalty\ \penalty\ \land\penalty\ \penalty\ (\forall U)\Big[\mathrm{Play}(\mathcal{G}\mathord{\upharpoonright}\{\sigma_{\mathsf{O}}\},v,U)\penalty\ \penalty\ \Rightarrow\penalty\ \penalty\ \lnot\mathcal{W}(U)\Big]

Consider the game

\mathcal{G}^{\prime}\quad\mathrel{:=}\quad(\mathsf{P},\,\mathsf{O},\,\{\sigma_{\mathsf{P}}\}_{\mathsf{P}},\,\{\sigma_{\mathsf{O}}\}_{\mathsf{O}})

Note that
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
is a subgame of both
\mathcal{G}\mathord{\upharpoonright}\{\sigma_{\mathsf{P}}\}

and
\mathcal{G}\mathord{\upharpoonright}\{\sigma_{\mathsf{O}}\}
.
We thus get

(\forall U)\Big[\mathrm{Play}(\mathcal{G}^{\prime},v,U)\penalty\ \penalty\ \Rightarrow\penalty\ \penalty\ \mathcal{W}(U)\land\lnot\mathcal{W}(U)\Big]

which implies that there is no
U

such that
\mathrm{Play}(\mathcal{G}^{\prime},v,U)
,
contradicting Lemma
38
.

5.5.
Parity Conditions

In this paper, we mostly consider winning conditions expressed as

parity conditions
.
Parity conditions are defined from
colorings

of game positions by natural numbers from a given finite interval.
We represent natural numbers and the operations and relations on them
using the Functions on
\mathrm{HF}
-Sets of
\mathsf{FSO}
and the axioms
of §
3.4.4
.

{conv}

In order to conveniently manipulate colorings and parity conditions,
we will use the following functions on finite ordinals
(a.k.a. natural numbers),
obtained from the
Axioms on
\mathrm{HF}
-Functions
(see §
3.4.4
).
We rely on the well-known fact that “
n
is an ordinal
”
can be expressed by an
\mathrm{HF}
-formula

\mathrm{Ord}(n)

(see e.g.
[
Jec06
, Lemma 12.10]
).

(1)

We consider unary
\mathrm{HF}
-Functions

[0,-]\,,\,[0,-)\,,\,(0,-]\penalty\ \penalty\ :\penalty\ \penalty\ V_{\omega}\penalty\ \penalty\ \longrightarrow\penalty\ \penalty\ V_{\omega}

such that for all finite ordinals
n
, we have

\mathbf{Sk}(\mathsf{ZFC}^{-})\vdash\quad[0,n]\penalty\ \mathrel{\doteq}\penalty\ \{0,\dots,n\}\penalty\ \penalty\ \land\penalty\ \penalty\ [0,n)\penalty\ \mathrel{\doteq}\penalty\ \{0,\dots,n-1\}\penalty\ \penalty\ \land\penalty\ \penalty\ (0,n]\penalty\ \mathrel{\doteq}\penalty\ \{1,\dots,n\}

(2)

We consider binary
\mathrm{HF}
-Functions

\dot{\mathsf{g}}_{\leq}\,,\,\dot{\mathsf{g}}_{<}\,,\,\dot{\mathsf{g}}_{\geq}\,,\,\dot{\mathsf{g}}_{>}\penalty\ \penalty\ :\penalty\ \penalty\ V_{\omega}\times V_{\omega}\penalty\ \penalty\ \longrightarrow\penalty\ \penalty\ 2

such that for finite ordinals
n,m

\begin{array}[]{c !{\qquad} c}\dot{\mathsf{g}}_{\leq}(n,m)=1\penalty\ \penalty\ \text{iff}\penalty\ \penalty\ n\leq m&\dot{\mathsf{g}}_{\geq}(n,m)=1\penalty\ \penalty\ \text{iff}\penalty\ \penalty\ n\geq m\\
\dot{\mathsf{g}}_{<}(n,m)=1\penalty\ \penalty\ \text{iff}\penalty\ \penalty\ n<m&\dot{\mathsf{g}}_{>}(n,m)=1\penalty\ \penalty\ \text{iff}\penalty\ \penalty\ n>m\end{array}

In
\mathsf{FSO}
-formulae, we write

n\leq m
for the formula
\dot{\mathsf{g}}_{\leq}(n,m)\mathrel{\doteq}1
, and so on.

(3)

We consider a unary
\mathrm{HF}
-Function

\dot{\mathsf{even}}\penalty\ \penalty\ :\penalty\ \penalty\ V_{\omega}\penalty\ \penalty\ \longrightarrow\penalty\ \penalty\ V_{\omega}

such that for each ordinal
n
,

\dot{\mathsf{even}}(n)
is the set of ordinals
m\in[0,n]

such that
m
represents an even number.

(4)

We consider
\mathrm{HF}
-Functions
\mathsf{max}(-,-)
and
(-)+1
,
computing respectively the maximum
of two finite ordinals and the successor ordinal of an ordinal.

Remark 42
.

Even if “
n
is an ordinal
” can be expressed by an

\mathrm{HF}
-formula, quantification over all finite ordinals

cannot
be expressed in
V_{\omega}
by an
\mathrm{HF}
-formula,
since for each finite ordinal
n>0

we have
n\in V_{n}\setminus V_{n-1}
.
In particular, induction over finite
\mathrm{HF}
-ordinals cannot be expressed
by an
\mathrm{HF}
-formula.

{defi}

[Parity Conditions]

Let
\mathcal{G}=(\mathsf{P},\mathsf{O},E_{\mathsf{P}},E_{\mathsf{O}})

where
\mathsf{P},\mathsf{O}
are
\mathrm{HF}
-variables and
E_{\mathsf{P}},E_{\mathsf{O}}
are Function variables.

(1)

A
coloring
is given by a Function
C
and an
\mathrm{HF}
-term
n

satisfying the following formula

\mathrm{Col}(\mathcal{G},C,n)\quad\mathrel{:=}\quad\mathrm{Ord}(n)\penalty\ \penalty\ \land\penalty\ \penalty\ C:\mathcal{G}\mathrel{\mathfrak{to}}[0,n]

(2)

We define the following formula:

\mathrm{Par}(\mathcal{G},C,n,U)\quad\mathrel{:=}\quad(\exists m\mathrel{\dot{\in}}\dot{\mathsf{even}}(n))\left[\begin{array}[]{r l}&(\forall u\in U)(\exists v\in U)(u\mathchoice{\mathrel{\overset{+}{\underset{}{\longrightarrow}}}}{\mathrel{\longrightarrow^{+}}}{\longrightarrow^{+}}{\longrightarrow^{+}}v\penalty\ \land\penalty\ C(v)\mathrel{\doteq}m)\\
\land&(\exists u\in U)(\forall v\in U)(u\mathchoice{\mathrel{\overset{+}{\underset{}{\longrightarrow}}}}{\mathrel{\longrightarrow^{+}}}{\longrightarrow^{+}}{\longrightarrow^{+}}v\penalty\ \Rightarrow\penalty\ C(v)\geq m)\end{array}\right]

Remark 43
.

The formula
\mathrm{Par}(\mathcal{G},C,n,U)
will be used to say that an infinite play
U

satisfies the (min) parity condition induced by the
coloring
C:\mathcal{G}\mathrel{\mathfrak{to}}[0,n]
.
In the standard model
\mathfrak{T}
, if
U
is an infinite play in
\mathcal{G}
, then

\mathrm{Par}(\mathcal{G},C,n,U)
holds if and only if there is an even
m\leq n

such that

U
has infinitely many positions colored by
m
, and

U
has only finitely many positions colored by any
k<m
.
Also, notice that any
U
(not necessarily a play) satisfying
\mathrm{Par}(\mathcal{G},C,n,U)
in
\mathfrak{T}
is infinite.

Remark 44
.

Assume that
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
is a subgame of
\mathcal{G}

(in the sense of Definition
5.1
).
Note that
\mathsf{FSO}
proves

\mathrm{Col}(\mathcal{G},C,n)\penalty\ \penalty\ \Longleftrightarrow\penalty\ \penalty\ \mathrm{Col}(\mathcal{G}^{\prime},C,n)

Furthermore, as noted earlier, every infinite play in
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}

is an infinite play in
\mathcal{G}
.
It follows that
\mathsf{FSO}
proves

\mathrm{Game}(\mathcal{G})\penalty\ \penalty\ \Longrightarrow\penalty\ \penalty\ \mathrm{Col}(\mathcal{G},C,n)\penalty\ \penalty\ \Longrightarrow\penalty\ \penalty\ \mathrm{Game}(\mathcal{G}^{\prime})\penalty\ \penalty\ \Longrightarrow\penalty\ \penalty\ \mathrm{Sub}(\mathcal{G}^{\prime},\mathcal{G})\penalty\ \penalty\ \Longrightarrow\penalty\ \penalty\ \\
(\forall U:\mathcal{G}^{\prime}\mathrel{\mathfrak{to}}2)(\forall v)\Big[\mathrm{Play}(\mathcal{G}^{\prime},v,U)\penalty\ \penalty\ \Longrightarrow\penalty\ \penalty\ \big(\mathrm{Par}(\mathcal{G}^{\prime},C,n,U)\penalty\ \penalty\ \Leftrightarrow\penalty\ \penalty\ \mathrm{Par}(\mathcal{G},C,n,U)\big)\Big]

Remark 45
.

When considering parity automata in §
6
,
it will actually be convenient to define acceptance via
the formula
\mathrm{Par}

for games of the form
\mathcal{G}(\trianglelefteq)
in the sense of Remark
32
.
It follows from Remarks
32
and
44

that
\mathsf{FSO}
proves

\mathrm{Game}(\mathcal{G})\penalty\ \penalty\ \Longrightarrow\penalty\ \penalty\ \mathrm{Col}(\mathcal{G}(\trianglelefteq),C,n)\penalty\ \penalty\ \Longrightarrow\penalty\ \penalty\ \\
(\forall U:\mathcal{G}\mathrel{\mathfrak{to}}2)(\forall v)\Big[\mathrm{Play}(\mathcal{G},v,U)\penalty\ \penalty\ \Longrightarrow\penalty\ \penalty\ \Big(\mathrm{Par}(\mathcal{G},C,n,U)\penalty\ \penalty\ \Leftrightarrow\penalty\ \penalty\ \mathrm{Par}(\mathcal{G}(\trianglelefteq),C,n,U)\Big)\Big]

We use the following more succinct notation for winning in the case parity games.

{nota}

[Winning in Parity Games]
Let
\mathcal{G}=(\mathsf{P},\mathsf{O},E_{\mathsf{P}},E_{\mathsf{O}})

where
\mathsf{P},\mathsf{O}
are
\mathrm{HF}
-variables and
E_{\mathsf{P}},E_{\mathsf{O}}
are Function variables.
Let
C
be a Function variable and
n
be an
\mathrm{HF}
-variable.
We write the following,
where
\mathsf{J}
is a player (either
\mathsf{P}
or
\mathsf{O}
).

\begin{array}[]{r !{\quad\deq\quad} l}\mathrm{WonGame}_{\mathsf{J}}(\mathcal{G},v,C,n)\lx@intercol\quad\mathrel{:=}&\mathrm{WonGame}_{\mathsf{J}}(\mathcal{G},v,\mathrm{Par}(\mathcal{G},C,n,-))\\
\mathrm{WinStrat}_{\mathsf{J}}(\mathcal{G},\sigma,v,C,n)\lx@intercol\quad\mathrel{:=}&\mathrm{WinStrat}_{\mathsf{J}}(\mathcal{G},\sigma,v,\mathrm{Par}(\mathcal{G},C,n,-))\end{array}

5.6.
The Axiom of Positional Determinacy of Parity Games.

We now formulate the axiom scheme
(\mathit{PosDet})
,
which states the (positional) determinacy of
parity games.
Intuitively
(\mathit{PosDet})
should consist of all formulae
of the form

\mathrm{Game}(\mathcal{G})\penalty\ \penalty\ \Rightarrow\penalty\ \penalty\ \mathrm{Col}(\mathcal{G},C,n)\penalty\ \penalty\ \Rightarrow\penalty\ \penalty\ \\
(\forall v\in\mathcal{G})\left[\begin{array}[]{c r l}&(\exists\sigma_{\mathsf{P}}:\mathcal{G}_{\mathsf{P}}\mathrel{\mathfrak{to}}\mathsf{O})&\left(\begin{array}[]{c l}&\mathrm{Strat}_{\mathsf{P}}(\mathcal{G},\sigma_{\mathsf{P}})\\
\land&\mathrm{WinStrat}_{\mathsf{P}}(\mathcal{G},\sigma_{\mathsf{P}},v,C,n)\end{array}\right)\\
\\
\lor&(\exists\sigma_{\mathsf{O}}:\mathcal{G}_{\mathsf{O}}\mathrel{\mathfrak{to}}\mathscr{D}\times\mathsf{P})&\left(\begin{array}[]{c l}&\mathrm{Strat}_{\mathsf{O}}(\mathcal{G},\sigma_{\mathsf{O}})\\
\land&\mathrm{WinStrat}_{\mathsf{O}}(\mathcal{G},\sigma_{\mathsf{O}},v,C,n)\end{array}\right)\end{array}\right]

But note that these formulae are open, and in particular

\mathcal{G}=(\mathsf{P},\mathsf{O},E_{\mathsf{P}},E_{\mathsf{O}})\quad\text{and}\quad C

contain free Function variables.
On the other hand, when formulating our completeness results in §
8
,
it will be interesting to have translations of instances of
(\mathit{PosDet})
in
\mathsf{MSO}
,
based on the map
\langle-\rangle:\mathsf{FSO}\to\mathsf{MSO}
of §
3.6
.
However, the translation
\langle-\rangle
only handles
\mathrm{HF}
-closed
formulae without free Function variables.
We therefore officially let
(\mathit{PosDet})
consist
of all formulae
\mathrm{PosDet}(\mathsf{P},\mathsf{O},n)
,
for
\mathsf{P}
,
\mathsf{O}
and
n
ranging over
\mathrm{HF}
-terms (see §
3.2
),
where

\mathrm{PosDet}(\mathsf{P},\mathsf{O},n)
is the formula

\mathrm{Labels}(\mathsf{P},\mathsf{O})\penalty\ \penalty\ \Rightarrow\penalty\ \penalty\ \mathrm{Ord}(n)\penalty\ \penalty\ \Rightarrow\penalty\ \penalty\ \\
\Big(\forall E_{\mathsf{P}}:\mathcal{G}_{\mathsf{P}}\mathrel{\mathfrak{to}}\mathcal{P}_{*}(\mathsf{O})\Big)\Big(\forall E_{\mathsf{O}}:\mathcal{G}_{\mathsf{O}}\mathrel{\mathfrak{to}}\mathcal{P}_{*}(\mathscr{D}\times\mathsf{P})\Big)\Big(\forall C:\mathcal{G}\mathrel{\mathfrak{to}}[0,n]\Big)\\
(\forall v\in\mathcal{G})\left[\begin{array}[]{c r l}&(\exists\sigma_{\mathsf{P}}:\mathcal{G}_{\mathsf{P}}\mathrel{\mathfrak{to}}\mathsf{O})&\left(\begin{array}[]{c l}&\mathrm{Strat}_{\mathsf{P}}(\mathcal{G},\sigma_{\mathsf{P}})\\
\land&\mathrm{WinStrat}_{\mathsf{P}}(\mathcal{G},\sigma_{\mathsf{P}},v,C,n)\end{array}\right)\\
\\
\lor&(\exists\sigma_{\mathsf{O}}:\mathcal{G}_{\mathsf{O}}\mathrel{\mathfrak{to}}\mathscr{D}\times\mathsf{P})&\left(\begin{array}[]{c l}&\mathrm{Strat}_{\mathsf{O}}(\mathcal{G},\sigma_{\mathsf{O}})\\
\land&\mathrm{WinStrat}_{\mathsf{O}}(\mathcal{G},\sigma_{\mathsf{O}},v,C,n)\end{array}\right)\end{array}\right]

It follows from the positional determinacy of parity games
[
EJ91
]

(see also
[
Tho97
,
Wal02
,
PP04
]
)
that all instances of
(\mathit{PosDet})
hold in the standard model

\mathfrak{T}
of
\mathsf{FSO}
.
We can thus extend Proposition
14

to the following.

Proposition 46
.

For each closed
\mathsf{FSO}
-formula
\varphi
,

\mathfrak{T}\models\varphi\qquad\text{whenever}\qquad\mathsf{FSO}+(\mathit{PosDet})\vdash\varphi

5.6.1.
The Axiom of Positional Determinacy in
\mathsf{MSO}
.

In order to obtain a complete axiomatization of
{\mathsf{MSO}_{\mathscr{D}}}
from
the completeness of
{\mathsf{FSO}_{\mathscr{D}}}+(\mathit{PosDet})
(see §
8
),
we extend the axioms of
{\mathsf{MSO}_{\mathscr{D}}}
with sufficiently many
translated instantiations
\langle\mathrm{PosDet}(\mathsf{P},\mathsf{O},n)\rangle

for
\mathsf{P}
,
\mathsf{O}
and
n

closed

\mathrm{HF}
-terms.
However, in general these terms may contain arbitrary
\mathrm{HF}
-Functions
symbols, which make the translation

\langle\mathrm{PosDet}(\mathsf{P},\mathsf{O},n)\rangle

in general uncomputable from
\mathsf{P}
,
\mathsf{O}
and
n

(see Remark
16
and §
3.4.4
).
However, for each closed
\mathrm{HF}
-
terms

\mathsf{P}
,
\mathsf{O}

and
n
, there are constant symbols for
\mathrm{HF}
-
sets

\dot{\mathsf{\mathsf{P}}}
,
\dot{\mathsf{\mathsf{O}}}
and
\dot{\mathsf{n}}
such that the formulae

\langle\mathrm{PosDet}(\mathsf{P},\mathsf{O},n)\rangle

and

\langle\mathrm{PosDet}(\dot{\mathsf{\mathsf{P}}},\dot{\mathsf{\mathsf{O}}},\dot{\mathsf{n}})\rangle

are syntactically identical.
We therefore officially take the following version of
(\mathit{PosDet})

in
{\mathsf{MSO}_{\mathscr{D}}}
.

{defi}

[The Axiom of Positional Determinacy in
\mathsf{MSO}
]

We let
\langle\mathit{PosDet}\rangle
consist of all formulae of the form

\langle\mathrm{PosDet}(\dot{\mathsf{\mathsf{P}}},\dot{\mathsf{\mathsf{O}}},\dot{\mathsf{n}})\rangle
,
for
\dot{\mathsf{\mathsf{P}}}
,
\dot{\mathsf{\mathsf{O}}}
and
\dot{\mathsf{n}}

ranging over constant symbols for
\mathrm{HF}
-sets.

6.
Alternating Tree Automata

We detail in this Section a representation of alternating tree automata
in
\mathsf{FSO}
.
We closely follow the presentation of
[
Wal02
]
.
Our main motivation to consider alternating automata is that when formulating
acceptance with (parity) games (of the kind of §
5
),
complementation follows from (positional) determinacy
(
i.e.
in our setting from the Axiom
(\mathit{PosDet})
).
Let us recall the main ideas underlying alternating automata.
The original formulation, as in e.g.
[
MS87
,
MS95
]
,
is for an automaton
\mathcal{A}
with state set
Q

to have transitions with values in the free distributive
lattice over
\mathscr{D}\times Q

(in other words, transitions have positive Boolean formulae over
\mathscr{D}\times Q

as values).
Actually, following
[
Wal02
]
we can
simply assume that transitions are of the form

\partial\penalty\ \penalty\ :\penalty\ \penalty\ Q\times\Sigma\penalty\ \penalty\ \longrightarrow\penalty\ \penalty\ \mathcal{P}_{*}(\mathcal{P}_{*}(\mathscr{D}\times Q))

and we read
\partial(q,\mathtt{a})

as the disjunctive normal form

\mathop{\bigvee}\limits_{\gamma\in\partial(q,\mathtt{a})}\mathop{\bigwedge}\limits_{(d,q^{\prime})\in\gamma}(d,q^{\prime})

This results in acceptance games
where intuitively
\mathsf{P}
plays from disjunctions while
\mathsf{O}

plays from conjunctions.
In the following we often call the
\gamma\in\partial(q,\mathtt{a})

conjunctions
.

We begin by giving basic definitions in
6.1
.
Because our setting is restricted to only describe

positional
strategies, and because parity
games are positionally determined, we give a special emphasis
to
parity
automata, whose acceptance conditions
are parity conditions generated from a coloring of their states.
We then present a series of operations on automata,
on which we rely in §
8.3
for the interpretation
of
\mathsf{MSO}
formulae as automata.
We recapitulate them in Table
1
.
First, §
6.2
and §
6.3
present
two simple constructions
implementing respectively a substitution and a disjunction
operation.
We discuss in §
6.4
and §
6.5
the important
special case of
non-deterministic
automata.
Non-deterministic automata are important because they allow us,
via the usual
projection
operation (§
6.5
),
to interpret the existential quantifier of
\mathsf{MSO}
(see §
8.3
).
To this end, an important result of the theory of automata on infinite trees
is the
Simulation Theorem

[
EJ91
,
MS95
]
,
which states that each alternating automaton is equivalent to a
non-deterministic one.
The formalization of this result in
\mathsf{FSO}
is deferred to §
9
.
This is the only part of this paper where we shall (momentarily)
use automata with acceptance conditions which are not parity conditions.
This result moreover relies on the complete axiomatization of

\mathsf{MSO}
on
\omega
-words for paths of
\mathsf{FSO}

(to be discussed in §
7
).
Finally, in §
6.6
we discuss complementation in the setting of
\mathsf{FSO}
,
and show that alternating automata can be complemented in
\mathsf{FSO}

when we assume the Axiom
(\mathit{PosDet})
of Positional Determinacy of Parity Games.

\begin{array}[]{l l !{\quad} l !{\quad} l l}\hline\cr\hline\cr\textbf{Name}&\textbf{Notation}&\textbf{Requirements}&\lx@intercol\textbf{Location in text}\hfil\lx@intercol\\
\hline\cr\text{Substitution}&\mathcal{A}[f]:\Gamma&\mathcal{A}:\Sigma\quad\text{and}\quad f:\Gamma\mathrel{\mathfrak{to}}\Sigma&\lx@sectionsign\ref{sec:aut:subst}&\text{(Lem.\penalty\ \ref{lem:aut:subst})}\\
\text{Disjunction}&(\mathcal{A}_{0}\oplus\mathcal{A}_{1}):\Sigma&\mathcal{A}_{i}:\Sigma\quad(i=0,1)&\lx@sectionsign\ref{sec:aut:disj}&\text{(Lem.\penalty\ \ref{lem:aut:disj})}\\
\text{Complementation}&{\mathord{\sim}}\mathcal{A}:\Sigma&\mathcal{A}:\Sigma\quad\text{parity}&\lx@sectionsign\ref{sec:neg}&\text{(Thm.\penalty\ \ref{thm:neg})}\\
&&\text{($\mathsf{FSO}$ + Axiom $(\mathit{PosDet})$)}&&\\
\text{Projection}&(\exists_{\Gamma}\mathcal{A}):\Sigma&\mathcal{A}:\Sigma\times\Gamma\quad\text{non-deterministic}&\lx@sectionsign\ref{sec:nd:proj}&\text{(Prop.\penalty\ \ref{prop:aut:nd:proj})}\\
\text{Simulation}&\mathrm{ND}(\mathcal{A}):\Sigma&\mathcal{A}:\Sigma\quad\text{$\mathrm{HF}$-closed}&\lx@sectionsign\ref{sec:sim}&\text{(Thm.\penalty\ \ref{thm:sim})}\\
\hline\cr\hline\cr\end{array}

Table 1.
Operations on Automata.

6.1.
Alternating Tree Automata in
{\mathsf{FSO}_{\mathscr{D}}}

We present here a representation of alternating tree automata
in
\mathsf{FSO}
.

{defi}

[Alternating Tree Automata]

Given an
\mathrm{HF}
-Set
\Sigma
,
an
Alternating Tree Automaton

(or simply
Automaton
)
\mathcal{A}
on
\Sigma

(notation
\mathcal{A}:\Sigma
)
is given by
\mathrm{HF}
-terms
Q_{\mathcal{A}}
,
{q}^{\iota}_{\mathcal{A}}

and
\partial_{\mathcal{A}}

together with
an
\mathsf{FSO}
-formula
\Omega_{\mathcal{A}}(U)

of a Function variable
U
,
which are required to satisfy the following formula:

\mathrm{Aut}(\Sigma,Q_{\mathcal{A}},{q}^{\iota}_{\mathcal{A}},\partial_{\mathcal{A}})\quad\mathrel{:=}\quad(\exists\mathtt{a}\mathrel{\dot{\in}}\Sigma)\penalty\ \penalty\ \land\penalty\ \penalty\ {q}^{\iota}_{\mathcal{A}}\mathrel{\dot{\in}}Q_{\mathcal{A}}\penalty\ \penalty\ \land\penalty\ \penalty\ \partial_{\mathcal{A}}:Q_{\mathcal{A}}\times\Sigma\mathrel{\mathfrak{to}}\mathcal{P}_{*}(\mathcal{P}_{*}(\mathscr{D}\times Q_{\mathcal{A}}))

where
\mathcal{P}_{*}(-)
is the
\mathrm{HF}
-Function of §
3.4.4
.
4
.
We write

\mathcal{A}:\Sigma\quad=\quad(Q_{\mathcal{A}},\,{q}^{\iota}_{\mathcal{A}},\,\partial_{\mathcal{A}},\,\Omega_{\mathcal{A}})

and adopt the following terminology:

\Sigma
is the
input alphabet

of
\mathcal{A}
,
Q_{\mathcal{A}}
is its set of
states

(with
{q}^{\iota}_{\mathcal{A}}

initial
),

\partial_{\mathcal{A}}
is the
transition function
of
\mathcal{A}

and
\Omega_{\mathcal{A}}
is its
acceptance condition
.

We often write
\mathrm{Aut}(\mathcal{A}:\Sigma)
or even
\mathrm{Aut}(\mathcal{A})
for

\mathrm{Aut}(\Sigma,Q_{\mathcal{A}},{q}^{\iota}_{\mathcal{A}},\partial_{\mathcal{A}})
.

An automaton
\mathcal{A}:\Sigma
is intended to run over
\Sigma
-labeled

\mathscr{D}
-ary trees, represented as Functions
F:\Sigma

(equivalently
F:\mathscr{D}^{*}\mathrel{\mathfrak{to}}\Sigma
, following §
3.7
).
As usual, acceptance is modeled using games,
which we formalize in the setting of §
5
.

{defi}

[Acceptance Games]

Given an automaton
\mathcal{A}:\Sigma
and a Function
F:\Sigma

we define the
acceptance game

\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{A},F)
as follows:

\mathsf{P}_{\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{A},F)}\quad\mathrel{:=}\quad Q_{\mathcal{A}}\qquad\qquad\mathsf{O}_{\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{A},F)}\quad\mathrel{:=}\quad Q_{\mathcal{A}}\times\mathcal{P}_{*}(\mathscr{D}\times Q_{\mathcal{A}})

and
E(\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{A},F))_{\mathsf{P}}
,
E(\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{A},F))_{\mathsf{O}}

are defined by

\mathrm{HF}
-Bounded Choice for Product Types (Theorem
22
)
and Comprehension for
\mathrm{HF}
-Sets (Remark
3.7.3
) as

\begin{array}[]{l !{\qquad} r !{\quad\text{iff}\quad} l}&(q^{\prime},\gamma)\in E(\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{A},F))_{\mathsf{P}}(x,q)&q^{\prime}\mathrel{\doteq}q\penalty\ \land\penalty\ \gamma\in\partial_{\mathcal{A}}(q,F(x))\\
\text{and}&(d,q^{\prime})\in E(\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{A},F))_{\mathsf{O}}(x,(q,\gamma))&(d,q^{\prime})\in\gamma\end{array}

Remark 47
.

Note that
\mathrm{Aut}(\mathcal{A})
implies
\mathrm{Game}(\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{A},F))
for

F:\Sigma
.
The edge relations of
\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{A},F)

(in the sense of Definition
5.1
)
are given by

\begin{array}[]{r c l !{\quad\text{iff}\quad} l}(x,q)&\mathchoice{\mathrel{\overset{}{\underset{\mathsf{P}}{\longrightarrow}}}}{\mathrel{\longrightarrow_{\mathsf{P}}}}{\longrightarrow_{\mathsf{P}}}{\longrightarrow_{\mathsf{P}}}&(x,(q,\gamma))&\gamma\in\partial_{\mathcal{A}}(q,F(x))\\
(x,(q,\gamma))&\mathchoice{\mathrel{\overset{}{\underset{\mathsf{O}}{\longrightarrow}}}}{\mathrel{\longrightarrow_{\mathsf{O}}}}{\longrightarrow_{\mathsf{O}}}{\longrightarrow_{\mathsf{O}}}&(\mathsf{S}_{d}(x),q^{\prime})&(d,q^{\prime})\in\gamma\end{array}

Note also that an
\mathsf{O}
-position
(x,(q,\gamma))

is equipped with the information

(x,q)\in\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{A},F)_{\mathsf{P}}
.
It follows that
an
\mathsf{O}
-position has at most one predecessor.
This is useful when complementing automata (§
6.6
).

{conv}

It the rest of this paper, unless explicitly stated otherwise,
when speaking of an infinite play in an acceptance game
\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{A},F)

(including infinite plays in strategies in such games),
we always mean an infinite play from position
(\dot{\varepsilon},{q}^{\iota}_{\mathcal{A}})
.

Given
\mathcal{A}:\Sigma
and
F:\Sigma
,
the acceptance condition
\Omega_{\mathcal{A}}(-)
of
\mathcal{A}

induces a winning condition in the sense of §
5.4

in the game
\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{A},F)
.
This gives the following notions of tree
acceptance and language generated by an automaton.

{defi}

[Language of an Automaton]

Given an automaton
\mathcal{A}:\Sigma
, a winning condition
\Omega_{\mathcal{A}}

(in the sense of Definition
5.4
) and
F:\Sigma
,
we say that
\mathcal{A}
accepts
F

when the following formula
F\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A})

holds.

F\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A})\quad\mathrel{:=}\quad(\exists\sigma_{\mathsf{P}}:\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{A},F)_{\mathsf{P}}\mathrel{\mathfrak{to}}\mathsf{O})\left(\begin{array}[]{c l}&\mathrm{Strat}_{\mathsf{P}}(\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{A},F),\sigma_{\mathsf{P}})\\
\land&\mathrm{WinStrat}_{\mathsf{P}}(\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{A},F),\sigma_{\mathsf{P}},v,\Omega_{\mathcal{A}})\end{array}\right)

Recall that the formulae
\mathrm{Strat}
and
\mathrm{WinStrat}
are defined
in Def.
5.3
(§
5.3
)
and Def.
5.4
(§
5.4
) respectively.
In words, the formula
F\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A})

of Definition
6.1

states that

\mathsf{P}
has a winning strategy from position

(\dot{\varepsilon},{q}^{\iota}_{\mathcal{A}})
in the game
\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{A},F)
.

Except for the Simulation Theorem in §
9
,
we shall only consider automata whose acceptance conditions
are given by
parity conditions

in the sense of §
5.5
.
Recall from Definition
5.5

that a parity condition on a game
\mathcal{G}
is given by the formula

\mathrm{Par}(\mathcal{G},C,n,U)

which depends on
\mathcal{G}
.
However, it is desirable that automata come, as in Definition
6.1
,
with acceptance conditions which are independent from any particular
acceptance game.
Note that for a given automaton
\mathcal{A}
, all acceptance games
\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{A},F)

have the same sets of
\mathsf{P}
and
\mathsf{O}
labels and positions;
the input trees
F
can only induce different edge relations.
Recall now the games
\mathcal{G}(\trianglelefteq)
from Remark
32
.
The game
\mathcal{G}(\trianglelefteq)
has the same labels and positions as
\mathcal{G}
,
but its edge relation is exactly the partial order
\trianglelefteq
discussed
in §
4
.
It follows that for a fixed automaton
\mathcal{A}:\Sigma
,
all acceptance games
\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{A},F)
for
F:\Sigma

induce the same
\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{A},F)(\trianglelefteq)
, that we shall write

\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{A})(\trianglelefteq)

(12)

{defi}

[Parity Automata]

Let

\mathcal{A}:\Sigma\quad=\quad(Q_{\mathcal{A}}\,,\,{q}^{\iota}_{\mathcal{A}}\,,\,\partial_{\mathcal{A}}\,,\,\Omega_{\mathcal{A}})

We say that
\mathcal{A}
is a
parity
automaton if

\mathcal{A}
comes equipped with
\mathrm{HF}
-terms
n_{\mathcal{A}}
and
C_{\mathcal{A}}

such that the two following conditions are satisfied.

(1)

The following formula holds

\mathrm{PAut}(\mathcal{A},C_{\mathcal{A}},n_{\mathcal{A}})\quad\mathrel{:=}\quad\mathrm{Aut}(\mathcal{A})\penalty\ \penalty\ \land\penalty\ \penalty\ \mathrm{Ord}(n_{\mathcal{A}})\penalty\ \penalty\ \land\penalty\ \penalty\ C_{\mathcal{A}}:Q_{\mathcal{A}}\mathrel{\mathfrak{to}}[0,n_{\mathcal{A}}]

(2)

The formula
\Omega_{\mathcal{A}}(U)

is
\mathrm{Par}(\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{A})(\trianglelefteq),\hat{C}_{\mathcal{A}},n_{\mathcal{A}})
,
where

\hat{C}_{\mathcal{A}}(x,k)\quad\mathrel{:=}\quad\left\{\begin{array}[]{l !{\quad} l !{\qquad} l}C_{\mathcal{A}}(q)&\text{if $k=q\in Q_{\mathcal{A}}$}&\text{($\mathsf{P}$-position)}\\
C_{\mathcal{A}}(q)&\text{if $k=(q,\gamma)\in Q_{\mathcal{A}}\times\mathcal{P}_{*}(\mathscr{D}^{*}\times Q_{\mathcal{A}})$}&\text{($\mathsf{O}$-position)}\end{array}\right.

We write

\mathcal{A}\quad=\quad(Q_{\mathcal{A}}\,,\,{q}^{\iota}_{\mathcal{A}}\,,\,\partial_{\mathcal{A}}\,,\,C_{\mathcal{A}}\,,\,n_{\mathcal{A}})

for a parity automaton
\mathcal{A}
with
C_{\mathcal{A}}
and
n_{\mathcal{A}}
as above.
Furthermore, we write

\mathrm{Par}(\mathcal{A},\hat{C}_{\mathcal{A}},n_{\mathcal{A}},U)

or even

\mathrm{Par}(\mathcal{A},U)

for the formula

\mathrm{Par}(\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{A})(\trianglelefteq),\hat{C}_{\mathcal{A}},n_{\mathcal{A}},U)
.

In Definition
6.1
,
the purpose of the coloring
\hat{C}_{\mathcal{A}}

is to equip the game
\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{A})(\trianglelefteq)
with a coloring in the sense of
Def.
5.5
(§
5.5
), namely a coloring of the
positions of the game, while the coloring
C_{\mathcal{A}}

only colors the states of
\mathcal{A}
.

Note that it follows
from Remarks
32
and
45

that
\mathsf{FSO}
proves

\mathrm{PAut}(\mathcal{A}:\Sigma)\quad\Longrightarrow\quad(\forall F:\Sigma)\bigg(\mathrm{Sub}\Big(\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{A},F),\,\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{A})(\trianglelefteq)\Big)\bigg)

where the formula
\mathrm{Sub}(\mathcal{G},\mathcal{G}^{\prime})
(stating that
\mathcal{G}
is a subgame of
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
)
is defined in Def.
5.1
(§
5.1
),
and

\mathrm{PAut}(\mathcal{A}:\Sigma)\quad\Longrightarrow\quad(\forall F:\Sigma)(\forall U:\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{A},F)\mathrel{\mathfrak{to}}2)\\
\bigg(\mathrm{Play}\big(\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{A},F),(\dot{\varepsilon},{q}^{\iota}_{\mathcal{A}}),U\big)\penalty\ \penalty\ \Rightarrow\penalty\ \penalty\ \Big[\mathrm{Par}\big(\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{A},F),\hat{C}_{\mathcal{A}},n_{\mathcal{A}},U\big)\penalty\ \penalty\ \Leftrightarrow\penalty\ \penalty\ \mathrm{Par}\big(\mathcal{A},\hat{C}_{\mathcal{A}},n_{\mathcal{A}},U\big)\Big]\bigg)

The following simple fact will be useful when proving the Simulation Theorem
in §
9
.

Remark 48
.

Given two plays
U
and
V
in
\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{A})(\trianglelefteq)
, if
U_{\mathsf{P}}=V_{\mathsf{P}}

then

\mathrm{Par}\big(\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{A})(\trianglelefteq),U\big)\penalty\ \penalty\ \Leftrightarrow\penalty\ \penalty\ \mathrm{Par}\big(\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{A})(\trianglelefteq),V\big)

Other than the Simulation Theorem
in §
9
,
all constructions we need on automata can be performed on automata

\mathcal{A}:\Sigma

where
\Sigma
,
Q_{\mathcal{A}}
,
{q}^{\iota}_{\mathcal{A}}
,
\partial_{\mathcal{A}}
,
C_{\mathcal{A}}

and
n_{\mathcal{A}}
are given by
arbitrary

\mathrm{HF}
-terms.
However, our completeness result (§
8
)
ultimately relies, via Proposition
78
,
on the completeness of
\mathsf{FSO}[\mathrel{\dot{<}}]^{\omega}
over
\omega
-words (§
7
)
and requires automata to be given by
closed

\mathrm{HF}
-terms.
In addition, our proof of the
Simulation Theorem uses McNaughton’s Theorem
[
McN66
]
,
and imports it into
\mathsf{FSO}
by Proposition
78
,
which also requires automata to be closed objects.
This leads to the following.

{defi}

A parity automaton
\mathcal{A}:\Sigma
is
\mathrm{HF}
-closed

if
\Sigma
,
Q_{\mathcal{A}}
,
{q}^{\iota}_{\mathcal{A}}
,
\partial_{\mathcal{A}}
,

C_{\mathcal{A}}
and
n_{\mathcal{A}}
are closed
\mathrm{HF}
-terms.

Remark 49
.

For each of our constructions on automata (see Table
1
),
the alphabets, states and colorings of new automata
will be obtained by composing simple Functions on
\mathrm{HF}
-Sets
from §
3.4.4

and Convention
5.5
.
In particular this means that the obtained automata
have
\mathrm{HF}
-closed alphabets, states and coloring
provided we started from
\mathrm{HF}
-closed ones.

On the other hand, transition functions may be more complex
(see §
6.6
or §
9
),
and we often present them in a way suggesting
the use of the Axiom of
\mathrm{HF}
-Bounded Choice for
\mathrm{HF}
-Sets
(§
3.4.5
).
This is unproblematic when
\mathrm{HF}
-closedness is not at issue.
To preserve
\mathrm{HF}
-closedness,
starting from
\mathrm{HF}
-closed automata, the transition functions
of the newly built automata must always be read as being
constructed from
concrete

\mathrm{HF}
-sets.

{conv}

In the rest of this paper, whenever we speak
of a (parity) automaton
\mathcal{A}
in formal statements, we always mean
that the formula
\mathrm{Aut}(\mathcal{A})
(resp.
\mathrm{PAut}(\mathcal{A})
) holds.
(By contrast,
\mathrm{HF}
-closedness is an external notion.)

6.2.
Substitution

Let
\mathcal{A}:\Sigma
be an automaton and let
\Gamma
and

f:\Gamma\mathrel{\mathfrak{to}}\Sigma
be
\mathrm{HF}
-sets.
The automaton
\mathcal{A}[f]:\Gamma
is defined to have
the same states and acceptance condition as
\mathcal{A}:\Sigma
,
and its transitions are given
by

(q,\mathtt{b})\quad\longmapsto\quad\partial_{\mathcal{A}}(q,f(\mathtt{b}))

Note that
\mathrm{Aut}(\mathcal{A})\land(\exists\mathtt{b}\mathrel{\dot{\in}}\Gamma)
implies
\mathrm{Aut}(\mathcal{A}[f])
.
Also,
\mathcal{A}[f]
is a parity automaton
whenever
\mathcal{A}
is.
Furthermore, it follows from Remark
49
that

\mathcal{A}[f]:\Gamma
is
\mathrm{HF}
-closed when
\mathcal{A}:\Gamma

is
\mathrm{HF}
-closed
and in addition
\Gamma
and
f
are closed
\mathrm{HF}
-terms.
A typical use of substitution,
on which we rely when translating formulae to automata in §
8.3
,
is to enlarge the input alphabet of an automaton.
For instance, given
\mathrm{HF}
-closed
\Sigma_{1},\dots,\Sigma_{n}

and an
\mathrm{HF}
-closed
\mathcal{A}:\Sigma_{i}
,
we obtain an
\mathrm{HF}
-closed

\mathcal{A}[\pi^{n}_{i}]:\Sigma_{1}\times\dots\times\Sigma_{n}

where

\pi^{n}_{i}\penalty\ \penalty\ :\penalty\ \penalty\ \Sigma_{1}\times\dots\times\Sigma_{n}\penalty\ \penalty\ \longrightarrow\penalty\ \penalty\ \Sigma_{i}

is a projection
\mathrm{HF}
-Function of §
3.4.4
.
6
.

Lemma 50
.

Given
\Gamma
,
f
and
\mathcal{A}
as above,

{\mathsf{FSO}_{\mathscr{D}}}
proves the following.

(\forall H:\Gamma)\Bigg[H\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A}[f])\penalty\ \penalty\ \Leftrightarrow\penalty\ \penalty\ (\forall F:\Sigma)\bigg((\forall x)\Big[F(x)=f(H(x))\Big]\penalty\ \Rightarrow\penalty\ F\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A})\bigg)\Bigg]

Note that by
\mathrm{HF}
-Bounded Choice,
{\mathsf{FSO}_{\mathscr{D}}}
proves that

(\forall H:\Gamma)(\exists F:\Sigma)(\forall x)\Big(F(x)=f(H(x))\Big)

so the above Lemma could have equivalently been stated with an existentially bound
F
.

6.3.
Disjunction

We use here the
\mathrm{HF}
-Functions from §
3.4.4
.
8

and Convention
5.5
.
4
.
Given parity automata
\mathcal{A}_{0},\mathcal{A}_{1}:\Sigma
,
the parity automaton
\mathcal{A}_{0}\oplus\mathcal{A}_{1}:\Sigma

has state set

Q_{\mathcal{A}_{0}}+Q_{\mathcal{A}_{1}}+\{{q}^{\iota}\}

with
{q}^{\iota}
initial,
transitions given
by

\begin{array}[]{r !{\quad}c!{\quad} l !{\qquad} l}({q}^{\iota},\mathtt{a})&\longmapsto&\partial_{\mathcal{A}_{0}}({q}^{\iota}_{\mathcal{A}_{0}},\mathtt{a})+\partial_{\mathcal{A}_{1}}({q}^{\iota}_{\mathcal{A}_{1}},\mathtt{a})&\text{(modulo $Q_{\mathcal{A}_{i}}\hookrightarrow Q_{\mathcal{A}_{0}\oplus\mathcal{A}_{1}}$)}\\
(q_{\mathcal{A}_{i}},\mathtt{a})&\longmapsto&\partial_{\mathcal{A}_{i}}(q_{\mathcal{A}_{i}},\mathtt{a})&\text{(for $q_{\mathcal{A}_{i}}\in Q_{\mathcal{A}_{i}}$)}\end{array}

and coloring
C:Q_{\mathcal{A}_{0}\oplus\mathcal{A}_{1}}\mathrel{\mathfrak{to}}[0,n]

(where
n=\mathsf{max}(n_{\mathcal{A}_{0}},n_{\mathcal{A}_{1}})
)
given by

\begin{array}[]{r !{\quad\deq\quad} l !{\qquad} l}C({q}^{\iota})\lx@intercol\quad\mathrel{:=}&n\\
C(q_{\mathcal{A}_{i}})\lx@intercol\quad\mathrel{:=}&C_{\mathcal{A}_{i}}(q_{\mathcal{A}_{i}})&\text{(for $q_{\mathcal{A}_{i}}\in Q_{\mathcal{A}_{i}}$)}\end{array}

We have

\mathrm{Aut}(\mathcal{A}_{0})\quad\Longrightarrow\quad\mathrm{Aut}(\mathcal{A}_{1})\quad\Longrightarrow\quad\mathrm{Aut}(\mathcal{A}_{0}\oplus\mathcal{A}_{1})

Moreover, if follows from Remark
49
that

\mathcal{A}_{0}\oplus\mathcal{A}_{1}:\Sigma
is

\mathrm{HF}
-closed
whenever
\mathcal{A}_{0}:\Sigma
and
\mathcal{A}_{1}:\Sigma
are.

Remark 51
.

Even in our positional setting,
strictly speaking the automaton
\mathcal{A}_{0}\oplus\mathcal{A}_{1}
does not
require
\mathcal{A}_{0}
and
\mathcal{A}_{1}
to be
parity
automata
(see Table
1
).
However, the acceptance condition of
\mathcal{A}_{0}\oplus\mathcal{A}_{1}

is actually simpler to define when both
\mathcal{A}_{0}
and
\mathcal{A}_{1}

are parity automata.
Since we shall only need
\mathcal{A}_{0}\oplus\mathcal{A}_{1}

for parity automata,
we only formally define disjunction
in this setting.

Lemma 52
.

{\mathsf{FSO}_{\mathscr{D}}}
proves the following.

(F:\Sigma)\Big(F\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A}_{0}\oplus\mathcal{A}_{1})\penalty\ \penalty\ \Longleftrightarrow\penalty\ \penalty\ \Big(F\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A}_{0})\penalty\ \lor\penalty\ F\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A}_{1})\Big)\Big)

Proof 6.1
.

Assume first that
F\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A}_{0}\oplus\mathcal{A}_{1})

for
F:\Sigma
,
and consider a winning
\mathsf{P}
-strategy

\sigma
in the acceptance game
\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{A}_{0}\oplus\mathcal{A}_{1},F)
.
We first look at the move of
\sigma
on the initial
position
(\dot{\varepsilon},{q}^{\iota})
.
By definition of
\mathcal{A}_{0}\oplus\mathcal{A}_{1}
we have

\sigma(\dot{\varepsilon},{q}^{\iota})=(q,\gamma)\qquad\text{with}\qquad\gamma\in\Big(\partial_{\mathcal{A}_{0}}\big({q}^{\iota}_{\mathcal{A}_{0}},F(\dot{\varepsilon})\big)+\partial_{\mathcal{A}_{1}}\big({q}^{\iota}_{\mathcal{A}_{1}},F(\dot{\varepsilon})\big)\Big)

Assume
\gamma\in\partial_{\mathcal{A}_{i}}({q}^{\iota}_{\mathcal{A}_{i}},F(\dot{\varepsilon}))
.
Then
\sigma
induces a
\mathsf{P}
-strategy
\sigma_{i}
in

\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{A}_{i},F)
.
The strategy
\sigma_{i}
is defined using

\mathrm{HF}
-Bounded Choice for Product Types (Theorem
22
)
by putting

\sigma_{i}(x,q_{\mathcal{A}_{i}})\quad=\quad\left\{\begin{array}[]{l l}\sigma(\dot{\varepsilon},{q}^{\iota})&\text{if $(x,q_{\mathcal{A}_{i}})=(\dot{\varepsilon},{q}^{\iota})$}\\
\sigma(x,q_{\mathcal{A}_{i}})&\text{otherwise}\end{array}\right.

It remains to show that
\sigma_{i}
is winning, that is

(\forall V:\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{A}_{i},F)\mathrel{\mathfrak{to}}2)\Big(\mathrm{Play}(\sigma_{i},\iota,V)\penalty\ \penalty\ \Rightarrow\penalty\ \penalty\ \mathrm{Par}(\mathcal{A}_{i},V)\Big)

for
\iota=(\dot{\varepsilon},{q}^{\iota}_{\mathcal{A}_{i}})
.
Consider an infinite play
V
of
\sigma_{i}
from
\iota
.
Then by Comprehension for Product Types (Theorem
23
),
define
U:\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{A}_{0}\oplus\mathcal{A}_{1},F)\mathrel{\mathfrak{to}}2

as the set of all
(x,\ell)
such that either

(x,\ell)=(\dot{\varepsilon},{q}^{\iota})
or
(x,\ell)\in V
.
It is clear that

\mathrm{Par}(\mathcal{A}_{i},V)\penalty\ \penalty\ \Leftrightarrow\penalty\ \penalty\ \mathrm{Par}(\mathcal{A}_{1}\oplus\mathcal{A}_{2},U)

The converse is proved similarly.

6.4.
Non-Deterministic Automata

We turn to the important class of alternating automata known as

non-deterministic
automata.
Non-deterministic automata are important because they allow us,
via the usual
projection
operation (§
6.5
),
to interpret the existential quantifier of
\mathsf{MSO}
(see §
8
).
An important result in the theory of automata on infinite
trees is the
Simulation Theorem

[
EJ91
,
MS95
]

(addressed in §
9
),
stating that each alternating automata can be simulated by a non-deterministic one.

Intuitively, an automaton
\mathcal{A}
is non-deterministic
if in acceptance games
\mathsf{O}
can only explicitly choose tree directions
but not states.

{defi}

[Non-Deterministic Automata]

An automaton
(\mathcal{A}:\Sigma)
in the sense of Definition
6.1
,
with

\partial_{\mathcal{A}}:Q_{\mathcal{A}}\times\Sigma\mathrel{\mathfrak{to}}\mathcal{P}_{*}(\mathcal{P}_{*}(\mathscr{D}\times Q_{\mathcal{A}}))

is
non-deterministic
if for every
q\in Q_{\mathcal{A}}
,
every
\mathtt{a}\in\Sigma
, every
\gamma\in\partial_{\mathcal{A}}(q,\mathtt{a})
, and every tree direction
d\in\mathscr{D}
, there is at most one

q^{\prime}\in Q_{\mathcal{A}}
such that
(d,q^{\prime})\in\gamma
.

The key property of non-deterministic automata
is that in each play of a
\mathsf{P}
-strategy
\sigma
in an acceptance game,
the sequence of states is uniquely determined from the tree positions.
We formally state this as follows.

Lemma 53
.

Consider a non-deterministic automaton
\mathcal{A}:\Sigma
,
and let
F:\Sigma
.
Furthermore let
\sigma
be a
\mathsf{P}
-strategy in
\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{A},F)
.
Then
{\mathsf{FSO}_{\mathscr{D}}}
proves that
for all
x\in\mathscr{D}^{*}

and all infinite plays
V
and
V^{\prime}
of
\sigma
, if

(\exists q\in Q_{\mathcal{A}})(x,q)\in V\penalty\ \penalty\ \land\penalty\ \penalty\ (\exists q^{\prime}\in Q_{\mathcal{A}})(x,q^{\prime})\in V^{\prime}

then
for all
y\mathrel{\dot{\leq}}x
, all
q\in Q_{\mathcal{A}}
,
and all
\gamma\in\mathcal{P}_{*}(\mathscr{D}\times Q_{\mathcal{A}})
,
we have

\Big[(y,q)\in V\penalty\ \penalty\ \Leftrightarrow\penalty\ \penalty\ (y,q)\in V^{\prime}\Big]\quad\land\quad\Big[(y,(q,\gamma))\in V\penalty\ \penalty\ \Leftrightarrow\penalty\ \penalty\ (y,(q,\gamma))\in V^{\prime}\Big]

Proof 6.2
.

Fix
\sigma
and
V,V^{\prime}
as in the statement of the Lemma
and let
x\in\mathscr{D}^{*}
.
First, note that for every
y\mathrel{\dot{\leq}}x

we have

Claim 54
.

(\exists q\in Q_{\mathcal{A}})\big((y,q)\in V\big)\penalty\ \penalty\ \land\penalty\ \penalty\ (\exists q\in Q_{\mathcal{A}})\big((y,q)\in V^{\prime}\big)

Proof 6.3

(Proof of Claim 54)
.

We use the Induction Axiom of
\mathsf{FSO}
(§
3.4.2
).
The property holds for
\dot{\varepsilon}\mathrel{\dot{\leq}}x
since

(\dot{\varepsilon},{q}^{\iota}_{\mathcal{A}})
belongs to both
V
and
V^{\prime}
.
Assume now the property for
y\mathrel{\dot{\leq}}x
, and consider some tree direction
d\in\mathscr{D}

such that
\mathsf{S}_{d}(y)\mathrel{\dot{\leq}}x
.
By assumption, we have some
q\in Q_{\mathcal{A}}
such that
(y,q)\in V
,
and by using
\mathrm{Game}(\sigma)
twice,
we get some
q^{\prime}\in Q_{\mathcal{A}}
and some
d^{\prime}\in\mathscr{D}

such that

\exists q^{\prime}\left((y,q)\mathchoice{\mathrel{\overset{}{\underset{\sigma}{\longrightarrow}}}}{\mathrel{\longrightarrow_{\sigma}}}{\longrightarrow_{\sigma}}{\longrightarrow_{\sigma}}\mathchoice{\mathrel{\overset{}{\underset{\sigma}{\longrightarrow}}}}{\mathrel{\longrightarrow_{\sigma}}}{\longrightarrow_{\sigma}}{\longrightarrow_{\sigma}}(\mathsf{S}_{d^{\prime}}(y),q^{\prime})\right)

But since
V
is a play of
\sigma
,
by Proposition
33

we must have
\mathsf{S}_{d^{\prime}}(y)\trianglelefteq x
, so that
d^{\prime}=d

and we are done.
The same reasoning gives the result for
V^{\prime}
.

Using the Induction Axiom of
\mathsf{FSO}
(§
3.4.2
), we now show that

(\forall y\mathrel{\dot{\leq}}x)(\forall q\in Q_{\mathcal{A}})\Big[(y,q)\in V\penalty\ \penalty\ \Leftrightarrow\penalty\ \penalty\ (y,q)\in V^{\prime}\Big]

First,
we have

(\dot{\varepsilon},q)\in V\quad\Leftrightarrow\quad(\dot{\varepsilon},q)\in V^{\prime}\quad\Leftrightarrow\quad q={q}^{\iota}_{\mathcal{A}}

Assume now the property for
y\mathrel{\dot{\leq}}x
and let us prove it for

\mathsf{S}_{d}(y)
with
\mathsf{S}_{d}(y)\mathrel{\dot{\leq}}x
.
It follows from the induction hypothesis and
Claim
54

that
we have
(y,q)\in V
and
(y,q)\in V^{\prime}

for some
q\in Q_{\mathcal{A}}
.
Again by Claim
54
,
let
q^{\prime},q^{\prime\prime}\in Q_{\mathcal{A}}
such that

(\mathsf{S}_{d}(y),q^{\prime})\in V
and
(\mathsf{S}_{d}(y),q^{\prime\prime})\in V^{\prime}
.
Now since
V
and
V^{\prime}
are plays of
\sigma
,
there are
\gamma
,
\gamma^{\prime}
such that

(y,(q,\gamma))\in V
and
(y,(q,\gamma^{\prime}))\in V^{\prime}
,
and
we necessarily have

(q,\gamma)\quad=\quad(q,\gamma^{\prime})\quad=\quad\sigma(x,q)

so that
\gamma=\gamma^{\prime}
.
Moreover, we have
(d,q^{\prime}),(d,q^{\prime\prime})\in\gamma
,
but this implies
q^{\prime}=q^{\prime\prime}
since
\mathcal{A}
is non-deterministic.

This concludes the proof of Lemma
53
.

Corollary 55
.

Given
\mathcal{A}
,
F
and
\sigma
as in Lemma
53
,

{\mathsf{FSO}_{\mathscr{D}}}
proves that for each
x\in\mathscr{D}^{*}
there is at most
one
q\in Q_{\mathcal{A}}

such that

(\exists U:\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{A},F)\mathrel{\mathfrak{to}}2)\Big(\mathrm{Play}(\sigma,\,(\dot{\varepsilon},{q}^{\iota}_{\mathcal{A}}),\,U)\penalty\ \penalty\ \land\penalty\ \penalty\ (x,q)\in U\Big)

We now state the
Simulation Theorem

[
EJ91
,
MS95
]
.
Its proof in
{\mathsf{FSO}_{\mathscr{D}}}
, requiring
\mathrm{HF}
-closedness of automata,
is deferred to §
9
.

Theorem 56

(Simulation)
.

For each
\mathrm{HF}
-closed parity automaton
\mathcal{A}:\Sigma

there is a non-deterministic
\mathrm{HF}
-closed parity automaton

\mathrm{ND}(\mathcal{A}):\Sigma

such that

\mathsf{FSO}\vdash\quad\mathcal{L}(\mathrm{ND}(\mathcal{A}))=\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A})

6.5.
Projection

We now discuss the usual operation of
projection
,
which allows us to interpret (existential) quantification in
\mathsf{MSO}

(see §
8.3
).
This operation is defined on arbitrary alternating automata,
but it only correctly computes the appropriate projection for non-deterministic ones.

Given an automaton
\mathcal{A}:\Sigma\times\Gamma

as in Definition
6.1
,
we define its
projection
on
\Sigma

to be the automaton
\exists_{\Gamma}\mathcal{A}:\Sigma
with

\exists_{\Gamma}\mathcal{A}\quad\mathrel{:=}\quad(Q_{\mathcal{A}},\,{q}^{\iota}_{\mathcal{A}},\,\partial_{\exists_{\Gamma}\mathcal{A}},\,C_{\mathcal{A}},\,n_{\mathcal{A}})

where

\partial_{\exists_{\Gamma}\mathcal{A}}\penalty\ \penalty\ :\penalty\ \penalty\ Q_{\mathcal{A}}\times\Sigma\penalty\ \penalty\ \longrightarrow\penalty\ \penalty\ \mathcal{P}_{*}(\mathcal{P}_{*}(\mathscr{D}\times Q_{\mathcal{A}}))

is given
by

\partial_{\exists_{\Gamma}\mathcal{A}}(q,\mathtt{a})\quad\mathrel{:=}\quad\bigcup_{\mathtt{b}\in\Gamma}\partial_{\mathcal{A}}(q,(\mathtt{a},\mathtt{b}))

Note that
\mathrm{Aut}(\mathcal{A}:\Sigma\times\Gamma)

implies
\mathrm{Aut}(\exists_{\Gamma}\mathcal{A})
.
Moreover,
\exists_{\Gamma}\mathcal{A}:\Sigma
is an (
\mathrm{HF}
-closed) parity
automaton whenever so is
\mathcal{A}:\Sigma\times\Gamma
.

We shall now prove that
\exists_{\Gamma}\mathcal{A}:\Sigma

indeed implements the projection of
\mathcal{A}:\Sigma\times\Gamma
.
This involves a notion of pairing for trees.
Given

F:\Sigma
and
G:\Gamma
,
we let

{\langle F,G\rangle}:\Gamma\times\Sigma

be given
(using the axiom of
\mathrm{HF}
-Bounded Choice for
\mathrm{HF}
-Functions (§
3.4.5
))
by

{\langle F,G\rangle}(x)\quad\mathrel{:=}\quad(F(x),G(x))

Proposition 57
.

Consider a
non-deterministic

\mathcal{A}:\Sigma\times\Gamma
and
let
\exists_{\Gamma}\mathcal{A}:\Sigma

be as defined above.
Then
{\mathsf{FSO}_{\mathscr{D}}}
proves the following.

(\forall F:\Sigma)\bigg[F\in\mathcal{L}(\exists_{\Gamma}\mathcal{A})\penalty\ \penalty\ \Leftrightarrow\penalty\ \penalty\ (\exists G:\Gamma)\Big({\langle F,G\rangle}\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A})\Big)\bigg]

Proof 6.4
.

Given
G:\Gamma

and a winning
\mathsf{P}
-strategy
\sigma
on
\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{A},{\langle F,G\rangle})
,
it is easy to see that
\sigma
is also a winning strategy on

\mathcal{G}(\exists_{\Gamma}\mathcal{A},F)
.

Conversely, assume that
\sigma
is a winning
\mathsf{P}
-strategy on

\mathcal{G}(\exists_{\Gamma}\mathcal{A},F)
.
We define a tree
G:\Gamma

by
\mathrm{HF}
-Bounded Choice for
\mathrm{HF}
-Functions (§
3.4.5
)
as follows:

•

For
x\in\mathscr{D}^{*}
, if there is some infinite play

U
of
\sigma
such that
(x,q)\in U
for some state
q\in Q_{\mathcal{A}}
,
then we let
G(x)
be some
\mathtt{b}\in\Gamma

such that
\sigma(x,q)\in\partial_{\mathcal{A}}(q,(F(x),\mathtt{b}))
.

•

Otherwise, we let
G(x)
be any element of
\Gamma
.

We now define a
\mathsf{P}
-strategy
\sigma_{G}
on
\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{A},{\langle F,G\rangle})

as follows,
again using

\mathrm{HF}
-Bounded Choice for
\mathrm{HF}
-Functions (§
3.4.5
).

•

If
(x,q)\in U
for some infinite play
U
of
\sigma
, then we let

\sigma_{G}(x,q)\mathrel{:=}\sigma(x,q)
.

•

Otherwise, we let
\sigma_{G}(x,q)=(q,\gamma)
, where

\gamma\in\partial_{\mathcal{A}}(q,{\langle F,G\rangle}(x))
.

We first check that
\sigma_{G}
is indeed a strategy on
\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{A},{\langle F,G\rangle})
,
namely that for all
(x,q)\in\mathscr{D}^{*}\times Q_{\mathcal{A}}
,
if
\sigma_{G}(x,q)=(q,\gamma)
then

\gamma\in\partial_{\mathcal{A}}(q,{\langle F,G\rangle}(x))
.
If
(x,q)
belongs to no infinite play of
\sigma
, then the result follows
by definition of
\sigma_{G}
.
Otherwise, by Corollary
55
,

q
is unique in
Q_{\mathcal{A}}
such that
(x,q)
belongs to an infinite play
of
\sigma
, and we are done since

\sigma_{G}(x,q)=\sigma(x,q)\in\partial_{\mathcal{A}}(q,{\langle F,G\rangle}(x))

In order to show that
\sigma_{G}
is winning, we show that any infinite play
of
\sigma_{G}
is also an infinite play of
\sigma
.
So let
U:\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{A},{\langle F,G\rangle})\mathrel{\mathfrak{to}}2

such that

\mathrm{Play}(\sigma_{G},\,(\dot{\varepsilon},{q}^{\iota}_{\mathcal{A}}),\,U)

We are done if we show that

(\forall(x,q)\in U)\left(\sigma(x,q)=\sigma_{G}(x,q)\right)

which follows from the fact that

Claim 58
.

(\forall(x,q)\in U)(\exists W:\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{A},{\langle F,G\rangle})\mathrel{\mathfrak{to}}2)\Big(\mathrm{Play}(\sigma,\,(\dot{\varepsilon},{q}^{\iota}_{\mathcal{A}}),\,W)\penalty\ \penalty\ \land\penalty\ \penalty\ (x,q)\in W\Big)

Proof 6.5

(Proof of Claim 58)
.

We apply the Induction Scheme of
{\mathsf{FSO}_{\mathscr{D}}}

(§
3.4.2
).
In the base case
x=\dot{\varepsilon}
,
and we conclude by Lemma
38
.

For the induction step consider the case of
\mathsf{S}_{d}(x)
,
assuming the property for
x
.
So let
q^{\prime}\in Q_{\mathcal{A}}
such that
(\mathsf{S}_{d}(x),q^{\prime})\in U
.
First, by applying
twice the Predecessor Lemma
37
for Infinite Plays,
we get some
q\in Q_{\mathcal{A}}
such that
(x,q)\in U
,
and by induction hypothesis, there is some infinite play
W
of
\sigma

such that
(x,q)\in W
.
But then, by definition of
\sigma_{G}
, we have
\sigma(x,q)=\sigma_{G}(x,q)
.
We thus have
(d,q^{\prime})\in\gamma
, where
(q,\gamma)=\sigma(x,q)
.
Using Lemma
38
, let now
W^{\prime}

be an infinite play of
\sigma
from position
(\mathsf{S}_{d}(x),q^{\prime})
.
By Comprehension for Product Types
(Theorem
23
),
we define an infinite play
W^{\prime\prime}
of
\sigma
from position
(\dot{\varepsilon},{q}^{\iota}_{\mathcal{A}})

as follows:

•

Given
u
a position of
\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{A},{\langle F,G\rangle})
, if

u\in W^{\prime}
then
u\in W^{\prime\prime}
.
Otherwise, we let
u\in W^{\prime\prime}
iff
u\in W
and

u\mathchoice{\mathrel{\overset{*}{\underset{\sigma}{\longrightarrow}}}}{\mathrel{\longrightarrow^{*}_{\sigma}}}{\longrightarrow^{*}_{\sigma}}{\longrightarrow^{*}_{\sigma}}(\mathsf{S}_{d}(x),q^{\prime})
.

It is then easy to check that
W^{\prime\prime}
is an infinite play of
\sigma
.

This concludes the proof of Proposition
57
.

6.6.
Complementation

It is known that, assuming the determinacy of acceptance games,
alternating tree automata are closed under complement
[
MS87
]
.
On the other hand, our setting only allows us to manipulate

positional
strategies on acceptance games,
which leads us to formulate complementation for
parity
automata,
since their acceptance games are always positionally determined.
Thus, in this section, we formalize the fact that, assuming the axiom

(\mathit{PosDet})
, each alternating parity automaton has a complement in
\mathsf{FSO}
.
More precisely, we prove the following.

Theorem 59

(Complementation of Tree Automata)
.

For each (
\mathrm{HF}
-closed) parity automaton
\mathcal{A}:\Sigma
,
there is an (
\mathrm{HF}
-closed) parity automaton
{\mathord{\sim}}\mathcal{A}:\Sigma

such that

\mathsf{FSO}+(\mathit{PosDet})\vdash\quad(\forall F:\Sigma)\Big(F\in\mathcal{L}({\mathord{\sim}}\mathcal{A})\penalty\ \penalty\ \Leftrightarrow\penalty\ \penalty\ F\notin\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A})\Big)

Alternating automata may be directly complemented in a locally syntactic fashion.
For an automaton
\mathcal{A}:\Sigma
we may define a complement automaton

{\mathord{\sim}}\mathcal{A}:\Sigma

with the same states as
\mathcal{A}
, and such that
\mathsf{P}
-strategies
in acceptance games for
{\mathord{\sim}}\mathcal{A}
correspond
(w.r.t. the visited states in infinite plays)
to
\mathsf{O}
-strategies in acceptance games for
\mathcal{A}
, and vice-versa.
Closely following
[
Wal02
]
,
the basic idea
is to see the transition function of
\mathcal{A}

\partial_{\mathcal{A}}\penalty\ \penalty\ :\penalty\ \penalty\ {Q_{\mathcal{A}}\times\Sigma}\penalty\ \penalty\ \longrightarrow\penalty\ \penalty\ {\mathcal{P}_{*}(\mathcal{P}_{*}(\mathscr{D}\times Q_{\mathcal{A}}))}

as taking
(q,\mathtt{a})

to the disjunctive normal form

\mathop{\bigvee}\limits_{\gamma\in\partial_{\mathcal{A}}(q,\mathtt{a})}\mathop{\bigwedge}\limits_{(d,q^{\prime})\in\gamma}(d,q^{\prime})

Then, for the complement
{\mathord{\sim}}\mathcal{A}:\Sigma
of
\mathcal{A}
,
we can let

{\partial_{{\mathord{\sim}}\mathcal{A}}}\penalty\ \penalty\ :\penalty\ \penalty\ {Q_{\mathcal{A}}\times\Sigma}\penalty\ \penalty\ \longrightarrow\penalty\ \penalty\ {\mathcal{P}_{*}(\mathcal{P}_{*}(\mathscr{D}\times Q_{\mathcal{A}}))}

take
(q,\mathtt{a})

to the De Morgan dual of
\partial_{\mathcal{A}}(q,\mathtt{a})
.

We now proceed to the formal definition.

{defi}

Given a parity automaton
\mathcal{A}:\Sigma
, we define
the parity automaton

{\mathord{\sim}}\mathcal{A}:\Sigma
as follows.
The automaton
{\mathord{\sim}}\mathcal{A}
has the same states and initial state as
\mathcal{A}
.
Its transitions are defined
as

\partial_{{\mathord{\sim}}\mathcal{A}}(q,a)\quad\mathrel{:=}\quad\Big\{\overline{\gamma}\in\mathcal{P}_{*}(\mathscr{D}\times Q_{\mathcal{A}})\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ |\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ (\forall\gamma\in\partial_{\mathcal{A}}(q,a))\big(\overline{\gamma}\cap\gamma\neq\varnothing\big)\Big\}

Its coloring is given as follows,
using Convention
5.5
.
4
:

C_{{\mathord{\sim}}\mathcal{A}}(q)\penalty\ \penalty\ \mathrel{:=}\penalty\ \penalty\ C_{\mathcal{A}}(q)+1

Note that by Remark
49
,

{\mathord{\sim}}\mathcal{A}:\Sigma
is
\mathrm{HF}
-closed
whenever so is
\mathcal{A}:\Sigma
.
We are now going to prove Theorem
59
.
To this end, fix a parity automaton
\mathcal{A}:\Sigma

and let
{\mathord{\sim}}\mathcal{A}:\Sigma
be as in Definition
6.6
.
Fix also some
F:\Sigma
.
We split Theorem
59
into the following statements.

Proposition 60
.

\mathsf{FSO}+(\mathit{PosDet})\vdash\penalty\ \penalty\ F\notin\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A})\penalty\ \penalty\ \Longrightarrow\penalty\ \penalty\ F\in\mathcal{L}({\mathord{\sim}}\mathcal{A})
.

Proposition 61
.

\mathsf{FSO}\vdash\penalty\ \penalty\ F\in\mathcal{L}({\mathord{\sim}}\mathcal{A})\penalty\ \penalty\ \Longrightarrow\penalty\ \penalty\ F\notin\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A})
.

The key
is that

\mathsf{P}
-strategies on
\mathcal{G}({\mathord{\sim}}\mathcal{A},F)

correspond to
\mathsf{O}
-strategies on
\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{A},F)
, and vice-versa.
We make this formal in §
6.6.1
and §
6.6.2
below.
First, notice that
Q_{{\mathord{\sim}}\mathcal{A}}=Q_{\mathcal{A}}
,
so that
the games
\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{A},F)
and
\mathcal{G}({\mathord{\sim}}\mathcal{A},F)

have the same sets of labels

\mathsf{P}\penalty\ \penalty\ \mathrel{:=}\penalty\ \penalty\ Q_{\mathcal{A}}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\mathsf{O}\penalty\ \penalty\ \mathrel{:=}\penalty\ \penalty\ Q_{\mathcal{A}}\times\mathcal{P}_{*}(\mathscr{D}\times Q_{\mathcal{A}})

In the following, we let

\mathcal{G}\penalty\ \penalty\ \mathrel{:=}\penalty\ \penalty\ \mathscr{D}^{*}\times\mathsf{P}\mathsf{O}

be the set of positions of the games

\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{A},F)
and
\mathcal{G}({\mathord{\sim}}\mathcal{A},F)
,
and we let

\iota\mathrel{:=}(\dot{\varepsilon},{q}^{\iota}_{\mathcal{A}})

be their (common) initial position.

6.6.1.
Proof of Proposition
60
.

We are going to show that

\mathsf{FSO}+(\mathit{PosDet})
proves

F\notin\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A})\penalty\ \penalty\ \Longrightarrow\penalty\ \penalty\ F\in\mathcal{L}({\mathord{\sim}}\mathcal{A})

First,
given an
\mathsf{O}
-strategy
\sigma_{\mathsf{O}}
on
\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{A},F)
,
we define a
\mathsf{P}
-strategy
\sigma_{\mathsf{P}}
on
\mathcal{G}({\mathord{\sim}}\mathcal{A},F)
.
Assuming that

\sigma_{\mathsf{O}}

satisfies

\mathrm{Strat}_{\mathsf{O}}(\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{A},F),\sigma_{\mathsf{O}})
,
the strategy
\sigma_{\mathsf{P}}

will satisfy
\mathrm{Strat}_{\mathsf{P}}(\mathcal{G}({\mathord{\sim}}\mathcal{A},F),\sigma_{\mathsf{P}})
.
Recall that this in particular means

\sigma_{\mathsf{O}}:\mathcal{G}_{\mathsf{O}}\mathrel{\mathfrak{to}}\mathscr{D}\times\mathsf{P}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\sigma_{\mathsf{P}}:\mathcal{G}_{\mathsf{P}}\mathrel{\mathfrak{to}}\mathsf{O}

By
\mathrm{HF}
-Bounded Choice for Product Types (Theorem
22
)
we are going to define
\sigma_{\mathsf{P}}
such that

\sigma_{\mathsf{P}}(x,q)\in\partial_{{\mathord{\sim}}\mathcal{A}}(q,F(x))

for each

(x,q)\in\mathscr{D}^{*}\times Q_{\mathcal{A}}
.
Assume fixed
(x,q)\in\mathscr{D}^{*}\times Q_{\mathcal{A}}
.
For all
\gamma\in\mathcal{P}_{*}(\mathscr{D}\times Q_{\mathcal{A}})

such that
\gamma\in\partial_{\mathcal{A}}(q,F(x))
,
we have
\sigma_{\mathsf{O}}(x,(q,\gamma))\in\gamma
.
By
\mathrm{HF}
-Comprehension (Remark
3.7.3
),
let

\overline{\gamma}\quad\mathrel{:=}\quad\{\sigma_{\mathsf{O}}(x,(q,\gamma))\penalty\ |\penalty\ \gamma\in\partial_{\mathcal{A}}(q,F(x))\}

By construction, we thus have
\overline{\gamma}\in\partial_{{\mathord{\sim}}\mathcal{A}}(q,F(x))
,
and
we let

\sigma_{\mathsf{P}}(x,q)\penalty\ \penalty\ \mathrel{:=}\penalty\ \penalty\ (q,\overline{\gamma})

We trivially have
\mathrm{Strat}_{\mathsf{P}}(\mathcal{G}({\mathord{\sim}}\mathcal{A},F),\sigma_{\mathsf{P}})
.

Lemma 62
.

Consider
\sigma_{\mathsf{O}}
and
\sigma_{\mathsf{P}}
as above.
For every infinite play
V
of
\sigma_{\mathsf{P}}
in
\mathcal{G}({\mathord{\sim}}\mathcal{A},F)

there is some infinite play
U
of
\sigma_{\mathsf{O}}
in
\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{A},F)

with
V_{\mathsf{P}}=U_{\mathsf{P}}
.

Proof 6.6
.

We define
U
by Comprehension for Product Types (Theorem
23
)
as follows.

•

First, for

(x,k)\in\mathcal{G}_{\mathsf{P}}
, if
(x,k)\in V_{\mathsf{P}}
then we let
(x,k)\in U_{\mathsf{P}}
.

•

Consider
(x,(q,\gamma))\in\mathcal{G}_{\mathsf{O}}
.
Using Remark
12
,
let
\prec
be a well-order on
\mathcal{P}_{*}(\mathscr{D}\times Q_{\mathcal{A}})
.
Then we let
(x,(q,\gamma))\in U_{\mathsf{O}}
iff

(x,q)\in V_{\mathsf{P}}

and
\gamma
is
\preceq
-minimal in
\partial_{\mathcal{A}}(q,F(x))

such that
(\mathsf{S}_{d}(x),q^{\prime})\in V_{\mathsf{P}}

for
(d,q^{\prime})=\sigma_{\mathsf{O}}(x,(q,\gamma))
.

Note that consecutive
\mathsf{P}
-positions in
U_{\mathsf{P}}
are indeed
connected by the edge relation of
\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{A},F)
:

Claim 63
.

(x,q),(\mathsf{S}_{d}(x),q^{\prime})\in U_{\mathsf{P}}\penalty\ \penalty\ \Rightarrow\penalty\ \penalty\ (\exists!u\in U_{\mathsf{O}})\left((x,q)\mathchoice{\mathrel{\overset{}{\underset{\sigma_{\mathsf{O}}}{\longrightarrow}}}}{\mathrel{\longrightarrow_{\sigma_{\mathsf{O}}}}}{\longrightarrow_{\sigma_{\mathsf{O}}}}{\longrightarrow_{\sigma_{\mathsf{O}}}}u\mathchoice{\mathrel{\overset{}{\underset{\sigma_{\mathsf{O}}}{\longrightarrow}}}}{\mathrel{\longrightarrow_{\sigma_{\mathsf{O}}}}}{\longrightarrow_{\sigma_{\mathsf{O}}}}{\longrightarrow_{\sigma_{\mathsf{O}}}}(\mathsf{S}_{d}(x),q^{\prime})\right)

Proof 6.7

(Proof of Claim 63)
.

We first show uniqueness. Let

(y_{0},(q_{0},\gamma_{0})),(y_{1},(q_{1},\gamma_{1}))\in U_{\mathsf{O}}

be between
(x,q)
and
(\mathsf{S}_{d}(x),q^{\prime})
.
Then we must have
y_{0}=y_{1}=x
and
q_{0}=q_{1}=q
.
Hence,
\gamma_{0}
and
\gamma_{1}
are both
\preceq
-minimal in
\partial_{\mathcal{A}}(q,F(x))

such that
\sigma_{\mathsf{O}}(x,(q,\gamma_{0}))=\sigma_{\mathsf{O}}(x,(q,\gamma_{1}))=(d,q^{\prime})
,
yielding
\gamma_{0}=\gamma_{1}
as required.

We now show the existence of an appropriate
(x,(q,\gamma))\in U_{\mathsf{O}}
.
Since

\mathrm{Play}(\sigma_{\mathsf{P}},\iota,V)
,
we have
(d,q^{\prime})\in\overline{\gamma}

with
(\ell,\overline{\gamma})\in\sigma_{\mathsf{P}}(y,\ell)

for some
(y,\ell)\in V_{\mathsf{P}}
.
But

\mathrm{Play}(\sigma_{\mathsf{P}},\iota,V)

moreover implies that either
(y,\ell)\vartriangleleft(x,q)
or
(x,q)\trianglelefteq(y,\ell)
,
from which follows that
(y,\ell)=(x,q)

and
(q,\overline{\gamma})\in\sigma_{\mathsf{P}}(x,q)
.
Since

\overline{\gamma}\quad\mathrel{:=}\quad\{\sigma_{\mathsf{O}}(x,\gamma)\penalty\ |\penalty\ \gamma\in\partial_{\mathcal{A}}(q,F(x))\}

it follows that
(d,q^{\prime})\in\sigma_{\mathsf{O}}(x,\gamma)
for some

\gamma\in\partial_{\mathcal{A}}(q,F(x))
, and we are done.

We now check that
U
is indeed an infinite play of
\sigma_{\mathsf{O}}
,
i.e. that

\mathrm{Play}(\sigma_{\mathsf{O}},\iota,U)

holds.
First, we have
\iota\in U
.
Moreover,

Claim 64
.

(\forall u\in U)\left(\iota\mathchoice{\mathrel{\overset{*}{\underset{\sigma_{\mathsf{O}}}{\longrightarrow}}}}{\mathrel{\longrightarrow^{*}_{\sigma_{\mathsf{O}}}}}{\longrightarrow^{*}_{\sigma_{\mathsf{O}}}}{\longrightarrow^{*}_{\sigma_{\mathsf{O}}}}u\right)

Proof 6.8

(Proof of Claim 64)
.

We reason by induction on

\mathchoice{\mathrel{\overset{}{\underset{\sigma_{\mathsf{O}}}{\longrightarrow}}}}{\mathrel{\longrightarrow_{\sigma_{\mathsf{O}}}}}{\longrightarrow_{\sigma_{\mathsf{O}}}}{\longrightarrow_{\sigma_{\mathsf{O}}}}{}

(Corollary
34
).
First, if
u\in U_{\mathsf{O}}
, then
u
is of the form
(x,(q,\gamma))
.
By definition of
U_{\mathsf{O}}
we have
(x,q)\in U_{\mathsf{P}}
with

(x,q)\mathchoice{\mathrel{\overset{}{\underset{\sigma_{\mathsf{O}}}{\longrightarrow}}}}{\mathrel{\longrightarrow_{\sigma_{\mathsf{O}}}}}{\longrightarrow_{\sigma_{\mathsf{O}}}}{\longrightarrow_{\sigma_{\mathsf{O}}}}(x,(q,\gamma))

and we conclude by induction hypothesis.

Consider now the case of
u\in U_{\mathsf{P}}=V_{\mathsf{P}}
.
In this case,
u
of the form
(x,q)
.
We apply Proposition
6
.(
5
),
stating that either
x\mathrel{\doteq}\dot{\varepsilon}
or

for some

.
In the former case,

V
is a play, we have

\iota\mathchoice{\mathrel{\overset{*}{\underset{\sigma_{\mathsf{P}}}{\longrightarrow}}}}{\mathrel{\longrightarrow^{*}_{\sigma_{\mathsf{P}}}}}{\longrightarrow^{*}_{\sigma_{\mathsf{P}}}}{\longrightarrow^{*}_{\sigma_{\mathsf{P}}}}(x,q)

and
Proposition

.(

)
implies

.
In the latter case, assume

is
\mathsf{S}_{d}(y)
.
We apply twice

37
for Infinite Plays,
which gives some
(y,q^{\prime})\in V_{\mathsf{P}}
such that

(y,q^{\prime})\mathchoice{\mathrel{\overset{+}{\underset{\sigma_{\mathsf{P}}}{\longrightarrow}}}}{\mathrel{\longrightarrow^{+}_{\sigma_{\mathsf{P}}}}}{\longrightarrow^{+}_{\sigma_{\mathsf{P}}}}{\longrightarrow^{+}_{\sigma_{\mathsf{P}}}}(\mathsf{S}_{d}(y),q)

By induction hypothesis we get

\iota\mathchoice{\mathrel{\overset{*}{\underset{\sigma_{\mathsf{O}}}{\longrightarrow}}}}{\mathrel{\longrightarrow^{*}_{\sigma_{\mathsf{O}}}}}{\longrightarrow^{*}_{\sigma_{\mathsf{O}}}}{\longrightarrow^{*}_{\sigma_{\mathsf{O}}}}(y,q^{\prime})

and we conclude by Claim
63
.

Also,

Claim 65
.

(\forall u\in U)(\exists!v\in U)\left(u\mathchoice{\mathrel{\overset{}{\underset{\sigma_{\mathsf{O}}}{\longrightarrow}}}}{\mathrel{\longrightarrow_{\sigma_{\mathsf{O}}}}}{\longrightarrow_{\sigma_{\mathsf{O}}}}{\longrightarrow_{\sigma_{\mathsf{O}}}}v\right)

Proof 6.9

(Proof of Claim 65)
.

The case of
u\in U_{\mathsf{P}}=V_{\mathsf{P}}
follows directly from the definition
of
U_{\mathsf{O}}
and the fact that
\sigma_{\mathsf{O}}:\mathcal{G}_{\mathsf{O}}\mathrel{\mathfrak{to}}\mathscr{D}\times\mathsf{P}

and

\mathrm{Play}(\sigma_{\mathsf{P}},\iota,V)
.
Consider now the case of

.
By definition of

there is some
v\in U_{\mathsf{P}}

such that

u\mathchoice{\mathrel{\overset{}{\underset{\sigma_{\mathsf{O}}}{\longrightarrow}}}}{\mathrel{\longrightarrow_{\sigma_{\mathsf{O}}}}}{\longrightarrow_{\sigma_{\mathsf{O}}}}{\longrightarrow_{\sigma_{\mathsf{O}}}}v
.
Uniqueness follows from the fact that

and

\mathrm{Play}(\sigma_{\mathsf{P}},\iota,V)
.

In order to obtain

,

35

and it remains to show:

Claim 66
.

(\forall u\in U)\left[u\neq\iota\penalty\ \penalty\ \Rightarrow\penalty\ \penalty\ (\exists v\in U)\left(v\mathchoice{\mathrel{\overset{}{\underset{\sigma_{\mathsf{O}}}{\longrightarrow}}}}{\mathrel{\longrightarrow_{\sigma_{\mathsf{O}}}}}{\longrightarrow_{\sigma_{\mathsf{O}}}}{\longrightarrow_{\sigma_{\mathsf{O}}}}u\right)\right]

Proof 6.10

(Proof of Claim 66)
.

The case of
u\in U_{\mathsf{O}}
follows from the definition of
U_{\mathsf{O}}
.
The case of
u\in U_{\mathsf{P}}
directly follow from
Claim
63

(together with Proposition

.(
9
))
and

\mathrm{Play}(\sigma_{\mathsf{P}},\iota,V)

This concludes the proof of Lemma

.

We use the following simple fact
in order to obtain from Lemma
62

that
\sigma_{\mathsf{P}}
is winning in
\mathcal{G}({\mathord{\sim}}\mathcal{A},F)

whenever
\sigma_{\mathsf{O}}
is winning in
\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{A},F)
.

Lemma 67
.

Given plays
U,V:\mathcal{G}\mathrel{\mathfrak{to}}2

as in Lemma
62
,
we have

\mathrm{Par}(\mathcal{A},U)\penalty\ \Leftrightarrow\penalty\ \lnot\mathrm{Par}({\mathord{\sim}}\mathcal{A},V)
.

We now have everything we need to obtain Proposition
60
,
namely

\mathsf{FSO}+(\mathit{PosDet})\vdash\quad F\notin\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A})\penalty\ \penalty\ \Longrightarrow\penalty\ \penalty\ F\in\mathcal{L}({\mathord{\sim}}\mathcal{A})

Assume
F\notin\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A})
.
By Definition
6.1
,
there is no winning
\mathsf{P}
-strategy in
\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{A},F)
.
By the axiom of positional determinacy of parity games
(\mathit{PosDet})

there is a winning
\mathsf{O}
-strategy
\sigma_{\mathsf{O}}
in
\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{A},F)
,
so that

(\forall U:\mathcal{G}\mathrel{\mathfrak{to}}2)\Big(\mathrm{Play}(\sigma_{\mathsf{O}},\iota,U)\penalty\ \penalty\ \Rightarrow\penalty\ \penalty\ \lnot\mathrm{Par}(\mathcal{A},U)\Big)

(13)

Consider now the
\mathsf{P}
-strategy
\sigma_{\mathsf{P}}
on
\mathcal{G}({\mathord{\sim}}\mathcal{A},F)

as defined above.
We claim that
\sigma_{\mathsf{P}}
is winning, that is

{clm}

(\forall V:\mathcal{G}\mathrel{\mathfrak{to}}2)\Big(\mathrm{Play}(\sigma_{\mathsf{P}},\iota,V)\penalty\ \penalty\ \Rightarrow\penalty\ \penalty\ \mathrm{Par}({\mathord{\sim}}\mathcal{A},V)\Big)

Proof 6.11

(Proof of Claim
\theclm
)
.

Given an infinite play
V
of
\sigma_{\mathsf{P}}
,
by Lemma
62

we can build an infinite play
U
of
\sigma_{\mathsf{O}}
,
which by (
13
)
satisfies
\lnot\mathrm{Par}(\mathcal{A},-)
,
so that
V
satisfies
\mathrm{Par}({\mathord{\sim}}\mathcal{A},-)

thanks to Lemma
67
.

We thus have
F\in\mathcal{L}({\mathord{\sim}}\mathcal{A},F)
.
This concludes the proof of Proposition
60
.

6.6.2.
Proof of Proposition
61
.

We are now going to show that

\mathsf{FSO}
proves

F\in\mathcal{L}({\mathord{\sim}}\mathcal{A})\penalty\ \penalty\ \Longrightarrow\penalty\ \penalty\ F\notin\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A})

We associate a (winning)
\mathsf{O}
-strategy

\sigma_{\mathsf{O}}
on
\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{A},F)

to each (winning)
\mathsf{P}
-strategy
\sigma_{\mathsf{P}}
on
\mathcal{G}({\mathord{\sim}}\mathcal{A},F)
.
Assuming that the
\mathsf{P}
-strategy
satisfies

\mathrm{Strat}_{\mathsf{P}}(\mathcal{G}({\mathord{\sim}}\mathcal{A},F),\sigma_{\mathsf{P}})
,
the
\mathsf{O}
-strategy
will satisfy

\mathrm{Strat}_{\mathsf{O}}(\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{A},F),\sigma_{\mathsf{O}})
.
Note that

\sigma_{\mathsf{P}}:\mathcal{G}_{\mathsf{P}}\mathrel{\mathfrak{to}}\mathsf{O}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\sigma_{\mathsf{O}}:\mathcal{G}_{\mathsf{O}}\mathrel{\mathfrak{to}}\mathscr{D}\times\mathsf{P}

We define
\sigma_{\mathsf{O}}(x,(q,\gamma))

for each position

(x,(q,\gamma))\in\mathscr{D}^{*}\times(Q_{\mathcal{A}}\times\mathcal{P}_{*}(\mathscr{D}\times Q_{\mathcal{A}}))

By definition of
\partial_{{\mathord{\sim}}\mathcal{A}}(q,F(p))
,
we have
\sigma_{\mathsf{P}}(p,q)=(q,\overline{\gamma})

where
\overline{\gamma}
intersects all
\gamma\in\partial_{\mathcal{A}}(q,F(p))
.
So if
\gamma\in\partial_{\mathcal{A}}(q,F(p))
,
by
\mathrm{HF}
-Bounded Choice for Product Types (Theorem
22
)
we let
\sigma_{\mathsf{O}}(p,(q,\gamma))
be some

(d,q^{\prime})
such that
(d,q^{\prime})\in\gamma\cap\overline{\gamma}
.
Otherwise, since
\gamma\neq\varnothing
,
we let
\sigma_{\mathsf{O}}(p,(q,\gamma))
be some

(d,q^{\prime})
such that
(d,q^{\prime})\in\gamma
.

We also trivially have that
\mathrm{Strat}_{\mathsf{O}}(\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{A},F),\sigma_{\mathsf{O}})
.

Lemma 68
.

Consider a
\mathsf{P}
-strategy
\sigma_{\mathsf{P}}
and
an
\mathsf{O}
-strategy
\sigma_{\mathsf{O}}
as in above.
For every infinite play
V

of
\sigma_{\mathsf{O}}
on
\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{A},F)

there is some infinite play
U
of
\sigma_{\mathsf{P}}
on
\mathcal{G}({\mathord{\sim}}\mathcal{A},F)

with
V_{\mathsf{P}}=U_{\mathsf{P}}
.

Proof 6.12
.

We define
U
by Comprehension for Product Types (Theorem
23
)
as follows.

•

Definition of
U
.

For
(x,k)\in\mathcal{G}_{\mathsf{P}}
, if
(x,k)\in V_{\mathsf{P}}
then we let
(x,k)\in U_{\mathsf{P}}
,
and for

(x,(q,\overline{\gamma}))\in U_{\mathsf{O}}

iff
(q,\overline{\gamma})=\sigma_{\mathsf{P}}(x,q)

for
(x,q)\in U_{\mathsf{P}}
.

Similarly as in Lemma
62
,
we have

Claim 69
.

(x,q),(\mathsf{S}_{d}(x),q^{\prime})\in U_{\mathsf{P}}\penalty\ \penalty\ \Rightarrow\penalty\ \penalty\ (\exists!u\in U_{\mathsf{O}})\left((x,q)\mathchoice{\mathrel{\overset{}{\underset{\sigma_{\mathsf{P}}}{\longrightarrow}}}}{\mathrel{\longrightarrow_{\sigma_{\mathsf{P}}}}}{\longrightarrow_{\sigma_{\mathsf{P}}}}{\longrightarrow_{\sigma_{\mathsf{P}}}}u\mathchoice{\mathrel{\overset{}{\underset{\sigma_{\mathsf{P}}}{\longrightarrow}}}}{\mathrel{\longrightarrow_{\sigma_{\mathsf{P}}}}}{\longrightarrow_{\sigma_{\mathsf{P}}}}{\longrightarrow_{\sigma_{\mathsf{P}}}}(\mathsf{S}_{d}(x),q^{\prime})\right)

Proof 6.13

(Proof of Claim 69)
.

Uniqueness directly follows from the fact that
u=(x,\sigma_{\mathsf{P}}(x,q))
.
As for existence, we directly have

(x,q)\mathchoice{\mathrel{\overset{}{\underset{\sigma_{\mathsf{P}}}{\longrightarrow}}}}{\mathrel{\longrightarrow_{\sigma_{\mathsf{P}}}}}{\longrightarrow_{\sigma_{\mathsf{P}}}}{\longrightarrow_{\sigma_{\mathsf{P}}}}u
,
so it remains to show

u\mathchoice{\mathrel{\overset{}{\underset{\sigma_{\mathsf{P}}}{\longrightarrow}}}}{\mathrel{\longrightarrow_{\sigma_{\mathsf{P}}}}}{\longrightarrow_{\sigma_{\mathsf{P}}}}{\longrightarrow_{\sigma_{\mathsf{P}}}}(\mathsf{S}_{d}(x),q^{\prime})
,
which amounts to
(d,q^{\prime})\in\overline{\gamma}
for
(q,\overline{\gamma})=\sigma_{\mathsf{P}}(x,q)
.
But
(\mathsf{S}_{d}(x),q^{\prime})\in V_{\mathsf{P}}

with

.

\mathrm{Play}(\sigma_{\mathsf{O}},\iota,V)

.
By definition of
\sigma_{\mathsf{O}}
,
we thus have
(d,q^{\prime})\in\gamma\cap\overline{\gamma}
and we are done.

We now check that

\mathrm{Play}(\sigma_{\mathsf{P}},\iota,U)
.
Note that
\iota\in U
.
Moreover, proceeding as in
Lemma
62
,
we have

Claim 70
.

(\forall u\in U)\left(\iota\mathchoice{\mathrel{\overset{*}{\underset{\sigma_{\mathsf{P}}}{\longrightarrow}}}}{\mathrel{\longrightarrow^{*}_{\sigma_{\mathsf{P}}}}}{\longrightarrow^{*}_{\sigma_{\mathsf{P}}}}{\longrightarrow^{*}_{\sigma_{\mathsf{P}}}}u\right)

Proof 6.14

(Proof of Claim 70)
.

By induction on
\mathchoice{\mathrel{\overset{}{\underset{\sigma_{\mathsf{P}}}{\longrightarrow}}}}{\mathrel{\longrightarrow_{\sigma_{\mathsf{P}}}}}{\longrightarrow_{\sigma_{\mathsf{P}}}}{\longrightarrow_{\sigma_{\mathsf{P}}}}

(Corollary
34
).
The case of

.
As for

,
using Claim

and

37
.

Continuing as in Lemma
62
,
we now invoke Proposition
35

and we are left with showing

Claim 71
.

(\forall u\in U)(\exists!v\in U)\left(u\mathchoice{\mathrel{\overset{}{\underset{\sigma_{\mathsf{P}}}{\longrightarrow}}}}{\mathrel{\longrightarrow_{\sigma_{\mathsf{P}}}}}{\longrightarrow_{\sigma_{\mathsf{P}}}}{\longrightarrow_{\sigma_{\mathsf{P}}}}v\right)\penalty\ \penalty\ \land\penalty\ \penalty\ (\forall u\in U)\left[u\neq\iota\penalty\ \penalty\ \Rightarrow\penalty\ \penalty\ (\exists v\in U)\left(v\mathchoice{\mathrel{\overset{}{\underset{\sigma_{\mathsf{P}}}{\longrightarrow}}}}{\mathrel{\longrightarrow_{\sigma_{\mathsf{P}}}}}{\longrightarrow_{\sigma_{\mathsf{P}}}}{\longrightarrow_{\sigma_{\mathsf{P}}}}u\right)\right]

(together with Proposition

.(

.

.

.

This concludes the proof of Lemma

.

Similarly as in §

,
we use the following simple fact.

Lemma 72
.

Given plays
U,V:\mathcal{G}\mathrel{\mathfrak{to}}2

68
,
we have

\mathrm{Par}({\mathord{\sim}}\mathcal{A},U)\penalty\ \Leftrightarrow\penalty\ \lnot\mathrm{Par}(\mathcal{A},V)

It is now easy to obtain Proposition
61
,
namely

\mathsf{FSO}\vdash\quad F\in\mathcal{L}({\mathord{\sim}}\mathcal{A})\penalty\ \penalty\ \Longrightarrow\penalty\ \penalty\ F\notin\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A})

Assume that
F\in\mathcal{L}({\mathord{\sim}}\mathcal{A})
.
By Definition
6.1
, we thus have a winning

\mathsf{P}
-strategy
\sigma_{\mathsf{P}}
in
\mathcal{G}({\mathord{\sim}}\mathcal{A},F)
,
so that

(\forall U:\mathcal{G}\mathrel{\mathfrak{to}}2)\Big(\mathrm{Play}(\sigma_{\mathsf{P}},\iota,U)\penalty\ \penalty\ \Rightarrow\penalty\ \penalty\ \mathrm{Par}({\mathord{\sim}}\mathcal{A},U)\Big)

Consider now the
\mathsf{O}
-strategy
\sigma_{\mathsf{O}}
on
\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{A},F)

as defined above.
Reasoning as in the case

),

and

imply

(\forall V:\mathcal{G}\mathrel{\mathfrak{to}}2)\Big(\mathrm{Play}(\sigma_{\mathsf{O}},\iota,V)\penalty\ \penalty\ \Rightarrow\penalty\ \penalty\ \lnot\mathrm{Par}(\mathcal{A},V)\Big)

It then follows from Lemma
