that there is no winning
\mathsf{P}
-strategy on
\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{A},F)
,
so that
F\notin\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A})
.

This concludes the proof of Proposition
61
.

7.
\mathsf{MSO}
on Infinite Words in Paths of
{\mathsf{FSO}_{\mathscr{D}}}

We discuss here the theory of
\mathsf{MSO}
over
\omega
-words
for the
infinite paths
of
{\mathsf{FSO}_{\mathscr{D}}}
.
Since
\mathsf{MSO}
on
\omega
-words admits a complete axiomatization
[
Sie70
]
,
this will allow us to
freely import results on
\mathsf{MSO}
over
\omega
-words
for the paths of
{\mathsf{FSO}_{\mathscr{D}}}
.
In particular, our completeness argument (§
8
)
relies on a version of the Büchi-Landweber’s Theorem
[
BL69
]

formulated with
\mathsf{MSO}
over
\omega
-words,
that we lift for free to
{\mathsf{FSO}_{\mathscr{D}}}
.
Also, to prove
the Simulation Theorem
56
in §
9
,
we use McNaughton’s Theorem
[
McN66
]
,
and similarly obtain it for free in
{\mathsf{FSO}_{\mathscr{D}}}
.

An obvious way to obtain
\mathsf{MSO}
over
\omega
-words is to consider
the system
\mathsf{MSO}_{1}
(that is
{\mathsf{MSO}_{\mathscr{D}}}
for
\mathscr{D}=1
).
However, recall that we want to see each path
of
{\mathsf{FSO}_{\mathscr{D}}}
(in the sense of (
16
) below)
as a model of
\mathsf{MSO}
on
\omega
-words.
This is technically simpler if, following
[
Rib12
]
,
one uses a version of
\mathsf{MSO}
on
\omega
-words
over a purely relational vocabulary with only
the strict order
\mathrel{\dot{<}}
on numbers as atomic relation
(besides equality
\mathrel{\doteq}
).

{defi}

[The Theory
\mathsf{FSO}[\mathrel{\dot{<}}]^{\omega}
]

The language of
\mathsf{FSO}[\mathrel{\dot{<}}]^{\omega}
is the language of
{\mathsf{FSO}_{\mathscr{D}}}

with the following restriction:

•

the only
Individual terms
of
\mathsf{FSO}[\mathrel{\dot{<}}]^{\omega}

are the constant
\dot{\varepsilon}
and
the individual variables (
x,y,z
etc.)

The deduction rules of
\mathsf{FSO}[\mathrel{\dot{<}}]^{\omega}
are the same as the rules of

{\mathsf{FSO}_{\mathscr{D}}}
.
The axioms of
\mathsf{FSO}[\mathrel{\dot{<}}]^{\omega}
are
the Equality Axioms of §
3.4.1
,
the Axioms on
\mathrm{HF}
-Sets of §
3.4.4
,
the Functional Choice Axioms of §
3.4.5
,
together with
the axioms displayed on Figure
6
,
stating that
\mathrel{\dot{<}}
is a discrete unbounded strict linear order with
\dot{\varepsilon}
as its minimal element
(see e.g.
[
Rib12
]
),
and with
the following induction scheme.

•

Well-Founded Induction.

For each formula
\varphi
, the axiom

(\forall x)\big[(\forall y\mathrel{\dot{<}}x)(\varphi(y))\penalty\ \penalty\ \Longrightarrow\penalty\ \penalty\ \varphi(x)\big]\penalty\ \penalty\ \Longrightarrow\penalty\ \penalty\ (\forall x)\varphi(x)

Remark 73
.

Note that all Individuals of
\mathsf{FSO}[\mathrel{\dot{<}}]^{\omega}

are Individuals of
{\mathsf{FSO}_{\mathscr{D}}}
, but not conversely.
As a consequence, all
\mathrm{HF}
-terms of
\mathsf{FSO}[\mathrel{\dot{<}}]^{\omega}

are
\mathrm{HF}
-terms of
{\mathsf{FSO}_{\mathscr{D}}}
, but not conversely.
Also, note that it may have seemed more natural not to include the individual
constant
\dot{\varepsilon}
in the language of
\mathsf{FSO}[\mathrel{\dot{<}}]^{\omega}
.
We have included it because this eases our concrete uses of
\mathsf{FSO}[\mathrel{\dot{<}}]^{\omega}

in §
8.4
and §
9.2
.

\begin{array}[]{c}(\forall x)\left(\dot{\varepsilon}\mathrel{\dot{\leq}}x\right)\qquad\lnot(\exists x)\left(x\mathrel{\dot{<}}x\right)\qquad(\forall x)(\forall y)(\forall z)\left(x\mathrel{\dot{<}}y\penalty\ \penalty\ \Rightarrow\penalty\ \penalty\ y\mathrel{\dot{<}}z\penalty\ \penalty\ \Rightarrow\penalty\ \penalty\ x\mathrel{\dot{<}}z\right)\\
\\
(\forall x)(\exists y)\left(x\mathrel{\dot{<}}y\right)\qquad(\forall x)(\forall y)\left[x\mathrel{\dot{<}}y\penalty\ \penalty\ \lor\penalty\ \penalty\ x\mathrel{\doteq}y\penalty\ \penalty\ \lor\penalty\ \penalty\ y\mathrel{\dot{<}}x\right]\\
\\
(\forall x)\Big[(\exists y\mathrel{\dot{<}}x)\penalty\ \penalty\ \Rightarrow\penalty\ \penalty\ (\exists y\mathrel{\dot{<}}x)\lnot(\exists z)\big(y\mathrel{\dot{<}}z\mathrel{\dot{<}}x\big)\Big]\\[10.00002pt]
\end{array}

Figure 6.
Axioms on the relation
\mathrel{\dot{<}}
of
\mathsf{FSO}[\mathrel{\dot{<}}]^{\omega}
.

Similarly to the case of
\mathscr{D}
-ary trees (§
2
),
the theory
\mathsf{FSO}[\mathrel{\dot{<}}]^{\omega}
is intended to be interpreted
in a theory
\mathsf{MSO}[\mathrel{\dot{<}}]^{\omega}
.
Intuitively,
\mathsf{MSO}[\mathrel{\dot{<}}]^{\omega}
is to
\mathsf{FSO}[\mathrel{\dot{<}}]^{\omega}
what
{\mathsf{MSO}_{\mathscr{D}}}
is to
{\mathsf{FSO}_{\mathscr{D}}}
.

{defi}

[The Theory
\mathsf{MSO}[\mathrel{\dot{<}}]^{\omega}
]

The language of
\mathsf{MSO}[\mathrel{\dot{<}}]^{\omega}
is the language of
{\mathsf{MSO}_{\mathscr{D}}}

with the following restriction:

•

the only
Individual terms
of
\mathsf{MSO}[\mathrel{\dot{<}}]^{\omega}

are individual variables (
x,y,z
etc.).

The axioms of
\mathsf{MSO}[\mathrel{\dot{<}}]^{\omega}
are the equality axioms
and the comprehension scheme of
{\mathsf{MSO}_{\mathscr{D}}}
(§
2.2
),
together with the induction scheme
and the axioms on
\mathrel{\dot{<}}
of
\mathsf{FSO}[\mathrel{\dot{<}}]^{\omega}

displayed in Figure
6
.

We write
\mathfrak{N}
both for the standard model of
\mathsf{FSO}[\mathrel{\dot{<}}]^{\omega}

and for the standard model of
\mathsf{MSO}[\mathrel{\dot{<}}]^{\omega}
.
In the case of
\mathsf{MSO}[\mathrel{\dot{<}}]^{\omega}
,
formulae are interpreted in
\mathfrak{N}
as expected:
individual variables range over
\mathbb{N}
, monadic predicate variables
range over
\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{N})
and
\mathrel{\dot{<}}
is the standard order
<
on
\mathbb{N}
.
The interpretation of
\mathsf{FSO}[\mathrel{\dot{<}}]^{\omega}
-formulae in
\mathfrak{N}
is similar,
with the obvious changes w.r.t. §
3.5
for the interpretation
of terms, and where Functions range over

\bigcup_{\kappa\in V_{\omega}}\left(\mathbb{N}\penalty\ \penalty\ \longrightarrow\penalty\ \penalty\ \kappa\right)

The key property of
\mathsf{MSO}[\mathrel{\dot{<}}]^{\omega}
we rely on is that it completely axiomatizes the
theory of the standard model
\mathfrak{N}

of
\omega
-words
[
Sie70
]

(see also
[
Rib12
]
).

Theorem 74

(
[
Sie70
]
)
.

For every closed
\mathsf{MSO}[\mathrel{\dot{<}}]^{\omega}
-formula
\varphi
,

\mathfrak{N}\models\varphi\qquad\text{if and only if}\qquad\mathsf{MSO}[\mathrel{\dot{<}}]^{\omega}\vdash\varphi

The formula translation from
{\mathsf{FSO}_{\mathscr{D}}}
to
{\mathsf{MSO}_{\mathscr{D}}}
of §
3.6

restricts to a translation of
\mathsf{FSO}[\mathrel{\dot{<}}]^{\omega}
-formulae
to
\mathsf{MSO}[\mathrel{\dot{<}}]^{\omega}
-formulae.
This easily extends to theories,
and we get the following version of Proposition
20
.

Proposition 75
.

For every closed
\mathsf{FSO}[\mathrel{\dot{<}}]^{\omega}
-formula
\varphi
,

\displaystyle\mathsf{FSO}[\mathrel{\dot{<}}]^{\omega}\vdash\varphi\qquad\text{if and only if}\qquad\mathsf{MSO}[\mathrel{\dot{<}}]^{\omega}\vdash\langle\varphi\rangle

(14)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{N}\models\varphi\qquad\text{if and only if}\qquad\mathfrak{N}\models\langle\varphi\rangle

(15)

Thanks to (
15
),
the completeness of
\mathsf{MSO}[\mathrel{\dot{<}}]^{\omega}
directly gives the completeness
of
\mathsf{MSO}[\mathrel{\dot{<}}]^{\omega}
w.r.t. the translation closed of
\mathsf{FSO}[\mathrel{\dot{<}}]^{\omega}
-formulae
\varphi
:

\mathsf{FSO}[\mathrel{\dot{<}}]^{\omega}\vdash\varphi\qquad\text{if and only if}\qquad\mathfrak{N}\models\langle\varphi\rangle

Our goal now is to prove that if a closed
\mathsf{FSO}[\mathrel{\dot{<}}]^{\omega}
formula holds in the
standard model
\mathfrak{N}
of
\omega
-words, then
{\mathsf{FSO}_{\mathscr{D}}}

proves its relativization to any rooted tree path.
Given a formula
\varphi
of
\mathsf{FSO}[\mathrel{\dot{<}}]^{\omega}
and a Function variable
P
,
write
\varphi^{P}
for the
{\mathsf{FSO}_{\mathscr{D}}}
formula
obtained from
\varphi

by relativizing all individual quantifications to
P

and by replacing all Function quantifications

F:K
by
F:P\mathrel{\mathfrak{to}}K
.
Moreover,
we say that
P:\mathscr{D}^{*}\mathrel{\mathfrak{to}}2
is a
rooted path

when the following formula
\mathrm{TPath}(P)
holds:

\mathrm{TPath}(P)\quad\mathrel{:=}\quad\left\{\begin{array}[]{l l}&(\dot{\varepsilon}\in P)\\
\land&(\forall x,y\in P)\left(x\mathrel{\dot{<}}y\penalty\ \penalty\ \lor\penalty\ \penalty\ x\mathrel{\doteq}y\penalty\ \penalty\ \lor\penalty\ \penalty\ y\mathrel{\dot{<}}x\right)\\
\land&(\forall x\in P)(\exists y\in P)(\mathrm{S}(x,y))\end{array}\right.

(16)

where
\mathrm{S}(x,y)
stands for

x\mathrel{\dot{<}}y\penalty\ \penalty\ \land\penalty\ \penalty\ \lnot(\exists z)\big[x\mathrel{\dot{<}}z\mathrel{\dot{<}}y\big]

We can now formally state the property we are targeting:

{{\mathsf{FSO}_{\mathscr{D}}}}\vdash(\forall P:2)\left(\mathrm{TPath}(P)\penalty\ \penalty\ \Rightarrow\penalty\ \penalty\ \varphi^{P}\right)\qquad\text{whenever}\qquad\mathfrak{N}\models\varphi

(17)

The proof of (
17
) is deferred to Proposition
78
.
It relies on two lemmas.
The first one is an adaptation of
Lemma
27
(§
4.3
)
to rooted tree paths, which will give the last axiom of Figure
6

for rooted tree paths.
The second one is a weakening of (
17
)
where
\mathsf{FSO}[\mathrel{\dot{<}}]^{\omega}\vdash\varphi
is assumed instead of
\mathfrak{N}\models\varphi
.

Lemma 76
.

{\mathsf{FSO}_{\mathscr{D}}}
proves the following, assuming
P:\mathscr{D}^{*}\mathrel{\mathfrak{to}}2

and
\mathrm{TPath}(P)
:

(\forall x\in P)\Big[(\exists y\in P)(y\mathrel{\dot{<}}x)\penalty\ \penalty\ \Rightarrow\penalty\ \penalty\ (\exists y\in P)\big(y\mathrel{\dot{<}}x\penalty\ \land\penalty\ \lnot(\exists z\in P)(y\mathrel{\dot{<}}z\mathrel{\dot{<}}x)\big)\Big]

Lemma 77
.

For all closed
\mathsf{FSO}[\mathrel{\dot{<}}]^{\omega}
-formula
\varphi
, we have

{\mathsf{FSO}_{\mathscr{D}}}\vdash\forall P:2\left(\mathrm{TPath}(P)\penalty\ \penalty\ \Rightarrow\penalty\ \penalty\ \varphi^{P}\right)\qquad\text{whenever}\qquad\mathsf{FSO}[\mathrel{\dot{<}}]^{\omega}\vdash\varphi

Proof 7.1
.

The proof is by induction on derivations of
\mathsf{FSO}[\mathrel{\dot{<}}]^{\omega}
-formulae.
For formulae
\bm{\psi},\varphi
with free Function variables

\bm{F}=F_{1},\dots,F_{p}
and free Individual variables

\bm{x}=x_{1},\dots,x_{q}

(and possibly further free
\mathrm{HF}
-variables), we show that
for all
\mathrm{HF}
-terms
\bm{K}=K_{1},\dots,K_{p}
of
\mathsf{FSO}[\mathrel{\dot{<}}]^{\omega}

we have

\bm{F:K}\penalty\ ,\penalty\ \bm{\psi}\vdash_{\mathsf{FSO}[\mathrel{\dot{<}}]^{\omega}}\varphi\qquad\text{implies}\qquad\mathrm{TPath}(P)\penalty\ ,\penalty\ \bm{F:P\mathrel{\mathfrak{to}}K}\penalty\ ,\penalty\ \bm{x}\mathrel{\dot{\in}}P\penalty\ ,\penalty\ \bm{\psi^{P}}\vdash_{{\mathsf{FSO}_{\mathscr{D}}}}\varphi^{P}

The cases for each inference rule are immediate from their respective
induction hypothesis, and we also easily obtain
the Equality Axioms (§
3.4.1
),
the Axioms of
\mathrm{HF}
-Sets (§
3.4.4
)
and the Axiom of
\mathrm{HF}
-Bounded Choice for
\mathrm{HF}
-Sets (§
3.4.5
).
We resort on Theorem
22

for the axioms of
\mathrm{HF}
-Bounded Choice for Functions
and of Iterated
\mathrm{HF}
-Bounded Choice.
Moreover, the Induction axiom of
\mathsf{FSO}[\mathrel{\dot{<}}]^{\omega}
on the formula
\varphi(x)

directly follows from Well-Founded Induction in
{\mathsf{FSO}_{\mathscr{D}}}

(Theorem
7
)
on the formula

\psi(x)\quad\mathrel{:=}\quad\left(x\in P\penalty\ \penalty\ \Rightarrow\penalty\ \penalty\ \varphi(x)\right)

It remains to deal with the
\mathrel{\dot{<}}
-axioms of Figure
6
.
The first five axioms
(stating that
\mathrel{\dot{<}}
is an unbounded linear order)
directly follow from the Tree axioms of
{\mathsf{FSO}_{\mathscr{D}}}
(Figure
3
)
and from relativization to
P
with
\mathrm{TPath}(P)
.
Finally, we have to show that
{\mathsf{FSO}_{\mathscr{D}}}
proves
that the translation of the predecessor axiom
holds within
P
whenever
\mathrm{TPath}(P)
is assumed:

\mathrm{TPath}(P)\quad\Rightarrow\quad\forall x\in P\left[\exists y\in P(y\mathrel{\dot{<}}x)\penalty\ \penalty\ \Rightarrow\penalty\ \penalty\ \exists y\in P\left(y\mathrel{\dot{<}}x\penalty\ \land\penalty\ \lnot\exists z\in P(y\mathrel{\dot{<}}z\mathrel{\dot{<}}x)\right)\right]

This is handled by Lemma
76
.

We have now everything we need to prove (
17
).

Proposition 78
.

Consider a closed formula
\varphi
of
\mathsf{FSO}[\mathrel{\dot{<}}]^{\omega}
.
Then

{{\mathsf{FSO}_{\mathscr{D}}}}\vdash(\forall P:2)\left(\mathrm{TPath}(P)\penalty\ \penalty\ \Rightarrow\penalty\ \penalty\ \varphi^{P}\right)\qquad\text{whenever}\qquad\mathfrak{N}\models\varphi

Proof 7.2
.

Assume

\mathfrak{N}\models\varphi
.
By (
15
)
we have

\mathfrak{N}\models\langle{\varphi}\rangle
.
Theorem
74
then implies

\mathsf{MSO}[\mathrel{\dot{<}}]^{\omega}\vdash\langle{\varphi}\rangle
,
and by (
14
)
we have that

\mathsf{FSO}[\mathrel{\dot{<}}]^{\omega}\vdash\varphi
.
We conclude by Lemma
77
.

8.
Completeness

This Section is devoted to the proof of our main result,
the completeness of
\mathsf{FSO}+(\mathit{PosDet})
.

Theorem 79

(Main Theorem)
.

For each closed formula
\varphi
of
\mathsf{FSO}
,

\mathsf{FSO}+(\mathit{PosDet})\vdash\varphi\qquad\text{or}\qquad\mathsf{FSO}+(\mathit{PosDet})\vdash\lnot\varphi

8.1.
Overview

The two main ingredients of Theorem
79
are the following.

(1)

The translations

\langle-\rangle\penalty\ \penalty\ :\penalty\ \penalty\ \mathsf{FSO}\penalty\ \penalty\ \longrightarrow\penalty\ \penalty\ \mathsf{MSO}\qquad\text{and}\qquad(-)^{\circ}\penalty\ \penalty\ :\penalty\ \penalty\ \mathsf{MSO}\penalty\ \penalty\ \longrightarrow\penalty\ \penalty\ \mathsf{FSO}

providing faithful mutual interpretations of
\mathsf{FSO}
and
\mathsf{MSO}

(§
3.6
, recapitulated in Table
2
).

(2)

The translation of
\mathsf{MSO}
-formulae to automata, that we detail
in §
8.2
and §
8.3
below.
This translation relies on the correctness of the constructions
on automata of §
6
, which are recapitulated in Table
1
.
In particular, we require the Axiom
(\mathit{PosDet})

of positional determinacy of parity games (§
5.6
)
for the complementation of tree automata (Theorem
59
).

\begin{array}[]{l !{\quad\text{if and only if}\quad} l !{~~} !{{~~}} l}\hline\cr\hline\cr\lx@intercol\hfil\mathsf{FSO}\vdash\penalty\ \penalty\ \varphi\Longleftrightarrow\langle\varphi\rangle^{\circ}\hfil\lx@intercol&\text{Proposition\penalty\ \ref{prop:cons:mso:cons}, \eqref{eq:cons:mso:cons:trans}}\\
\mathsf{FSO}\vdash\varphi&\mathsf{MSO}\vdash\langle\varphi\rangle\hfil\lx@intercol\penalty\ \penalty\ \lx@intercol{\penalty\ \penalty\ &\text{Proposition\penalty\ \ref{prop:cons:mso:cons}, \eqref{eq:cons:mso:cons:fso:mso}}\\
\mathsf{FSO}\vdash\varphi^{\circ}&\mathsf{MSO}\vdash\varphi\hfil\lx@intercol\penalty\ \penalty\ \lx@intercol{\penalty\ \penalty\ &\text{Theorem\penalty\ \ref{thm:cons:mso:fso:mso}}\\
\mathfrak{T}\models\varphi^{\circ}&\mathfrak{T}\models\varphi\hfil\lx@intercol\penalty\ \penalty\ \lx@intercol{\penalty\ \penalty\ &\text{Lemma\penalty\ \ref{lem:cons:fso:mso:std}}\\
\hline\cr\hline\cr\end{array}}}}

Table 2.
3.6.
Mutual Interpretability of
\mathsf{FSO}
and
\mathsf{MSO}
(§
3.6
).

The mutual interpretability results of Table
2

also allows us to obtain a completeness result for
\mathsf{MSO}
.
Recall that
\langle\mathit{PosDet}\rangle
is defined in Definition
5.6.1
,
§
5.6.1
.
We then get the following corollary to Theorem
79
.

Corollary 80
.

For each closed formula
\varphi
of
\mathsf{MSO}
,

\mathsf{MSO}+\langle\mathit{PosDet}\rangle\vdash\varphi\qquad\text{or}\qquad\mathsf{MSO}+\langle\mathit{PosDet}\rangle\vdash\lnot\varphi

Proof 8.1
.

Consider a closed
\mathsf{MSO}
-formula
\varphi
.
Assume
\mathsf{FSO}+(\mathit{PosDet})\vdash\varphi^{\circ}
.
Let
\mathrm{PosDet}(\mathsf{P}_{i},\mathsf{O}_{i},n_{i})
(
i=1,\dots,k
)
be the instances of
(\mathit{PosDet})
used in the proof, so that

\mathsf{FSO}\vdash\quad\land_{1\leq i\leq k}\mathrm{PosDet}(\mathsf{P}_{i},\mathsf{O}_{i},n_{i})\penalty\ \penalty\ \Longrightarrow\penalty\ \penalty\ \varphi^{\circ}

By (
6
) (Proposition
20
),
we get

\mathsf{FSO}\vdash\quad\land_{1\leq i\leq k}\langle\mathrm{PosDet}(\mathsf{P}_{i},\mathsf{O}_{i},n_{i})\rangle^{\circ}\penalty\ \penalty\ \Longrightarrow\penalty\ \penalty\ \varphi^{\circ}

and since
(-)^{\circ}
commutes over propositional connectives,
by Theorem
19
we obtain

\mathsf{MSO}\vdash\quad\land_{1\leq i\leq k}\langle\mathrm{PosDet}(\mathsf{P}_{i},\mathsf{O}_{i},n_{i})\rangle\penalty\ \penalty\ \Longrightarrow\penalty\ \penalty\ \varphi

Moreover, since
\varphi^{\circ}
is
\mathrm{HF}
-closed, we can assume
the
\mathrm{HF}
-terms
\mathsf{P}_{i}
,
\mathsf{O}_{i}
and
n_{i}
to be closed.
It follows that there are constants for
\mathrm{HF}
-sets

\dot{\mathsf{\mathsf{P}}}_{i}
,
\dot{\mathsf{\mathsf{O}}}_{i}
and
\dot{\mathsf{n}}_{i}
(
i=1\dots,k
)
such that
each formula
\langle\mathrm{PosDet}(\mathsf{P}_{i},\mathsf{O}_{i},n_{i})\rangle

is syntactically identical to

\langle\mathrm{PosDet}(\dot{\mathsf{\mathsf{P}}}_{i},\dot{\mathsf{\mathsf{O}}}_{i},\dot{\mathsf{n}}_{i})\rangle
.
We thus obtain

\mathsf{MSO}\vdash\quad\land_{1\leq i\leq k}\langle\mathrm{PosDet}(\dot{\mathsf{\mathsf{P}}}_{i},\dot{\mathsf{\mathsf{O}}}_{i},\dot{\mathsf{n}}_{i})\rangle\penalty\ \penalty\ \Longrightarrow\penalty\ \penalty\ \varphi

which implies that
\mathsf{MSO}+\langle\mathit{PosDet}\rangle
proves
\varphi
.

If
\mathsf{FSO}+(\mathit{PosDet})
does not prove
\varphi^{\circ}
,
Theorem
79
gives

\mathsf{FSO}+(\mathit{PosDet})\vdash\lnot(\varphi^{\circ})

and we conclude similarly.

In particular, it follows from Proposition
46

that
\mathsf{FSO}+(\mathit{PosDet})
completely axiomatizes the standard
model
\mathfrak{T}
of
\mathscr{D}
-ary trees.

Corollary 81
.

•

For each closed formula
\varphi
of
\mathsf{FSO}
,

\mathfrak{T}\models\varphi\qquad\text{if and only if}\qquad\mathsf{FSO}+(\mathit{PosDet})\vdash\varphi

•

For each closed formula
\varphi
of
\mathsf{MSO}
,

\mathfrak{T}\models\varphi\qquad\text{if and only if}\qquad\mathsf{MSO}+\langle\mathit{PosDet}\rangle\vdash\varphi

Remark 82
.

Note that it follows from Remark
9

that Theorem
79
together with Corollary
81

implies the decidability of
\mathsf{FSO}
over its standard model
\mathfrak{T}
.
By Lemma
18
(see Table
2
)
we thus obtain a proof of Rabin’s Tree Theorem
[
Rab69
]
,
namely the decidability of
\mathsf{MSO}
over
\mathfrak{T}
.
However, even if provability in
\mathsf{FSO}
is semi-recursive,
the axiom set of
\mathsf{FSO}
is not recursive and the interpretation
of
\mathrm{HF}
-Functions is not computable
(see Remarks
9
and
10

in §
3.4.4
,
as well as Remark
16
in §
3.6.1
).
We further elaborate on this in §
8.5
.

We will actually deduce Theorem
79

via Proposition
20
,
(
6
)
(see Table
2
)
from the following.

Theorem 83
.

For each closed formula
\varphi
of
\mathsf{MSO}
,

\mathsf{FSO}+(\mathit{PosDet})\vdash\varphi^{\circ}\qquad\text{or}\qquad\mathsf{FSO}+(\mathit{PosDet})\vdash\lnot\varphi^{\circ}

The proof of Theorem
83

proceeds as expected via a translation of
\mathsf{MSO}
-formulae
to automata.
As usual, such translations are easier to define when one starts from a version
of
\mathsf{MSO}
with a purely relational and individual-free language.
We perform a translation of
\mathsf{MSO}
to such a language in §
8.2
.
Then, the translation of formulae to automata is presented in §
8.3
.
It relies on the constructions of §
6
.
We thus arrive at Proposition
87
,
namely that
for each closed formula
\varphi
of
\mathsf{MSO}

there is an
\mathrm{HF}
-closed parity automaton
\mathcal{A}
over the
singleton alphabet
1

such that

\mathsf{FSO}+(\mathit{PosDet})\vdash\quad\varphi^{\circ}\penalty\ \penalty\ \Longleftrightarrow\penalty\ \penalty\ (\exists F:1)\big(F\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A})\big)

In order to obtain Theorem
83
,
it remains to show that

\mathsf{FSO}
actually
decides
the emptiness of such automata:

\mathsf{FSO}\vdash(\exists F:1)\big(F\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A}))\qquad\text{or}\qquad\mathsf{FSO}\vdash\lnot(\exists F:1)\big(F\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A}))

This is Proposition
88
.
Its proof relies on the fact that the acceptance games of
(\mathcal{A}:1)

are actually generated from closed
\mathrm{HF}
-Sets.
We call such games
reduced parity games
.
Section
8.4
is devoted to defining reduced parity games
and to showing that
\mathsf{FSO}
decides winning for them
(Theorem
93
).
This essentially amounts to
a version of the Büchi-Landweber Theorem
[
BL69
]

(see also e.g.
[
Tho97
,
PP04
]
),
the effective determinacy
of parity games on finite graphs,
which is obtained thanks to the completeness of
\mathsf{FSO}[\mathrel{\dot{<}}]^{\omega}
(§
7
).
Theorem
93
then follows from the lifting of
\mathsf{FSO}[\mathrel{\dot{<}}]^{\omega}

to the paths of
\mathsf{FSO}
(Proposition
78
).

8.2.
Restricted Languages for
{\mathsf{MSO}_{\mathscr{D}}}

For the translation of formulae to automata, it is
useful and customary to work with formulae in a slightly different syntax,
based on a purely relational, individual-free vocabulary.

8.2.1.
Restriction to a Relational Language

We first restrict to a purely relational vocabulary,
based on the defined formulae

\mathrm{S}_{d}(x,y)\quad\mathrel{:=}\quad(\mathsf{S}_{d}(x)\mathrel{\doteq}y)

(for each
d\in\mathscr{D}
)

The
relational formulae

\varphi,\psi\in\bm{\Lambda}^{R}_{\mathscr{D}}

are built from atomic formulae
Xy
and
\mathrm{S}_{d}(x,y)

by means of
\lnot
,
\lor
,
\exists x
and
\exists X
.
To each
\mathsf{MSO}
-formula
\varphi\in\bm{\Lambda}

we associate a formula
\varphi^{R}
as follows.
For
t
a term of
\mathsf{MSO}
,
define the formula
(z\eqcirc t)
by structural induction on
t
:

\begin{array}[]{r !{\quad\deq\quad} l}(z\eqcirc y)\lx@intercol\quad\mathrel{:=}&(z\mathrel{\doteq}y)\\
(z\eqcirc\dot{\varepsilon})\lx@intercol\quad\mathrel{:=}&\lnot(\exists z^{\prime})\mathop{\bigvee}_{d\in\mathscr{D}}\mathrm{S}_{d}(z^{\prime},z)\\
(z\eqcirc\mathsf{S}_{d}(t))\lx@intercol\quad\mathrel{:=}&(\exists z^{\prime})\big(z^{\prime}\eqcirc t\land\mathrm{S}_{d}(z^{\prime},z)\big)\end{array}

Note that

\mathsf{MSO}\vdash\quad(z\eqcirc t)\penalty\ \penalty\ \Leftrightarrow\penalty\ \penalty\ (z\mathrel{\doteq}t)

Then,
\varphi^{R}
is obtained from
\varphi

by replacing each atomic formula
Xt
, where
t
is not a variable,
by
(\exists z)[(z\eqcirc t)\land Xz]
, where
z
is a fresh variable.

Lemma 84
.

For every
\mathsf{MSO}
-formula
\varphi
, we have

\mathsf{MSO}\vdash\varphi\Leftrightarrow\varphi^{R}
.

8.2.2.
Restriction to an Individual-Free Language

The next step is to get rid of individual quantifiers.
Consider the defined formulae:

\begin{array}[]{r !{\quad\deq\quad} l}(X\mathrel{\dot{\subseteq}}Y)\lx@intercol\quad\mathrel{:=}&(\forall x)(Xx\penalty\ \penalty\ \Rightarrow\penalty\ \penalty\ Yx)\\
\mathrm{S}_{d}(X,Y)\lx@intercol\quad\mathrel{:=}&(\exists x)(\exists y)\left[Xx\penalty\ \penalty\ \land\penalty\ \penalty\ Yy\penalty\ \penalty\ \land\penalty\ \penalty\ \mathrm{S}_{d}(x,y)\right]\end{array}

The
individual-free formulae

\varphi,\psi\in{\bm{\Lambda}}^{\textit{IF}}_{\mathscr{D}}

are built from atomic formulae

(X\mathrel{\dot{\subseteq}}Y)

and
\mathrm{S}_{d}(X,Y)

by means of negation, disjunction and

second-order monadic
quantification
\exists X
only.
Let
\varphi\in\bm{\Lambda}^{R}

with free variables among
x_{1},\dots,x_{p},Y_{1},\dots,Y_{q}
.
We inductively associate to
\varphi
a formula
{\varphi}^{\textit{IF}}\in{\bm{\Lambda}}^{\textit{IF}}

with free variables among

X_{1},\dots,X_{p},Y_{1},\dots,Y_{q}

as follows.
Let

{((\exists x_{p+1})\varphi)}^{\textit{IF}}\quad\mathrel{:=}\quad(\exists X_{p+1})\left[\mathrm{Sing}(X_{p+1})\penalty\ \penalty\ \land\penalty\ \penalty\ {\varphi}^{\textit{IF}}\right]

where

\begin{array}[]{r !{\quad\deq\quad} l}\mathrm{Sing}(X)\lx@intercol\quad\mathrel{:=}&\lnot(X\mathrel{\doteq}\varnothing)\penalty\ \penalty\ \land\penalty\ \penalty\ (\forall Y)\left[Y\mathrel{\dot{\subseteq}}X\penalty\ \penalty\ \Rightarrow\penalty\ \penalty\ \left(Y\mathrel{\doteq}\varnothing\penalty\ \penalty\ \lor\penalty\ \penalty\ X\mathrel{\dot{\subseteq}}Y\right)\right]\\
(X\mathrel{\doteq}\varnothing)\lx@intercol\quad\mathrel{:=}&(\forall Y)(X\mathrel{\dot{\subseteq}}Y)\end{array}

The other inductive cases are given as follows:

\begin{array}[]{r !{\quad\deq\quad}l !{\qquad} r !{\quad\deq\quad} l}{(Y_{j}(x_{i}))}^{\textit{IF}}\lx@intercol\quad\mathrel{:=}&X_{i}\mathrel{\dot{\subseteq}}Y_{j}&{(\mathrm{S}_{d}(x_{i},x_{j}))}^{\textit{IF}}\lx@intercol\quad\mathrel{:=}&\mathrm{S}_{d}(X_{i},X_{j})\\
{(\lnot\varphi)}^{\textit{IF}}\lx@intercol\quad\mathrel{:=}&\lnot{\varphi}^{\textit{IF}}&{(\varphi\lor\psi)}^{\textit{IF}}\lx@intercol\quad\mathrel{:=}&{\varphi}^{\textit{IF}}\penalty\ \lor\penalty\ {\psi}^{\textit{IF}}\\
{((\exists Y_{q+1})\varphi)}^{\textit{IF}}\lx@intercol\quad\mathrel{:=}&(\exists Y_{q+1}){\varphi}^{\textit{IF}}&\hfil\hfil\lx@intercol\end{array}

Lemma 85
.

For every formula
\varphi\in\bm{\Lambda}^{R}

with free variables among
\bm{x},\bm{Y}
,
we have

\bm{Xx}\,,\,\mathrm{Sing}(\bm{X})\vdash_{\mathsf{MSO}}\quad\varphi\penalty\ \penalty\ \Leftrightarrow\penalty\ \penalty\ {\varphi}^{\textit{IF}}

By composing the translations

(-)^{R}:\bm{\Lambda}\to\bm{\Lambda}^{R}

and

{(-)}^{\textit{IF}}:\bm{\Lambda}^{R}\to{\bm{\Lambda}}^{\textit{IF}}
,
we obtain:

Corollary 86
.

For every closed
\mathsf{MSO}
-formula
\varphi
,
there is a closed formula
\psi\in{\bm{\Lambda}}^{\textit{IF}}

such that
\mathsf{MSO}\vdash\varphi\Leftrightarrow\psi
.

8.3.
From Formulae to Automata

We are now going to associate to each formula
\varphi\in{\bm{\Lambda}}^{\textit{IF}}

with free variables among
X_{1},\dots,X_{p}

an
\mathrm{HF}
-closed parity automaton
\mathcal{A}(\varphi):2^{p}

such that

\mathsf{FSO}+(\mathit{PosDet})\vdash\quad(\forall F_{X_{1}}:2)\dots(\forall F_{X_{p}}:2)\Big({\langle F_{X_{1}},\dots,F_{X_{p}}\rangle}\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A}(\varphi))\penalty\ \penalty\ \Leftrightarrow\penalty\ \penalty\ \varphi^{\circ}\Big)

Note that the correctness of
\mathcal{A}(\varphi)
w.r.t.
\varphi

is proved in
\mathsf{FSO}
using the translation

(-)^{\circ}:\mathsf{MSO}\to\mathsf{FSO}

of §
3.6.2
.
Recall that
(-)^{\circ}
replaces each monadic variable
X_{i}
of
\varphi

by a Function variable
(F_{X_{i}}:2)
.
The construction of
\mathcal{A}(\varphi)
from
\varphi

is done by induction on
\varphi

using the operations on automata devised in §
6

(see Table
1
).
The base cases are provided by
the automata
\mathcal{A}(X_{i}\mathrel{\dot{\subseteq}}X_{j})
and
\mathcal{A}(\mathrm{S}_{d}(X_{i},X_{j}))

discussed in §
8.3.1
below
for the atomic formulae of
{\bm{\Lambda}}^{\textit{IF}}
.
The inductive cases are performed as follows,
where we implicitly apply substitutions (cf. §
6.2
)
when necessary:

\begin{array}[]{r !{\quad\deq\quad} l !{\qquad} l}\mathcal{A}(\varphi\lor\psi)\lx@intercol\quad\mathrel{:=}&\mathcal{A}(\varphi)\oplus\mathcal{A}(\psi)&\text{(Lemma\penalty\ \ref{lem:aut:disj})}\\
\mathcal{A}(\lnot\varphi)\lx@intercol\quad\mathrel{:=}&{\mathord{\sim}}\mathcal{A}(\varphi)&\text{(Theorem\penalty\ \ref{thm:neg})}\\
\mathcal{A}((\exists X_{p+1})\varphi)\lx@intercol\quad\mathrel{:=}&\exists_{2}\mathrm{ND}(\mathcal{A}(\varphi))&\text{(Proposition\penalty\ \ref{prop:aut:nd:proj} \penalty\ \&\penalty\ Theorem\penalty\ \ref{thm:aut:nd:sim})}\end{array}

In particular, if
\varphi
is closed then
\mathcal{A}(\varphi)

is an automaton over the singleton alphabet
1
,
whence by Corollary
86

we have:

Proposition 87
.

For each closed formula
\varphi
of
\mathsf{MSO}

there is an
\mathrm{HF}
-closed parity automaton
(\mathcal{A}:1)

such that

\mathsf{FSO}+(\mathit{PosDet})\vdash\quad\varphi^{\circ}\penalty\ \penalty\ \Leftrightarrow\penalty\ \penalty\ (\exists F:1)\big(F\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A})\big)

In order to obtain Theorem
83
from Proposition
87
,
it remains to show that
\mathsf{FSO}
actually
decides
the emptiness
of
\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A})
for an
\mathrm{HF}
-closed parity automaton
\mathcal{A}
over the singleton
alphabet
1
.

Proposition 88
.

Given an
\mathrm{HF}
-closed parity automaton
(\mathcal{A}:1)
,

\mathsf{FSO}\vdash(\exists F:1)\big(F\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A}))\qquad\text{or}\qquad\mathsf{FSO}\vdash\lnot(\exists F:1)\big(F\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A}))

Proposition
88
is proved in §
8.4
below.

8.3.1.
Automata for Atomic Formulae

We provide
\mathrm{HF}
-closed parity automata for the atomic formulae
(X_{1}\mathrel{\dot{\subseteq}}X_{2})

and
\mathrm{S}_{d}(X_{1},X_{2})
of the individual-free syntax

{\bm{\Lambda}}^{\textit{IF}}
of
\mathsf{MSO}
.

•

The automaton
\mathcal{A}(X_{1}\mathrel{\dot{\subseteq}}X_{2})
over
2\times 2

has state set
\mathbb{B}=\{\mathtt{tt},\mathtt{ff}\}
, with
\mathtt{tt}
initial,
transitions given by

\begin{array}[]{r !{\quad}c!{\quad} l !{\quad} l}(\mathtt{tt},(i,j))&\longmapsto&\{\{(d,\mathtt{ff})\penalty\ |\penalty\ d\in\mathscr{D}\}\}&\text{if $i=1$ and $j=0$}\\
(\mathtt{tt},(i,j))&\longmapsto&\{\{(d,\mathtt{tt})\penalty\ |\penalty\ d\in\mathscr{D}\}\}&\text{otherwise}\\
(\mathtt{ff},(-,-))&\longmapsto&\{\{(d,\mathtt{ff})\penalty\ |\penalty\ d\in\mathscr{D}\}\}\end{array}

and coloring
C:\mathbb{B}\mathrel{\mathfrak{to}}2

given by

C(\mathtt{tt})\quad\mathrel{:=}\quad 0\qquad\text{and}\qquad C(\mathtt{ff})\quad\mathrel{:=}\quad 1

•

For
d\in\mathscr{D}
,
the automaton
\mathcal{A}(\mathrm{S}_{d}(X_{1},X_{2}))
over
2\times 2

has state set
Q_{\mathrm{S}}\mathrel{:=}\mathbb{B}+\{\mathtt{w}\}
,
with
\mathtt{ff}
initial,
transitions given by

\begin{array}[]{r !{\quad}c!{\quad} l}(\mathtt{ff},(0,-))&\longmapsto&\{\{(d^{\prime},\mathtt{ff})\}\penalty\ |\penalty\ d^{\prime}\in\mathscr{D}\}\\
(\mathtt{ff},(1,-))&\longmapsto&\{\{(d,\mathtt{w})\}\}\\
(\mathtt{w},(-,1))&\longmapsto&\{\{(d^{\prime},\mathtt{tt})\penalty\ |\penalty\ d^{\prime}\in\mathscr{D}\}\}\\
(\mathtt{w},(-,0))&\longmapsto&\{\{(d^{\prime},\mathtt{ff})\}\penalty\ |\penalty\ d^{\prime}\in\mathscr{D}\}\\
(\mathtt{tt},(-,-))&\longmapsto&\{\{(d^{\prime},\mathtt{tt})\penalty\ |\penalty\ d^{\prime}\in\mathscr{D}\}\}\end{array}

and with coloring given by

C(\mathtt{tt})\quad\mathrel{:=}\quad 0\qquad\qquad C(\mathtt{ff})\quad\mathrel{:=}\quad 1\qquad\qquad C(\mathtt{w})\quad\mathrel{:=}\quad 0

Remark 89
.

Recall from §
8.2.2

that the formula
\mathrm{S}_{d}(X,Y)
of the individual-free syntax

{\bm{\Lambda}}^{\textit{IF}}
amounts in
\mathsf{MSO}
to the formula

(\exists x)(\exists y)\left[Xx\land Yy\land y\mathrel{\doteq}\mathsf{S}_{d}(x)\right]
.
So the automaton
\mathcal{A}(\mathrm{S}_{d}(X,Y))
only looks
for some

x\in X
and
y\in Y
such that
y
is the
d
-successor of
x
,
but is does not check whether
X
and
Y
are singletons.

Lemma 90
.

\mathsf{FSO}
proves that

\begin{array}[]{l @{} l !{~~\liff~~} l}(\forall F_{X_{1}}:2)(\forall F_{X_{2}}:2)\Big(&{\langle F_{X_{1}},F_{X_{2}}\rangle}\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A}(X_{1},X_{2}))\hfil\lx@intercol\penalty\ \penalty\ \Leftrightarrow\penalty\ \penalty\ &(X_{1}\mathrel{\dot{\subseteq}}X_{2})^{\circ}\Big)\\
(\forall F_{X_{1}}:2)(\forall F_{X_{2}}:2)\Big(&{\langle F_{X_{1}},F_{X_{2}}\rangle}\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A}(\mathrm{S}_{d}(X_{1},X_{2})))\hfil\lx@intercol\penalty\ \penalty\ \Leftrightarrow\penalty\ \penalty\ &(\mathrm{S}_{d}(X_{1},X_{2}))^{\circ}\Big)\end{array}

8.4.
Reduced Parity Games

The goal of this Section is to prove Proposition
88
,
namely that for
an
\mathrm{HF}
-closed parity automaton
\mathcal{A}

over the singleton alphabet
1
,

\mathsf{FSO}\vdash(\exists F:1)\big(F\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A}))\qquad\text{or}\qquad\mathsf{FSO}\vdash\lnot(\exists F:1)\big(F\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A}))

Consider an
\mathrm{HF}
-closed automaton
\mathcal{A}
over the singleton alphabet
1=\{0\}
.
Then for any
(F:1)

the game
\mathcal{G}\mathrel{:=}\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{A},F)
has edge relations induced by functions

e_{\mathsf{P}}:\mathsf{P}_{\mathcal{G}}\mathrel{\mathfrak{to}}\mathcal{P}_{*}(\mathsf{O}_{\mathcal{G}})\qquad\text{and}\qquad e_{\mathsf{O}}:\mathsf{O}_{\mathcal{G}}\mathrel{\mathfrak{to}}\mathcal{P}_{*}(\mathscr{D}\times\mathsf{P}_{\mathcal{G}})

(18)

given (following Remark
49
)
by

\begin{array}[]{l !{\quad\text{iff}\quad} l}(q^{\prime},\gamma)\mathrel{\dot{\in}}e_{\mathsf{P}}(q)&q^{\prime}\mathrel{\doteq}q\penalty\ \penalty\ \land\penalty\ \penalty\ \gamma\in\partial_{\mathcal{A}}(q,0)\\
(d,q^{\prime})\mathrel{\dot{\in}}e_{\mathsf{O}}(q,\gamma)&(d,q^{\prime})\mathrel{\dot{\in}}\gamma\end{array}

So in particular the edge relations of
\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{A},F)

are independent from
F
.
But also,
since

\mathsf{P}_{\mathcal{G}}\penalty\ \penalty\ \mathrel{:=}\penalty\ \penalty\ Q_{\mathcal{A}}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\mathsf{O}_{\mathcal{G}}\penalty\ \penalty\ \mathrel{:=}\penalty\ \penalty\ Q_{\mathcal{A}}\times\mathcal{P}_{*}(\mathscr{D}\times Q_{\mathcal{A}})

the whole game
\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{A},F)

is actually generated from
\mathrm{HF}
-Sets.

In this Section, we discuss games generated from
\mathrm{HF}
-Sets,
that we call
reduced games
.
We show that for reduced parity games, winning can actually be defined
within
\mathsf{FSO}[\mathrel{\dot{<}}]^{\omega}
.
Thanks to the completeness of
\mathsf{FSO}[\mathrel{\dot{<}}]^{\omega}
w.r.t. its standard model
(§
7
),
this implies that
\mathsf{FSO}[\mathrel{\dot{<}}]^{\omega}
itself decides winning in such games.
This essentially amounts to a version of the Büchi-Landweber Theorem
[
BL69
]

using the completeness of
\mathsf{MSO}[\mathrel{\dot{<}}]^{\omega}
over its standard model.
Using Proposition
78

we can then lift this result to
\mathsf{FSO}
.

In §
8.4.1
and §
8.4.2

we repeat some material of §
5
,
but for the slightly different setting of reduced games.
We then obtain that
\mathsf{FSO}[\mathrel{\dot{<}}]^{\omega}
decides winning in reduced parity games,
and we lift this to
\mathsf{FSO}
in Theorem
93
, §
8.4.3
.
This directly entails Proposition
88
.

8.4.1.
Reduced Games as
\mathrm{HF}
-Sets

The purpose of this Section is to give
adaptations of the notions of §
5

to those parity games which are entirely generated from
\mathrm{HF}
-Sets.
All the formulae of this Section are
\mathrm{HF}
-formulae
in the sense of Definition
3.4.4
.
Hence, thanks to the Axioms of
\mathrm{HF}
-Sets
(Remark
11
, §
3.4.4
)
their closed instances are provable (both in
\mathsf{FSO}
and
\mathsf{FSO}[\mathrel{\dot{<}}]^{\omega}
)
if and only if they hold in
V_{\omega}
.

{defi}

[Reduced Games]

A
reduced game

G

is given by
\mathrm{HF}
-terms
\mathsf{P},\mathsf{O},e_{\mathsf{P}},e_{\mathsf{O}}

which satisfy the following formula

\mathrm{Game}_{0}(\mathsf{P},\mathsf{O},e_{\mathsf{P}},e_{\mathsf{O}})\quad\mathrel{:=}\quad\Big(\mathrm{Labels}(\mathsf{P},\mathsf{O})\penalty\ \penalty\ \land\penalty\ \penalty\ e_{\mathsf{P}}:\mathsf{P}\mathrel{\mathfrak{to}}\mathcal{P}_{*}(\mathsf{O})\penalty\ \penalty\ \land\penalty\ \penalty\ e_{\mathsf{O}}:\mathsf{O}\mathrel{\mathfrak{to}}\mathcal{P}_{*}(\mathscr{D}\times\mathsf{P})\Big)

We often write
\mathrm{Game}_{0}(G)
for

\mathrm{Game}_{0}(\mathsf{P},\mathsf{O},e_{\mathsf{P}},e_{\mathsf{O}})
.
Moreover, when no ambiguity arises, we abbreviate

G=(\mathsf{P},\mathsf{O},e_{\mathsf{P}},e_{\mathsf{O}})
as

G=(\mathsf{P},\mathsf{O},e(G))
.

{defi}

[Reduced Subgame]

We say that
G^{\prime}=(\mathsf{P}^{\prime},\mathsf{O}^{\prime},e_{\mathsf{P}}^{\prime},e_{\mathsf{O}}^{\prime})

is a
reduced subgame
of
G=(\mathsf{P},\mathsf{O},e_{\mathsf{P}},e_{\mathsf{O}})
whenever the following
formula holds

\mathrm{Sub}_{0}(G^{\prime},G)\quad\mathrel{:=}\quad\left\{\begin{array}[]{l l}&\mathsf{P}^{\prime}\mathrel{\doteq}\mathsf{P}\penalty\ \penalty\ \land\penalty\ \penalty\ \mathsf{O}^{\prime}\mathrel{\doteq}\mathsf{O}\\
\land&(\forall k\in\mathsf{P}^{\prime})\big(e_{\mathsf{P}}^{\prime}(k)\mathrel{\dot{\subseteq}}e_{\mathsf{P}}(k)\big)\\
\land&(\forall\ell\in\mathsf{O}^{\prime})\big(e_{\mathsf{O}}^{\prime}(\ell)\mathrel{\dot{\subseteq}}e_{\mathsf{O}}(\ell)\big)\end{array}\right.

{defi}

[Reduced Strategies]

Let
G=(\mathsf{P},\mathsf{O},e_{\mathsf{P}},e_{\mathsf{O}})

where
\mathsf{P},\mathsf{O},e_{\mathsf{P}},e_{\mathsf{O}}
are
\mathrm{HF}
-variables.

(1)

A
reduced
\mathsf{P}
-strategy on
G

is an
\mathrm{HF}
-set
s

which satisfies the formula

\mathrm{Strat}^{0}_{\mathsf{P}}(G,s)\quad\mathrel{:=}\quad s:\mathsf{P}\mathrel{\mathfrak{to}}\mathsf{O}\penalty\ \penalty\ \land\penalty\ \penalty\ (\forall k\in\mathsf{P})\left(s(k)\in e_{\mathsf{P}}(k)\right)

(2)

A
reduced
\mathsf{O}
-strategy on
G
is an
\mathrm{HF}
-set

s

which satisfies the formula

\mathrm{Strat}^{0}_{\mathsf{O}}(G,s)\quad\mathrel{:=}\quad s:\mathsf{O}\mathrel{\mathfrak{to}}\mathscr{D}\times\mathsf{P}\penalty\ \penalty\ \land\penalty\ \penalty\ (\forall\ell\in\mathsf{O})\left(s(\ell)\in e_{\mathsf{O}}(\ell)\right)

{defi}

[Reduced Subgame induced by a Reduced Strategy]
Given a player
\mathsf{J}
(either
\mathsf{P}
or
\mathsf{O}
)
and a
\mathsf{J}
-strategy
s
on
G
,
we let

G\mathord{\upharpoonright}\{s\}_{\mathsf{J}}\quad\mathrel{:=}\quad\big(\mathsf{P}_{\mathcal{G}}\,,\,\mathsf{O}_{\mathcal{G}}\,,\,e(G)\mathord{\upharpoonright}\{\sigma\}_{\mathsf{J}}\big)

where

e(G)\mathord{\upharpoonright}\{\sigma\}_{\mathsf{P}}\quad\mathrel{:=}\quad\big(\{s\}_{\mathsf{P}},e(\mathcal{G})_{\mathsf{O}}\big)\qquad\text{and}\qquad e(G)\mathord{\upharpoonright}\{\sigma\}_{\mathsf{O}}\quad\mathrel{:=}\quad\big(e(\mathcal{G})_{\mathsf{P}},\{s\}_{\mathsf{O}}\big)

and where
\{s\}_{\mathsf{J}}\subseteq e_{\mathsf{J}}

is defined
(following the method of Remark
49
)
to be the function
taking
k\in G_{\mathsf{J}}

to the singleton
\{s(k)\}
.

Whenever possible, we write
G\mathord{\upharpoonright}\{s\}

or even just
s
for
G\mathord{\upharpoonright}\{s\}_{\mathsf{J}}
.

8.4.2.
Reduced Games in
\mathsf{FSO}[\mathrel{\dot{<}}]^{\omega}

In §
8.4.1
we gave notions
of reduced parity games and reduced strategies.
In this Section, we work within
\mathsf{FSO}[\mathrel{\dot{<}}]^{\omega}
and show
that this setting suffices to define
winning

for reduced parity games.
Thanks to the completeness of
\mathsf{FSO}[\mathrel{\dot{<}}]^{\omega}
w.r.t. the standard model of
\omega
-words
(§
7
),
we obtain that
\mathsf{FSO}[\mathrel{\dot{<}}]^{\omega}

decides
winning in such games.
This is essentially the Büchi-Landweber Theorem
[
BL69
]
.

We use the following
\mathsf{FSO}[\mathrel{\dot{<}}]^{\omega}
-formula:

\mathrm{S}(x,y)\quad\mathrel{:=}\quad x\mathrel{\dot{<}}y\penalty\ \penalty\ \land\penalty\ \penalty\ \lnot(\exists z)\big[x\mathrel{\dot{<}}z\mathrel{\dot{<}}y\big]

{defi}

[Infinite Plays in Reduced Games]

Working in
\mathsf{FSO}[\mathrel{\dot{<}}]^{\omega}
,
let
G=(\mathsf{P},\mathsf{O},e_{\mathsf{P}},e_{\mathsf{O}})
,
where
\mathsf{P},\mathsf{O},e_{\mathsf{P}},e_{\mathsf{O}}
are
\mathrm{HF}
-variables.
Given an
\mathrm{HF}
set
K
and a Function
(\tilde{V}:\mathsf{P})
,
we say that

\tilde{V}
is an
infinite play in
G
from
K
when the following
formula
\mathrm{Play}[\mathrel{\dot{<}}](G,K,\tilde{V})
holds:

\tilde{V}(\dot{\varepsilon})\mathrel{\doteq}K\penalty\ \penalty\ \land\penalty\ \penalty\ (\forall x)(\forall y)\Big(\mathrm{S}(x,y)\penalty\ \penalty\ \Rightarrow\penalty\ \penalty\ \big(\exists\ell\mathrel{\dot{\in}}e_{\mathsf{P}}(\tilde{V}(x))\big)\big(\exists d\mathrel{\dot{\in}}\mathscr{D}\big)\Big[(d,\tilde{V}(y))\in e_{\mathsf{O}}(\ell)\Big]\Big)

Note that in Definition
8.4.2
above,
we use the notation
\tilde{V}
for a play
in a reduced games,
to mark the difference with the notion of
plays (and more generally sets of game positions)
in the setting of §
4
.

{defi}

[Parity Conditions for Reduced Games]

Working in
\mathsf{FSO}[\mathrel{\dot{<}}]^{\omega}
,
let
G=(\mathsf{P},\mathsf{O},e_{\mathsf{P}},e_{\mathsf{O}})
,
where
\mathsf{P},\mathsf{O},e_{\mathsf{P}},e_{\mathsf{O}}
are
\mathrm{HF}
-variables.

(1)

A
coloring
is given by Function
C
and an
\mathrm{HF}
-set
n

satisfying the following formula

\mathrm{Col}_{0}(G,C,n)\quad\mathrel{:=}\quad\mathrm{Ord}(n)\penalty\ \penalty\ \land\penalty\ \penalty\ C:\mathsf{P}\mathrel{\mathfrak{to}}[0,n]

(2)

We define the following formula:

\mathrm{Par}[\mathrel{\dot{<}}](C,n,\tilde{V})\quad\mathrel{:=}\quad\big(\exists m\in\dot{\mathsf{even}}(n)\big)\left[\begin{array}[]{r l}&(\forall x)(\exists y)\big(x\mathrel{\dot{<}}y\penalty\ \penalty\ \land\penalty\ \penalty\ C(\tilde{V}(y))\mathrel{\doteq}m\big)\\
\land&(\exists x)(\forall y)\big(x\mathrel{\dot{<}}y\penalty\ \penalty\ \Rightarrow\penalty\ \penalty\ C(\tilde{V}(y))\geq m\big)\end{array}\right]

{defi}

[Winning of Reduced Parity Games]

Working in
\mathsf{FSO}[\mathrel{\dot{<}}]^{\omega}
,
let
G=(\mathsf{P},\mathsf{O},e_{\mathsf{P}},e_{\mathsf{O}})
,
where
\mathsf{P},\mathsf{O},e_{\mathsf{P}},e_{\mathsf{O}}
are
\mathrm{HF}
-variables.
Furthermore let
C
be a Function variable and
n
be an
\mathrm{HF}
-variable.

(1)

We define the following formulae.

\begin{array}[]{r !{\quad\deq\quad} l}\mathrm{WonGame}[\mathrel{\dot{<}}]_{\mathsf{P}}(G,K,C,n)\lx@intercol\quad\mathrel{:=}&(\forall\tilde{V}:\mathsf{P})\big(\mathrm{Play}[\mathrel{\dot{<}}](G,K,\tilde{V})\penalty\ \penalty\ \Rightarrow\penalty\ \penalty\ \mathrm{Par}[\mathrel{\dot{<}}](C,n,\tilde{V})\big)\\
\mathrm{WonGame}[\mathrel{\dot{<}}]_{\mathsf{O}}(G,K,C,n)\lx@intercol\quad\mathrel{:=}&(\forall\tilde{V}:\mathsf{P})\big(\mathrm{Play}[\mathrel{\dot{<}}](G,K,\tilde{V})\penalty\ \penalty\ \Rightarrow\penalty\ \penalty\ \lnot\mathrm{Par}[\mathrel{\dot{<}}](C,n,\tilde{V})\big)\end{array}

(2)

Given a player
\mathsf{J}
(either
\mathsf{P}
or
\mathsf{O}
),
we say that a
\mathsf{J}
-strategy
s
is

winning in
(G,C,n)
from
K

if the game
(G\{s\}_{\mathsf{J}},\mathrm{Par}[\mathrel{\dot{<}}](C,n,-))
is won by
\mathsf{J}
from
K
,

i.e.
if
the following formula holds

\mathrm{WinStrat}[\mathrel{\dot{<}}]_{\mathsf{J}}(G,s,K,C,n)\quad\mathrel{:=}\quad\mathrm{WonGame}[\mathrel{\dot{<}}]_{\mathsf{J}}(G\mathord{\upharpoonright}\{s\}_{\mathsf{J}},K,C,n)

Note that in Definition
8.4.2
,
we have denoted strategies in reduced games with a lower case roman
s
.
This notation contrasts with our notation
\sigma
for games
in the sense of §
5
in order to insist on the fact that
strategies on reduced games are
\mathrm{HF}
-sets.

Consider now
G=(\mathsf{P},\mathsf{O},e_{\mathsf{P}},e_{\mathsf{O}})

where
\mathsf{P}
,
\mathsf{O}
,
e_{\mathsf{P}}
and
e_{\mathsf{O}}
are closed
\mathrm{HF}
-terms
such that

V_{\omega}\models\mathrm{Game}_{0}(G)

Assume also given closed
\mathrm{HF}
-terms
n
and
C

such that

V_{\omega}\models\mathrm{Col}_{0}(G,C,n)

Then the positional determinacy of parity games (cf.
[
EJ91
]
)
implies
that for every
\mathrm{HF}
-set
\kappa\in\mathsf{P}
,
the following holds in the standard model
\mathfrak{N}

of
\mathsf{FSO}[\mathrel{\dot{<}}]^{\omega}
:

•

For some player
\mathsf{J}
(either
\mathsf{P}
or
\mathsf{O}
)
there an
\mathrm{HF}
-set
\mathtt{s}
such that

\mathfrak{N}\models\quad\mathrm{Strat}^{0}_{\mathsf{J}}(G,\mathtt{s})\penalty\ \penalty\ \land\penalty\ \penalty\ \mathrm{WinStrat}[\mathrel{\dot{<}}]_{\mathsf{J}}(G,\mathtt{s},\kappa,C,n)

Thanks to the completeness of
\mathsf{FSO}[\mathrel{\dot{<}}]^{\omega}
w.r.t.
\mathfrak{N}

(Theorem
74
and Proposition
75
),
we obtain the following result,
that may be viewed as a formulation of the Büchi-Landweber Theorem
[
BL69
]

(see also e.g.
[
Tho97
,
PP04
]
).
Recall that
\mathrm{Strat}^{0}_{\mathsf{J}}(G,\mathtt{s})
holds in

\mathfrak{N}
(resp.
\mathsf{FSO}[\mathrel{\dot{<}}]^{\omega}
,
\mathsf{FSO}
) if and only if it holds
in
V_{\omega}
.

Proposition 91
.

Assume given closed
\mathrm{HF}
-terms
G=(\mathsf{P},\mathsf{O},e_{\mathsf{P}},e_{\mathsf{O}})
,
n
and
C
such that

V_{\omega}\models\quad\mathrm{Game}_{0}(G)\penalty\ \penalty\ \land\penalty\ \mathrm{Col}_{0}(G,C,n)

Then for every
\kappa\in\mathsf{P}
,
there is a player
\mathsf{J}
(either
\mathsf{P}
or
\mathsf{O}
)
and an
\mathrm{HF}
-set
\mathtt{s}
such that

\mathsf{FSO}[\mathrel{\dot{<}}]^{\omega}\vdash\quad\mathrm{WinStrat}[\mathrel{\dot{<}}]_{\mathsf{J}}(G,\mathtt{s},\kappa,C,n)

Proposition
78
,
namely

{{\mathsf{FSO}_{\mathscr{D}}}}\vdash(\forall P:2)\left(\mathrm{TPath}(P)\penalty\ \penalty\ \Rightarrow\penalty\ \penalty\ \varphi^{P}\right)\qquad\text{whenever}\qquad\mathfrak{N}\models\varphi

(for
\varphi
a closed
\mathsf{FSO}[\mathrel{\dot{<}}]^{\omega}
-formula)
moreover gives the following.

Proposition 92
.

Assume given closed
\mathrm{HF}
-terms
G=(\mathsf{P},\mathsf{O},e_{\mathsf{P}},e_{\mathsf{O}})
,
n
and
C
such that

V_{\omega}\models\quad\mathrm{Game}_{0}(G)\penalty\ \penalty\ \land\penalty\ \mathrm{Col}_{0}(G,C,n)

Then for every
\kappa\in\mathsf{P}
,
there is a player
\mathsf{J}
(either
\mathsf{P}
or
\mathsf{O}
)
and an
\mathrm{HF}
-set
\mathtt{s}
such that

\mathsf{FSO}\vdash\quad(\forall X:2)\Big(\mathrm{TPath}(X)\penalty\ \penalty\ \Rightarrow\penalty\ \penalty\ \mathrm{WinStrat}[\mathrel{\dot{<}}]^{X}_{\mathsf{J}}(G,\mathtt{s},\kappa,C,n)\Big)

8.4.3.
Reduced Games in
\mathsf{FSO}

We now come back to
\mathsf{FSO}
.
In this Section, we show, using Proposition
92
,
that
\mathsf{FSO}
decides winning for parity games
induced from reduced parity games
(Theorem
93
).
This directly gives Proposition
88
.

A reduced game
G=(\mathsf{P},\mathsf{O},e_{\mathsf{P}},e_{\mathsf{O}})

induces a game
\mathcal{G}=(\mathsf{P},\mathsf{O},E_{\mathsf{P}},E_{\mathsf{O}})
in the sense
of Definition
5.1
,
where

E_{\mathsf{P}}:\mathscr{D}^{*}\times\mathsf{P}\mathrel{\mathfrak{to}}\mathcal{P}_{*}(\mathsf{O})\qquad\text{and}\qquad E_{\mathsf{O}}:\mathscr{D}^{*}\times\mathsf{O}\mathrel{\mathfrak{to}}\mathcal{P}_{*}(\mathscr{D}\times\mathsf{P})

are defined using

\mathrm{HF}
-Bounded Choice for Product Types (Theorem
22
)
as

E_{\mathsf{P}}(x,k)\quad\mathrel{:=}\quad e_{\mathsf{P}}(k)\qquad\text{and}\qquad E_{\mathsf{O}}(x,\ell)\quad\mathrel{:=}\quad e_{\mathsf{O}}(\ell)

Similarly, a strategy
s
in a reduced game
G
induces
a strategy
\sigma
in
\mathcal{G}
in the sense of Definition
5.3
,
with

\sigma(x,k)\quad\mathrel{:=}\quad s(k)

As for colorings, from
(C:\mathsf{P}\mathrel{\mathfrak{to}}[0,n])
we define

\hat{C}:\mathcal{G}\mathrel{\mathfrak{to}}[0,n]
as in Definition
6.1
:

\hat{C}(x,k)\quad\mathrel{:=}\quad\left\{\begin{array}[]{l !{\quad} l !{\qquad} l}C(k)&\text{if $k\in\mathsf{P}$}&\text{($\mathsf{P}$-position)}\\
n_{\mathcal{A}}&\text{if $k\in\mathsf{O}$}&\text{($\mathsf{O}$-position)}\end{array}\right.

We clearly have the following:

\begin{array}[]{l !{\qquad\text{whenever}\qquad} l}\mathsf{FSO}\vdash\mathrm{Game}(\mathcal{G})&V_{\omega}\models\mathrm{Game}_{0}(G)\\
\mathsf{FSO}\vdash\mathrm{Strat}_{\mathsf{J}}(\mathcal{G},\sigma)&V_{\omega}\models\mathrm{Strat}^{0}_{\mathsf{J}}(G,s)\\
\mathsf{FSO}\vdash\mathrm{Col}(\mathcal{G},\hat{C},n)&V_{\omega}\models\mathrm{Col}_{0}(G,C,n)\end{array}

Theorem 93
.

Assume given closed
\mathrm{HF}
-terms
G=(\mathsf{P},\mathsf{O},e_{\mathsf{P}},e_{\mathsf{O}})
,
n
and
C
such that

V_{\omega}\models\quad\mathrm{Game}_{0}(G)\penalty\ \penalty\ \land\penalty\ \mathrm{Col}_{0}(G,C,n)

Then for every
\kappa\in\mathsf{P}
,

\begin{array}[]{r !{\qquad} l r l}\text{either}&\mathsf{FSO}\vdash&(\exists\sigma_{\mathsf{P}}:\mathcal{G}_{\mathsf{P}}\mathrel{\mathfrak{to}}\mathsf{O})&\left(\begin{array}[]{c l}&\mathrm{Strat}_{\mathsf{P}}(\mathcal{G},\sigma_{\mathsf{P}})\\
\land&\mathrm{WinStrat}_{\mathsf{P}}(\mathcal{G}(\trianglelefteq),\sigma_{\mathsf{P}},\kappa,C,n)\end{array}\right)\\
\\
\text{or}&\mathsf{FSO}\vdash&(\exists\sigma_{\mathsf{O}}:\mathcal{G}_{\mathsf{O}}\mathrel{\mathfrak{to}}\mathscr{D}\times\mathsf{P})&\left(\begin{array}[]{c l}&\mathrm{Strat}_{\mathsf{O}}(\mathcal{G},\sigma_{\mathsf{O}})\\
\land&\mathrm{WinStrat}_{\mathsf{O}}(\mathcal{G}(\trianglelefteq),\sigma_{\mathsf{O}},\kappa,C,n)\end{array}\right)\end{array}

In the statement of Theorem
93
,

\mathcal{G}(\trianglelefteq)
refers to the the game of Remark
32

(see also Remark
45
).

Remark 94
.

The crucial differences between Theorem
93

and the axiom
(\mathit{PosDet})

are the following.
On one hand, Theorem
93

allows us to derive
(\mathit{PosDet})
for games on
finite
graphs only,
while
(\mathit{PosDet})
speaks about arbitrary
\mathsf{FSO}
-definable games
(in the sense of §
5
).
On the other hand,
Theorem
93

says that
\mathsf{FSO}

decides
winning for games on finite graphs,
while
(\mathit{PosDet})
is a statement of
determinacy
, i.e. that one of the players wins,
but not
which
player wins.

Proof 8.2

(Proof of Theorem
93
)
.

Fix
G
,
n
,
C
and
\kappa
as in the statement.
Let
\mathsf{J}
and
\mathtt{s}
be given by Proposition
92
,
and let
\sigma
be induced from
\mathtt{s}
as above.
We are going to show that
\sigma
is winning in
\mathcal{G}
from position
\kappa
:

(\forall V:\mathcal{G}\mathrel{\mathfrak{to}}2)\Big(\mathrm{Play}(\sigma,\kappa,V)\penalty\ \penalty\ \Rightarrow\penalty\ \penalty\ \mathrm{Par}(\mathcal{G},\hat{C},n,V)\Big)

So let
V:\mathcal{G}\mathrel{\mathfrak{to}}2
be an infinite play of
\sigma
from
\kappa
.
Our plan is to obtain
\mathrm{Par}(\mathcal{G},\hat{C},n,V)

from Proposition
92
.
By Comprehension for Product Types (Theorem
23
),
let
|V|:\mathscr{D}^{*}\mathrel{\mathfrak{to}}2

be the set of all
x\in\mathscr{D}^{*}
such that

(x,k)\in V
for some
k\in\mathsf{P}
.
Note that
\mathrm{TPath}(|V|)
holds in
\mathsf{FSO}
.
Proposition
92

then gives

\mathsf{FSO}\vdash\quad\mathrm{WinStrat}[\mathrel{\dot{<}}]^{|V|}_{\mathsf{J}}(G,\mathtt{s},\kappa,C,n)

Note that

\mathrm{WinStrat}[\mathrel{\dot{<}}]^{|V|}_{\mathsf{P}}(G,\mathtt{s},\kappa,C,n)\penalty\ \penalty\ \Longleftrightarrow\\
(\forall\tilde{V}:|V|\mathrel{\mathfrak{to}}\mathsf{P})\Big(\mathrm{Play}[\mathrel{\dot{<}}]^{|V|}(\mathtt{s},\kappa,\tilde{V})\penalty\ \penalty\ \Rightarrow\penalty\ \penalty\ \mathrm{Par}[\mathrel{\dot{<}}]^{|V|}(C,n,\tilde{V})\Big)

and similarly for

\mathrm{WinStrat}[\mathrel{\dot{<}}]^{|V|}_{\mathsf{O}}(G,\mathtt{s},\kappa,C,n)
.
By
\mathrm{HF}
-Bounded Choice for Functions (§
3.4.5
),
let
\tilde{V}:|V|\mathrel{\mathfrak{to}}\mathsf{P}

take
x\in|V|
to the unique
k\in\mathsf{P}

such that
(x,k)\in V
.
Then we are done as soon as we show

Claim 95
.

\mathrm{Play}[\mathrel{\dot{<}}]^{|V|}(\mathtt{s},\kappa,\tilde{V})\quad\land\quad\Big(\mathrm{Par}(\mathcal{G},\hat{C},n,V)\penalty\ \penalty\ \Leftrightarrow\penalty\ \penalty\ \mathrm{Par}[\mathrel{\dot{<}}]^{|V|}(C,n,\tilde{V})\Big)

Proof 8.3

(Proof of Claim 95)
.

The property on parity conditions follows from the fact that
for all
m\in[0,n]
we have

\begin{array}[]{l @{\quad} l}&\left[\begin{array}[]{r l}&(\forall x\in|V|)(\exists y\in|V|)\big(x\mathrel{\dot{<}}y\penalty\ \penalty\ \land\penalty\ \penalty\ C(\tilde{V}(y))\mathrel{\doteq}m\big)\\
\land&(\exists x\in|V|)(\forall y\in|V|)\big(x\mathrel{\dot{<}}y\penalty\ \penalty\ \Rightarrow\penalty\ \penalty\ C(\tilde{V}(y))\geq m\big)\end{array}\right]\\
\Longleftrightarrow\\
&\left[\begin{array}[]{r l}&(\forall u\in V)(\exists v\in V)(u\vartriangleleft v\penalty\ \land\penalty\ \hat{C}(v)\mathrel{\doteq}m)\\
\land&(\exists u\in V)(\forall v\in V)(u\vartriangleleft v\penalty\ \Rightarrow\penalty\ \hat{C}(v)\geq m)\end{array}\right]\end{array}

As for
\mathrm{Play}[\mathrel{\dot{<}}]^{|V|}(\mathtt{s},\kappa,\tilde{V})
,
note that it unfolds to

\begin{array}[]{l}\tilde{V}(\dot{\varepsilon})\mathrel{\doteq}\kappa\quad\land\\
(\forall x\in|V|)(\forall y\in|V|)\Big(\mathrm{S}^{|V|}(x,y)\penalty\ \penalty\ \Rightarrow\penalty\ \penalty\ \big(\exists\ell\mathrel{\dot{\in}}e(\mathtt{s})_{\mathsf{P}}(\tilde{V}(x))\big)\big(\exists d\mathrel{\dot{\in}}\mathscr{D}\big)\Big[(d,\tilde{V}(y))\in e(\mathtt{s})_{\mathsf{O}}(\ell)\Big]\Big)\end{array}

where

\mathrm{S}^{|V|}(x,y)\quad=\quad(x\mathrel{\dot{<}}y)\penalty\ \penalty\ \land\penalty\ \penalty\ \lnot(\exists z\in|V|)\big[x\mathrel{\dot{<}}z\mathrel{\dot{<}}y\big]

But this directly follows from the definition of
\sigma
from
\mathtt{s}

together with the fact that
V
is a play of
\sigma
from
\kappa
.

This concludes the proof of Theorem
93
.

We are now ready to prove Proposition
88
,
thus completing the proof of Theorem
83
.

Proof 8.4

(Proof of Proposition
88
)
.

We have to show that for
an
\mathrm{HF}
-closed parity automaton
(\mathcal{A}:1)
,

\mathsf{FSO}\vdash(\exists F:1)\big(F\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A}))\qquad\text{or}\qquad\mathsf{FSO}\vdash\lnot(\exists F:1)\big(F\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A}))

For any
(F:1)
,
the game
\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{A},F)
is generated as above from the edge
relations (
18
).
Moreover, recall from Definition
6.1

that the winning condition of
\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{A},F)

is generated, as in the statement of Theorem
93
,
by the game
\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{A},F)(\trianglelefteq)
of Remark
32
.
We then conclude by Theorem
93
,
and this completes the proof of Proposition
88
.

8.5.
Remarks on Recursiveness

We noted in Remark
82

that
the completeness of
\mathsf{FSO}+(\mathit{PosDet})
indeed allows us
to decide
\mathsf{FSO}
and
\mathsf{MSO}
formulae in the standard model
\mathfrak{T}
of §
3.5
.
This however comes with two apparent defects.
The first one is that the interpretation
\llbracket-\rrbracket
of
\mathrm{HF}
-terms fixed
in Convention
3.4.4
is not computable
(see Remarks
10
and
15
),
because provability in
\mathbf{Sk}(\mathsf{ZFC}^{-})
is not decidable
(as this theory contains the
\Pi^{0}_{1}
fragment of arithmetic).
The second one is that, although the axiom set
\mathsf{MSO}+\langle\mathit{PosDet}\rangle

is even polynomial-time recognizable (recall that
\langle\mathit{PosDet}\rangle
is defined in
Definition
5.6.1
, §
5.6.1
),
the interpretation
\langle-\rangle
for
\mathrm{HF}
-terms relies on Convention
3.4.4

(fixing the interpretation of
\mathrm{HF}
-Functions),
and is thus not computable.
We discuss here a workaround for this involving a slightly different setting for
\mathsf{FSO}
.
We chose to not officially work in that setting because we found it less uniform
and elegant than the current presentation of
\mathsf{FSO}
,
which nonetheless still allows us to derive Rabin’s Tree Theorem
[
Rab69
]
.

Rather than taking all the axioms on
\mathrm{HF}
-sets
of §
3.4.4
, in particular considering
the whole theory
\mathbf{Sk}(\mathsf{ZFC}^{-})
there, we may work in systems
parametrized by chosen sets of
\mathrm{HF}
-Functions.
A way to implement this would be to consider systems
\mathsf{FSO}(\mathbf{SK})
,
where the parameter
\mathbf{SK}
specifies
some
interpretations

\mathtt{g}_{n,m}
for constants
\dot{\mathsf{g}}_{n,m}

such that (
2
) is assumed to hold.
Concretely, a specification
\mathbf{SK}
consists of a set
\textrm{SK}\subseteq\mathbb{N}\times\mathbb{N}

together with functions

\mathtt{g}_{n,m}\penalty\ \penalty\ :\penalty\ \penalty\ V^{n}_{\omega}\penalty\ \penalty\ \longrightarrow\penalty\ \penalty\ V_{\omega}

(for each
(n,m)\in\textrm{SK}
)

Given a set
\textrm{SK}\subseteq\mathbb{N}\times\mathbb{N}
,
we let
\mathsf{ZFC}^{-}(\textrm{SK})
consist of
\mathsf{ZFC}^{-}
augmented with
the axioms

(\forall k_{1},\dots,k_{n})(\exists!\ell)(\varphi_{n,m})\penalty\ \penalty\ \Rightarrow\penalty\ \penalty\ (\forall k_{1},\dots,k_{n})\varphi_{n,m}[\dot{\mathsf{g}}_{n,m}(k_{1},\dots,k_{n})/\ell]

(for each
(n,m)\in\textrm{SK}
)

We say that
\mathbf{SK}
is a
specification
if

\mathbf{SK}\quad=\quad(\textrm{SK},(\mathtt{g}_{n,m})_{(n,m)\in\textrm{SK}})

where,
for each
(n,m)\in\textrm{SK}
,

•

\mathtt{g}_{n,m}

is a computable function
V^{n}_{\omega}\to V_{\omega}
, and

•

for each each
\dot{\mathsf{g}}_{n^{\prime},m^{\prime}}
occurring in
\varphi_{n,m}
,
we have
(n^{\prime},m^{\prime})\in\textrm{SK}
, and

•

\mathsf{ZFC}^{-}(\textrm{SK})\vdash(\forall k_{1},\dots,k_{n})(\exists!\ell)\varphi_{n,m}
,
and

•

V_{\omega}\models(\forall k_{1},\dots,k_{n})\varphi_{n,m}[\mathtt{g}_{n,m}(k_{1},\dots,k_{n})/\ell]

Given a specification
\mathbf{SK}
, one can fix the interpretation
of all constants
(\dot{\mathsf{g}}_{n,m})_{n,m\in\mathbb{N}}

by taking for
\dot{\mathsf{g}}_{n,m}
with
(n,m)\notin\textrm{SK}

the function
V^{n}_{\omega}\to V_{\omega}

with constant value
\varnothing
.

For the formal definition of
\mathsf{FSO}(\mathbf{SK})
,
instead of the Axioms on
\mathrm{HF}
-Sets of §
3.4.4
, one has the following.

•

For each
(n,m)\in\textrm{SK}
, and for all
\mathrm{HF}
-terms
\bm{K}=K_{1},\dots,K_{n}
,
the axiom

\varphi_{n,m}[\bm{K}/\bm{k}][\dot{\mathsf{g}}_{n,m}(\bm{K})/\ell]

•

For each closed
\mathrm{HF}
-formula
\varphi
such that
V_{\omega}\models\varphi
,
the axiom

\varphi

Given a specification
\mathbf{SK}
, the interpretations
\llbracket-\rrbracket

and
\langle-\rangle
are computable.
All results of this paper
hold for sufficiently large specifications.

Theorem 96
.

Let
\mathbf{SK}
be a specification defining all the
\mathrm{HF}
-Functions
of
1
–
8
, §
3.4.4
,
as well as those of Convention
5.5
,
§
5.5
.
Then all the results stated in §
8

hold for
\mathsf{FSO}(\mathbf{SK})
instead of
\mathsf{FSO}
.

9.
The Simulation Theorem

This Section is devoted to the proof of the

Simulation Theorem
, cf.
[
EJ91
,
MS95
]
.

Theorem 97

(Simulation Theorem
56
)
.

For each
\mathrm{HF}
-closed parity automaton
\mathcal{A}:\Sigma

there is a non-deterministic
\mathrm{HF}
-closed parity automaton

\mathrm{ND}(\mathcal{A}):\Sigma

such that

\mathsf{FSO}\vdash\quad\mathcal{L}(\mathrm{ND}(\mathcal{A}))=\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A})

We assume that
\mathcal{A}
is
\mathrm{HF}
-closed in Theorem
97

because we rely on McNaughton’s Theorem
[
McN66
]
,
in the standard model for
\omega
-words,
which we import into
\mathsf{FSO}
thanks to Proposition
78
.

Before a detailed exposition, let us explain the main idea
behind Theorem
97
.
We momentarily work in the usual mathematical universe
(
i.e.
not in the formal theory
\mathsf{FSO}
).
Recall that in a non-deterministic automaton
\mathcal{N}
,
\mathsf{O}
can only explicitly choose
tree directions, since for each possible
\gamma_{\mathcal{N}}
in the image of
\partial_{\mathcal{N}}
,
if
(d,q),(d,q^{\prime})\in\gamma_{\mathcal{N}}
then
q=q^{\prime}
, by definition.
In order to obtain a non-deterministic automaton
\mathcal{N}

from an alternating automaton
\mathcal{A}
,
the idea is to perform a subset construction,
such that each
\gamma_{\mathcal{N}}
in the image of
\partial_{\mathcal{N}}
is of the form

\gamma_{\mathcal{N}}\quad=\quad\{(d,S^{\prime}_{d})\penalty\ |\penalty\ d\in\mathscr{D}\}

where each
S^{\prime}_{d}
gathers states
q
such that
(d,q)\in\gamma_{\mathcal{A}}

with
\gamma_{\mathcal{A}}
in the image of
\partial_{\mathcal{A}}
.

More precisely, assuming
S\in\mathcal{P}_{*}(Q_{\mathcal{A}})
, one may consider functions

\begin{array}[]{c !{~~}c!{~~} c !{~~}c!{~~} l}f\hfil\lx@intercol\penalty\ \penalty\ &:\hfil\lx@intercol\penalty\ \penalty\ &S\hfil\lx@intercol\penalty\ \penalty\ &\longrightarrow\hfil\lx@intercol\penalty\ \penalty\ &\mathcal{P}_{*}(\mathscr{D}\times Q_{\mathcal{A}})\\
\hfil\lx@intercol\penalty\ \penalty\ &\hfil\lx@intercol\penalty\ \penalty\ &q\hfil\lx@intercol\penalty\ \penalty\ &\longmapsto\hfil\lx@intercol\penalty\ \penalty\ &\gamma_{q}\in\partial_{\mathcal{A}}(q,\mathtt{a})\end{array}

(19)

Each such
f
induces

\gamma_{\mathcal{N}}(f)=\{(d,S^{\prime}_{d}(f))\penalty\ |\penalty\ d\in\mathscr{D}\}\quad\text{where}\quad S^{\prime}_{d}(f)=\{q\penalty\ |\penalty\ (d,q)\in f(q)\}

and we can let

\partial_{\mathcal{N}}(S,\mathtt{a})\quad\mathrel{:=}\quad\{\gamma_{\mathcal{N}}(f)\penalty\ |\penalty\ \text{$f$ is as in\penalty\ \eqref{eq:sim:f}}\}

Then, for each
\gamma_{\mathcal{N}}(f)
in the image of
\partial_{\mathcal{N}}

and for each tree direction
d\in\mathscr{D}
, the set
S^{\prime}_{d}

is unique such that
(d,S^{\prime}_{d})\in\gamma_{\mathcal{N}}(f)
,
and
\mathcal{N}
satisfies the property asked in Definition
6.4

to non-deterministic automata.

There is however a difficulty in the definition
of the acceptance condition of
\mathcal{N}
.
We follow here the construction of
[
Wal02
]

where the states of
\mathcal{N}
, rather than being simply sets of states, are
sets of pairs of states
S\in\mathcal{P}(Q_{\mathcal{A}}\times Q_{\mathcal{A}})
.
Then an infinite sequence of states
S_{0},S_{1},\ldots\in Q_{\mathcal{N}}

induces a set of
traces

q_{0},q_{1},\ldots\in Q_{\mathcal{A}}

with
(q_{i},q_{i+1})\in S_{i+1}
.
For
(S_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\in Q_{\mathcal{N}}^{\omega}
to be accepting, one may then require
all its traces
(q_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\in Q_{\mathcal{A}}^{\omega}

to be accepting.
We may obtain a parity condition for
\mathcal{N}

by noticing that its acceptance condition is
\omega
-regular
(
i.e.
definable in
\mathsf{MSO}
over
\omega
-words).
This allows us to apply McNaughton’s Theorem
[
McN66
]
,
and to obtain a deterministic
\omega
-word parity automaton
\mathcal{D}

whose language is the set of accepting sequences

(S_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\in Q_{\mathcal{N}}^{\omega}
.
A suitable product of
\mathcal{N}
with
\mathcal{D}
then gives a non-deterministic
parity automaton equivalent to
\mathcal{A}
.

The organization of this Section follows the above construction.
Working in
\mathsf{FSO}
, consider a parity automaton
\mathcal{A}:\Sigma
.
We will build a
non-deterministic
automaton

\mathrm{ND}(\mathcal{A}):\Sigma
with the same language.
The automaton
\mathrm{ND}(\mathcal{A})
will be defined in three steps:

(1)

We first define in §
9.1
a non-deterministic automaton
\oc\mathcal{A}

in the sense of Definition
6.1
.
The acceptance condition of
\oc\mathcal{A}
will be given by an
\mathsf{FSO}
-formula
with a free Function variable
(intended to be range over infinite plays) rather than a parity condition.

(2)

For an
\mathrm{HF}
-closed
parity automaton
\mathcal{A}
,
the formula describing the acceptance condition of
\oc\mathcal{A}

is then transformed in §
9.2
to a
\mathsf{FSO}[\mathrel{\dot{<}}]^{\omega}
formula
relativized to infinite rooted tree paths
(see §
7
).
This construction relies,
via Proposition
78
,
on
Proposition
75

(
i.e.
Proposition
20
)
which requires the manipulation of closed (and in particular
\mathrm{HF}
-closed)
objects.

(3)

Using the tools of §
7
,
and relying on McNaughton’s Theorem
[
McN66
]

(see also e.g.
[
Tho97
,
PP04
]
),
in §
9.3

we will then turn
\oc\mathcal{A}
into an equivalent non-deterministic

parity
automaton
\mathrm{ND}(\mathcal{A})
,
in the sense of Definition
6.1
.

In this Section, it is convenient
to work with the following games.

{defi}

Given an automaton
\mathcal{A}:\Sigma
, we let
\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{A})
be the game with

\mathsf{P}_{\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{A})}\penalty\ \penalty\ \mathrel{:=}\penalty\ \penalty\ Q_{\mathcal{A}}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\mathsf{O}_{\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{A})}\penalty\ \penalty\ \mathrel{:=}\penalty\ \penalty\ Q_{\mathcal{A}}\times\mathcal{P}_{*}(\mathscr{D}\times Q_{\mathcal{A}})

and with transitions defined by

\mathrm{HF}
-Bounded Choice for Product Types (Theorem
22
)
and Comprehension for
\mathrm{HF}
-Sets (Remark
3.7.3
) as

\begin{array}[]{l !{\qquad} r !{\quad\text{iff}\quad} l}&(q^{\prime},\gamma)\in E(\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{A}))_{\mathsf{P}}(x,q)&q^{\prime}\mathrel{\doteq}q\penalty\ \penalty\ \land\penalty\ \penalty\ (\exists\mathtt{a}\in\Sigma)\left[\gamma\in\partial_{\mathcal{A}}(q,\mathtt{a})\right]\\
\text{and}&(d,q^{\prime})\in E(\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{A}))_{\mathsf{O}}(x,(q,\gamma))&(d,q^{\prime})\in\gamma\end{array}

As for winning,
we will consider the game
\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{A})
as being equipped
with the winning condition
\Omega_{\mathcal{A}}
in the sense of §
5.4
.
Note that for
F:\Sigma
, the acceptance game
\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{A},F)

is a subgame of
\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{A})
in the sense of Def.
5.1
.

Remark 98
.

Note that if
\mathrm{Aut}(\mathcal{A})
then
\Sigma
is non-empty,
so we indeed have
\mathrm{Game}(\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{A}))
.
For each
F:\Sigma
,
the acceptance game

\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{A},F)
is a subgame of
\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{A})

(in the sense of Def.
5.1
).
In particular infinite plays in
\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{A},F)
are infinite plays in
\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{A})
.
Moreover, it is easy to see that (winning) strategies on
\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{A},F)

are (winning) strategies on
\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{A})
.

Furthermore, note that the game
\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{A})(\trianglelefteq)
induced by Remark
5.1

from Definition
9

is precisely the game
\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{A})(\trianglelefteq)
of (
12
).
It follows that in the case of a parity automaton
\mathcal{A}
,
we unambiguously extend the notation of Definition
6.1

and write

\mathrm{Par}(\mathcal{A},\hat{C}_{\mathcal{A}},n_{\mathcal{A}},U)

or

\mathrm{Par}(\mathcal{A},U)

for the formula

\mathrm{Par}(\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{A})(\trianglelefteq),\hat{C}_{\mathcal{A}},n_{\mathcal{A}},U)
.

9.1.
The Construction of
\oc\mathcal{A}
.

Consider an alternating parity automaton
\mathcal{A}
,
in the sense of Definition
6.1
.
So we have

\mathcal{A}=(Q_{\mathcal{A}},\,{q}^{\iota}_{\mathcal{A}},\,\partial_{\mathcal{A}},\,C_{\mathcal{A}},\,n_{\mathcal{A}})

where

\partial_{\mathcal{A}}\penalty\ \penalty\ :\penalty\ \penalty\ Q_{\mathcal{A}}\times\Sigma\penalty\ \penalty\ \longrightarrow\penalty\ \penalty\ \mathcal{P}_{*}(\mathcal{P}_{*}(\mathscr{D}\times Q_{\mathcal{A}}))\qquad\text{and}\qquad C_{\mathcal{A}}:Q_{\mathcal{A}}\mathrel{\mathfrak{to}}[0,n_{\mathcal{A}}]

We define the state set and the initial state of
\oc\mathcal{A}
as:

Q_{\oc\mathcal{A}}\quad\mathrel{:=}\quad\mathcal{P}_{*}(Q_{\mathcal{A}}\times Q_{\mathcal{A}})\qquad\text{and}\qquad{q}^{\iota}_{\oc\mathcal{A}}\quad\mathrel{:=}\quad({q}^{\iota}_{\mathcal{A}},{q}^{\iota}_{\mathcal{A}})

The transition function of
\oc\mathcal{A}
is defined as follows,
using Remark
49
.
For
\mathtt{a}\in\Sigma
and
S\in Q_{\oc\mathcal{A}}

we let
\oc\gamma\in\partial_{\oc\mathcal{A}}(S,\mathtt{a})

if and only if there is some
\mathrm{HF}
-set

\begin{array}[]{c c c c l}f&:&S&\longrightarrow&\mathcal{P}_{*}(\mathscr{D}\times Q_{\mathcal{A}})\\
&&q&\longmapsto&\gamma_{q}\in\partial_{\mathcal{A}}(q,\mathtt{a})\end{array}

such that

\oc\gamma=\{(d,S^{\prime}_{d})\penalty\ |\penalty\ d\in\mathscr{D}\land S^{\prime}_{d}\neq\varnothing\}
,
where

S^{\prime}_{d}\quad=\quad\{(q,q^{\prime})\penalty\ |\penalty\ q\in\pi_{2}(S)\penalty\ \penalty\ \land\penalty\ \penalty\ (d,q^{\prime})\in f(q)\}

(20)

and where
\pi_{2}

is a projection
\mathrm{HF}
-Function of §
3.4.4
.
6
.

Remark 99
.

We indeed have

\partial_{\oc\mathcal{A}}\penalty\ \penalty\ :\penalty\ \penalty\ Q_{\oc\mathcal{A}}\times\Sigma\penalty\ \penalty\ \longrightarrow\penalty\ \penalty\ \mathcal{P}_{*}(\mathcal{P}_{*}(\mathscr{D}\times Q_{\oc\mathcal{A}}))

since for
S\in Q_{\oc\mathcal{A}}=\mathcal{P}_{*}(Q_{\mathcal{A}}\times Q_{\mathcal{A}})
,
by

\mathrm{HF}
-Bounded Choice for
\mathrm{HF}
-Sets
(§
3.4.5
)
there is always some
f\in\mathcal{P}_{*}(\mathscr{D}\times Q_{\mathcal{A}})^{\pi_{2}(S)}

with
\forall q\in\pi_{2}(S)(f(q)\in\partial_{\mathcal{A}}(q,\mathtt{a}))
,
and moreover such that

S^{\prime}_{d}
is non-empty for at least one
d\in\mathscr{D}
.

Note our unusual choice of taking
non-empty
sets as states of
\oc\mathcal{A}
.
It would have been more natural to allow the empty set as a state,
in particular because it would have allowed us to strengthen
Corollary
55
to an “exists unique” statement.
This could also have worked in our setting where games are assumed to
have no dead ends, and in which transitions of alternating automata
range over non-empty sets of non-empty subsets of
\mathscr{D}\times Q_{\mathcal{A}}
.
However, the empty state would have appeared in the transitions of

\oc\mathcal{A}
only in case there is some tree direction
d\in\mathscr{D}

which is not available to
\mathsf{O}
at some stage.
Since the empty state of
\oc\mathcal{A}

would have been unconditionally winning for
\mathsf{P}
,
this would have lead to an additional case to handle in
the proof of completeness of
\oc\mathcal{A}

(Proposition
101
below).

So far we have defined for
\oc\mathcal{A}
a state set (with an initial state)
and a transition function.
As explained above, we will not directly equip it with a parity condition.
Instead, we will define its acceptance condition via an
\mathsf{FSO}
-formula

\mathcal{W}_{\oc\mathcal{A}}
,
in the sense of Definition
6.1
.
Consider first
V:\mathcal{G}(\oc\mathcal{A})\mathrel{\mathfrak{to}}2

and
T:\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{A})\mathrel{\mathfrak{to}}2
.
We say that
T
is a
trace
in
V
if the following
formula
\mathrm{Trace}(T,V)
holds,

\begin{array}[]{c @{~~} l}\hfil\penalty\ \penalty\ &\mathrm{Path}(\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{A}),(\dot{\varepsilon},{q}^{\iota}_{\mathcal{A}}),T)\\[5.0pt]
\land\hfil\penalty\ \penalty\ &\big(\forall(x,q)\in T_{\mathsf{P}}\big)\big(\exists S\in Q_{\oc\mathcal{A}}\big)\Big[(x,S)\in V_{\mathsf{P}}\penalty\ \penalty\ \land\penalty\ \penalty\ q\in\pi_{2}(S)\Big]\\[5.0pt]
\land\hfil\penalty\ \penalty\ &\big(\forall(x,q),(y,q^{\prime})\in T_{\mathsf{P}}\big)\big(\forall S\in Q_{\oc\mathcal{A}}\big)\left[(x,q)\vartriangleleft^{\mathsf{P};\mathsf{O}}_{\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{A})}(y,q^{\prime})\penalty\ \penalty\ \Rightarrow\penalty\ \penalty\ (y,S)\in V_{\mathsf{P}}\penalty\ \penalty\ \Rightarrow\penalty\ \penalty\ (q,q^{\prime})\in S\right]\end{array}

where we use the following formula:

\begin{array}[]{r !{\quad\deq\quad} l}u\vartriangleleft^{\mathsf{P};\mathsf{O}}_{\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{A})}v\lx@intercol\quad\mathrel{:=}&\big(\exists w\in\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{A})_{\mathsf{O}}\big)\left(\mathsf{s}^{\vartriangleleft}_{\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{A})}(u,w)\penalty\ \land\penalty\ \mathsf{s}^{\vartriangleleft}_{\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{A})}(w,v)\right)\end{array}

The formula
\mathcal{W}_{\oc\mathcal{A}}(V)
is defined to be:

\mathcal{W}_{\oc\mathcal{A}}(V)\quad\mathrel{:=}\quad\big(\forall T:\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{A})\mathrel{\mathfrak{to}}2\big)\left[\mathrm{Trace}(T,V)\penalty\ \penalty\ \Rightarrow\penalty\ \penalty\ \mathrm{Par}(\mathcal{A},\hat{C}_{\mathcal{A}},n_{\mathcal{A}},T)\right]

Recall our notation
\mathrm{Par}(\mathcal{A},\hat{C}_{\mathcal{A}},n_{\mathcal{A}},-)

from Remark
98
.
Note that
\mathcal{W}_{\oc\mathcal{A}}

requires no condition
w.r.t. the
transitions
of
\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{A})
.
We are now going to show that
\oc\mathcal{A}
has the same language as
\mathcal{A}
.

Theorem 100
.

Fix a parity automaton
\mathcal{A}:\Sigma
and consider the automaton

\oc\mathcal{A}:\Sigma
as defined above.
Then
{\mathsf{FSO}_{\mathscr{D}}}
proves that for all
F:\Sigma
,

\oc\mathcal{A}
accepts
F
if and only if
\mathcal{A}
accepts
F
.

The proof of Theorem
100

is split into Propositions
101
and
102
below.

{conv}

In Propositions
101
and
102
,
for fixed automata
\mathcal{A}
and
\oc\mathcal{A}
,
we let

\iota\penalty\ \penalty\ \mathrel{:=}\penalty\ \penalty\ (\dot{\varepsilon},{q}^{\iota}_{\mathcal{A}})\qquad\text{and}\qquad\iota_{\oc}\penalty\ \penalty\ \mathrel{:=}\penalty\ \penalty\ (\dot{\varepsilon},{q}^{\iota}_{\oc\mathcal{A}})

Proposition 101
.

Fix a parity automaton
\mathcal{A}:\Sigma
and consider the automaton

\oc\mathcal{A}:\Sigma
as defined above.
Then
{\mathsf{FSO}_{\mathscr{D}}}
proves that for all
F:\Sigma
,
if
\mathcal{A}
accepts
F
then
\oc\mathcal{A}
accepts
F
.

Proof 9.1
.

Let
\sigma
be a winning
\mathsf{P}
-strategy in
\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{A},F)
.
We define a winning
\mathsf{P}
-strategy
\tau
in
\mathcal{G}(\oc\mathcal{A},F)
.
Note that

\sigma:\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{A})_{\mathsf{P}}\mathrel{\mathfrak{to}}\mathsf{O}_{\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{A})}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\tau:\mathcal{G}(\oc\mathcal{A})_{\mathsf{P}}\mathrel{\mathfrak{to}}\mathsf{O}_{\mathcal{G}(\oc\mathcal{A})}

First, given a
\mathsf{P}
-position
(x,S)
in
\mathcal{G}(\oc\mathcal{A},F)
,
we define a conjunction

\gamma_{(x,S)}\penalty\ \in\penalty\ \partial_{\oc\mathcal{A}}(S,F(x))\penalty\ \subseteq\penalty\ \mathcal{P}_{*}(\mathscr{D}\times Q_{\oc\mathcal{A}})

as follows.

•

Definition of
\gamma_{(x,S)}
.
For each
q\in\pi_{2}(S)
,
\sigma(x,q)

gives some
\gamma_{q}\in\partial_{\mathcal{A}}(q,F(x))
.

\mathrm{HF}
-Bounded
\mathrm{HF}
-Choice (§

)
then gives

\begin{array}[]{c c c c l}f&:&\pi_{2}(S)&\longrightarrow&\mathcal{P}_{*}(\mathscr{D}\times Q_{\mathcal{A}})\\
&&q&\longmapsto&\gamma_{q}\in\partial_{\mathcal{A}}(q,F(x))\end{array}

By
\mathrm{HF}
-Comprehension (Remark
3.7.3
), we then let
\gamma_{(x,S)}

be
\{(d,S^{\prime}_{d})\penalty\ |\penalty\ d\in\mathscr{D}\land S^{\prime}_{d}\neq\varnothing\}

where each
S^{\prime}_{d}
is defined as in (
20
).

We now define the
\mathsf{P}
-strategy
\tau
on
\mathcal{G}(\oc\mathcal{A},F)
.
By
\mathrm{HF}
-Bounded Choice for Functions (§
3.4.5
),
we let

\tau(x,S)\quad\mathrel{:=}\quad(S,\gamma_{(x,S)})\qquad\text{for each $(x,S)\in\mathcal{G}(\oc\mathcal{A})_{\mathsf{P}}$}

We have
\mathrm{Strat}_{\mathsf{P}}(\mathcal{G}(\oc\mathcal{A},F),\tau)
directly by definition of

\partial_{\oc\mathcal{A}}
.
It remains to check that
\tau
is winning in
\mathcal{G}(\oc\mathcal{A},F)
.
Consider an infinite play of
\tau
, that is some

V:\mathcal{G}(\oc\mathcal{A})\mathrel{\mathfrak{to}}2

such that

\mathrm{Play}(\tau,\iota_{\oc},V)
.
Since
\sigma
is winning in
\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{A},F)
,
by definition of
\mathcal{W}_{\oc\mathcal{A}}

and by Remarks

and
98
,
we are done if we show that:

\big(\forall T:\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{A})\mathrel{\mathfrak{to}}2\big)\Big(\mathrm{Trace}(T,V)\penalty\ \penalty\ \Rightarrow\penalty\ \penalty\ \big(\exists U:\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{A})\mathrel{\mathfrak{to}}2\big)\big[U_{\mathsf{P}}=T_{\mathsf{P}}\penalty\ \penalty\ \land\penalty\ \penalty\ \mathrm{Play}(\sigma,\iota,U)\big]\Big)

Assume
\mathrm{Trace}(T,V)
.
By
\mathrm{HF}
-Comprehension for Product Types,
we let
U:\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{A})\mathrel{\mathfrak{to}}2

be such that

and such that

U_{\mathsf{O}}
consists of the
\{(x,\sigma(x,q))\}
for

.
Note that we actually have
U:\sigma\mathrel{\mathfrak{to}}2
.
It remains to check that

\mathrm{Play}(\sigma,\,\iota,\,U)

We apply Lemma
39
,
whence it remains to show:

\displaystyle\mathrm{Path}(\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{A}),\iota,U)

(21)

\displaystyle\big(\forall u,u^{\prime}\in U\big)\left[\mathsf{s}^{\vartriangleleft}_{\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{A})}(u,u^{\prime})\penalty\ \penalty\ \Rightarrow\penalty\ \penalty\ u\mathchoice{\mathrel{\overset{}{\underset{\sigma}{\longrightarrow}}}}{\mathrel{\longrightarrow_{\sigma}}}{\longrightarrow_{\sigma}}{\longrightarrow_{\sigma}}u^{\prime}\right]

(22)

Note that
\mathrm{Path}(\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{A}),\iota,T)
since
\mathrm{Trace}(T,V)
.

Proof 9.2

(Proof of (
21
))
.

We obviously have

\iota\in U_{\mathsf{P}}=T_{\mathsf{P}}
.
Also, given
u\in U
,
if
u\in U_{\mathsf{P}}=T_{\mathsf{P}}
then
\iota\trianglelefteq_{\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{A})}u
,
and if
u\in U_{\mathsf{O}}
, then
v\mathchoice{\mathrel{\overset{}{\underset{\sigma}{\longrightarrow}}}}{\mathrel{\longrightarrow_{\sigma}}}{\longrightarrow_{\sigma}}{\longrightarrow_{\sigma}}u
for some

, so that

\iota\trianglelefteq_{\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{A})}v\vartriangleleft_{\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{A})}u
.
Moreover, for each
u\in U_{\mathsf{P}}
, we have

u\mathchoice{\mathrel{\overset{}{\underset{\sigma}{\longrightarrow}}}}{\mathrel{\longrightarrow_{\sigma}}}{\longrightarrow_{\sigma}}{\longrightarrow_{\sigma}}v
for some
v\in U_{\mathsf{O}}
,
and we get
\mathsf{s}^{\vartriangleleft}_{\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{A})}(u,v)
by Proposition
33
.

It remains to show that
U
is linearly ordered w.r.t.
\trianglelefteq_{\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{A})}
.
For
U_{\mathsf{P}}
this follows from the same property for

.
Now let
u\in U_{\mathsf{P}}
and
v^{\prime}\in U_{\mathsf{O}}
.
Hence
v^{\prime}
is of the form
(x,\sigma(x,q))
with
v\mathrel{:=}(x,q)\in U_{\mathsf{P}}=T_{\mathsf{P}}
.
If

and we are done.
Otherwise,
v\vartriangleleft_{\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{A})}u
.
But by definition of

, this implies
u=(y,q^{\prime})

with

, so that
v^{\prime}\vartriangleleft_{\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{A})}u
.
Consider now
u^{\prime},v^{\prime}\in U_{\mathsf{O}}

and let
u,v\in U_{\mathsf{P}}
be their immediate predecessors.
If
u\vartriangleleft_{\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{A})}v
then
u^{\prime}\vartriangleleft_{\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{A})}v^{\prime}
and we are done.
Otherwise, without loss of generality we have that
u=v
.
But then
u^{\prime}=v^{\prime}
by definition of
U
.

Proof 9.3

(Proof of (
22
))
.

Assume first
u\in U_{\mathsf{P}}
.
In this case,

u
is of the form
(x,q)
with

,
and

is of the form

(x,q^{\prime})\in T_{\mathsf{P}}
.
But
T

,
so that

.
It follows that
u\mathchoice{\mathrel{\overset{}{\underset{\sigma}{\longrightarrow}}}}{\mathrel{\longrightarrow_{\sigma}}}{\longrightarrow_{\sigma}}{\longrightarrow_{\sigma}}u^{\prime}
.

Assume now that
u\in U_{\mathsf{O}}
.
In this case,

u
is of the form
(x,(q,\gamma_{\mathcal{A}}))
with
(x,q)\in U_{\mathsf{P}}=T_{\mathsf{P}}

and
(q,\gamma_{\mathcal{A}})=\sigma(x,q)
.
Moreover,

is of the form

(\mathsf{S}_{d}(x),q^{\prime})
.
We thus get
u\mathchoice{\mathrel{\overset{}{\underset{\sigma}{\longrightarrow}}}}{\mathrel{\longrightarrow_{\sigma}}}{\longrightarrow_{\sigma}}{\longrightarrow_{\sigma}}u^{\prime}
as soon as

(d,q^{\prime})\in\gamma_{\mathcal{A}}

Since
\mathrm{Trace}(T,V)

and since

is a play of

,
there are unique

with

.
Moreover, we necessarily have

.
But

,
and it follows

by definition of

This concludes the proof of Proposition
