.

Proposition 102
.

Fix a parity automaton
\mathcal{A}:\Sigma
and consider the automaton

\oc\mathcal{A}:\Sigma
as defined above.
Then
{\mathsf{FSO}_{\mathscr{D}}}
proves that for all
F:\Sigma
,
if
\oc\mathcal{A}
accepts
F
then
\mathcal{A}
accepts
F
.

Proof 9.4
.

Let
\tau
be a winning
\mathsf{P}
-strategy in
\mathcal{G}(\oc\mathcal{A},F)
.
We will define a winning
\mathsf{P}
-strategy
\sigma
in
\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{A},F)
.
To this end, we invoke Corollary
55
,
which tells us that since
\oc\mathcal{A}
is non-deterministic,
for each
x\in\mathscr{D}^{*}
there is at most one
S\in Q_{\oc\mathcal{A}}

such that
(x,S)
belongs to an infinite play of
\tau
.
Moreover,
using Remark
12
,
for each
S\in Q_{\oc\mathcal{A}}

we fix a well-order
\preceq
on
\mathcal{P}_{*}(\mathscr{D}\times Q_{\mathcal{A}})^{\pi_{2}(S)}
.

We now define the strategy
\sigma
.

•

Definition of
\sigma
.
We apply
\mathrm{HF}
-Bounded Choice for Product Types (Theorem
22
).
Consider
(x,q)\in\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{A},F)_{\mathsf{P}}
.
We first assign to
(x,q)
an
S\in Q_{\oc\mathcal{A}}
such that
q\in\pi_{2}(S)
.
If there exists such an
S
where furthermore
(x,S)

belongs to an infinite play of
\tau
, then this
S
is unique
and we choose that one.
Otherwise,
by Comprehension for
\mathrm{HF}
-Sets (Remark
3.7.3
),
we define an ad hoc
S\in Q_{\oc\mathcal{A}}
with
q\in\pi_{2}(S)
.

Let now
(S,\gamma_{(x,S)})\mathrel{:=}\tau(x,S)
.
By definition of
\gamma_{(x,S)}

there is some

\begin{array}[]{c c c c l}f&:&\pi_{2}(S)&\longrightarrow&\mathcal{P}_{*}(\mathscr{D}\times Q_{\mathcal{A}})\\
&&q&\longmapsto&\gamma_{q}\in\partial_{\mathcal{A}}(q,F(x))\end{array}

such that

\gamma_{(x,S)}=\{(d,S^{\prime}_{d})\penalty\ |\penalty\ d\in\mathscr{D}\land S^{\prime}_{d}\neq\varnothing\}

where each
S^{\prime}_{d}
is as in (
20
).
Consider the
\prec
-least such
f
.
We let

\sigma(x,q)\quad\mathrel{:=}\quad(q,f(q))

It remains to show that
\sigma
is winning.
To this end, given an infinite play
T
of
\sigma
,
we will define an infinite play
V
of
\tau
such that:

\mathrm{Trace}(T,V)

Since
\tau
is assumed to be winning,
thanks to
Remarks
48
and
98
,
this will imply that
\sigma

is also winning.
Assume
\mathrm{Play}(\sigma,\iota,T)
.
We define
V
using the Recursion Theorem (Proposition
26
).
Let
\varphi(V,v)
be a
\mathsf{FSO}
-formula stating that:

•

either
v=\iota_{\oc}
,

•

or
v=(x,\tau(x,S))
with
(x,S)\in V
,

•

or
v=(\mathsf{S}_{d}(x),S^{\prime}_{d})
and

–

for some
q^{\prime}\in Q_{\mathcal{A}}
we have
(\mathsf{S}_{d}(x),q^{\prime})\in T
,

–

and for some
S\in Q_{\oc\mathcal{A}}
, we have

(x,S)\in V
and
\tau(x,S)=(S,\gamma_{(x,S)})
with
(d,S^{\prime}_{d})\in\gamma_{(x,S)}
.

Note that
\varphi(V,v)
indeed satisfies the assumptions of the
Recursion Theorem (Proposition
26
), since

•

in the second clause we always have
(x,S)\vartriangleleft_{\mathcal{G}(\oc\mathcal{A})}(x,\tau(x,S))
,
and
\tau(x,S)
is uniquely determined from
(x,S)
;

•

in the last clause,
we always have
(x,S)\vartriangleleft_{\mathcal{G}(\oc\mathcal{A})}(\mathsf{S}_{d}(x),S^{\prime}_{d})
.

Note also that since
T
is a play of
\sigma
, there is at most one
d\in\mathscr{D}

such that
(\mathsf{S}_{d}(x),q)\in T
for some
q\in Q_{\mathcal{A}}
,
and
S^{\prime}_{d}
is uniquely determined from
d
and
\tau(x,S)

by construction of
\oc\mathcal{A}
.
So by the Recursion Theorem (Proposition
26
) we indeed let

V:\mathcal{G}(\oc\mathcal{A})\mathrel{\mathfrak{to}}2
be unique such that

\big(\forall v\in\mathcal{G}(\oc\mathcal{A})\big)\Big[v\in V\penalty\ \penalty\ \Leftrightarrow\penalty\ \penalty\ \varphi(V,v)\Big]

We begin with a series of easy claims on
V
.

Claim 103
.

For every
x\in\mathscr{D}^{*}
, there is at most one
S\in Q_{\oc\mathcal{A}}

such that
(x,S)\in V
.

Proof 9.5

(Proof of Claim
103
)
.

We apply the Induction Axiom of
\mathsf{FSO}
(§
3.4.2
).
The property holds for
\dot{\varepsilon}
, since
(\dot{\varepsilon},S)\in V

implies
S={q}^{\iota}_{\oc\mathcal{A}}
by definition of
V
.
Now assume the property for
x
and let us show it for
\mathsf{S}_{d}(x)
.
So assume
(\mathsf{S}_{d}(x),S^{\prime}_{d}),(\mathsf{S}_{d}(x),\tilde{S}^{\prime}_{d})\in V
.
By definition of
V
, there are
(x,S),(x,\tilde{S})\in V

such that
(d,S^{\prime}_{d})\in\gamma_{(x,S)}

and
(d,\tilde{S}^{\prime}_{d})\in\gamma_{(x,\tilde{S})}

where

(S,\gamma_{(x,S)})=\tau(x,S)

and

(\tilde{S},\gamma_{(x,\tilde{S})})=\tau(x,\tilde{S})
.
But by induction hypothesis we get
S=\tilde{S}
,
which implies
\gamma_{(x,S)}=\gamma_{(x,\tilde{S})}
.
This in turn implies
S^{\prime}_{d}=\tilde{S}^{\prime}_{d}
by construction of
\oc\mathcal{A}
.

Claim 104
.

For every
u\in V
, the set
\{v\in V\penalty\ |\penalty\ v\trianglelefteq_{\mathcal{G}(\oc\mathcal{A})}u\}

is linearly ordered w.r.t.
\mathchoice{\mathrel{\overset{*}{\underset{\tau}{\longrightarrow}}}}{\mathrel{\longrightarrow^{*}_{\tau}}}{\longrightarrow^{*}_{\tau}}{\longrightarrow^{*}_{\tau}}
.

Proof 9.6

(Proof of Claim
104
)
.

We reason by
\vartriangleleft
-Induction (Theorem
25
).
So let
u\in V
be such that the property holds for all
w\vartriangleleft_{\mathcal{G}(\oc\mathcal{A})}u
.

Assume first that
u\in V_{\mathsf{O}}
.
In this case, we must have
u=(x,\tau(x,S))
with
(x,S)\in V
.
By induction hypothesis, the set

\{v\in V\penalty\ |\penalty\ v\trianglelefteq_{\mathcal{G}(\oc\mathcal{A})}(x,S)\}

is linearly ordered w.r.t.
\mathchoice{\mathrel{\overset{*}{\underset{\tau}{\longrightarrow}}}}{\mathrel{\longrightarrow^{*}_{\tau}}}{\longrightarrow^{*}_{\tau}}{\longrightarrow^{*}_{\tau}}
.
On the other hand, it follows from Claim
103

that
(x,S)
is the only immediate
\mathchoice{\mathrel{\overset{}{\underset{\tau}{\longrightarrow}}}}{\mathrel{\longrightarrow_{\tau}}}{\longrightarrow_{\tau}}{\longrightarrow_{\tau}}
-predecessor of
u
in
V
.
Since
(x,S)\mathchoice{\mathrel{\overset{}{\underset{\tau}{\longrightarrow}}}}{\mathrel{\longrightarrow_{\tau}}}{\longrightarrow_{\tau}}{\longrightarrow_{\tau}}u
,
we get the result by Proposition
33
.

Assume now that
u\in V_{\mathsf{P}}
.
If
u=\iota_{\oc}
then the result is trivial.
Otherwise,
u
is of the form
(\mathsf{S}_{d}(x),S^{\prime}_{d})

and its membership to
V
is given by the last clause defining
V
.
Let
S
be such that
(x,S)\in V
and such that

\tau(x,S)=(S,\gamma_{(x,S)})
with
(d,S^{\prime}_{d})\in\gamma_{(x,S)}
.
Since
(x,\tau(x,S))\mathchoice{\mathrel{\overset{}{\underset{\tau}{\longrightarrow}}}}{\mathrel{\longrightarrow_{\tau}}}{\longrightarrow_{\tau}}{\longrightarrow_{\tau}}u
with
(x,\tau(x,S))\in V
,
by induction hypothesis
the set

\{v\penalty\ |\penalty\ v\trianglelefteq_{\mathcal{G}(\oc\mathcal{A})}(x,\tau(x,S))\}

is linearly ordered w.r.t.
\mathchoice{\mathrel{\overset{*}{\underset{\tau}{\longrightarrow}}}}{\mathrel{\longrightarrow^{*}_{\tau}}}{\longrightarrow^{*}_{\tau}}{\longrightarrow^{*}_{\tau}}
.
In order to obtain the result for

\{v\penalty\ |\penalty\ v\trianglelefteq_{\mathcal{G}(\oc\mathcal{A})}(\mathsf{S}_{d}(x),S^{\prime}_{d})\}

we need to show that
(x,\tau(x,S))
is the unique
immediate
\mathchoice{\mathrel{\overset{}{\underset{\tau}{\longrightarrow}}}}{\mathrel{\longrightarrow_{\tau}}}{\longrightarrow_{\tau}}{\longrightarrow_{\tau}}
-predecessor of
(\mathsf{S}_{d}(x),S^{\prime}_{d})
in
V
.
But if
(x,\tau(x,\tilde{S}))\in V

then we should have
(x,\tilde{S})\in V
,
so that
\tilde{S}=S
by Claim
103
.

Claim 105
.

For every
u\in V
, there is an infinite play
U
of
\tau

such that:

\big(\forall v\trianglelefteq_{\mathcal{G}(\oc\mathcal{A})}u\big)\Big(v\in V\penalty\ \penalty\ \Leftrightarrow\penalty\ \penalty\ u\in U\Big)

Proof 9.7

(Proof of Claim
105
)
.

Let
u\in V
.
First, by Lemma
38

there is an infinite play
U_{0}
in the game
\mathcal{G}(\oc\mathcal{A})\mathord{\upharpoonright}\{\tau\}

such that
u\in U_{0}

and
u\mathchoice{\mathrel{\overset{*}{\underset{\tau}{\longrightarrow}}}}{\mathrel{\longrightarrow^{*}_{\tau}}}{\longrightarrow^{*}_{\tau}}{\longrightarrow^{*}_{\tau}}v
for all
v\in U_{0}
.
By Comprehension for Product Types (Theorem
23
)
we let

U\quad\mathrel{:=}\quad U_{0}\cup\{v\in V\penalty\ |\penalty\ v\trianglelefteq_{\mathcal{G}(\oc\mathcal{A})}u\}

We then get
\mathrm{Play}(\tau,\iota_{\oc},U)

from Claim
104

and
\mathrm{Play}(\tau,u,U_{0})
.

Claim 106
.

Let
(x,S)\in V
, and assume
(x,q),(\mathsf{S}_{d}(x),q^{\prime})\in T

with
q\in\pi_{2}(S)
.
Then there is some
S^{\prime}_{d}\in Q_{\oc\mathcal{A}}
such that
(\mathsf{S}_{d}(x),S^{\prime}_{d})\in V

and
(q,q^{\prime})\in S^{\prime}_{d}
.
Moreover, we have
(d,S^{\prime}_{d})\in\gamma_{(x,S)}
for
(S,\gamma_{(x,S)})=\tau(x,S)
.

Proof 9.8

(Proof of Claim 106)
.

Since
T
is a play of
\sigma
, we have
(d,q^{\prime})\in\gamma

for
(q,\gamma)=\sigma(x,q)
.
Moreover, by Claim
105
,

(x,S)
belongs to an infinite play of
\tau
.
Since
q\in\pi_{2}(S)
, by definition of
\sigma

this implies that there is some
S^{\prime}_{d}
such that

(d,S^{\prime}_{d})\in\gamma_{(x,S)}
for
(S,\gamma_{(x,S)})=\tau(x,S)

and
(q,q^{\prime})\in S^{\prime}_{d}
.
We then obtain
(\mathsf{S}_{d}(x),S^{\prime}_{d})\in V
by definition of
V
.

We now proceed to show:

\mathrm{Play}(\tau,\,\iota_{\oc},\,V)\quad\land\quad\mathrm{Trace}(T,V)

We begin with
\mathrm{Trace}(T,V)
.
First, we have

\mathrm{Path}(\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{A}),\iota,T)

since
T
is a play of
\sigma
.
Moreover

Claim 107
.

\big(\forall(x,q)\in T_{\mathsf{P}}\big)\big(\exists S\in Q_{\oc\mathcal{A}}\big)\Big((x,S)\in V_{\mathsf{P}}\penalty\ \penalty\ \land\penalty\ \penalty\ q\in\pi_{2}(S)\Big)

Proof 9.9

(Proof of Claim 107)
.

Using the Induction Axiom of
\mathsf{FSO}

(§
3.4.2
), we show

(\forall x)\big(\forall q\in Q_{\mathcal{A}}\big)\Big((x,q)\in T_{\mathsf{P}}\penalty\ \penalty\ \Rightarrow\penalty\ \penalty\ \big(\exists S\in Q_{\oc\mathcal{A}}\big)\Big[(x,S)\in V_{\mathsf{P}}\penalty\ \land\penalty\ q\in\pi_{2}(S)\Big]\Big)

For the base case
x=\dot{\varepsilon}
, if
(x,q)\in T
then we must have

q={q}^{\iota}_{\mathcal{A}}
, so
q\in\pi_{2}({q}^{\iota}_{\oc\mathcal{A}})
.
Assume now the property for
x
, and consider
\mathsf{S}_{d}(x)
and
q,q^{\prime}\in Q_{\mathcal{A}}

such that
(x,q)\in T
and
(\mathsf{S}_{d}(x),q^{\prime})\in T
.
Furthermore, by induction hypothesis, let
S\in Q_{\oc\mathcal{A}}

such that
(x,S)\in V
and
q\in\pi_{2}(S)
.
By Claim
106
,
we then get
(\mathsf{S}_{d}(x),S^{\prime}_{d})\in V
for some
S^{\prime}_{d}\in Q_{\oc\mathcal{A}}

with
q^{\prime}\in\pi_{2}(S^{\prime}_{d})
.

We can now show the last required property for
\mathrm{Trace}(T,V)
,
namely:

Claim 108
.

\big(\forall(x,q),(y,q^{\prime})\in T_{\mathsf{P}}\big)\big(\forall S\in Q_{\oc\mathcal{A}}\big)\left[(x,q)\vartriangleleft^{\mathsf{P};\mathsf{O}}_{\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{A})}(y,q^{\prime})\penalty\ \penalty\ \Rightarrow\penalty\ \penalty\ (y,S)\in V_{\mathsf{P}}\penalty\ \penalty\ \Rightarrow\penalty\ \penalty\ (q,q^{\prime})\in S\right]

Proof 9.10

(Proof of Claim 108)
.

Let
(x,q),(y,q^{\prime})\in T

and
S^{\prime}\in Q_{\oc\mathcal{A}}

such that

(x,q)\vartriangleleft^{\mathsf{P};\mathsf{O}}_{\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{A})}(y,q^{\prime})

and

(y,S^{\prime})\in V
.
Then by definition of
\vartriangleleft_{\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{A})}
we must have
y=\mathsf{S}_{d}(x)

for some
d\in\mathscr{D}
.
Moreover, by Claim
107

there is some
S\in Q_{\oc\mathcal{A}}
such that
(x,S)\in V

and
q\in\pi_{2}(S)
.
By Claim
106
,
we then have
(\mathsf{S}_{d}(x),S^{\prime}_{d})\in V
for some
S^{\prime}_{d}\in Q_{\oc\mathcal{A}}

with
(q,q^{\prime})\in S^{\prime}_{d}
.
It follows from Claim
103

that
S^{\prime}=S^{\prime}_{d}

so that
(q,q^{\prime})\in S^{\prime}

and we are done.

We now turn to showing

\mathrm{Play}(\tau,\iota_{\oc},V)
.
Since
\iota_{\oc}\in V
,
thanks to Proposition
35

it remains to show:

\left\{\begin{array}[]{c l}&(\forall u\in V)\big(\iota_{\oc}\mathchoice{\mathrel{\overset{*}{\underset{\tau}{\longrightarrow}}}}{\mathrel{\longrightarrow^{*}_{\tau}}}{\longrightarrow^{*}_{\tau}}{\longrightarrow^{*}_{\tau}}u\big)\\
\land&(\forall u\in V)(\exists!v\in V)\big(u\mathchoice{\mathrel{\overset{}{\underset{\tau}{\longrightarrow}}}}{\mathrel{\longrightarrow_{\tau}}}{\longrightarrow_{\tau}}{\longrightarrow_{\tau}}v\big)\\
\land&(\forall v\in V)\Big(v\neq\iota_{\oc}\penalty\ \penalty\ \Rightarrow\penalty\ \penalty\ (\exists u\in V)\big(u\mathchoice{\mathrel{\overset{}{\underset{\tau}{\longrightarrow}}}}{\mathrel{\longrightarrow_{\tau}}}{\longrightarrow_{\tau}}{\longrightarrow_{\tau}}v\big)\Big)\end{array}\right.

First, we easily have:

Claim 109
.

(\forall v\in V)\Big(v\neq\iota_{\oc}\penalty\ \penalty\ \Rightarrow\penalty\ \penalty\ (\exists u\in V)\big(u\mathchoice{\mathrel{\overset{}{\underset{\tau}{\longrightarrow}}}}{\mathrel{\longrightarrow_{\tau}}}{\longrightarrow_{\tau}}{\longrightarrow_{\tau}}v\big)\Big)

Proof 9.11

(Proof of Claim 109)
.

The result follows from Claim
105
,
but it can be proved directly,
without the inductions underlying Claim
105
.
Indeed, if
v=(x,\tau(x,S))
, with
(x,S)\in V
, then the result
directly follows from the definitions of
V
and of
the game
\mathcal{G}(\oc\mathcal{A})\mathord{\upharpoonright}\{\tau\}
.
Otherwise, we have
v=(\mathsf{S}_{d}(x),S^{\prime}_{d})
,
and there is
(x,S)\in V
such that

\tau(x,S)=(S,\gamma_{(x,S)})
with
(d,S^{\prime}_{d})\in\gamma_{(x,S)}
.
But
(x,S)\in V
implies

(x,\tau(x,S))\in V
, and again the result
directly follows from the definition of
\mathcal{G}(\oc\mathcal{A})\mathord{\upharpoonright}\{\tau\}
.

It then easily follows that:

Claim 110
.

(\forall u\in V)\left(\iota_{\oc}\mathchoice{\mathrel{\overset{*}{\underset{\tau}{\longrightarrow}}}}{\mathrel{\longrightarrow^{*}_{\tau}}}{\longrightarrow^{*}_{\tau}}{\longrightarrow^{*}_{\tau}}u\right)

Proof 9.12

(Proof of Claim 110)
.

First, we have
\iota_{\oc}\in V
by definition
of
V
.
Moreover, given
u\in V

we have either
\iota_{\oc}\mathchoice{\mathrel{\overset{*}{\underset{\tau}{\longrightarrow}}}}{\mathrel{\longrightarrow^{*}_{\tau}}}{\longrightarrow^{*}_{\tau}}{\longrightarrow^{*}_{\tau}}u

or
u\mathchoice{\mathrel{\overset{*}{\underset{\tau}{\longrightarrow}}}}{\mathrel{\longrightarrow^{*}_{\tau}}}{\longrightarrow^{*}_{\tau}}{\longrightarrow^{*}_{\tau}}\iota_{\oc}

by Claim
104
.
The result then follows from
Proposition
33
.

It remains to show:

(\forall u\in V)(\exists!v\in V)\left(u\mathchoice{\mathrel{\overset{}{\underset{\tau}{\longrightarrow}}}}{\mathrel{\longrightarrow_{\tau}}}{\longrightarrow_{\tau}}{\longrightarrow_{\tau}}v\right)

(23)

To this end, we first show:

Claim 111
.

\big(\forall(x,S)\in V_{\mathsf{P}}\big)\big(\exists q\in Q_{\mathcal{A}}\big)\Big((x,q)\in T_{\mathsf{P}}\penalty\ \penalty\ \land\penalty\ \penalty\ q\in\pi_{2}(S)\Big)

Proof 9.13

(Proof of Claim 111)
.

Using the Induction Axiom of
\mathsf{FSO}

(§
3.4.2
), we show

(\forall x)(\forall S\in Q_{\oc\mathcal{A}})\Big((x,S)\in V_{\mathsf{P}}\penalty\ \penalty\ \Rightarrow\penalty\ \penalty\ (\exists q\in Q_{\mathcal{A}})\big((x,q)\in T_{\mathsf{P}}\penalty\ \penalty\ \land\penalty\ \penalty\ q\in\pi_{2}(S)\big)\Big)

For the base case
x=\dot{\varepsilon}
, if
(x,S)\in V
then we must have

S={q}^{\iota}_{\oc\mathcal{A}}
.
Then we are done since
\iota\in T

and
{q}^{\iota}_{\mathcal{A}}\in\pi_{2}(S)
.
Assume the property for
x
, and consider
\mathsf{S}_{d}(x)
and
S,S^{\prime}\in Q_{\oc\mathcal{A}}

such that
(x,S),(\mathsf{S}_{d}(x),S^{\prime})\in V
.
Furthermore, by induction hypothesis, let
q\in Q_{\mathcal{A}}

such that
(x,q)\in T
and
q\in\pi_{2}(S)
.
By definition of
V
, we have
(\mathsf{S}_{d}(x),q^{\prime})\in T
for some
q^{\prime}\in Q_{\mathcal{A}}
.
It then follows from Claim
106

that
q^{\prime}\in\pi_{2}(S^{\prime}_{d})
for some
S^{\prime}_{d}\in Q_{\oc\mathcal{A}}

such that
(\mathsf{S}_{d}(x),S^{\prime}_{d})\in V
.
But Claim
103

implies
S^{\prime}=S^{\prime}_{d}

so that
q^{\prime}\in\pi_{2}(S^{\prime})

and we are done.

We can now prove (
23
).

Proof 9.14

(Proof of (
23
))
.

If
u=(x,S)\in V
, then
v=(x,\tau(x,S))\in V
and is unique such that

u\mathchoice{\mathrel{\overset{}{\underset{\tau}{\longrightarrow}}}}{\mathrel{\longrightarrow_{\tau}}}{\longrightarrow_{\tau}}{\longrightarrow_{\tau}}v
.
Otherwise,
u=(x,\tau(x,S))
for some
(x,S)\in V
,
and we have to show that there are some unique

d\in\mathscr{D}
and
S^{\prime}_{d}\in Q_{\oc\mathcal{A}}
such that

(\mathsf{S}_{d}(x),S^{\prime}_{d})\in V
.
First, by Clim
111

there is some
q\in Q_{\mathcal{A}}
such that
(x,q)\in T

and
q\in\pi_{2}(S)
.
Moreover, since
T
is a play of
\sigma
, with have
(\mathsf{S}_{d}(x),q^{\prime})\in T

for some unique
d\in\mathscr{D}
and
q^{\prime}\in Q_{\mathcal{A}}
.
It then follows from Claim
106

that there is some
S^{\prime}_{d}\in Q_{\oc\mathcal{A}}

such that
(\mathsf{S}_{d}(x),S^{\prime}_{d})\in V

and
u\mathchoice{\mathrel{\overset{}{\underset{\tau}{\longrightarrow}}}}{\mathrel{\longrightarrow_{\tau}}}{\longrightarrow_{\tau}}{\longrightarrow_{\tau}}(\mathsf{S}_{d}(x),S^{\prime}_{d})
.
The uniqueness of
S^{\prime}_{d}
follows from
Claim
103
.

This concludes the proof of Proposition
102
.

In the proof of
Proposition
102
above,
we have used Claim
111

in order to show that
V
is a play of
\sigma
.
Let us state here for the record that this has a more general converse:
Claim
111

holds whenever
T
is a trace in
V
for
V
a play
in
\mathcal{G}(\oc\mathcal{A})
:

Lemma 112
.

Given
V:\mathcal{G}(\oc\mathcal{A})\mathrel{\mathfrak{to}}2
and
T:\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{A})\mathrel{\mathfrak{to}}2
,
in
{\mathsf{FSO}_{\mathscr{D}}}
we have

\big(\forall(x,S)\in V_{\mathsf{P}}\big)\big(\exists q\in Q_{\mathcal{A}}\big)\Big[(x,q)\in T_{\mathsf{P}}\penalty\ \penalty\ \land\penalty\ \penalty\ q\in\pi_{2}(S)\Big]

whenever

\mathrm{Play}\big(\mathcal{G}(\oc\mathcal{A}),\,(\dot{\varepsilon},{q}^{\iota}_{\oc\mathcal{A}}),\,V\big)\penalty\ \penalty\ \land\penalty\ \penalty\ \mathrm{Trace}(T,V)

Proof 9.15
.

Using the Induction Axiom of
\mathsf{FSO}

(§
3.4.2
), we show

(\forall x)\big(\forall S\in Q_{\oc\mathcal{A}}\big)\Big((x,S)\in V_{\mathsf{P}}\penalty\ \penalty\ \Rightarrow\penalty\ \penalty\ (\exists q\in Q_{\mathcal{A}})\Big[(x,q)\in T_{\mathsf{P}}\penalty\ \penalty\ \land\penalty\ \penalty\ q\in\pi_{2}(S)\Big]\Big)

For the base case
x=\dot{\varepsilon}
, if
(x,S)\in V
,
since

\mathrm{Play}(\mathcal{G}(\oc\mathcal{A}),(\dot{\varepsilon},{q}^{\iota}_{\oc\mathcal{A}}),V)

we must have

S={q}^{\iota}_{\oc\mathcal{A}}
, so
{q}^{\iota}_{\mathcal{A}}\in\pi_{2}(S)
.
Assume now the property for
x
, and consider

d\in\mathscr{D}
and
S,S^{\prime}\in Q_{\oc\mathcal{A}}

such that
(x,S)\in V
and
(\mathsf{S}_{d}(x),S^{\prime})\in V
.
Furthermore, by induction hypothesis, let
q\in Q_{\mathcal{A}}

such that
(x,q)\in T
and
q\in\pi_{2}(S)
.
It follows from
\mathrm{Path}(\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{A}),(\dot{\varepsilon},{q}^{\iota}_{\mathcal{A}}),T)

that
(\mathsf{S}_{d^{\prime}}(x),q^{\prime})\in T
for some
d^{\prime}\in\mathscr{D}

and some
q^{\prime}\in Q_{\mathcal{A}}
.
Moreover,
\mathrm{Trace}(T,V)
implies

q^{\prime}\in\pi_{2}(S^{\prime\prime})
for some
S^{\prime\prime}
such that

(\mathsf{S}_{d^{\prime}}(x),S^{\prime\prime})\in V
.
But

\mathrm{Play}(\mathcal{G}(\oc\mathcal{A}),(\dot{\varepsilon},{q}^{\iota}_{\oc\mathcal{A}}),V)

implies
d^{\prime}=d
and
S^{\prime\prime}=S^{\prime}
and we are done.

9.2.
Reformulating the Acceptance Condition of
\oc\mathcal{A}
.

For an automaton
\mathcal{A}
which we now assume to be
\mathrm{HF}
-closed
(in the sense of Definition
6.1
),
we are going to formulate the
\mathsf{FSO}
-formula
\mathcal{W}_{\oc\mathcal{A}}

as a parity condition, which will allow us to obtain a parity automaton
\mathrm{ND}(\mathcal{A})

in §
9.3
.
In order to obtain a parity condition from
\mathcal{W}_{\oc\mathcal{A}}
we note
(following
[
Wal02
]
) that (when read in the standard model) it defines
an
\omega
-regular condition, which can thus
by McNaughton’s Theorem
[
McN66
]

(see also e.g.
[
Tho97
,
PP04
]
)
be formulated with a deterministic parity automaton on
\omega
-words.
We are actually not going to formalize McNaughton’s Theorem in our setting.
Rather, we will apply Proposition
78
,
which allows us to import in
\mathsf{FSO}
any true
\mathsf{FSO}
-formula on the infinite
paths of
\mathscr{D}^{*}
.
Our way to the application of
Proposition
78

proceeds with constructions similar to some of those in the proof of
Theorem
93
.

Consider some
V:\mathcal{G}(\oc\mathcal{A})\mathrel{\mathfrak{to}}2

such that:

\mathrm{Play}(\mathcal{G}(\oc\mathcal{A}),\,(\dot{\varepsilon},{q}^{\iota}_{\oc\mathcal{A}}),\,V)

By Comprehension for Product Types (Theorem
23
),
let
|V|:\mathscr{D}^{*}\mathrel{\mathfrak{to}}2

be the set of all
x\in\mathscr{D}^{*}
such that

(x,S)\in V
for some
S\in Q_{\oc\mathcal{A}}
.
Note that
\mathrm{TPath}(|V|)

(recall that
\mathrm{TPath}
is defined in (
16
)).
Furthermore, by
\mathrm{HF}
-Bounded Choice for Functions (§
3.4.5
),
let
\tilde{V}:|V|\mathrel{\mathfrak{to}}Q_{\oc\mathcal{A}}

take
x\in|V|
to the unique
S\in Q_{\oc\mathcal{A}}

such that
(x,S)\in V
.

In
\mathsf{FSO}
we have that
\mathcal{W}_{\oc\mathcal{A}}

is equivalent to the following formula

\mathcal{W}[\mathrel{\dot{<}}]^{|V|}_{\oc\mathcal{A}}(\tilde{V})
:

\left(\forall\tilde{T}:|V|\mathrel{\mathfrak{to}}Q_{\mathcal{A}}\right)\left[\mathrm{Trace}[\mathrel{\dot{<}}]^{|V|}(\tilde{T},\tilde{V})\penalty\ \penalty\ \Rightarrow\penalty\ \penalty\ \mathrm{Par}[\mathrel{\dot{<}}]^{|V|}(C_{\mathcal{A}},n_{\mathcal{A}},\tilde{T})\right]

where

•

the formula

\mathrm{Trace}[\mathrel{\dot{<}}]^{|V|}(\tilde{T},\tilde{V})

is

\left\{\begin{array}[]{r l}&(\forall x\in|V|)\left[\tilde{T}(x)\in\pi_{2}(\tilde{V}(x))\right]\\
\land&(\forall x,y\in|V|)\left[\mathsf{S}_{\mathrel{\dot{<}}}(x,y)\penalty\ \penalty\ \Rightarrow\penalty\ \penalty\ (\tilde{T}(x),\tilde{T}(y))\in\tilde{V}(y)\right]\end{array}\right.

with

\mathsf{S}_{\mathrel{\dot{<}}}(x,y)\quad\mathrel{:=}\quad x\mathrel{\dot{<}}y\penalty\ \land\penalty\ \lnot\exists z\left(x\mathrel{\dot{<}}z\mathrel{\dot{<}}y\right)

•

and, for
C:Q_{\mathcal{A}}\mathrel{\mathfrak{to}}[0,n]
,
the formula

\mathrm{Par}[\mathrel{\dot{<}}]^{|V|}(C,n,\tilde{T})

is (using Convention
5.5
):

\big(\exists m\in\dot{\mathsf{even}}(n)\big)\left[\begin{array}[]{r l}&(\forall x\in|V|)(\exists y\in|V|)\big(x\mathrel{\dot{<}}y\penalty\ \penalty\ \land\penalty\ \penalty\ C(\tilde{T}(y))\mathrel{\doteq}m\big)\\
\land&(\exists x\in|V|)(\forall y\in|V|)\big(x\mathrel{\dot{<}}y\penalty\ \penalty\ \Rightarrow\penalty\ \penalty\ C(\tilde{T}(y))\geq m\big)\end{array}\right]

Let us first note the following simple property.
Recall from Definition
6.1

that
C_{\mathcal{A}}:Q_{\mathcal{A}}\mathrel{\mathfrak{to}}[0,n_{\mathcal{A}}]

is a coloring of the states of
\mathcal{A}
,
while
\hat{C}_{\mathcal{A}}
colors the positions of
\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{A})
,
by taking for
\mathsf{P}
-positions
(x,q)

the color given by
C_{\mathcal{A}}
to
q
and for
\mathsf{O}
-positions the maximal color

n_{\mathcal{A}}
.

Lemma 113
.

Assume given
V
and
|V|
as above.
Let
T:\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{A})\mathrel{\mathfrak{to}}2

and
\tilde{T}:|V|\mathrel{\mathfrak{to}}Q_{\mathcal{A}}

such that

\mathrm{Path}\big(\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{A}),(\dot{\varepsilon},{q}^{\iota}_{\mathcal{A}}),T\big)\quad\land\quad\big(\forall(x,q)\in\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{A})_{\mathsf{P}}\big)\left[(x,q)\in T\penalty\ \penalty\ \Leftrightarrow\penalty\ \penalty\ \left(x\in|V|\penalty\ \land\penalty\ \tilde{T}(x)=q\right)\right]

Then:

\mathrm{Par}\big(\mathcal{A},\hat{C}_{\mathcal{A}},n_{\mathcal{A}},T\big)\penalty\ \penalty\ \Leftrightarrow\penalty\ \penalty\ \mathrm{Par}[\mathrel{\dot{<}}]^{|V|}\big(C_{\mathcal{A}},n_{\mathcal{A}},\tilde{T}\big)

Lemma 114
.

Given

V
,
|V|
and
\tilde{V}
as above,

{\mathsf{FSO}_{\mathscr{D}}}
proves that

\mathcal{W}_{\oc\mathcal{A}}(V)\penalty\ \penalty\ \Leftrightarrow\penalty\ \penalty\ \mathcal{W}[\mathrel{\dot{<}}]^{|V|}_{\oc\mathcal{A}}(\tilde{V})

Proof 9.16
.

Recall that the formula
\mathcal{W}_{\oc\mathcal{A}}

requires no condition

of
\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{A})
.
We proceed as follows:

•

Assume first
\mathcal{W}_{\oc\mathcal{A}}(V)
, and let

\tilde{T}:|V|\mathrel{\mathfrak{to}}Q_{\mathcal{A}}
such that

\mathrm{Trace}[\mathrel{\dot{<}}]^{|V|}(\tilde{T},\tilde{V})
.
Using Remark
12
,
let
\prec
be a well-order on
\mathcal{P}_{*}(\mathscr{D}\times Q_{\mathcal{A}})
.
By Comprehension for Product Types
(Theorem
23
),
let
T:\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{A})\mathrel{\mathfrak{to}}2

such that
for all
(x,q)\in\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{A})_{\mathsf{P}}
, we have

(x,q)\in T_{\mathsf{P}}\penalty\ \penalty\ \Leftrightarrow\penalty\ \penalty\ \left(x\in|V|\penalty\ \land\penalty\ \tilde{T}(x)=q\right)

and such that
(x,\gamma)\in T_{\mathsf{O}}
iff

\gamma\in\mathcal{P}_{*}(\mathscr{D}\times Q_{\mathcal{A}})
is
\preceq
-minimal
such that
\tilde{T}(\mathsf{S}_{d}(x))=q
for some
(d,q)\in\gamma
.
Since

V
is an infinite play of
\mathcal{G}(\oc\mathcal{A})
from

(\dot{\varepsilon},{q}^{\iota}_{\oc\mathcal{A}})
,
we may conclude by Lemma
113

as soon as we show:

\mathrm{Trace}(T,V)

We obviously have
\mathrm{Path}(\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{A}),T)

as well as
(\dot{\varepsilon},{q}^{\iota}_{\mathcal{A}})\trianglelefteq_{\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{A})}u

for all
u\in T
.
Moreover we have:

\big(\forall(x,q)\in T_{\mathsf{P}}\big)\big(\exists S\in Q_{\oc\mathcal{A}}\big)\Big[(x,S)\in V_{\mathsf{P}}\penalty\ \penalty\ \land\penalty\ \penalty\ q\in\pi_{2}(S)\Big]

To see this, let
(x,q)\in T
, so that
\tilde{T}(x)=q
.
So by assumption we have
q\in\pi_{2}(\tilde{V}(x))
,
and we are done since
(x,\tilde{V}(x))\in V(x)
.

Finally we have

\big(\forall(x,q),(y,q^{\prime})\in T_{\mathsf{P}}\big)\big(\forall S\in Q_{\oc\mathcal{A}}\big)\left[(x,q)\vartriangleleft^{\mathsf{P};\mathsf{O}}_{\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{A})}(y,q^{\prime})\penalty\ \penalty\ \Rightarrow\penalty\ \penalty\ (y,S)\in V_{\mathsf{P}}\penalty\ \penalty\ \Rightarrow\penalty\ \penalty\ (q,q^{\prime})\in S\right]

To see this,
given
(x,q),(y,q^{\prime})\in T

such that

(x,q)\vartriangleleft^{\mathsf{P};\mathsf{O}}_{\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{A})}(y,q^{\prime})

we necessarily have

\mathsf{S}_{\mathrel{\dot{<}}}(x,y)
,
so that
(q,q^{\prime})\in\tilde{V}(y)

since
\tilde{T}(x)=q
and
\tilde{T}(y)=q^{\prime}
.
But then we are done since
\tilde{V}(y)
is the unique

S\in Q_{\oc\mathcal{A}}
such that
(y,S)\in V
.

•

Conversely, assume

\mathcal{W}[\mathrel{\dot{<}}]^{|V|}_{\oc\mathcal{A}}(\tilde{V})

and let
T:\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{A})\mathrel{\mathfrak{to}}2

such that
\mathrm{Trace}(T,V)
.
Since

V
is an infinite play of
\mathcal{G}(\oc\mathcal{A})
from

(\dot{\varepsilon},{q}^{\iota}_{\oc\mathcal{A}})
,
Lemma

implies:

\big(\forall(x,S)\in V_{\mathsf{P}}\big)\big(\exists q\in Q_{\mathcal{A}}\big)\Big[(x,q)\in T_{\mathsf{P}}\penalty\ \penalty\ \land\penalty\ \penalty\ q\in\pi_{2}(S)\Big]

It follows that for all
x\in|V|
there is
q\in Q_{\mathcal{A}}

such that
(x,q)\in T
, and this defines

\tilde{T}:|V|\mathrel{\mathfrak{to}}Q_{\mathcal{A}}

by
\mathrm{HF}
-Bounded Choice for Functions (§
3.4.5
).
Note that we have:

(\forall x)(\forall q\in Q_{\mathcal{A}})\left[(x,q)\in T_{\mathsf{P}}\penalty\ \penalty\ \Leftrightarrow\penalty\ \penalty\ \left(x\in|V|\penalty\ \land\penalty\ \tilde{T}(x)=q\right)\right]

We can then conclude by Lemma
113

as soon as we show:

\mathrm{Trace}[\mathrel{\dot{<}}]^{|V|}(\tilde{T},\tilde{V})

To see this,
first, for all
x\in|V|
, we have
(x,\tilde{T}(x))\in T
,
so that
T(x)\in\pi_{2}(\tilde{V}(x))
by definition of
\tilde{V}
.
Moreover, given

\mathsf{S}_{\mathrel{\dot{<}}}(x,y)

we have

so that
(\tilde{T}(x),\tilde{T}(y))\in\tilde{V}(y)

since
\mathrm{Trace}(T,V)
.

This concludes the proof of Lemma

.

We are now going to show that

\mathcal{W}[\mathrel{\dot{<}}]^{|V|}_{\oc\mathcal{A}}(\tilde{V})

\mathsf{FSO}
to a

automaton on
\omega
-words.
This relies on McNaughton’s Theorem
[
McN66
]

applied in the usual standard model
\mathfrak{N}
of
\omega
-words,
and, via Proposition

, on the completeness of

.
In order to apply Proposition
78
,
we rewrite

\mathcal{W}[\mathrel{\dot{<}}]^{|V|}_{\oc\mathcal{A}}(\tilde{V})

as the relativization to
|V|
of the
\mathsf{FSO}[\mathrel{\dot{<}}]^{\omega}
-formula

\mathcal{W}[\mathrel{\dot{<}}]_{\oc\mathcal{A}}(\tilde{V})\quad\mathrel{:=}\quad\big(\forall\tilde{T}:Q_{\mathcal{A}}\big)\left[\mathrm{Trace}[\mathrel{\dot{<}}](\tilde{V},\tilde{T})\penalty\ \penalty\ \Rightarrow\penalty\ \penalty\ \mathrm{Par}[\mathrel{\dot{<}}](C_{\mathcal{A}},n_{\mathcal{A}},\tilde{T})\right]

where

\mathrm{Par}[\mathrel{\dot{<}}](C,n,\tilde{T})
is the formula of
Definition
8.4.2
,
and where

\mathrm{Trace}[\mathrel{\dot{<}}](\tilde{V},\tilde{T})\quad\mathrel{:=}\quad\left\{\begin{array}[]{r l}&(\forall x)\left[\tilde{T}(x)\in\pi_{2}(\tilde{V}(x))\right]\\
\land&(\forall x)(\forall y)\left[\mathsf{S}_{\mathrel{\dot{<}}}(x,y)\penalty\ \penalty\ \Rightarrow\penalty\ \penalty\ (\tilde{T}(x),\tilde{T}(y))\in\tilde{V}(y)\right]\end{array}\right.

Note that

\mathcal{W}[\mathrel{\dot{<}}]^{|V|}_{\oc\mathcal{A}}(\tilde{V})
is the relativization
to
|V|
of

\mathcal{W}[\mathrel{\dot{<}}]_{\oc\mathcal{A}}(\tilde{V})
:

\mathcal{W}[\mathrel{\dot{<}}]^{|V|}_{\oc\mathcal{A}}(\tilde{V})\quad=\quad\left(\mathcal{W}[\mathrel{\dot{<}}]_{\oc\mathcal{A}}\right)^{|V|}(\tilde{V})

Since
\mathcal{A}
is
\mathrm{HF}
-closed, the formula

\mathcal{W}[\mathrel{\dot{<}}]_{\oc\mathcal{A}}(\tilde{V})
is also
\mathrm{HF}
-closed,
and we can look at it
in the standard model
\mathfrak{N}
of
\omega
-words (see §

).
By McNaughton’s Theorem
[

]

(see also e.g.
[

),
there is a deterministic parity

-word automaton

\mathcal{D}=(Q_{\mathcal{D}},\,{q}^{\iota}_{\mathcal{D}},\,\partial_{\mathcal{D}},\,c_{\mathcal{D}})

over

,
which

\tilde{V}
exactly when:

\mathfrak{N}\models\mathcal{W}[\mathrel{\dot{<}}]_{\oc\mathcal{A}}(\tilde{V})

It then follows
that in
\mathfrak{N}

for all
\tilde{V}:Q_{\oc\mathcal{A}}
,
the formula

\mathcal{W}[\mathrel{\dot{<}}]_{\oc\mathcal{A}}(\tilde{V})

is equivalent to

\big(\forall\tilde{R}:Q_{\mathcal{D}}\big)\bigg(\tilde{R}(\dot{\varepsilon})={q}^{\iota}_{\mathcal{D}}\penalty\ \penalty\ \Rightarrow\penalty\ \penalty\ \\
(\forall x)(\forall y)\left[\mathsf{S}_{\mathrel{\dot{<}}}(x,y)\penalty\ \penalty\ \Rightarrow\penalty\ \penalty\ \tilde{R}(y)=\partial_{\mathcal{D}}\big(\tilde{R}(x),\tilde{V}(x)\big)\right]\penalty\ \penalty\ \Rightarrow\penalty\ \penalty\ \mathrm{Par}[\mathrel{\dot{<}}]\big(C_{\mathcal{D}},n_{\mathcal{D}},\tilde{R}\big)\bigg)

Proposition
78
then implies that
\mathsf{FSO}

proves that for
\tilde{V}:|V|\mathrel{\mathfrak{to}}Q_{\oc\mathcal{A}}
,
the formula

\mathcal{W}[\mathrel{\dot{<}}]_{\oc\mathcal{A}}^{|V|}(\tilde{V})

is equivalent to

\big(\forall\tilde{R}:|V|\mathrel{\mathfrak{to}}Q_{\mathcal{D}}\big)\bigg(\tilde{R}(\dot{\varepsilon})={q}^{\iota}_{\mathcal{D}}\penalty\ \penalty\ \Rightarrow\penalty\ \penalty\ \\
(\forall x\in|V|)(\forall y\in|V|)\Big[\mathsf{S}_{\mathrel{\dot{<}}}(x,y)\penalty\ \penalty\ \Rightarrow\penalty\ \penalty\ \tilde{R}(y)=\partial_{\mathcal{D}}\big(\tilde{R}(x),\tilde{V}(x)\big)\Big]\penalty\ \penalty\ \Rightarrow\penalty\ \penalty\ \\
\mathrm{Par}[\mathrel{\dot{<}}]^{|V|}\big(C_{\mathcal{D}},n_{\mathcal{D}},\tilde{R}\big)\bigg)

9.3.
Definition of the Parity Automaton
\mathrm{ND}(\mathcal{A})
.

Consider an alternating parity tree automaton
\mathcal{A}:\Sigma

as in the beginning of §
9
,
and assume it to be
\mathrm{HF}
-closed.
Let
\oc\mathcal{A}:\Sigma
be defined as in §
9.1
.
Moreover, let
\mathcal{D}:Q_{\oc\mathcal{A}}
be the parity
deterministic
\omega
-word automaton
of §
9.2
.
We then let

\mathrm{ND}(\mathcal{A})\quad\mathrel{:=}\quad(Q_{\oc\mathcal{A}}\times Q_{\mathcal{D}}\penalty\ ,\penalty\ ({q}^{\iota}_{\oc\mathcal{A}},{q}^{\iota}_{\mathcal{D}})\penalty\ ,\penalty\ \partial_{\mathrm{ND}(\mathcal{A})}\penalty\ ,\penalty\ C_{\mathrm{ND}(\mathcal{A})}\penalty\ ,\penalty\ n_{\mathcal{D}})

where:

•

the transition function

\partial_{\mathrm{ND}(\mathcal{A})}\quad:\quad(Q_{\oc\mathcal{A}}\times Q_{\mathcal{D}})\times\Sigma\quad\longrightarrow\quad\mathcal{P}_{*}(\mathcal{P}_{*}(\mathscr{D}\times(Q_{\oc\mathcal{A}}\times Q_{\mathcal{D}})))

takes
((S,q),\mathtt{a})

to the set of all
\gamma\in\mathcal{P}_{*}(\mathcal{P}_{*}(\mathscr{D}\times(Q_{\oc\mathcal{A}}\times Q_{\mathcal{D}})))

such that for some
\gamma_{\oc\mathcal{A}}\in\partial_{\oc\mathcal{A}}(S,\mathtt{a})
,

\gamma\quad=\quad\Big\{\big(d\penalty\ ,\penalty\ \big(S^{\prime}_{d}\penalty\ ,\penalty\ \partial_{\mathcal{D}}(q,S)\big)\big)\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ |\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ (d,S^{\prime}_{d})\in\gamma_{\oc\mathcal{A}}\Big\}

•

the coloring

C_{\mathrm{ND}(\mathcal{A})}:Q_{\oc\mathcal{A}}\times Q_{\mathcal{D}}\mathrel{\mathfrak{to}}[0,n_{\mathcal{D}}]

takes
(S,q)
to
C_{\mathcal{D}}(q)
.

Note that
\mathrm{ND}(\mathcal{A}):\Sigma
is
\mathrm{HF}
-closed by
Remark
49
.
We shall now show that
\mathrm{ND}(\mathcal{A})
is equivalent to
\mathcal{A}
,
thus completing the proof of the Simulation Theorem

.
The proof is split into

and

.
As expected, we invoke Theorem

, that
{\mathsf{FSO}_{\mathscr{D}}}
proves the equivalence of
!\mathcal{A}
and
\mathcal{A}
.

{conv}

In Propositions

and

,
for fixed automata

\iota_{\oc}\penalty\ \penalty\ \mathrel{:=}\penalty\ \penalty\ (\dot{\varepsilon},{q}^{\iota}_{\oc\mathcal{A}})\qquad\text{and}\qquad\iota_{\mathrm{ND}}\penalty\ \penalty\ \mathrel{:=}\penalty\ \penalty\ (\dot{\varepsilon},{q}^{\iota}_{\mathrm{ND}(\mathcal{A})})

.

.

When proving that

Proposition 116
.

q_{\mathcal{D}}\in Q_{\mathcal{D}}

(d,(S^{\prime}_{d},q_{\mathcal{D}}))\in\gamma_{\mathrm{ND}(\mathcal{A})}

for
((S,{q}^{\iota}_{\mathcal{D}}),\gamma_{\mathrm{ND}(\mathcal{A})})=\tau(x,(S,{q}^{\iota}_{\mathcal{D}}))
.

We are now going to show that

is winning.
To this end, consider an infinite play of

, that is some

such that

\mathrm{Play}(\sigma,\,\iota_{\oc},\,V)

We are going to define an infinite play of

First, note that we are done if

Since

\mathrm{Par}(\mathcal{G}(\mathrm{ND}(\mathcal{A})),\hat{C}_{\mathrm{ND}(\mathcal{A})},n_{\mathrm{ND}(\mathcal{A})},U)

\mathcal{W}[\mathrel{\dot{<}}]^{|V|}_{\oc\mathcal{A}}(\tilde{V})

We now define an infinite play

By the Recursion Theorem (Proposition

) we let

\big(\forall u\in\mathcal{G}(\mathrm{ND}(\mathcal{A}))\big)\Big[u\in U\penalty\ \penalty\ \Leftrightarrow\penalty\ \penalty\ \varphi(U,u)\Big]

)
and:

\mathrm{Play}(\tau,\,\iota_{\mathrm{ND}},\,U)

We first show

.

\left\{\begin{array}[]{c l}&(\forall u\in U)\big(\iota_{\mathrm{ND}}\mathchoice{\mathrel{\overset{*}{\underset{\tau}{\longrightarrow}}}}{\mathrel{\longrightarrow^{*}_{\tau}}}{\longrightarrow^{*}_{\tau}}{\longrightarrow^{*}_{\tau}}u\big)\\

We proceed similarly to the proof of

.
First, we prove:

Proof 9.19

.

If

But then we are done since we indeed have:

\big(x\penalty\ ,\penalty\ \tau(x,(S,q_{\mathcal{D}}))\big)\penalty\ \penalty\ \mathchoice{\mathrel{\overset{}{\underset{\tau}{\longrightarrow}}}}{\mathrel{\longrightarrow_{\tau}}}{\longrightarrow_{\tau}}{\longrightarrow_{\tau}}\penalty\ \penalty\ \big(\mathsf{S}_{d}(x)\penalty\ ,(S^{\prime}_{d},q^{\prime}_{\mathcal{D}})\big)

.

(\forall u\in U)\left(\iota_{\mathrm{ND}}\mathchoice{\mathrel{\overset{*}{\underset{\tau}{\longrightarrow}}}}{\mathrel{\longrightarrow^{*}_{\tau}}}{\longrightarrow^{*}_{\tau}}{\longrightarrow^{*}_{\tau}}u\right)

.

We proceed by

So let

.

.

again.

It remains to show

(25)

We first prove:

Claim 119
.

\big(\forall(x,(S,q_{\mathcal{D}}))\in U\big)\Big[(x,S)\in V\Big]

Proof 9.21

(Proof of Claim 119)
.

The property follows from a case analysis according

(\forall x)\left(x\mathrel{\doteq}\dot{\varepsilon}\penalty\ \penalty\ \lor\penalty\ \penalty\ (\exists y)\mathop{\bigvee}_{d\in\mathscr{D}}x\mathrel{\doteq}\mathsf{S}_{d}(y)\right)

In the case of

S={q}^{\iota}_{\oc\mathcal{A}}
, so that

.
Consider now the case of
x\mathrel{\doteq}\mathsf{S}_{d}(y)
.
If
u=(x,(S,q_{\mathcal{D}}))\in U
, then it follows from

\varphi(U,u)
that we have
(x,S)\in V
and we are done.

We can now prove (

Proof 9.22

(Proof of (
25
))
.

If
u=(x,(S,q_{\mathcal{D}}))

(x,\tau(x,(S,q_{\mathcal{D}})))

is the unique successor of

in

.
Assume
u=(x,\tau(x,(S,q_{\mathcal{D}})))
.
It then follows from Claim

(x,(S,q_{\mathcal{D}}))\in U
,

Since

(\mathsf{S}_{d}(x),S^{\prime}_{d})\in V

and

q^{\prime}_{\mathcal{D}}=\partial_{\mathcal{D}}(q_{\mathcal{D}},S)
.

u\mathchoice{\mathrel{\overset{}{\underset{\tau}{\longrightarrow}}}}{\mathrel{\longrightarrow_{\tau}}}{\longrightarrow_{\tau}}{\longrightarrow_{\tau}}v

Uniqueness follows from

and the fact that
V
is a play,
so it remains to show

u\mathchoice{\mathrel{\overset{}{\underset{\tau}{\longrightarrow}}}}{\mathrel{\longrightarrow_{\tau}}}{\longrightarrow_{\tau}}{\longrightarrow_{\tau}}v
.
Note that
(x,(S,q_{\mathcal{D}}))\in U
implies

(x,\tau(x,(S,q_{\mathcal{D}})))\in U
,
and similarly, that
(x,S)\in V
implies
(s,\sigma(x,S))\in V
.
By definition of
\sigma
, we have
\sigma(x,S)=(S,\gamma_{\oc\mathcal{A}})

where
\gamma_{\oc\mathcal{A}}
is the set of all
(\tilde{d},S^{\prime}_{\tilde{d}})

(\tilde{d},(S^{\prime}_{\tilde{d}},q^{\prime}_{\mathcal{D}}))\in\gamma_{\mathrm{ND}(\mathcal{A})}
,
where

((S,q_{\mathcal{D}}),\gamma_{\mathrm{ND}(\mathcal{A})})=\tau(x,(S,q_{\mathcal{D}}))

This finishes the proof since we indeed have:

\big(x\penalty\ ,\penalty\ \tau(x,(S,q_{\mathcal{D}}))\big)\penalty\ \penalty\ \mathchoice{\mathrel{\overset{}{\underset{\tau}{\longrightarrow}}}}{\mathrel{\longrightarrow_{\tau}}}{\longrightarrow_{\tau}}{\longrightarrow_{\tau}}\penalty\ \penalty\ \big(\mathsf{S}_{d}(x)\penalty\ ,\penalty\ (S^{\prime}_{d},q^{\prime}_{\mathcal{D}})\big)

Finally, we prove (
24
),
that is:

\big(\forall(x,S)\in V\big)\big(\exists q_{\mathcal{D}}\in Q_{\mathcal{D}}\big)\Big[(x,(S,q_{\mathcal{D}}))\in U\Big]

Proof 9.23

(Proof of (
24
))
.

Using the Induction Axiom of
\mathsf{FSO}

(§
3.4.2
), we show

(\forall x)(\forall S\in Q_{\oc\mathcal{A}})\Big((x,S)\in V\penalty\ \penalty\ \Rightarrow\penalty\ \penalty\ (\exists q_{\mathcal{D}}\in Q_{\mathcal{D}})\big((x,(S,q_{\mathcal{D}}))\in U\big)\Big)

For the base case
x\mathrel{\doteq}\dot{\varepsilon}
,
if
(x,S)\in V
then we must have
S={q}^{\iota}_{\oc\mathcal{A}}
,
and we indeed obtain
(x,(S,{q}^{\iota}_{\mathcal{D}}))\in U
.
Assume now the property for
x
, and consider some
d\in\mathscr{D}

and
S^{\prime}_{d}
such that
(\mathsf{S}_{d}(x),S^{\prime}_{d})\in V
.
Since
V
is a play, it follows from
the Predecessor Lemma
37
for Infinite Plays
(applied twice)
that
(x,S)\in V
for some
S\in Q_{\oc\mathcal{A}}
.
It follows by induction hypothesis that
(x,(S,q_{\mathcal{D}}))\in U

for some
q\in Q_{\mathcal{D}}
.
But now, taking
q^{\prime}_{\mathcal{D}}=\partial_{\mathcal{D}}(q_{\mathcal{D}},S)
,
we have

(\mathsf{S}_{d}(x),(S^{\prime}_{d},q^{\prime}_{\mathcal{D}}))\in U
and we are done.

This concludes the proof of Proposition
116
.

10.
Conclusion

In this paper, we proposed for each non-empty (hereditarily) finite
set
\mathscr{D}
the theory
{\mathsf{FSO}_{\mathscr{D}}}
of

Functional (Monadic) Second-Order Logic

on the full (infinite)
\mathscr{D}
-ary tree.
The theory
{\mathsf{FSO}_{\mathscr{D}}}
(henceforth
\mathsf{FSO}
) is
a uniform extension of
\mathsf{MSO}
on the full
\mathscr{D}
-ary tree
with hereditarily finite sets.
We formalized in
\mathsf{FSO}
a basic theory of (alternating) tree automata
and (acceptance) games.
This allowed us, in the theory of
\mathsf{FSO}

augmented with an axiom
(\mathit{PosDet})
of positional determinacy of parity games,
to formalize a translation of
\mathsf{MSO}
-formulae to automata
adapted from
[
Wal02
]
.
We then deduced the completeness of
\mathsf{FSO}+(\mathit{PosDet})

thanks to a variant of the Büchi-Landweber Theorem
[
BL69
]
,
stating that
\mathsf{MSO}
decides winning for (definable) games of finite graphs
(and obtained thanks to the completeness of
\mathsf{MSO}
over

\omega
-words
[
Sie70
]
).
By naive proof enumeration, this gives a proof
of Rabin’s Tree Theorem
[
Rab69
]
,
the decidability of
\mathsf{MSO}
on infinite trees.
Moreover, since the formal theory
\mathsf{FSO}
is conservative
(w.r.t. the faithful translation
(-)^{\circ}:\mathsf{MSO}\to\mathsf{FSO}
)
over a natural set of axioms for
\mathsf{MSO}
,
we also get a complete axiomatization of
\mathsf{MSO}
on infinite trees, namely
{\mathsf{MSO}_{\mathscr{D}}}+\langle\mathit{PosDet}\rangle
(cf. Definition
5.6.1
, §
5.6.1
).

10.1.
Proof theoretic strength of complementation

The present paper does not discuss proof theoretic strength.
In the context of second-order arithmetic
(in the sense of
[
Sim10
]
), it is known that
complementation of tree automata is between
\Pi^{1}_{3}
and

\Delta^{1}_{3}
-comprehension
[
KM16
]
.
As far as only games are concerned
(as opposed to proving the correctness of
an internal function for complementation), only
\Pi^{1}_{2}
-comprehension
is required for the positional determinacy of (each level of)
parity games
[
KM15
, Lemma 4.6]
.

10.2.
Clarifying the status of
\langle\mathit{PosDet}\rangle

A problem arising from this work is whether the axiom schema
\langle\mathit{PosDet}\rangle
is indeed independent of
\mathsf{MSO}
. The latter may be seen as the monadic fragment of
\mathsf{PA}2
(over the appropriate language) and, as we have mentioned, is complete when restricted to infinite words.
While it might therefore be natural to suspect that
\mathsf{MSO}
is already complete without
\langle\mathit{PosDet}\rangle
, we point out that the axiomatization of Weak
\mathsf{MSO}

over infinite trees given in
[
Sie78
]
also augments the natural fragment of Peano arithmetic by an axiom of induction over finite trees.
As we mentioned in the Introduction, the completeness of
{\mathsf{MSO}_{\mathscr{D}}}
was erroneously claimed in the preliminary version of this work
[
DR15
]
.

10.3.
On the notion of proof for
\mathsf{MSO}

One outcome of this work is that our complete deduction
system for
\mathsf{MSO}

gives a new decision algorithm.
Of course, the naive decision algorithm by proof enumeration is
not very sophisticated,
and it is worth
restating that its correctness is itself driven by the usual automata-theoretic argument.
Such an algorithm, nonetheless, makes no mention of automata and so can be
adapted and improved purely in the setting of proof theory.
In this sense, the algorithm is the first of its kind:
a decision procedure for
\mathsf{MSO}
on infinite trees that remains internal to the language, rather than requiring intermediate translations to automata.

A basic motivation for such algorithms
is that, even if Rabin’s Tree Theorem proves the existence
of decision procedures for
\mathsf{MSO}
on infinite trees, there is
(as far as we know) no working implementation of such procedures.
3
3

3

To our knowledge, the
Mona
tool
(
https://www.brics.dk/mona/
) only handles
Weak

\mathsf{MSO}
.

Our axiomatization instead allows the targeting of
(semi) automatic approaches, for instance based on proof assistants.
As we mentioned in the Introduction, our axiomatization is polynomial-time recognizable and so indeed yields a meaningful notion of ‘proof certificate’: a proof of a theorem may be easily checked, without having to reprove the theorem again.

10.4.
Constructive systems and proof interpretations.

A further direction of research is to look for constructive interpretations of
\mathsf{MSO}
.
In the case of
\omega
-words, preliminary steps were made
in
[
PR19
]
.
The general idea is to proceed along the following steps:

(1)

Determine the relevant computational information one should be able to extract
from constructive proofs.

(2)

Devise constructive variants of
\mathsf{MSO}

(together with suitable proof interpretations),
which are correct and complete
for the chosen class of functions
w.r.t. to their provable
\forall\exists
-sentences.

A realizability model for
\mathsf{MSO}
has been proposed in
[
Rib20
]
,
in which the underlying logic is not only constructive but also

linear
(in the sense of
[
Gir87
]
).
Of course, similar approaches may also be considered in more
traditional settings for constructive interpretations of
proofs
[
TvD88
,
Koh08
]
.
